## Foreword / 序言
===Original===

There has of late years crept so much confusion into the mind of the student as well as of the general reader as to the identity of the Scarlet Pimpernel with that of the Gascon Royalist plotter known to history as the Baron de Batz, that the time seems opportune for setting all doubts on that subject at rest.
The identity of the Scarlet Pimpernel is in no way whatever connected with that of the Baron de Batz, and even superficial reflection will soon bring the mind to the conclusion that great fundamental differences existed in these two men, in their personality, in their character, and, above all, in their aims.
According to one or two enthusiastic historians, the Baron de Batz was the chief agent in a vast network of conspiracy, entirely supported by foreign money﻿—both English and Austrian﻿—and which had for its object the overthrow of the Republican Government and the restoration of the monarchy in France.
In order to attain this political goal, it is averred that he set himself the task of pitting the members of the revolutionary Government one against the other, and bringing hatred and dissensions amongst them, until the cry of “Traitor!” resounded from one end of the Assembly of the Convention to the other, and the Assembly itself became as one vast den of wild beasts wherein wolves and hyenas devoured one another and, still unsatiated, licked their streaming jaws hungering for more prey.
Those same enthusiastic historians, who have a firm belief in the so-called “Foreign Conspiracy,” ascribe every important event of the Great Revolution﻿—be that event the downfall of the Girondins, the escape of the Dauphin from the Temple, or the death of Robespierre﻿—to the intrigues of Baron de Batz. He it was, so they say, who egged the Jacobins on against the Mountain, Robespierre against Danton, Hébert against Robespierre. He it was who instigated the massacres of September, the atrocities of Nantes, the horrors of Thermidor, the sacrileges, the noyades: all with the view of causing every section of the National Assembly to vie with the other in excesses and in cruelty, until the makers of the Revolution, satiated with their own lust, turned on one another, and Sardanapalus-like buried themselves and their orgies in the vast hecatomb of a self-consumed anarchy.

===Chinese===

近年来，无论学者还是普通读者，心中都滋生出越来越多的困惑：红花侠（the Scarlet Pimpernel）与史上号称德巴茨男爵（Baron de Batz）的加斯科尼保王党阴谋家，究竟是不是同一个人。看来，是时候把这个问题上的一切疑虑一笔勾销了。
红花侠的身份同德巴茨男爵绝无半点干系；哪怕浮光掠影地想一想，也很快会得出结论：这两人之间存在重大的根本差异——个性上的，品格上的，尤其是目标上的。
照一两位热情过望的史家的说法，德巴茨男爵是一张庞大阴谋网的首席代理人，其经费全靠外国银子——英国的、奥地利的——撑持，其目的则是推翻共和政府，在法兰西复辟君主制。
据说，为达成这一政治鹄的，他给自己派下的差使，便是叫革命政府的成员们彼此相斗，在他们中间播下仇恨与不和，直到“叛徒！”的呼喊响彻国民公会（the Assembly of the Convention）的首尾两端，公会本身化作一座巨大的兽窟，狼与鬣狗互相吞噬，犹嫌不足，还舔着流涎的口颚，觊觎更多猎物。
也正是这班笃信所谓“外国阴谋”的热情史家，把大革命的每一桩大事——吉伦特派的垮台也好，王太子（the Dauphin）逃出圣殿塔也好，罗伯斯庇尔（Robespierre）之死也好——统统记到德巴茨男爵的阴谋账上。他们说，是他撺掇雅各宾派斗山岳派，撺掇罗伯斯庇尔斗丹东（Danton），撺掇埃贝尔（Hébert）斗罗伯斯庇尔。是他煽动了九月屠杀、南特的暴行、热月（Thermidor）的恐怖、渎神与溺刑（noyades）：一切都是为着叫国民议会的每一派同别的派竞相穷凶、竞相极恶，直到大革命的制造者们餍足了自己的贪欲，掉头互噬，像萨达纳帕勒斯那样，把自己和自己的狂欢，一同葬进一场自我吞噬的无政府状态所堆起的巨大血祭里。

===Original===

Whether the power thus ascribed to Baron de Batz by his historians is real or imaginary it is not the purpose of this preface to investigate. Its sole object is to point out the difference between the career of this plotter and that of the Scarlet Pimpernel.
The Baron de Batz himself was an adventurer without substance, save that which he derived from abroad. He was one of those men who have nothing to lose and everything to gain by throwing themselves headlong in the seething cauldron of internal politics.
Though he made several attempts at rescuing King Louis first, and then the Queen and Royal Family from prison and from death, he never succeeded, as we know, in any of these undertakings, and he never once so much as attempted the rescue of other equally innocent, if not quite so distinguished, victims of the most bloodthirsty revolution that has ever shaken the foundations of the civilised world.
Nay more; when on the 29th Prairial those unfortunate men and women were condemned and executed for alleged complicity in the so-called “Foreign Conspiracy,” de Batz, who is universally admitted to have been the head and prime-mover of that conspiracy﻿—if, indeed, conspiracy there was﻿—never made either the slightest attempt to rescue his confederates from the guillotine, or at least the offer to perish by their side if he could not succeed in saving them.
And when we remember that the martyrs of the 29th Prairial included women like Grandmaison, the devoted friend of de Batz, the beautiful Émilie de St. Amaranthe, little Cécile Renault﻿—a mere child not sixteen years of age﻿—also men like Michonis and Roussell, faithful servants of de Batz, the Baron de Lézardière, and the Comte de St. Maurice, his friends, we no longer can have the slightest doubt that the Gascon plotter and the English gentleman are indeed two very different persons.
The latter’s aims were absolutely nonpolitical. He never intrigued for the restoration of the monarchy, or even for the overthrow of that Republic which he loathed.
His only concern was the rescue of the innocent, the stretching out of a saving hand to those unfortunate creatures who had fallen into the nets spread out for them by their fellow-men; by those who﻿—godless, lawless, penniless themselves﻿—had sworn to exterminate all those who clung to their belongings, to their religion, and to their beliefs.

===Chinese===

他的史家们加在德巴茨男爵头上的这份神通，究竟是真是幻，不是这篇序言要考究的。序言唯一的用意，是指明这位阴谋家的行状同红花侠的行状之别。
德巴茨男爵本人是个身无长物的冒险家，除了从国外领取的那份进项。他属于这样一种人：一头扎进国内政治这口滚沸的大锅里，一无所有可输，一切皆可赢得。
诚如我们所知，他几次三番试图营救国王路易，继而又营救王后与王室，救他们出监狱、出死地，却从未有一桩得手；而且，对那场史上最嗜血、撼动过文明世界根基的大革命的其他受害者——他们同样清白，只是不那么显赫——他连一次营救的尝试都不曾做过。
更有甚者：牧月（Prairial）二十九日，那批不幸的男女因涉嫌参与所谓“外国阴谋”被判死刑处决之时，公认是那场阴谋——倘若真有阴谋的话——的首脑与主谋的德巴茨，非但丝毫没有设法把同党从断头台刀下救出，甚至连与他们并肩赴死的心意也不曾表示过一分。
再想想牧月二十九日的殉难者中有哪些人：格朗梅宗，德巴茨忠贞的朋友；美丽的埃米莉·德·圣阿马朗特（Émilie de St. Amaranthe）；小小的塞西尔·雷诺（Cécile Renault）——一个还不满十六岁的孩子；还有米绍尼和鲁塞尔，德巴茨忠实的仆从；莱扎迪埃尔男爵和圣莫里斯伯爵，他的朋友——我们便再不能有丝毫怀疑：那位加斯科尼阴谋家与那位英国绅士，实在是迥然不同的两个人。
后者的目标绝对不涉政治。他从不为复辟君主制搞阴谋，甚至也不为推翻那个他深恶痛绝的共和国搞阴谋。
他唯一挂怀的，是搭救无辜者，是向那些落进同类所布罗网的不幸者伸出救援之手；布网的那些人，自己无神、无法、无钱，却发誓要灭绝所有执着于自己的财产、自己的宗教、自己的信仰的人。

===Original===

The Scarlet Pimpernel did not take it upon himself to punish the guilty; his care was solely of the helpless and of the innocent.
For this aim he risked his life every time that he set foot on French soil, for it he sacrificed his fortune, and even his personal happiness, and to it he devoted his entire existence.
Moreover, whereas the French plotter is said to have had confederates even in the Assembly of the Convention, confederates who were sufficiently influential and powerful to secure his own immunity, the Englishman when he was bent on his errands of mercy had the whole of France against him.
The Baron de Batz was a man who never justified either his own ambitions or even his existence; the Scarlet Pimpernel was a personality of whom an entire nation might justly be proud.

===Chinese===

红花侠不曾把惩罚罪人引为己任；他唯一顾念的，是无助者与无辜者。
为着这个目标，他每一次踏上法兰西的土地，都是把性命押上；为着它，他舍了家财，甚至舍了个人的幸福；为着它，他献上了整个一生。
再者，据说那位法兰西阴谋家连国民公会里都安插着同党，同党势力够大、权柄够重，保得他自身无恙；而那位英国人一旦踏上他的仁义差使，是整个法兰西都与他为敌。
德巴茨男爵这个人，从未让他自己的野心、甚至连他的存在本身，有什么可以说得过去的理由；红花侠，却是一个整个民族都可以引以为豪的人物。

## Part I / 第一部
===Original===



===Chinese===

第一部

## I In the Théâtre National / 在国家剧院
===Original===

And yet people found the opportunity to amuse themselves, to dance and to go to the theatre, to enjoy music and open-air cafés and promenades in the Palais Royal.
New fashions in dress made their appearance, milliners produced fresh “creations,” and jewellers were not idle. A grim sense of humour, born of the very intensity of ever-present danger, had dubbed the cut of certain tunics “*tête tranchée*,” or a favourite ragoût was called “à la guillotine.”
On three evenings only during the past memorable four and a half years did the theatres close their doors, and these evenings were the ones immediately following that terrible 2nd of September﻿—the day of the butchery outside the Abbaye prison, when Paris herself was aghast with horror, and the cries of the massacred might have drowned the calls of the audience whose hands upraised for plaudits would still be dripping with blood.
On all other evenings of these same four and a half years the theatres in the Rue de Richelieu, in the Palais Royal, the Luxembourg, and others, had raised their curtains and taken money at their doors. The same audience that earlier in the day had whiled away the time by witnessing the ever-recurrent dramas of the Place de la Révolution assembled here in the evenings and filled stalls, boxes, and tiers, laughing over the satires of Voltaire or weeping over the sentimental tragedies of persecuted Romeos and innocent Juliets.
Death knocked at so many doors these days! He was so constant a guest in the houses of relatives and friends that those who had merely shaken him by the hand, those on whom he had smiled, and whom he, still smiling, had passed indulgently by, looked on him with that subtle contempt born of familiarity, shrugged their shoulders at his passage, and envisaged his probable visit on the morrow with lighthearted indifference.
Paris﻿—despite the horrors that had stained her walls﻿—had remained a city of pleasure, and the knife of the guillotine did scarce descend more often than did the drop-scenes on the stage.
On this bitterly cold evening of the 27th Nivôse, in the second year of the Republic﻿—or, as we of the old style still persist in calling it, the 16th of January, 1794﻿—the auditorium of the Théâtre National was filled with a very brilliant company.

===Chinese===

然而，人们照样寻得寻欢作乐的机会：跳舞、看戏、听音乐、坐露天咖啡座、在王宫花园里漫步。
衣饰的新式样层出不穷，女帽商推出一件件新鲜“作品”，珠宝匠也不得闲。一种从无时不在的危险之极致里生出的阴森幽默，给某些外衣的裁法起名叫“*断头式*”，一道人们喜爱的炖肉则唤作“断头台式”。
在过去那难忘的四年半里，戏院统共只有三个晚上歇业，那便是恐怖的九月二日之后的头三个晚上——阿贝伊监狱外大屠杀的日子，那时连巴黎自己也惊骇欲绝，被屠戮者的哀号，怕是要淹没看客的喝彩——那一只只为喝彩而举起的手，鲜血还未滴尽。
而这四年半里的其余每一个晚上，黎塞留街的、王宫的、卢森堡的各家戏院，照旧升起幕布，照旧在门口收钱。白天里靠革命广场上一幕幕轮番上演的真戏消磨时光的，正是同一批看客，晚上聚到这里，坐满正厅、包厢和楼座，为伏尔泰的讽刺剧而笑，为受迫害的罗密欧和无辜的朱丽叶那种缠绵悲剧而哭。
这些日子，死神敲了多少人家的门啊！他在亲朋宅中做客做得太勤了，以致那些不过同他握过手的、他微笑相待、又含笑宽容放过的，都对他生出一种熟稔生的轻蔑，他走过时只耸一耸肩，想到他明天或许登门，也不过付之一笑，满不在乎。
巴黎——尽管恐怖染污了她的墙壁——依旧是座享乐之城，断头台的铡刀落下的次数，也未必比舞台上的落幕更勤。
共和国二年雨月二十七日——照我们这些旧历派的顽固叫法，即1794年1月16日——这个严寒刺骨的晚上，国家剧院的观众席上冠盖云集。

===Original===

The appearance of a favourite actress in the part of one of Molière’s volatile heroines had brought pleasure-loving Paris to witness this revival of *Le Misanthrope*, with new scenery, dresses, and the aforesaid charming actress to add piquancy to the master’s mordant wit.
The *Moniteur*, which so impartially chronicles the events of those times, tells us under that date that the Assembly of the Convention voted on that same day a new law giving fuller power to its spies, enabling them to effect domiciliary searches at their discretion without previous reference to the Committee of General Security, authorising them to proceed against all enemies of public happiness, to send them to prison at their own discretion, and assuring them the sum of thirty-five livres “for every piece of game thus beaten up for the guillotine.” Under that same date the *Moniteur* also puts it on record that the Théâtre National was filled to its utmost capacity for the revival of the late *citoyen* Molière’s comedy.
The Assembly of the Convention having voted the new law which placed the lives of thousands at the mercy of a few human bloodhounds, adjourned its sitting and proceeded to the Rue de Richelieu.
Already the house was full when the fathers of the people made their way to the seats which had been reserved for them. An awed hush descended on the throng as one by one the men whose very names inspired horror and dread filed in through the narrow gangways of the stalls or took their places in the tiny boxes around.
Citizen Robespierre’s neatly bewigged head soon appeared in one of these; his bosom friend St. Just was with him, and also his sister Charlotte. Danton, like a big, shaggy-coated lion, elbowed his way into the stalls, whilst Sauterre, the handsome butcher and idol of the people of Paris, was loudly acclaimed as his huge frame, gorgeously clad in the uniform of the National Guard, was sighted on one of the tiers above.
The public in the parterre and in the galleries whispered excitedly; the awe-inspiring names flew about hither and thither on the wings of the overheated air. Women craned their necks to catch sight of heads which mayhap on the morrow would roll into the gruesome basket at the foot of the guillotine.
In one of the tiny *avant-scène* boxes two men had taken their seats long before the bulk of the audience had begun to assemble in the house.

===Chinese===

一位当红女演员出演莫里哀笔下一位轻盈善变的女主角，把贪恋欢乐的巴黎都引来看《愤世者》的重演——新布景、新行头，加上前述那位迷人的女演员，为大师辛辣的机智又添了几分滋味。
《导报》——那份将当时诸事记录得如此不偏不倚的报纸——在那一日记着：就在同一天，国民公会表决通过了一部新法，赋予它的密探们更充分的权力：可以自行其是地搜查民宅，无须事先知会治安委员会；可以着手惩办一切“公共幸福之敌”，随意把他们投入监狱；并保证每“为断头台赶起一件猎物”，赏三十五里弗。同一天的《导报》还记下一笔：为着已故*公民*莫里哀这出喜剧的重演，国家剧院座无虚席，人满为患。
国民公会表决完这部把千万人的性命交到几条人形警犬手里的新法，便散了会，往黎塞留街去了。
人民之父们鱼贯而入走向为他们留好的座位时，剧院里已经满座。那一个个单是名字就叫人心惊胆战的人物，沿着正厅的窄甬道鱼贯而入，或落座四周的小包厢，人群里霎时静了下来，满是敬畏。公民罗伯斯庇尔那颗戴着齐整假发的脑袋很快出现在其中一个包厢里；同他在一起的，是他的密友圣茹斯特，还有他的妹妹夏洛特。丹东像一头鬃毛蓬乱的大狮子，挤进正厅座位；而索泰尔——英俊的屠夫、巴黎民众的偶像——当他那裹在国民自卫队华美军服里的魁伟身躯在上层楼座露面时，四下响起了热烈的喝彩。
正厅后座和顶层楼座里，观众兴奋地交头接耳；那些令人胆寒的名字，借着窒热空气的翅膀飞来飞去。女人们伸长脖颈，只为看一眼那些脑袋——也许明天，它们就要滚落进断头台脚下那只阴森的篮子里。
在一间临台的小包厢里，两个男人早早落了座，比大批看客进场上座早得多。

===Original===

The inside of the box was in complete darkness, and the narrow opening which allowed but a sorry view of one side of the stage helped to conceal rather than display the occupants.
The younger one of these two men appeared to be something of a stranger in Paris, for as the public men and the well-known members of the Government began to arrive he often turned to his companion for information regarding these notorious personalities.
“Tell me, de Batz,” he said, calling the other’s attention to a group of men who had just entered the house, “that creature there in the green coat﻿—with his hand up to his face now﻿—who is he?”
“Where? Which do you mean?”
“There! He looks this way now, and he has a playbill in his hand. The man with the protruding chin and the convex forehead, a face like a marmoset, and eyes like a jackal. What?”
The other leaned over the edge of the box, and his small, restless eyes wandered over the now closely-packed auditorium.
“Oh!” he said as soon as he recognised the face which his friend had pointed out to him, “that is citizen Foucquier-Tinville.”
“The Public Prosecutor?”
“Himself. And Héron is the man next to him.”
“Héron?” said the younger man interrogatively.
“Yes. He is chief agent to the Committee of General Security now.”
“What does that mean?”
Both leaned back in their chairs, and their sombrely-clad figures were once more merged in the gloom of the narrow box. Instinctively, since the name of the Public Prosecutor had been mentioned between them, they had allowed their voices to sink to a whisper.
The older man﻿—a stoutish, florid-looking individual, with small, keen eyes, and skin pitted with smallpox﻿—shrugged his shoulders at his friend’s question, and then said with an air of contemptuous indifference:
“It means, my good St. Just, that these two men whom you see down there, calmly conning the programme of this evening’s entertainment, and preparing to enjoy themselves tonight in the company of the late M. de Molière, are two hellhounds as powerful as they are cunning.”
“Yes, yes,” said St. Just, and much against his will a slight shudder ran through his slim figure as he spoke. “Foucquier-Tinville I know; I know his cunning, and I know his power﻿—but the other?”
“The other?” retorted de Batz lightly. “Héron?

===Chinese===

包厢里头一片漆黑；那道狭窄的开口，看舞台只能瞧见寒碜的一侧，倒更适于藏住坐客，而非照亮他们。
两人中较年轻的那位，在巴黎看来人生地不熟：政界要人和政府名流陆续到场时，他不时转向同伴，打听这些鼎鼎大名的人物。
“告诉我，德巴茨，”他把同伴的注意引向刚进场的一群人，“绿衣服的那个——现在抬手遮着脸的——他是谁？”
“哪儿？你说哪个？”
“那儿！他朝这边看了，手里拿着戏单。下巴前凸、额头后削，脸像只狨猴，眼睛像豺狼的那位。怎么样？”
另一人俯身到包厢边沿，一双细小不安分的眼睛在已经挤得满满当当的观众席上扫来扫去。
“哦！”他一认出朋友指的那张脸，便说，“那是公民富基耶-坦维尔。”
“公诉人？”
“正是他。他旁边那个是埃龙。”
“埃龙？”年轻人疑问道。
“对。他如今是治安委员会的首席密探。”
“这话怎么讲？”
两人往椅背上一靠，深色衣着的身影重新没入窄包厢的幽暗里。自打两人中间提到公诉人的名字，他们便不由自主地把嗓音压成了耳语。
年长的那个——身材微胖、面色红润，一双细小锐利的眼睛，皮肤上布着麻点——听了朋友的问话耸耸肩，带着轻蔑的漠然说道：
“这话的意思是，我亲爱的圣茹斯特：你在下面看见的这两位，正安然读着今晚余兴的戏目、准备在已故莫里哀先生的戏文里消磨今夜的，是两条地狱的猎犬，狡诈无比，权势也无比。”
“是，是，”圣茹斯特说，不由自主地，一阵轻微的战栗掠过他瘦长的身形。“富基耶-坦维尔我知道；我知道他的奸狡，也知道他的权势——可另外那个？”
“另外那个？”德巴茨轻巧地反问。“埃龙？

===Original===

Let me tell you, my friend, that even the might and lust of that damned Public Prosecutor pale before the power of Héron!”
“But how? I do not understand.”
“Ah! you have been in England so long, you lucky dog, and though no doubt the main plot of our hideous tragedy has reached your ken, you have no cognisance of the actors who play the principal parts on this arena flooded with blood and carpeted with hate. They come and go, these actors, my good St. Just﻿—they come and go. Marat is already the man of yesterday, Robespierre is the man of tomorrow. Today we still have Danton and Foucquier-Tinville; we still have Père Duchesne, and your own good cousin Antoine St. Just, but Héron and his like are with us always.”
“Spies, of course?”
“Spies,” assented the other. “And what spies! Were you present at the sitting of the Assembly today?”
“I was. I heard the new decree which already has passed into law. Ah! I tell you, friend, that we do not let the grass grow under our feet these days. Robespierre wakes up one morning with a whim; by the afternoon that whim has become law, passed by a servile body of men too terrified to run counter to his will, fearful lest they be accused of moderation or of humanity﻿—the greatest crimes that can be committed nowadays.”
“But Danton?”
“Ah! Danton? He would wish to stem the tide that his own passions have let loose; to muzzle the raging beasts whose fangs he himself has sharpened. I told you that Danton is still the man of today; tomorrow he will be accused of moderation. Danton and moderation!﻿—ye gods! Eh? Danton, who thought the guillotine too slow in its work, and armed thirty soldiers with swords, so that thirty heads might fall at one and the same time. Danton, friend, will perish tomorrow accused of treachery against the Revolution, of moderation towards her enemies; and curs like Héron will feast on the blood of lions like Danton and his crowd.”
He paused a moment, for he dared not raise his voice, and his whispers were being drowned by the noise in the auditorium. The curtain, timed to be raised at eight o’clock, was still down, though it was close on half-past, and the public was growing impatient. There was loud stamping of feet, and a few shrill whistles of disapproval proceeded from the gallery.

===Chinese===

我告诉你，朋友，就连那位该死的公诉人的威势和贪欲，在埃龙的权柄面前也要黯然失色！”
“可怎么会？我不明白。”
“啊！你在英格兰待得太久了，你这条幸运的狗；我们这出骇人悲剧的主线固然传到了你耳朵里，可在这片血水横流、仇恨铺地的戏台上担纲主演的角儿们，你却认不得了。他们来来去去，这些角儿，我亲爱的圣茹斯特——来来去去。马拉已经是昨日之人，罗伯斯庇尔是明日之人。今天我们还有丹东和富基耶-坦维尔；还有‘杜歇老爹’，还有你那位好堂兄安托万·圣茹斯特；而埃龙和他那一类，却永远同我们在一起。”
“密探，自然是？”
“密探，”对方首肯，“怎样的密探！你今天到场旁听公会了吗？”
“到了。我听到了那条已经生效的新法令。啊！我告诉你，朋友，这些日子我们脚下可是寸草不生。罗伯斯庇尔哪天早晨醒来心头起个怪念头；到下午，那怪念头就成了法律——由一群奴性十足的表决机器通过，他们怕透了忤逆他的意志，唯恐被扣上温和派或有人性的帽子——那是如今能犯下的最大的罪。”
“可丹东呢？”
“啊！丹东？他想拦住他自己亲手放纵的洪流；想给那些狂怒的野兽套上嘴套——獠牙还是他亲手磨利的。我告诉过你，丹东还是今日之人；明天他就要被控温和了。丹东和温和！——诸神在上！嗯？这个丹东，嫌断头台干活太慢，给三十名士兵配了长剑，好让三十颗脑袋同时落地。丹东啊，朋友，明天就要顶着背叛革命、对革命的敌人手软的罪名完蛋；而埃龙这样的癞皮狗，将饱餐丹东和他那一伙雄狮的鲜血。”
他停了片刻——他不敢抬高嗓门，而他的耳语正被观众席的喧闹淹没。定于八点升起的幕布还垂着，虽然已快八点半，看客们不耐烦起来。跺脚声大作，顶层楼座传来几声尖厉的倒彩哨。

===Original===

“If Héron gets impatient,” said de Batz lightly, when the noise had momentarily subsided, “the manager of this theatre and mayhap his leading actor and actress will spend an unpleasant day tomorrow.”
“Always Héron!” said St. Just, with a contemptuous smile.
“Yes, my friend,” rejoined the other imperturbably, “always Héron. And he has even obtained a longer lease of existence this afternoon.”
“By the new decree?”
“Yes. The new decree. The agents of the Committee of General Security, of whom Héron is the chief, have from today powers of domiciliary search; they have full powers to proceed against all enemies of public welfare. Isn’t that beautifully vague? And they have absolute discretion; everyone may become an enemy of public welfare, either by spending too much money or by spending too little, by laughing today or crying tomorrow, by mourning for one dead relative or rejoicing over the execution of another. He may be a bad example to the public by the cleanliness of his person or by the filth upon his clothes, he may offend by walking today and by riding in a carriage next week; the agents of the Committee of General Security shall alone decide what constitutes enmity against public welfare. All prisons are to be opened at their bidding to receive those whom they choose to denounce; they have henceforth the right to examine prisoners privately and without witnesses, and to send them to trial without further warrants; their duty is clear﻿—they must ‘beat up game for the guillotine.’ Thus is the decree worded; they must furnish the Public Prosecutor with work to do, the tribunals with victims to condemn, the Place de la Révolution with death-scenes to amuse the people, and for their work they will be rewarded thirty-five livres for every head that falls under the guillotine. Ah! if Héron and his like and his myrmidons work hard and well they can make a comfortable income of four or five thousand livres a week. We are getting on, friend St. Just﻿—we are getting on.”

===Chinese===

“要是埃龙等得不耐烦了，”喧声暂歇时，德巴茨轻描淡写地说，“这家戏院的经理，说不定还有他的男女主演，明天就要过上不愉快的一天了。”
“开口闭口都是埃龙！”圣茹斯特鄙夷地一笑。
“是啊，朋友，”对方不动声色地应道，“开口闭口都是埃龙。而且就在今天下午，他连身家的租期又续长了。”
“靠那条新法令？”
“对。那条新法令。治安委员会的密探们——埃龙是他们的头目——从今天起有了搜查民宅之权；有了惩办一切‘公共福利之敌’的全权。这措辞妙不妙，模糊得漂亮？而且他们有绝对的裁量权：人人都可能沦为公共福利之敌——钱花得太多也罢，花得太少也罢；今天笑了，明天哭了；为一位死去的亲人服丧，或为另一位被处决而欢欣，都算。他可能因为身上太干净而给公众立了坏榜样，也可能因为衣上太肮脏；可能因为今天步行、下星期坐了马车而获罪：什么算敌视公共福利，全凭治安委员会的密探们一言而决。各处监狱须凭他们一声令下就开门，收押他们看中要告发的人；他们从此有权私下提审囚犯、不设见证，无须更多手续便送他们上法庭；他们的职责写得明明白白——‘为断头台赶猎物’。法令原文就是如此措辞；他们必须给公诉人找活干，给法庭找可判的牺牲品，给革命广场找娱民的死刑戏；而每颗断头台下的落头，赏他们三十五里弗。啊！埃龙之流连同他手下的爪牙若肯卖力干、好好干，一星期挣个四五千里弗的舒坦进项不成问题。我们大有长进啊，圣茹斯特朋友——大有长进。”

===Original===

He had not raised his voice while he spoke, nor in the recounting of such inhuman monstrosity, such vile and bloodthirsty conspiracy against the liberty, the dignity, the very life of an entire nation, did he appear to feel the slightest indignation; rather did a tone of amusement and even of triumph strike through his speech; and now he laughed good-humouredly like an indulgent parent who is watching the naturally cruel antics of a spoilt boy.
“Then from this hell let loose upon earth,” exclaimed St. Just hotly, “must we rescue those who refuse to ride upon this tide of blood.”
His cheeks were glowing, his eyes sparkled with enthusiasm. He looked very young and very eager. Armand St. Just, the brother of Lady Blakeney, had something of the refined beauty of his lovely sister, but the features﻿—though manly﻿—had not the latent strength expressed in them which characterised every line of Marguerite’s exquisite face. The forehead suggested a dreamer rather than a thinker, the blue-grey eyes were those of an idealist rather than of a man of action.
De Batz’s keen piercing eyes had no doubt noted this, even whilst he gazed at his young friend with that same look of good-humoured indulgence which seemed habitual to him.
“We have to think of the future, my good St. Just,” he said after a slight pause, and speaking slowly and decisively, like a father rebuking a hotheaded child, “not of the present. What are a few lives worth beside the great principles which we have at stake?”
“The restoration of the monarchy﻿—I know,” retorted St. Just, still unsobered, “but, in the meanwhile﻿—”
“In the meanwhile,” rejoined de Batz earnestly, “every victim to the lust of these men is a step towards the restoration of law and order﻿—that is to say, of the monarchy. It is only through these violent excesses perpetrated in its name that the nation will realise how it is being fooled by a set of men who have only their own power and their own advancement in view, and who imagine that the only way to that power is over the dead bodies of those who stand in their way. Once the nation is sickened by these orgies of ambition and of hate, it will turn against these savage brutes, and gladly acclaim the restoration of all that they are striving to destroy.

===Chinese===

说话时他不曾抬高嗓门；讲述这等非人的滔天罪恶、这等针对整个民族的自由、尊严乃至生命的卑劣嗜血阴谋时，他也似乎毫不愤慨；他的话音里反倒透着一种赏玩乃至得胜的调子；此刻他正心平气和地笑着，像一个溺爱的父亲看着娇惯坏了的孩子耍他那天性残忍的把戏。
“那么，从这个放纵到人间的地狱里，”圣茹斯特激愤地喊道，“我们必须救出那些不肯在这血潮上乘浪的人。”
他双颊发烫，眼中闪着热忱的光。他看上去非常年轻，非常急切。阿尔芒·圣茹斯特，布莱克尼夫人的胞弟，颇有几分他那位美丽妹妹的清秀之美；只是那眉眼虽是男子的，却没有蕴藏在玛格丽特那张精巧脸上每一根线条里的那股内在的力量。他的额头属于梦想家，而非思想家；那双蓝灰色的眼睛，属于理想主义者，而非实干家。
德巴茨那双敏锐刺人的眼睛无疑早看在眼里，尽管他望着这位年轻朋友时的神情，依旧是那副仿佛长在脸上的宽厚纵容。
“我们得想着将来，我亲爱的圣茹斯特，”他略停片刻，缓缓而决然地说，像一位父亲训斥性急的孩子，“不要只顾眼前。同我们押上赌桌的伟大原则相比，几条人命算得了什么？”
“复辟君主制——我知道，”圣茹斯特余怒未消地顶了一句，“可是，在这期间——”
“在这期间，”德巴茨恳切地接道，“这班人的贪欲每多一个牺牲品，就是向法律与秩序复归——也就是君主制的复归——又迈进了一步。唯有借这些打着革命旗号犯下的暴行，这个民族才会看清自己是怎样被一伙人愚弄的——这伙人眼里只有自己的权势和自己的腾达，还以为通往权势的唯一道路，就是踏开挡道者的尸体。一旦这民族对这些野心与仇恨的狂欢倒了胃口，它就会掉头反对这帮野蛮畜生，欢天喜地地迎回他们正在拼命毁掉的一切。

===Original===

This is our only hope for the future, and, believe me, friend, that every head snatched from the guillotine by your romantic hero, the Scarlet Pimpernel, is a stone laid for the consolidation of this infamous Republic.”
“I’ll not believe it,” protested St. Just emphatically.
De Batz, with a gesture of contempt indicative also of complete self-satisfaction and unalterable self-belief, shrugged his broad shoulders. His short fat fingers, covered with rings, beat a tattoo upon the ledge of the box.
Obviously, he was ready with a retort. His young friend’s attitude irritated even more than it amused him. But he said nothing for the moment, waiting while the traditional three knocks on the floor of the stage proclaimed the rise of the curtain. The growing impatience of the audience subsided as if by magic at the welcome call; everybody settled down again comfortably in their seats, they gave up the contemplation of the fathers of the people, and turned their full attention to the actors on the boards.

===Chinese===

这是我们对将来唯一的指望；相信我，朋友，你那位传奇英雄红花侠从断头台下抢回的每一颗脑袋，都是给这个无耻共和国添砖加瓦的垫脚石。”
“我不信。”圣茹斯特斩钉截铁地抗议。
德巴茨耸了耸宽阔的肩膀，那手势既含轻蔑，又透着十足的自我满足和不可动摇的自信。他戴着戒指的短胖手指，在包厢的栏沿上敲出一串鼓点。
显然，他已备好回敬的话。年轻朋友的态度叫他恼火的多，觉着好笑的少。但他暂且一言不发，只等着舞台地板上那传统的三下敲响——幕布升起的号令。观众的躁动仿佛被这一声久候的敲响施了法术，霎时平息；人人重新舒舒服服坐回座位，不再端详那些人民之父，把全副注意力转向台上的演员。

## II Widely Divergent Aims / 目标迥异
===Original===

This was Armand S. Just’s first visit to Paris since that memorable day when first he decided to sever his connection from the Republican party, of which he and his beautiful sister Marguerite had at one time been amongst the most noble, most enthusiastic followers. Already a year and a half ago the excesses of the party had horrified him, and that was long before they had degenerated into the sickening orgies which were culminating today in wholesale massacres and bloody hecatombs of innocent victims.
With the death of Mirabeau the moderate Republicans, whose sole and entirely pure aim had been to free the people of France from the autocratic tyranny of the Bourbons, saw the power go from their clean hands to the grimy ones of lustful demagogues, who knew no law save their own passions of bitter hatred against all classes that were not as self-seeking, as ferocious as themselves.
It was no longer a question of a fight for political and religious liberty only, but one of class against class, man against man, and let the weaker look to himself. The weaker had proved himself to be, firstly, the man of property and substance, then the law-abiding citizen, lastly the man of action who had obtained for the people that very same liberty of thought and of belief which soon became so terribly misused.
Armand St. Just, one of the apostles of liberty, fraternity, and equality, soon found that the most savage excesses of tyranny were being perpetrated in the name of those same ideals which he had worshipped.
His sister Marguerite, happily married in England, was the final temptation which caused him to quit the country the destinies of which he no longer could help to control. The spark of enthusiasm which he and the followers of Mirabeau had tried to kindle in the hearts of an oppressed people had turned to raging tongues of unquenchable flames. The taking of the Bastille had been the prelude to the massacres of September, and even the horror of these had since paled beside the holocausts of today.
Armand, saved from the swift vengeance of the revolutionaries by the devotion of the Scarlet Pimpernel, crossed over to England and enrolled himself under the banner of the heroic chief. But he had been unable hitherto to be an active member of the League. The chief was loath to allow him to run foolhardy risks. The St.

===Chinese===

自从那个难忘的日子——他决意同共和党一刀两断的日子——以来，这是阿尔芒·圣茹斯特头一回重返巴黎；想当年，他和他美丽的妹妹玛格丽特，都曾是那个党最赤诚、最热烈的信徒。早在一年半以前，这个党的暴行就已令他胆寒；而那还远在它堕落成今日这令人作呕的狂欢之前——如今这一切正登峰造极：成批的屠杀，无辜牺牲者流血的祭坛。
米拉波一死，温和派共和党人——他们唯一而且全然纯洁的目标，本是使法兰西人民摆脱波旁王朝的专制暴政——眼睁睁看着权力从自己干净的双手，落到煽动家们污黑的手里；这班人不知有法，只知有自己那腔对一切不像他们那样自私、那样凶残的阶级咬牙切齿的恨。
争的已不再仅仅是政治与信仰自由，而是阶级对阶级、人对人的厮杀，弱者只好自求多福。而弱者，先是证明为有产有业之人，继而证明为奉公守法的良民，最后证明为那些行动家——他们为人民争来的正是思想与信仰的自由，而这自由转眼便被糟蹋到可怕的地步。
阿尔芒·圣茹斯特，自由、博爱、平等的一位使徒，很快便发现：最野蛮的暴虐，正顶着他昔日礼拜的那些理想之名横行。
妹妹玛格丽特在英格兰婚姻美满，成了叫他离国的最后的引诱——这个国家的命运，他已无力再加左右。他和米拉波的追随者们力图在受压迫人民心头点燃的那点热情的火星，已化作扑不灭的烈焰，火舌狂卷。攻占巴士底狱不过是九月屠杀的序曲；而连那般的恐怖，比起今日的大屠杀也已黯然失色。
阿尔芒靠红花侠的赤诚搭救，逃脱了革命者的迅疾报复，渡海去了英格兰，投到那位英雄首领的旗下。但直到如今，他始终未能成为同盟里的一名积极分子。首领不愿让他去冒莽撞的险。圣

===Original===

Justs﻿—both Marguerite and Armand﻿—were still very well-known in Paris. Marguerite was not a woman easily forgotten, and her marriage with an English “aristo” did not please those republican circles who had looked upon her as their queen. Armand’s secession from his party into the ranks of the émigrés had singled him out for special reprisals, if and whenever he could be got hold of, and both brother and sister had an unusually bitter enemy in their cousin Antoine St. Just﻿—once an aspirant to Marguerite’s hand, and now a servile adherent and imitator of Robespierre, whose ferocious cruelty he tried to emulate with a view to ingratiating himself with the most powerful man of the day.
Nothing would have pleased Antoine St. Just more than the opportunity of showing his zeal and his patriotism by denouncing his own kith and kin to the Tribunal of the Terror, and the Scarlet Pimpernel, whose own slender fingers were held on the pulse of that reckless revolution, had no wish to sacrifice Armand’s life deliberately, or even to expose it to unnecessary dangers.
Thus it was that more than a year had gone by before Armand St. Just﻿—an enthusiastic member of the League of the Scarlet Pimpernel﻿—was able to do aught for its service. He had chafed under the enforced restraint placed upon him by the prudence of his chief, when, indeed, he was longing to risk his life with the comrades whom he loved and beside the leader whom he revered.
At last, in the beginning of ’94 he persuaded Blakeney to allow him to join the next expedition to France. What the principal aim of that expedition was the members of the League did not know as yet, but what they did know was that perils﻿—graver even than hitherto﻿—would attend them on their way.
The circumstances had become very different of late. At first the impenetrable mystery which had surrounded the personality of the chief had been a full measure of safety, but now one tiny corner of that veil of mystery had been lifted by two rough pairs of hands at least; Chauvelin, ex-ambassador at the English Court, was no longer in any doubt as to the identity of the Scarlet Pimpernel, whilst Collot d’Herbois had seen him at Boulogne, and had there been effectually foiled by him.

===Chinese===

茹斯特兄妹——玛格丽特和阿尔芒——在巴黎依旧名声赫赫。玛格丽特不是轻易叫人忘掉的女人；她嫁给一位英国“贵族”，早惹恼了那些一度把她奉为女王的共和派圈子。阿尔芒脱党投奔流亡贵族的行列，早被单独记上一笔，专等拿获之日加倍清算；而兄妹俩还搭着一位格外刻毒的仇人——堂兄安托万·圣茹斯特：此人当年曾求娶玛格丽特而不得，如今是罗伯斯庇尔卑躬屈膝的党羽和效颦者，竭力摹仿他的凶残，好取悦当世最有权势的人。
天底下最能叫安托万·圣茹斯特痛快的，莫过于有机会把自家骨肉告到恐怖法庭面前，以显其热忱与爱国；而红花侠——他那纤细的手指始终搭着这场脱缰革命的脉搏——不愿故意拿阿尔芒的性命去填，甚至不肯让他暴露在多余的险境里。
就这样，一年多的光阴过去了，阿尔芒·圣茹斯特——红花侠同盟的一名热血会员——才得以为它效一臂之力。首领的谨慎强加给他的束缚，磨得他心焦；他明明渴望同他所爱的伙伴们、他所敬的首领一起，把性命押上去。
终于，九四年初，他说动了布莱克尼，准他参加下一趟法兰西远征。那趟远征的主要目标是什么，同盟的会员们还不知情；他们只知道，一路的凶险——比以往更甚的凶险——将随行在侧。
时局近来已大不相同。起初，笼罩着首领身份的那团透不进风的神秘，便是十足的安全；可如今，这层神秘的面纱，至少已被两双粗暴的手各掠开了一角：前任驻英宫廷大使肖维兰，对红花侠是谁已再无怀疑；科洛·代布瓦则在布洛涅亲眼见过他，并在那里叫他挫败得干干净净。

===Original===

Four months had gone by since that day, and the Scarlet Pimpernel was hardly ever out of France now; the massacres in Paris and in the provinces had multiplied with appalling rapidity, the necessity for the selfless devotion of that small band of heroes had become daily, hourly more pressing. They rallied round their chief with unbounded enthusiasm, and let it be admitted at once that the sporting instinct﻿—inherent in these English gentlemen﻿—made them all the more keen, all the more eager now that the dangers which beset their expeditions were increased tenfold.
At a word from the beloved leader, these young men﻿—the spoilt darlings of society﻿—would leave the gaieties, the pleasures, the luxuries of London or of Bath, and, taking their lives in their hands, they placed them, together with their fortunes, and even their good names, at the service of the innocent and helpless victims of merciless tyranny. The married men﻿—Ffoulkes, my Lord Hastings, Sir Jeremiah Wallescourt﻿—left wife and children at a call from the chief, at the cry of the wretched. Armand﻿—unattached and enthusiastic﻿—had the right to demand that he should no longer be left behind.
He had only been away a little over fifteen months, and yet he found Paris a different city from the one he had left immediately after the terrible massacres of September. An air of grim loneliness seemed to hang over her despite the crowds that thronged her streets; the men whom he was wont to meet in public places fifteen months ago﻿—friends and political allies﻿—were no longer to be seen; strange faces surrounded him on every side﻿—sullen, glowering faces, all wearing a certain air of horrified surprise and of vague, terrified wonder, as if life had become one awful puzzle, the answer to which must be found in the brief interval between the swift passages of death.
Armand St. Just, having settled his few simple belongings in the squalid lodgings which had been assigned to him, had started out after dark to wander somewhat aimlessly through the streets. Instinctively he seemed to be searching for a familiar face, someone who would come to him out of that merry past which he had spent with Marguerite in their pretty apartment in the Rue St. Honoré.
For an hour he wandered thus and met no one whom he knew.

===Chinese===

那一天过去四个月了，如今红花侠几乎没离开过法兰西；巴黎和外省的屠杀成倍地翻，其速骇人；那一小队英雄舍身奉献的必要，一天比一天、一个时辰比一个时辰更迫切。他们怀着无限的热忱聚在首领周围；而且不妨直说，这班英国绅士骨子里的竞技天性，也正因远征路上的凶险翻了十倍，而叫他们愈发兴致高昂、跃跃欲试。
这班年轻人——社交场上的天之骄子——只消爱戴的首领一句话，便撂下伦敦或巴斯的声色繁华，把性命提在手里，连同家财、连同名声，一齐献给无情暴政下那些无辜无告的牺牲者。有家室的——福菲克斯、黑斯廷斯勋爵、杰里迈亚·沃尔斯科特爵士——一闻首领召唤、一闻落难者的呼号，便撇下妻儿。阿尔芒——无家室之累，一腔热血——更有权利要求不再被留下。
他离开了不过十五个月多一点，却发现巴黎已不是他在九月大屠杀后随即离开的那座城。尽管街头依旧熙熙攘攘，一种冷森森的孤独却罩在城头；十五个月前他惯常在公共场所遇到的那些人——朋友和政治盟友——都不见了；四面八方围着他的是陌生的脸——阴郁的、怒视的脸，一律带着某种惊怖错愕、某种惶惑恐惧的神情，仿佛生命成了一道可怕的谜，谜底必须在死亡一次次疾掠而过的短暂间隙里求解。
阿尔芒·圣茹斯特把几件简单的行安置进分派给他的那间破败住处，天黑之后便出门上街，漫无目的地游荡。他仿佛在本能地寻觅一张熟面孔，某个从欢乐往昔里向他走来的人——那往昔，是他同玛格丽特在圣奥诺雷街那套漂亮公寓里度过的。
他这样游荡了一个钟头，没遇见一个相识。

===Original===

At times it appeared to him as if he did recognise a face or figure that passed him swiftly by in the gloom, but even before he could fully make up his mind to that, the face or figure had already disappeared, gliding furtively down some narrow unlighted by-street, without turning to look to right or left, as if dreading fuller recognition. Armand felt a total stranger in his own native city.
The terrible hours of the execution on the Place de la Révolution were fortunately over, the tumbrils no longer rattled along the uneven pavements, nor did the death-cry of the unfortunate victims resound through the deserted streets. Armand was, on this first day of his arrival, spared the sight of this degradation of the once lovely city; but her desolation, her general appearance of shamefaced indigence and of cruel aloofness struck a chill in the young man’s heart.
It was no wonder, therefore, when anon he was wending his way slowly back to his lodging he was accosted by a pleasant, cheerful voice, that he responded to it with alacrity. The voice, of a smooth, oily timbre, as if the owner kept it well greased for purposes of amiable speech, was like an echo of the past, when jolly, irresponsible Baron de Batz, erstwhile officer of the Guard in the service of the late King, and since then known to be the most inveterate conspirator for the restoration of the monarchy, used to amuse Marguerite by his vapid, senseless plans for the overthrow of the newly-risen power of the people.
Armand was quite glad to meet him, and when de Batz suggested that a good talk over old times would be vastly agreeable, the younger man gladly acceded. The two men, though certainly not mistrustful of one another, did not seem to care to reveal to each other the place where they lodged. De Batz at once proposed the *avant-scène* box of one of the theatres as being the safest place where old friends could talk without fear of spying eyes or ears.
“There is no place so safe or so private nowadays, believe me, my young friend,” he said, “I have tried every sort of nook and cranny in this accursed town, now riddled with spies, and I have come to the conclusion that a small *avant-scène* box is the most perfect den of privacy there is in the entire city.

有时他仿佛真认出了昏暗里匆匆擦身而过的一张脸或一个身影，可不等他拿定主意，那脸或身影早已没了踪影，鬼鬼祟祟溜进某条没有灯的窄巷，头也不回，左右不看，仿佛生怕被人认得更全。阿尔芒觉得自己成了故乡城里彻底的异乡人。
幸而革命广场上行刑的可怕时辰已经过去，死刑囚车不再在坎坷的路面上隆隆驶过，不幸牺牲者的死嚎也不再回荡在空寂的街上。抵达的头一天，阿尔芒幸免于目睹这座曾经可爱的城市如何自贬；可她的荒凉，她那副整体上羞于见人的寒伧和冷酷的疏远，仍叫年轻人心头一寒。
因此，不一会儿，当他正缓缓折回住处，一声愉快爽朗的招呼向他传来，他忙不迭地应了——这也难怪。那嗓音圆润油亮，仿佛主人专为说好听的话而常年为它上油；它像往昔的一声回响：那时候，快快活活、没心没肺的德巴茨男爵——先王御前的近卫军官，后来成了众所周知的复辟君主制死心塌地的阴谋家——常用他那套推翻新生民权的空洞无稽的盘算逗玛格丽特发笑。
阿尔芒见到他相当高兴；德巴茨提议找个地方好好叙叙旧，年轻人欣然从命。两人虽谈不上互相猜疑，却都不愿向对方吐露各自的落脚处。德巴茨当即提议某家戏院的临台包厢——老朋友在那儿说话，最不必怕耳目。
“如今再没有比那更稳妥、更私密的地方了，相信我，我年轻的朋友，”他说，“这座该死的城市如今密探遍地，各处的犄角旮旯我都试过了，我的结论是：一间小小的临台包厢，是全城最完美的密谈之所。

===Original===

The voices of the actors on the stage and the hum among the audience in the house will effectually drown all individual conversation to every ear save the one for whom it is intended.”
It is not difficult to persuade a young man who feels lonely and somewhat forlorn in a large city to while away an evening in the companionship of a cheerful talker, and de Batz was essentially good company. His vapourings had always been amusing, but Armand now gave him credit for more seriousness of purpose; and though the chief had warned him against picking up acquaintances in Paris, the young man felt that that restriction would certainly not apply to a man like de Batz, whose hot partisanship of the Royalist cause and harebrained schemes for its restoration must make him at one with the League of the Scarlet Pimpernel.
Armand accepted the other’s cordial invitation. He, too, felt that he would indeed be safer from observation in a crowded theatre than in the streets. Among a closely packed throng bent on amusement the sombrely-clad figure of a young man, with the appearance of a student or of a journalist, would easily pass unperceived.
But somehow, after the first ten minutes spent in de Batz’ company within the gloomy shelter of the small *avant-scène* box, Armand already repented of the impulse which had prompted him to come to the theatre tonight, and to renew acquaintanceship with the ex-officer of the late King’s Guard. Though he knew de Batz to be an ardent Royalist, and even an active adherent of the monarchy, he was soon conscious of a vague sense of mistrust of this pompous, self-complacent individual, whose every utterance breathed selfish aims rather than devotion to a forlorn cause.
Therefore, when the curtain rose at last on the first act of Molière’s witty comedy, St. Just turned deliberately towards the stage and tried to interest himself in the wordy quarrel between Philinte and Alceste.
But this attitude on the part of the younger man did not seem to suit his newly-found friend. It was clear that de Batz did not consider the topic of conversation by any means exhausted, and that it had been more with a view to a discussion like the present interrupted one that he had invited St. Just to come to the theatre with him tonight, rather than for the purpose of witnessing Mlle. Lange’s debut in the part of Célimène.

阿尔芒接受了对方热诚的邀请。他自己也觉得，待在人头攒动的戏院里，确实比在街上更少惹眼。在一心找乐子的稠密人群中间，一个衣着深色、一副学生或记者模样的年轻人，很容易便无人注意。
可是不知怎么，在那间昏暗的小临台包厢里陪德巴茨坐了头十分钟，阿尔芒便已懊悔起来——悔自己今晚一时冲动来戏院，来续这位先王旧军官的交情。虽说他知道德巴茨是热烈的保王党，甚至是君主制的奔走者，他很快便隐隐觉出对这浮夸自满之人的不信任：此公出口吐纳的，尽是自私的算盘，不是对一项惨淡事业的忠忱。
于是，当莫里哀那出妙趣喜剧的第一幕终于开演，圣茹斯特有意转向舞台，勉强去关心菲兰特同阿尔赛斯特那场拌嘴。
可年轻人这副姿态，似乎不合新交朋友的意。显然，德巴茨认为话匣子远没有倒完；今晚邀圣茹斯特来戏院，与其说为看郎格小姐初次登台演塞利梅娜，不如说正为着把方才打断的那场议论接着谈下去。

===Original===

Just in Paris had as a matter of fact astonished de Batz not a little, and had set his intriguing brain busy on conjectures. It was in order to turn these conjectures into certainties that he had desired private talk with the young man.
He waited silently now for a moment or two, his keen, small eyes resting with evident anxiety on Armand’s averted head, his fingers still beating the impatient tattoo upon the velvet-covered cushion of the box. Then at the first movement of St. Just towards him he was ready in an instant to reopen the subject under discussion.
With a quick nod of his head he called his young friend’s attention back to the men in the auditorium.
“Your good cousin Antoine St. Just is hand and glove with Robespierre now,” he said. “When you left Paris more than a year ago you could afford to despise him as an empty-headed windbag; now, if you desire to remain in France, you will have to fear him as a power and a menace.”
“Yes, I knew that he had taken to herding with the wolves,” rejoined Armand lightly. “At one time he was in love with my sister. I thank God that she never cared for him.”
“They say that he herds with the wolves because of this disappointment,” said de Batz. “The whole pack is made up of men who have been disappointed, and who have nothing more to lose. When all these wolves will have devoured one another, then and then only can we hope for the restoration of the monarchy in France. And they will not turn on one another whilst prey for their greed lies ready to their jaws. Your friend the Scarlet Pimpernel should feed this bloody revolution of ours rather than starve it, if indeed he hates it as he seems to do.”
His restless eyes peered with eager interrogation into those of the younger man. He paused as if waiting for a reply; then, as St. Just remained silent, he reiterated slowly, almost in the tones of a challenge:
“If indeed he hates this bloodthirsty revolution of ours as he seems to do.”
The reiteration implied a doubt. In a moment St. Just’s loyalty was up in arms.
“The Scarlet Pimpernel,” he said, “cares naught for your political aims. The work of mercy that he does, he does for justice and for humanity.”
“And for sport,” said de Batz with a sneer, “so I’ve been told.”
“He is English,” assented St. Just, “and as such will never own to sentiment. Whatever be the motive, look at the result!
“Yes!

茹斯特来到巴黎，实则叫德巴茨吃惊不小，也让他那专事算计的脑子转个不停。正是为了把猜想坐实，他才存心同年轻人密谈。
此刻他默默等了一两分钟，细小锐利的眼睛带着明显的焦灼，盯住阿尔芒扭过去的头，手指还在包厢的天鹅绒软垫上敲着不耐烦的鼓点。圣茹斯特刚一朝他侧过身，他便立刻接上了先前的话题。他急速一点头，把年轻朋友的注意重新引回观众席上那些人物。
“你那位好堂兄安托万·圣茹斯特，如今同罗伯斯庇尔好得穿一条裤子，”他说，“一年多前你离开巴黎时，尽可以把他当作空脑壳的牛皮筒子；如今你若想留在法兰西，就得把他当作一股势力、一个威胁来怕了。”
“是，我知道他入了狼群，”阿尔芒淡淡应道，“他从前追求过我妹妹。感谢上帝，她从没把他放在心上。”
“人说，他正是吃了这个瘪才去投狼群的，”德巴茨说，“整群狼全是失意人凑的，全是再没什么可输的人。等这群狼互相吞噬殆尽，也唯有到那时，我们才谈得上盼望法兰西复辟君主制。可只要贪婪的口中还有现成的猎物，他们是不会自相残杀的。你那位朋友红花侠，倘若真像他看上去那样恨这场血腥革命，就该喂饱它，而不是饿瘪它。”
他那不安分的眼睛带着急切的探问，紧盯着年轻人的眼睛。他顿了顿，像是等一句回话；见圣茹斯特依旧沉默，他便缓缓地、几乎是挑衅的口吻，把话重说了一遍：
“倘若他真像看上去那样，恨我们这场嗜血的革命的话。”
这重复里带着存疑。圣茹斯特的忠心霎时奋起。
“红花侠，”他说，“对你们的政治目标毫无兴味。他行的那份仁义，为的是公道，为人道。”
“还为猎奇寻乐，”德巴茨冷笑，“人家是这么告诉我的。”
“他是英国人，”圣茹斯特认可，“英国人从不认账自己讲感情。动机是什么且不论，看看结果吧！
是啊！

“Women and children﻿—innocent victims﻿—would have perished but for his devotion.”
“The more innocent they were, the more helpless, the more pitiable, the louder would their blood have cried for reprisals against the wild beasts who sent them to their death.”
St. Just made no reply. It was obviously useless to attempt to argue with this man, whose political aims were as far apart from those of the Scarlet Pimpernel as was the North Pole from the South.
“If any of you have influence over that hotheaded leader of yours,” continued de Batz, unabashed by the silence of his friend, “I wish to God you would exert it now.”
“In what way?” queried St. Just, smiling in spite of himself at the thought of his or anyone else’s control over Blakeney and his plans.
It was de Batz’ turn to be silent. He paused for a moment or two, then he asked abruptly:
“Your Scarlet Pimpernel is in Paris now, is he not?”
“I cannot tell you,” replied Armand.
“Bah! there is no necessity to fence with me, my friend. The moment I set eyes on you this afternoon I knew that you had not come to Paris alone.”
“You are mistaken, my good de Batz,” rejoined the young man earnestly; “I came to Paris alone.”
“Clever parrying, on my word﻿—but wholly wasted on my unbelieving ears. Did I not note at once that you did not seem overpleased today when I accosted you?”
“Again you are mistaken. I was very pleased to meet you, for I had felt singularly lonely all day, and was glad to shake a friend by the hand. What you took for displeasure was only surprise.”
“Surprise? Ah, yes! I don’t wonder that you were surprised to see me walking unmolested and openly in the streets of Paris﻿—whereas you had heard of me as a dangerous conspirator, eh?﻿—and as a man who has the entire police of his country at his heels﻿—on whose head there is a price﻿—what?”
“I knew that you had made several noble efforts to rescue the unfortunate King and Queen from the hands of these brutes.”
“All of which efforts were unsuccessful,” assented de Batz imperturbably, “every one of them having been either betrayed by some d﻿—﻿—﻿d confederate or ferreted out by some astute spy eager for gain. Yes, my friend, I made several efforts to rescue King Louis and Queen Marie Antoinette from the scaffold, and every time I was foiled, and yet here I am, you see, unscathed and free.

“你又猜错了。见到你我很高兴；我一整天孤零零的，正乐意同朋友握握手。你当作不乐意的，不过是吃惊。”
“吃惊？啊，不错！看到我在巴黎街上大摇大摆地走，谁也不来招惹，你不吃惊才怪呢——你先前听说的是个危险的阴谋家，嗯？——一个被本国全部警察追着脚跟、脑袋上挂着赏格的人——怎么样？”

I walk about the streets boldly, and talk to my friends as I meet them.”
“You are lucky,” said St. Just, not without a tinge of sarcasm.
“I have been prudent,” retorted de Batz. “I have taken the trouble to make friends there where I thought I needed them most﻿—the mammon of unrighteousness, you know﻿—what?”
And he laughed a broad, thick laugh of perfect self-satisfaction.
“Yes, I know,” rejoined St. Just, with the tone of sarcasm still more apparent in his voice now. “You have Austrian money at your disposal.”
“Any amount,” said the other complacently, “and a great deal of it sticks to the grimy fingers of these patriotic makers of revolutions. Thus do I ensure my own safety. I buy it with the Emperor’s money, and thus am I able to work for the restoration of the monarchy in France.”
Again St. Just was silent. What could he say? Instinctively now, as the fleshy personality of the Gascon Royalist seemed to spread itself out and to fill the tiny box with his ambitious schemes and his far-reaching plans, Armand’s thoughts flew back to that other plotter, the man with the pure and simple aims, the man whose slender fingers had never handled alien gold, but were ever there ready stretched out to the helpless and the weak, whilst his thoughts were only of the help that he might give them, but never of his own safety.
De Batz, however, seemed blandly unconscious of any such disparaging thoughts in the mind of his young friend, for he continued quite amiably, even though a note of anxiety seemed to make itself felt now in his smooth voice:
“We advance slowly, but step by step, my good St. Just,” he said. “I have not been able to save the monarchy in the person of the King or the Queen, but I may yet do it in the person of the Dauphin.”
“The Dauphin,” murmured St. Just involuntarily.
That involuntary murmur, scarcely audible, so soft was it, seemed in some way to satisfy de Batz, for the keenness of his gaze relaxed, and his fat fingers ceased their nervous, intermittent tattoo on the ledge of the box.
“Yes! the Dauphin,” he said, nodding his head as if in answer to his own thoughts, “or rather, let me say, the reigning King of France﻿—Louis XVII, by the grace of God﻿—the most precious life at present upon the whole of this earth.”

我大摇大摆满街走，碰见朋友就攀谈。”
“你运气好。”圣茹斯特说，不无一点讥诮。
“我是谨慎，”德巴茨回敬，“我不辞辛苦，在最用得着朋友的地方交了朋友——不义之财嘛，你懂的——怎么样？”
说罢他开怀大笑，笑声宽厚油亮，透着十足的自我满足。
“是，我知道，”圣茹斯特应道，讥诮的调子这回更明显了，“你手头调得动奥地利的钱。”
“要多少有多少，”对方洋洋自得，“其中相当一部分，粘在这班爱国的革命制造者乌黑的手指头上。我的平安就是这么买下的。用皇帝的银子买平安，我也就腾得出手来，为法兰西复辟君主制效力。”
圣茹斯特又沉默了。他还能说什么呢？此刻，这位加斯科尼保王党人油脂般肥厚的气性仿佛铺展开来，用他的野心盘算、他的深谋远虑填满了这间小包厢；阿尔芒的思绪不由飞向另一位谋划者——那个目标纯粹而简单的人，那双纤细的手从不曾沾过异邦的金子，却永远在那里，随时向无助者与弱者伸出；他心里想的只是能帮他们什么，从不曾想过自身的安危。
德巴茨却浑然不觉年轻朋友心里这类不敬的念头，照样和颜悦色地说下去，只是那圆润的嗓音里，此刻透出一丝按捺不住的焦灼：
“我们进展虽慢，却在步步为营，我亲爱的圣茹斯特，”他说，“我没能以国王或王后之身保全君主制，但或许还能以王太子之身做到。”
“王太子。”圣茹斯特不由自主地喃喃道。
那声不自主的低语轻得几乎听不见，却似乎正中德巴茨下怀：他目光里的锐利松了下来，肥胖的手指也停住了在包厢栏沿上时断时续的紧张敲击。
“对！王太子，”他点着头说，像是应和自己心里的念头，“或者更准确地说，让我说：当今法兰西在位的国王——蒙神恩典的路易十七——眼下整个地球上最珍贵的性命。”

===Original===

“You are right there, friend de Batz,” assented Armand fervently, “the most precious life, as you say, and one that must be saved at all costs.”
“Yes,” said de Batz calmly, “but not by your friend the Scarlet Pimpernel.”
“Why not?”
Scarce were those two little words out of St. Just’s mouth than he repented of them. He bit his lip, and with a dark frown upon his face he turned almost defiantly towards his friend.
But de Batz smiled with easy bonhomie.
“Ah, friend Armand,” he said, “you were not cut out for diplomacy, nor yet for intrigue. So then,” he added more seriously, “that gallant hero, the Scarlet Pimpernel, has hopes of rescuing our young King from the clutches of Simon the cobbler and of the herd of hyenas on the watch for his attenuated little corpse, eh?”
“I did not say that,” retorted St. Just sullenly.
“No. But I say it. Nay! nay! do not blame yourself, my over-loyal young friend. Could I, or anyone else, doubt for a moment that sooner or later your romantic hero would turn his attention to the most pathetic sight in the whole of Europe﻿—the child-martyr in the Temple prison? The wonder were to me if the Scarlet Pimpernel ignored our little King altogether for the sake of his subjects. No, no; do not think for a moment that you have betrayed your friend’s secret to me. When I met you so luckily today I guessed at once that you were here under the banner of the enigmatical little red flower, and, thus guessing, I even went a step further in my conjecture. The Scarlet Pimpernel is in Paris now in the hope of rescuing Louis XVII from the Temple prison.”
“If that is so, you must not only rejoice but should be able to help.”
“And yet, my friend, I do neither the one now nor mean to do the other in the future,” said de Batz placidly. “I happen to be a Frenchman, you see.”
“What has that to do with such a question?”
“Everything; though you, Armand, despite that you are a Frenchman too, do not look through my spectacles. Louis XVII is King of France, my good St. Just; he must owe his freedom and his life to us Frenchmen, and to no one else.”
“That is sheer madness, man,” retorted Armand. “Would you have the child perish for the sake of your own selfish ideas?”
“You may call them selfish if you will; all patriotism is in a measure selfish. What does the rest of the world care if we are a republic or a monarchy, an oligarchy or hopeless anarchy?

===Chinese===

“这话你说对了，德巴茨朋友，”阿尔芒热烈地附和，“最珍贵的性命，正如你所说，无论如何必须救下。”
“是，”德巴茨平静地说，“但不能由你那位朋友红花侠来救。”
“为什么不能？”
两个字刚出口，圣茹斯特便后悔了。他咬住嘴唇，一脸阴沉，几乎是挑衅地转向朋友。
德巴茨却憨然一笑。
“啊，阿尔芒朋友，”他说，“你天生不是干外交的料，也非搞阴谋的坯子。这么说，”他添了一句，口气郑重了些，“那位豪侠英雄红花侠，是指望把我们的少年国王从鞋匠西蒙的魔掌、从那群盯他瘦骨嶙峋小尸首的鬣狗嘴里救出去了，嗯？”
“我没这么说。”圣茹斯特绷着脸顶回去。
“没有。可我替你说了。不！不！别怪自己，我这过分忠心的年轻朋友。难道我，或者随便谁，会有一刻怀疑：你那位传奇英雄迟早要把目光转向全欧洲最凄惨的景象——圣殿塔监狱里的儿童殉道者？依我看，红花侠若为了他的子民而全然置我们的小国王于不顾，那才叫怪呢。不，不；你想都别想自己向我泄露了朋友的秘密。今天侥幸撞见你，我立刻便猜到：你是打着那朵神秘小红花的旗号来的；这一猜，我索性又往前多猜了一步——红花侠眼下正在巴黎，指望把路易十七从圣殿塔监狱里救出去。”
“若真如此，你不但该欢欣，还该帮得上忙。”
“然而，朋友，这两样我现在一样不做，将来也不打算做，”德巴茨心平气和地说，“你瞧，我恰巧是个法国人。”
“这跟这个问题有什么相干？”
“处处相干；虽说你阿尔芒也是法国人，却不肯透过我的眼镜看事。路易十七是法兰西的国王，我亲爱的圣茹斯特；他的自由和性命，必须归功于我们法国人，不能归功于任何旁人。”
“这简直是疯了，老兄，”阿尔芒驳道，“你要那孩子为了你一己的私见送命吗？”
“你尽可以管它们叫私见；一切爱国主义都有几分私心。我们是共和还是君临，是寡头还是无可救药的无政府，世界上的其他人管吗？

===Original===

We work for ourselves and to please ourselves, and I for one will not brook foreign interference.”
“Yet you work with foreign money!”
“That is another matter. I cannot get money in France, so I get it where I can; but I can arrange for the escape of Louis XVII from the Temple Prison, and to us Royalists of France should belong the honour and glory of having saved our King.”
For the third time now St. Just allowed the conversation to drop; he was gazing wide-eyed, almost appalled at this impudent display of well-nigh ferocious selfishness and vanity. De Batz, smiling and complacent, was leaning back in his chair, looking at his young friend with perfect contentment expressed in every line of his pockmarked face and in the very attitude of his well-fed body. It was easy enough now to understand the remarkable immunity which this man was enjoying, despite the many foolhardy plots which he hatched, and which had up to now invariably come to naught.
A regular braggart and empty windbag, he had taken but one good care, and that was of his own skin. Unlike other less fortunate Royalists of France, he neither fought in the country nor braved dangers in town. He played a safer game﻿—crossed the frontier and constituted himself agent of Austria; he succeeded in gaining the Emperor’s money for the good of the Royalist cause, and for his own most especial benefit.
Even a less astute man of the world than was Armand St. Just would easily have guessed that de Batz’ desire to be the only instrument in the rescue of the poor little Dauphin from the Temple was not actuated by patriotism, but solely by greed. Obviously there was a rich reward waiting for him in Vienna the day that he brought Louis XVII safely into Austrian territory; that reward he would miss if a meddlesome Englishman interfered in this affair. Whether in this wrangle he risked the life of the child-King or not mattered to him not at all. It was de Batz who was to get the reward, and whose welfare and prosperity mattered more than the most precious life in Europe.

“可你干事的银子是外国的！”
“那是另一码事。法兰西弄不到钱，我就哪儿弄得到哪儿弄；但安排路易十七逃出圣殿塔监狱这事，我能办；而救下我们国王的荣誉与光荣，理应归于我们法兰西的保王党人。”
这是圣茹斯特第三回任话头掉在地上；他睁大了眼睛，几乎被这近乎凶残的自私与虚荣的厚颜展览惊得发怔。
德巴茨含笑自得，仰在椅背上，望着他年轻的朋友：麻脸上的每一道纹路、养尊处优的身架的每一分姿态，都写着十足的心满意足。此刻不难明白，此人何以享有那份非同寻常的豁免——尽管他炮制过许多莽撞的阴谋，而且至今无一不败。
一个彻头彻尾的吹牛匠、空壳子，他只有一样照管得好，就是自己的皮。同法兰西其他时运不济的保王党人不同，他既不在乡间作战，也不在城里涉险。他玩的是更稳的一手——越境出境，自任奥地利的代理人；他成功地弄到了皇帝的银子，既充保王事业之用，更是特特为他自己。
便是处世阅历不及阿尔芒·圣茹斯特的人，也不难猜到：德巴茨一心要独占从圣殿塔救出可怜小王太子这份功业，驱使他的不是爱国心，纯粹是贪欲。显然，哪一天他把路易十七平安带进奥地利境内，维也纳正有一份厚赏候着他；倘若一个爱管闲事的英国人插进一杠子，这份赏就飞了。这场争执里，那位童王的性命是否被他押上风险，在他毫不要紧。要紧的是拿赏的必须是德巴茨；他的安乐与发达，比欧洲最珍贵的性命更要紧。

## III The Demon Chance / 恶魔机缘
===Original===

St. Just would have given much to be back in his lonely squalid lodgings now. Too late did he realise how wise had been the dictum which had warned him against making or renewing friendships in France.
Men had changed with the times. How terribly they had changed! Personal safety had become a fetish with most﻿—a goal so difficult to attain that it had to be fought for and striven for, even at the expense of humanity and of self-respect.
Selfishness﻿—the mere, cold-blooded insistence for self-advancement﻿—ruled supreme. De Batz, surfeited with foreign money, used it firstly to ensure his own immunity, scattering it to right and left to still the ambition of the Public Prosecutor or to satisfy the greed of innumerable spies.
What was left over he used for the purpose of pitting the bloodthirsty demagogues one against the other, making of the National Assembly a gigantic bear-den, wherein wild beasts could rend one another limb from limb.
In the meanwhile, what cared he﻿—he said it himself﻿—whether hundreds of innocent martyrs perished miserably and uselessly? They were the necessary food whereby the Revolution was to be satiated and de Batz’ schemes enabled to mature. The most precious life in Europe even was only to be saved if its price went to swell the pockets of de Batz, or to further his future ambitions.
Times had indeed changed an entire nation. St. Just felt as sickened with this self-seeking Royalist as he did with the savage brutes who struck to right or left for their own delectation. He was meditating immediate flight back to his lodgings, with a hope of finding there a word for him from the chief﻿—a word to remind him that men did live nowadays who had other aims besides their own advancement﻿—other ideals besides the deification of self.
The curtain had descended on the first act, and traditionally, as the works of M. de Molière demanded it, the three knocks were heard again without any interval. St. Just rose ready with a pretext for parting with his friend. The curtain was being slowly drawn up on the second act, and disclosed Alceste in wrathful conversation with Célimène.
Alceste’s opening speech is short. Whilst the actor spoke it Armand had his back to the stage; with hand outstretched, he was murmuring what he hoped would prove a polite excuse for thus leaving his amiable host while the entertainment had only just begun.

===Chinese===

此刻圣茹斯特情愿付出许多，只要能回到他那冷清破败的住处。他悔悟得太迟了——那句告诫他莫在法兰西结交新友、续旧谊的箴言，何等英明。
人随时势变了。变得何等可怕！自身安全在多数人那里成了偶像——一个如此难得的目标，以至要为它搏、为它拼，哪怕搭上人性与自尊。
自私——那纯粹的、冷血的自我发达之执念——君临一切。德巴茨手里的外国银子多得发腻，首先用来保自己的豁免，左右开弓地撒，去按住公诉人的野心，或喂饱无数密探的贪心。
剩下的，他便用来挑动那些嗜血的煽动家互斗，把国民议会变成一座巨大的兽窟，让野兽们在那里你撕我咬，肢解分尸。
至于这期间成百上千无辜的殉道者凄凄惨惨、白白地死掉——他自己说的——关他何事？他们是必需的食粮，好餍足革命，好让德巴茨的图谋瓜熟蒂落。连欧洲最珍贵的那条性命，也须得它的价钱胀了德巴茨的腰包、助了他的前程，才配得救。
时代当真改造了整个民族。圣茹斯特对这自私自利的保王党人感到的恶心，同对那班为自家快活左右开弓乱咬的野兽一样。他正打算立刻逃回住处，盼着那里有首领留给他的只言片语——一句话，提醒他世上如今毕竟还活着另一种人，他们的目标不止于自己的腾达，他们的理想不止于把自我奉为神明。
第一幕落幕，按惯例——莫里哀先生的作品当得起这份规矩——那三下敲击紧跟着响起，中间毫无间歇。圣茹斯特站起身，告辞的托词已备好。第二幕的幕布缓缓升起，露出正在同塞利梅娜怒声交谈的阿尔赛斯特。
阿尔赛斯特的开场白不长。演员念它的时候，阿尔芒背对着舞台；他伸着手，正低声斟酌着一套客套说辞，指望能体面地告退——戏才刚开锣，便撇下这殷勤的主人。

===Original===

De Batz﻿—vexed and impatient﻿—had not by any means finished with his friend yet. He thought that his specious arguments﻿—delivered with boundless conviction﻿—had made some impression on the mind of the young man. That impression, however, he desired to deepen, and whilst Armand was worrying his brain to find a plausible excuse for going away, de Batz was racking his to find one for keeping him here.
Then it was that the wayward demon Chance intervened. Had St. Just risen but two minutes earlier, had his active mind suggested the desired excuse more readily, who knows what unspeakable sorrow, what heartrending misery, what terrible shame might have been spared both him and those for whom he cared? Those two minutes﻿—did he but know it﻿—decided the whole course of his future life. The excuse hovered on his lips, de Batz reluctantly was preparing to bid him goodbye, when Célimène, speaking commonplace words enough in answer to her quarrelsome lover, caused him to drop the hand which he was holding out to his friend and to turn back towards the stage.
It was an exquisite voice that had spoken﻿—a voice mellow and tender, with deep tones in it that betrayed latent power. The voice had caused Armand to look, the lips that spoke forged the first tiny link of that chain which riveted him forever after to the speaker.
It is difficult to say if such a thing really exists as love at first sight. Poets and romancists will have us believe that it does; idealists swear by it as being the only true love worthy of the name.
I do not know if I am prepared to admit their theory with regard to Armand St. Just. Mlle. Lange’s exquisite voice certainly had charmed him to the extent of making him forget his mistrust of de Batz and his desire to get away. Mechanically almost he sat down again, and leaning both elbows on the edge of the box, he rested his chin in his hand, and listened. The words which the late M. de Molière puts into the mouth of Célimène are trite and flippant enough, yet every time that Mlle. Lange’s lips moved Armand watched her, entranced.
There, no doubt, the matter would have ended: a young man fascinated by a pretty woman on the stage﻿—’tis a small matter, and one from which there doth not often spring a weary trail of tragic circumstances. Armand, who had a passion for music, would have worshipped at the shrine of Mlle.

===Chinese===

德巴茨——又恼又急——同他朋友的交道还远没有打完。他自觉那套虚有其表的宏论——宣讲时信念十足——已在年轻人心里刻下了几分印记。这印记他还想加深；于是阿尔芒绞尽脑汁寻一个体面的脱身借口时，德巴茨也在绞尽脑汁寻一个留人的由头。
就在此时，那位任性无常的机缘之魔插手了。圣茹斯特若早起身两分钟，他那机敏的脑子若更快一点把借口送到嘴边——谁知道呢——多少难言的哀恸、多少摧心的苦楚、多少可怕的耻辱，本可从他和他所爱的人身上免去？那两分钟——他若知道便好了——决定了他此后一生的走向。借口已到了唇边，德巴茨正悻悻地预备道别，忽然塞利梅娜开口应答她那位好吵嘴的情人——说的不过是稀松平常的词句——却叫他垂下了伸向朋友的手，转身回到了舞台前。
那是一副精妙绝伦的嗓音——圆润、温柔，深沉的音色里透着蕴而未发的力量。这嗓音引得阿尔芒抬眼；说话的那两片唇，铸成了那条锁链的第一环——从此把他同这嗓音的主人铐在一起，永生难解。
一见钟情这回事究竟有没有，很难说。诗人和传奇作家偏要我们信它有；理想主义者指天誓日，说唯有这种爱才配称真爱。
至于阿尔芒·圣茹斯特，我不知是否也肯把这套理论用在他身上。郎格小姐精妙的嗓音确实把他迷住了，迷得他忘掉了对德巴茨的戒心，忘掉了脱身的念头。他几乎是机械地重新落座，两肘支在包厢边沿，手托着下巴，听了下去。已故莫里哀先生放进塞利梅娜嘴里的台词，本够陈腐轻浮的了；可郎格小姐的唇每动一动，阿尔芒都看得神魂颠倒。
事情无疑本该就此了结：一个年轻人被台上的漂亮女人迷住——区区小事一桩，殊少由此牵出一长串累人的悲剧。阿尔芒素来痴迷音乐，本该在郎

===Original===

Lange’s perfect voice until the curtain came down on the last act, had not his friend de Batz seen the keen enchantment which the actress had produced on the young enthusiast.
Now de Batz was a man who never allowed an opportunity to slip by, if that opportunity led towards the furtherance of his own desires. He did not want to lose sight of Armand just yet, and here the good demon Chance had given him an opportunity for obtaining what he wanted.
He waited quietly until the fall of the curtain at the end of Act II; then, as Armand, with a sigh of delight, leaned back in his chair, and closing his eyes appeared to be living the last half-hour all over again, de Batz remarked with well-assumed indifference:
“Mlle. Lange is a promising young actress. Do you not think so, my friend?”
“She has a perfect voice﻿—it was exquisite melody to the ear,” replied Armand. “I was conscious of little else.”
“She is a beautiful woman, nevertheless,” continued de Batz with a smile. “During the next act, my good St. Just, I would suggest that you open your eyes as well as your ears.”
Armand did as he was bidden. The whole appearance of Mlle. Lange seemed in harmony with her voice. She was not very tall, but eminently graceful, with a small, oval face and slender, almost childlike figure, which appeared still more so above the wide hoops and draped panniers of the fashions of Molière’s time.
Whether she was beautiful or not the young man hardly knew. Measured by certain standards, she certainly was not so, for her mouth was not small, and her nose anything but classical in outline. But the eyes were brown, and they had that half-veiled look in them﻿—shaded with long lashes that seemed to make a perpetual tender appeal to the masculine heart: the lips, too, were full and moist, and the teeth dazzling white. Yes!﻿—on the whole we might easily say that she was exquisite, even though we did not admit that she was beautiful.
Painter David has made a sketch of her; we have all seen it at the Musée Carnavalet, and all wondered why that charming, if irregular, little face made such an impression of sadness.
There are five acts in *Le Misanthrope*, during which Célimène is almost constantly on the stage. At the end of the fourth act de Batz said casually to his friend:
“I have the honour of personal acquaintanceship with Mlle. Lange.

===Chinese===

格小姐那完美嗓音的神龛前膜拜到末幕落帘——若不是他的朋友德巴茨看出了这位女演员在这热心少年身上施下的有力魔法。
而德巴茨这人，只要机会通向他欲望的进路，从不肯放它溜走。他还不想让阿尔芒走出视线；好个机缘之魔，这一回给了他一个到手的良机。
他不动声色，直等到第二幕落幕；随后，阿尔芒舒坦地叹一口气，仰进椅背，阖上眼，仿佛把方才那半个钟头重新过了一遍；德巴茨这才用装得很像的淡漠口吻说道：
“郎格小姐是个前程无量的年轻演员。你不觉得吗，我的朋友？”
“她有一副十全的嗓子——听在耳朵里真是精妙的音乐，”阿尔芒答道，“旁的我就顾不上了。”
“不过她也是位美丽的女子，”德巴茨含笑接着说，“下一幕里，我亲爱的圣茹斯特，我劝你把眼睛也一并张开。”
阿尔芒从命。郎格小姐整个的模样，同她的嗓音再和谐不过。她个子不高，却风姿绝佳，一张小小的鹅蛋脸，身材纤瘦，近乎孩童——衬在莫里哀时代那宽大的裙撑和垂褶的侧裙之上，愈显得小了。
她算不算美，年轻人说不清楚。拿某些尺子量，她当然不算：嘴不小，鼻子的轮廓也全不合古典。可那双眼睛是褐色的，眼里笼着那层半遮半掩的光——长长的睫毛覆着，仿佛向男子的心发出永恒的温柔恳求；唇也是，丰润潮湿，牙齿白得耀眼。是啊！——大体上说，尽可称她精妙动人，纵使我们不肯认她作美人。
画家大卫给她画过一幅速写；我们在卡纳瓦莱博物馆都见过，也都不免纳闷：这张讨人喜欢却不够端正的小脸，为何透着那样一股愁绪。
《愤世者》共五幕，塞利梅娜几乎场场在台上。第四幕末了，德巴茨随口对朋友说：
“我有幸同郎格小姐有一面之雅。

===Original===

An you care for an introduction to her, we can go round to the greenroom after the play.”
Did prudence then whisper, “Desist”? Did loyalty to the leader murmur, “Obey”? It were indeed difficult to say. Armand St. Just was not five-and-twenty, and Mlle. Lange’s melodious voice spoke louder than the whisperings of prudence or even than the call of duty.
He thanked de Batz warmly, and during the last half-hour, while the misanthropical lover spurned repentant Célimène, he was conscious of a curious sensation of impatience, a tingling of his nerves, a wild, mad longing to hear those full moist lips pronounce his name, and have those large brown eyes throw their half-veiled look into his own.

===Chinese===

你若有兴致引见，散了戏咱们就到后台去。”
那时谨慎可曾低语“住手”？对首领的忠义可曾呢喃“守戒”？实在难说。阿尔芒·圣茹斯特还不满二十五岁；郎格小姐婉转的歌喉，说得比谨慎的耳语响，也比职责的召唤响。
他热诚地谢了德巴茨；最后半个钟头里，台上那位厌世的情人拒斥着悔恨的塞利梅娜，他却只觉得一阵古怪的焦躁，神经阵阵发麻，一股狂热的、难以按捺的渴望：想听那两片丰润潮湿的唇念出他的名字，想让那双褐色大眼把半遮半掩的目光，投进他自己的眼睛。

## IV Mademoiselle Lange / IV 朗热小姐
===Original===

The greenroom was crowded when de Batz and St. Just arrived there after the performance. The older man cast a hasty glance through the open door. The crowd did not suit his purpose, and he dragged his companion hurriedly away from the contemplation of Mlle. Lange, sitting in a far corner of the room, surrounded by an admiring throng, and by innumerable floral tributes offered to her beauty and to her success.
De Batz without a word led the way back towards the stage. Here, by the dim light of tallow candles fixed in sconces against the surrounding walls, the scene-shifters were busy moving drop-scenes, back cloths and wings, and paid no heed to the two men who strolled slowly up and down silently, each wrapped in his own thoughts.
Armand walked with his hands buried in his breeches pockets, his head bent forward on his chest; but every now and again he threw quick, apprehensive glances round him whenever a firm step echoed along the empty stage or a voice rang clearly through the now deserted theatre.
“Are we wise to wait here?” he asked, speaking to himself rather than to his companion.
He was not anxious about his own safety; but the words of de Batz had impressed themselves upon his mind: “Héron and his spies we have always with us.”
From the greenroom a separate foyer and exit led directly out into the street. Gradually the sound of many voices, the loud laughter and occasional snatches of song which for the past half-hour had proceeded from that part of the house, became more subdued and more rare. One by one the friends of the artists were leaving the theatre, after having paid the usual banal compliments to those whom they favoured, or presented the accustomed offering of flowers to the brightest star of the night.
The actors were the first to retire, then the older actresses, the ones who could no longer command a court of admirers round them. They all filed out of the greenroom and crossed the stage to where, at the back, a narrow, rickety wooden stairs led to their so-called dressing-rooms﻿—tiny, dark cubicles, ill-lighted, unventilated, where some half-dozen of the lesser stars tumbled over one another while removing wigs and greasepaint.
Armand and de Batz watched this exodus, both with equal impatience. Mlle. Lange was the last to leave the greenroom.

===Chinese===

散场之后，德巴茨和圣茹斯特来到后台休息室，里面已经挤满了人。年长的那位从敞开的门口匆匆扫了一眼。人多碍事，不合他的来意，便一把拽走同伴，不让他再看朗热小姐（Mlle. Lange）——她坐在屋子最远的一角，被一群爱慕者团团围住，脚边堆着数不清的花束，都是献给她的美貌和她的成功的。
德巴茨一言不发，领路回到舞台上。这里，四壁烛台上插着的牛油烛光昏昏沉沉，换景工人们正忙着挪吊幕、背景布和侧幕，谁也不理会这两个一言不发来回踱步的人——各自想着各自的心事。
阿尔芒双手插在马裤口袋里，头垂在胸前走着；可每当坚实的脚步声在空荡的舞台上一路回响，或是说话声在已无人迹的剧场里清清亮亮地荡起，他便时不时飞快而警觉地四下张望。
“我们在这儿等，妥当么？”他问，倒像自言自语，不是说给同伴听。
他倒不是担心自己的安危；只是德巴茨那句话已刻进了他心里：“埃龙和他的密探，永远跟咱们同在。”
后台另有一间休息厅和一道旁门，直通街面。那半点钟里从那边传来的人声喧哗、放声大笑和不时的几句歌吟，渐渐低下去、稀下去了。艺人们的朋友们，在对各自捧的角儿照例客套一番、或是给今夜最亮的明星献上例行的花束之后，一个接一个离了剧场。
男演员们最先退场，随后是年长的女演员——那些再也召不来一圈爱慕者簇拥的。他们鱼贯走出休息室，穿过舞台，到后头一道又窄又晃的木楼梯那儿去——楼梯通向所谓化装间：一间间又小又黑的格子，光线昏暗，不通风，六七个二三流的角儿在里面摘假发、卸油彩，挤作一团。
阿尔芒和德巴茨看着这一场退场，两人同样心焦。朗热小姐是最后一个离开休息室的。

===Original===

For some time, since the crowd had become thinner round her, Armand had contrived to catch glimpses of her slight, elegant figure. A short passage led from the stage to the greenroom door, which was wide open, and at the corner of this passage the young man had paused from time to time in his walk, gazing with earnest admiration at the dainty outline of the young girl’s head, with its wig of powdered curls that seemed scarcely whiter than the creamy brilliance of her skin.
De Batz did not watch Mlle. Lange beyond casting impatient looks in the direction of the crowd that prevented her leaving the greenroom. He did watch Armand, however﻿—noted his eager look, his brisk and alert movements, the obvious glances of admiration which he cast in the direction of the young actress, and this seemed to afford him a considerable amount of contentment.
The best part of an hour had gone by since the fall of the curtain before Mlle. Lange finally dismissed her many admirers, and de Batz had the satisfaction of seeing her running down the passage, turning back occasionally in order to bid gay “good nights” to the loiterers who were loath to part from her. She was a child in all her movements, quite unconscious of self or of her own charms, but frankly delighted with her success. She was still dressed in the ridiculous hoops and panniers pertaining to her part, and the powdered peruke hid the charm of her own hair; the costume gave a certain stilted air to her unaffected personality, which, by this very sense of contrast, was essentially fascinating.
In her arms she held a huge sheaf of sweet-scented narcissi, the spoils of some favoured spot far away in the South. Armand thought that never in his life had he seen anything so winsome or so charming.
Having at last said the positively final adieu, Mlle. Lange with a happy little sigh turned to run down the passage.
She came face to face with Armand, and gave a sudden little gasp of terror. It was not good these days to come on any loiterer unawares.
But already de Batz had quickly joined his friend, and his smooth, pleasant voice, and podgy, beringed hand extended towards Mlle. Lange, were sufficient to reassure her.
“You were so surrounded in the greenroom, mademoiselle,” he said courteously, “I did not venture to press in among the crowd of your admirers.

===Chinese===

有一阵子了，她身边的人群渐见稀落，阿尔芒已能望见她那纤细苗条的身影。从舞台到休息室的门有一条短短的过道，门大敞着；年轻人踱步时，时时在这过道口停下，满心爱慕地端详那姑娘头部秀气的轮廓——头上扑粉的鬈发假套，似乎也不见得比她肌肤乳白的光泽更白。
德巴茨对朗热小姐本无多少留意，只是不耐烦地朝那群拦住她脱身的人瞥上几眼。他留意的倒是阿尔芒——留意他热切的神色，他轻快警觉的举动，他朝年轻女演员投去的显而易见的倾慕目光；这一切似乎让他大为称心。
落幕后足有大半个钟头，朗热小姐才终于打发走她那一大群爱慕者；德巴茨满意地看见她顺着过道跑来，间或回头，向那些恋恋不舍的闲人抛去快活的“晚安”。她的一举一动全是个孩子，浑然不觉有自己、也不觉得自己俏丽，只是一派由衷的、成功的欢喜。她身上还套着剧中那套可笑的裙撑和腰篮，扑粉的假发掩住了她本头发的风致；这身行头给她不假雕饰的天性平添了几分呆板的架子——而恰恰是这份对照，教人格外着迷。
她怀里抱着一大捧芬芳的水仙，是远在南方哪处宝地的出产。阿尔芒心想，平生从没见过这样娇俏、这样动人的景象。
终于道过了千真万确是最后一声的再会，朗热小姐心满意足地轻叹一声，转身顺着过道跑来。
她同阿尔芒打了个照面，惊得猛吸了一小口气。这些日子，冷不丁撞见什么守候的人，可不是玩的。
好在德巴茨已快步赶到朋友身边；他那圆滑悦耳的声音，那只朝朗热小姐伸过来的、戴着戒指的胖手，足以叫她安下心来。
“小姐，您在休息室里被围得那样严实，”他彬彬有礼地说，“我不敢贸然挤进您那群爱慕者当中去。

===Original===

Yet I had the great wish to present my respectful congratulations in person.”
“Ah! *c’est ce cher de Batz!*” exclaimed mademoiselle gaily, in that exquisitely rippling voice of hers. “And where in the world do you spring from, my friend?
“Hush-sh-sh!” he whispered, holding her small bemittened hand in his, and putting one finger to his lips with an urgent entreaty for discretion; “not my name, I beg of you, fair one.”
“Bah!” she retorted lightly, even though her full lips trembled now as she spoke and belied her very words. “You need have no fear whilst you are in this part of the house. It is an understood thing that the Committee of General Security does not send its spies behind the curtain of a theatre. Why, if all of us actors and actresses were sent to the guillotine there would be no play on the morrow. Artistes are not replaceable in a few hours; those that are in existence must perforce be spared, or the citizens who govern us now would not know where to spend their evenings.”
But though she spoke so airily and with her accustomed gaiety, it was easily perceived that even on this childish mind the dangers which beset everyone these days had already imprinted their mark of suspicion and of caution.
“Come into my dressing-room,” she said. “I must not tarry here any longer, for they will be putting out the lights. But I have a room to myself, and we can talk there quite agreeably.”
She led the way across the stage towards the wooden stairs. Armand, who during this brief colloquy between his friend and the young girl had kept discreetly in the background, felt undecided what to do. But at a peremptory sign from de Batz he, too, turned in the wake of the gay little lady, who ran swiftly up the rickety steps, humming snatches of popular songs the while, and not turning to see if indeed the two men were following her.
She had the sheaf of narcissi still in her arms, and the door of her tiny dressing-room being open, she ran straight in and threw the flowers down in a confused, sweet-scented mass upon the small table that stood at one end of the room, littered with pots and bottles, letters, mirrors, powder-puffs, silk stockings, and cambric handkerchiefs.
Then she turned and faced the two men, a merry look of unalterable gaiety dancing in her eyes.

===Chinese===

可我又实在想当面呈上我恭敬的道贺。”
“啊！*这不是亲爱的德巴茨嘛！*（*c'est ce cher de Batz*!）小姐欢快地叫道，用的还是她那莺啼般清脆婉转的嗓音。“我的朋友，你到底是从哪儿冒出来的？
“‘嘘——嘘——嘘！’他低声道，把她戴着小手套的手握在自己手里，一根手指竖在唇边，急切地恳求她谨慎；“美人儿，求你别叫我的名字。”
“呸！”她轻快地回嘴——虽然说话时她那饱满的嘴唇已在发颤，泄露了嘴上的强硬。“你在这剧场里这一角，用不着怕。治安委员会（the Committee of General Security）不往戏幕后面派密探，这是心照不宣的规矩。你想啊，把我们这些男女演员统统送上断头台，明天晚上的戏谁来演？艺才不是几个钟头里补得上的；现存的这批人少不得要刀下留情，不然如今当家做主的公民们，晚上就没地方消遣了。”
话虽说得这样轻飘飘，同她惯常的欢快一样，可谁也看得出：就连这样一颗孩子气的心，这些日子四面逼人的凶险，也早已烙下了疑惧和提防的印记。
“到我化装间来吧，”她说，“我不能在这儿再耽搁了，他们就要熄灯。我有一间自己独用的屋子，咱们在那儿说话，舒舒服服。”
她领头穿过舞台，朝木楼梯走去。方才朋友同姑娘短短对答的当口，阿尔芒一直识趣地退在后头，此刻正不知如何是好。但德巴茨不容分说地做了个手势，他也便随着那位快活的小姐走了——她轻捷地蹬上摇晃的梯级，嘴里哼着流行歌曲的片段，头也不回，不管那两个男人跟没跟上。
她怀里还抱着那捧水仙；化装间的小门开着，她径直跑了进去，把花往屋角那张小桌上一撂——乱糟糟、香喷喷的一堆，落在满桌的膏子罐、瓶子、书信、镜子、粉扑、丝袜和细麻纱手帕中间。
然后她转过身来，面对两位来客，眼里跳动着一抹快活的神情——那是撼不动、改不了的欢快。

===Original===

“Shut the door, *mon ami*,” she said to de Batz, “and after that sit down where you can, so long as it is not on my most precious pot of unguent or a box of costliest powder.”
While de Batz did as he was told, she turned to Armand and said with a pretty tone of interrogation in her melodious voice:
“Monsieur?”
“St. Just, at your service, mademoiselle,” said Armand, bowing very low in the most approved style obtaining at the English Court.
“St. Just?” she repeated, a look of puzzlement in her brown eyes. “Surely﻿—”
“A kinsman of citizen St. Just, whom no doubt you know, mademoiselle,” he exclaimed.
“My friend Armand St. Just,” interposed de Batz, “is practically a newcomer in Paris. He lives in England habitually.”
“In England?” she exclaimed. “Oh! do tell me all about England. I would love to go there. Perhaps I may have to go some day. Oh! do sit down, de Batz,” she continued, talking rather volubly, even as a delicate blush heightened the colour in her cheeks under the look of obvious admiration from Armand St. Just’s expressive eyes.
She swept a handful of delicate cambric and silk from off a chair, making room for de Batz’ portly figure. Then she sat upon the sofa, and with an inviting gesture and a call from the eyes she bade Armand sit down next to her. She leaned back against the cushions, and the table being close by, she stretched out a hand and once more took up the bunch of narcissi, and while she talked to Armand she held the snow-white blooms quite close to her face﻿—so close, in fact, that he could not see her mouth and chin, only her dark eyes shone across at him over the heads of the blossoms.
“Tell me all about England,” she reiterated, settling herself down among the cushions like a spoilt child who is about to listen to an oft-told favourite story.
Armand was vexed that de Batz was sitting there. He felt he could have told this dainty little lady quite a good deal about England if only his pompous, fat friend would have had the good sense to go away.
As it was, he felt unusually timid and gauche, not quite knowing what to say, a fact which seemed to amuse Mlle. Lange not a little.
“I am very fond of England,” he said lamely; “my sister is married to an Englishman, and I myself have taken up my permanent residence there.”
“Among the society of émigrés?” she queried.
Then, as Armand made no reply, de Batz interposed quickly:
“Oh!

===Chinese===

“把门关上，*我的朋友*（*mon ami*），”她对德巴茨说，“关了门，哪儿能坐坐哪儿，只别坐了我那罐顶金贵的香膏，也别坐了一盒顶值钱的香粉。”
德巴茨依言关门去了；她转向阿尔芒，那音乐似的嗓音里带着一声娇俏的问询：
“先生贵姓？”
“圣茹斯特，听候小姐差遣。”阿尔芒说着，深深一躬——行的是英国宫廷里最时新的礼数。
“圣茹斯特？”她重复了一遍，褐色的眼睛里露出困惑。“该不会——”
“小姐想必认识圣茹斯特公民；在下是他的一位亲戚。”他急忙说。
“我的朋友阿尔芒·圣茹斯特，”德巴茨插进来，“在巴黎差不多算个新客。他常年住在英国。”
“住在英国？”她叫起来，“啊！快把英国的事统统讲给我听。我真想去那儿。说不定哪天还非去不可呢。啊！你也坐呀，德巴茨。”她接着说，话头连珠一般——阿尔芒·圣茹斯特那双会说话的眼睛明明白白倾慕地看着她，一层娇羞的红晕悄悄爬上她的脸颊，把颜色衬得更足了。
她一挥手，把椅上一把细麻纱和丝绸扫开，给德巴茨肥硕的身躯腾出地方。她自己坐上沙发，一手相邀，眼波一招，叫阿尔芒挨着她坐下。她向后靠进软垫里；桌子就在手边，她伸手又拿起那束水仙，同阿尔芒说话的当口，把那一片雪白的花捧得离脸极近——近得他看不见她的嘴和下颌，只看见她那双黑眼睛在花头上方灼灼望过来。
“把英国的事统统讲给我听嘛。”她又催了一遍，在软垫里坐得舒舒服服，像个等着听讲过千百遍的心爱故事的被宠坏的孩子。
阿尔芒懊恼德巴茨坐在那里。他心想，倘若他那神气活现的胖朋友有这点识相，肯走开，关于英国，他满可以讲给这位娇俏的小姐听的事多着呢。
眼下呢，他觉得异乎寻常地羞怯、笨拙，不知说什么好——这一层，似乎叫朗热小姐觉得颇有乐趣。
“我很喜欢英国，”他讷讷地说，“我姐姐嫁了一位英国人，我自己也在那里落了籍，长住下来。”
“混在流亡贵族的圈子里？”她问。
阿尔芒没有作声；德巴茨忙插进来：
“哦！

===Original===

you need not fear to admit it, my good Armand; Mademoiselle Lange, has many friends among the émigrés﻿—have you not, mademoiselle?”
“Yes, of course,” she replied lightly; “I have friends everywhere. Their political views have nothing to do with me. Artistes, I think, should have naught to do with politics. You see, citizen St. Just, I never inquired of you what were your views. Your name and kinship would proclaim you a partisan of citizen Robespierre, yet I find you in the company of M. de Batz; and you tell me that you live in England.”
“He is no partisan of citizen Robespierre,” again interposed de Batz; “in fact, mademoiselle, I may safely tell you, I think, that my friend has but one ideal on this earth, whom he has set up in a shrine, and whom he worships with all the ardour of a Christian for his God.”
“How romantic!” she said, and she looked straight at Armand. “Tell me, monsieur, is your ideal a woman or a man?”
His look answered her, even before he boldly spoke the two words:
“A woman.”
She took a deep draught of sweet, intoxicating scent from the narcissi, and his gaze once more brought blushes to her cheeks. De Batz’ good-humoured laugh helped her to hide this unwonted access of confusion.
“That was well turned, friend Armand,” he said lightly; “but I assure you, mademoiselle, that before I brought him here tonight his ideal was a man.”
“A man!” she exclaimed, with a contemptuous little pout. “Who was it?”
“I know no other name for him but that of a small, insignificant flower﻿—the Scarlet Pimpernel,” replied de Batz.
“The Scarlet Pimpernel!” she ejaculated, dropping the flowers suddenly, and gazing on Armand with wide, wondering eyes. “And do you know him, monsieur?”
He was frowning despite himself, despite the delight which he felt at sitting so close to this charming little lady, and feeling that in a measure his presence and his personality interested her. But he felt irritated with de Batz, and angered at what he considered the latter’s indiscretion. To him the very name of his leader was almost a sacred one; he was one of those enthusiastic devotees who only care to name the idol of their dreams with bated breath, and only in the ears of those who would understand and sympathise.

===Chinese===

你不用怕认这一层，我的好阿尔芒；朗热小姐在流亡贵族里朋友多着呢——是不是，小姐？”
