“是啊，这个自然，”她轻巧地答，“我到处都有朋友。他们的政见与我无干。依我看，艺人与政治本该两不相干。您瞧，圣茹斯特公民，我可从没问过您持什么见解。论您的姓氏亲谊，您该是罗伯斯庇尔公民一党；可我见您同德巴茨先生做伴，您又说您住在英国。”
“他才不是罗伯斯庇尔公民那一党，”德巴茨又插嘴，“实不相瞒，小姐，我想我可以放心对您说：我的朋友在这世上只有一个偶像，供在神龛里，他以基督徒敬上帝那样的热诚敬奉着。”
“多罗曼蒂克！”她说，眼睛直直望着阿尔芒。“告诉我，先生，您那偶像，是个女人，还是个男人？”
他还没开口，眼神已经替他答了；随即他又堂堂正正吐出两个字：
“女人。”
她深深吸了一口水仙馥郁醉人的香气；他的目光又一次把红晕送上了她的面颊。多亏德巴茨一声好脾气的笑，替她遮过了这阵不寻常的失措。
“答得妙，阿尔芒朋友，”他轻巧地说，“不过小姐，我向您担保，今晚我带他来之前，他的偶像还是个男人。”
“男人！”她叫起来，不屑地撇了撇小嘴。“是谁？”
“他么，除了一个小小的、不起眼的花名，我没有别的叫法——红花侠（the Scarlet Pimpernel）。”德巴茨答道。
“红花侠！”她失声叫道，手里的花陡然落下，睁大了一双惊奇的眼睛望着阿尔芒。“那么先生，您认识他？”
他不自觉地皱起了眉头——虽然坐得离这迷人的小姐这样近是桩乐事，虽然觉出自己的人、自己的性情多少引起了她的兴味。可他对德巴茨着恼，为他认定是失于轻率的那句话动气。在他心里，首领的名字几乎是神圣的；他是那样一派热烈的信徒——梦中的偶像，只肯屏着气去提，只肯在懂得、也体贴这份心意的人耳边提。

===Original===

Again he felt that if only he could have been alone with mademoiselle he could have told her all about the Scarlet Pimpernel, knowing that in her he would find a ready listener, a helping and a loving heart; but as it was he merely replied tamely enough:
“Yes, mademoiselle, I do know him.”
“You have seen him?” she queried eagerly; “spoken to him?”
“Yes.”
“Oh! do tell me all about him. You know quite a number of us in France have the greatest possible admiration for your national hero. We know, of course, that he is an enemy of our Government﻿—but, oh! we feel that he is not an enemy of France because of that. We are a nation of heroes, too, monsieur,” she added with a pretty, proud toss of the head; “we can appreciate bravery and resource, and we love the mystery that surrounds the personality of your Scarlet Pimpernel. But since you know him, monsieur, tell me what is he like?”
Armand was smiling again. He was yielding himself up wholly to the charm which emanated from this young girl’s entire being, from her gaiety and her unaffectedness, her enthusiasm, and that obvious artistic temperament which caused her to feel every sensation with superlative keenness and thoroughness.
“What is he like?” she insisted.
“That, mademoiselle,” he replied, “I am not at liberty to tell you.”
“Not at liberty to tell me!” she exclaimed; “but monsieur, if I command you﻿—”
“At risk of falling forever under the ban of your displeasure, mademoiselle, I would still remain silent on that subject.”
She gazed on him with obvious astonishment. It was quite an unusual thing for this spoilt darling of an admiring public to be thus openly thwarted in her whims.
“How tiresome and pedantic!” she said, with a shrug of her pretty shoulders and a moue of discontent. “And, oh! how ungallant! You have learnt ugly, English ways, monsieur; for there, I am told, men hold their womenkind in very scant esteem. There!” she added, turning with a mock air of hopelessness towards de Batz, “am I not a most unlucky woman? For the past two years I have used my best endeavours to catch sight of that interesting Scarlet Pimpernel; here do I meet monsieur, who actually knows him (so he says), and he is so ungallant that he even refuses to satisfy the first cravings of my just curiosity.”
“Citizen St.

===Chinese===

他又一次想：倘若能同小姐单独相处，他尽可以把红花侠的事原原本本讲给她听——他知道在她这里，他准能寻到一只现成的耳朵、一副热心肠、一颗有爱的心；可眼下的情形，他只能干巴巴地答一句：
“是的，小姐，我认识他。”
“您见过他？”她急切地问，“同他说过话？”
“见过，说过。”
“啊！快把他的一切讲给我听。您知道，我们法国人里头，有好大一批人佩服您们这位民族英雄佩服得五体投地。我们当然知道他是我们政府的敌人——可是，啊！我们觉得，他并不因此就是法兰西的敌人。我们也是一个英雄的国度呢，先生，”她添了一句，娇俏而骄傲地把头一扬，“勇毅和急智，我们懂得赏识；您们那位红花侠浑身上下的神秘，我们爱得不得了。您既然认识他，先生，就说说吧，他生得什么模样？”
阿尔芒又微笑起来。他整个儿沉进了这少女浑身散发出的魅力里——她的欢快，她的天真不做作，她的热诚，还有那显而易见的艺人气质：每一种感受，她都体味到十二分的敏锐、十二分的彻底。
“他生得什么模样嘛？”她追着不放。
“小姐，”他答道，“这个，我无权奉告。”
“无权奉告！”她嚷起来，“可是先生，要是我命令您——”
“哪怕从此永远失宠于小姐、遭小姐的白眼，这个题目，我也只有守口如瓶。”
她望着他，明明白白地惊愕了。这位被满堂看客宠坏了的娇女儿，任性使性竟这样当众碰壁，还是破天荒头一遭。
“真讨厌，真迂！”她说，俏丽的肩一耸，噘起小嘴，一脸的不痛快。“而且，啊！多不会献殷勤！先生，您学了一身英国人的坏做派；听说在那边，男人们是不大把女人放在眼里的。瞧！”她转向德巴茨，装出一副无可奈何的样儿，“我还不算天下头一号倒运的女人么？整整两年，我想尽法子要见一见那位有意思的红花侠；好不容易遇上这位先生——他当真认识那位（他自己是这么说的）——偏偏又这样不解风情，连我一星半点正当的好奇心都不肯满足。”
“圣茹斯特公民——”

===Original===

Just will tell you nothing now, mademoiselle,” rejoined de Batz with his good-humoured laugh; “it is my presence, I assure you, which is setting a seal upon his lips. He is, believe me, aching to confide in you, to share in your enthusiasm, and to see your beautiful eyes glowing in response to his ardour when he describes to you the exploits of that prince of heroes. *En tête-à-tête* one day, you will, I know, worm every secret out of my discreet friend Armand.”
Mademoiselle made no comment on this﻿—that is to say, no audible comment﻿—but she buried the whole of her face for a few seconds among the flowers, and Armand from amongst those flowers caught sight of a pair of very bright brown eyes which shone on him with a puzzled look.
She said nothing more about the Scarlet Pimpernel or about England just then, but after awhile she began talking of more indifferent subjects: the state of the weather, the price of food, the discomforts of her own house, now that the servants had been put on perfect equality with their masters.
Armand soon gathered that the burning questions of the day, the horrors of massacres, the raging turmoil of politics, had not affected her very deeply as yet. She had not troubled her pretty head very much about the social and humanitarian aspect of the present seething revolution. She did not really wish to think about it at all. An artiste to her fingertips, she was spending her young life in earnest work, striving to attain perfection in her art, absorbed in study during the day, and in the expression of what she had learnt in the evenings.
The terrors of the guillotine affected her a little, but somewhat vaguely still. She had not realised that any dangers could assail her whilst she worked for the artistic delectation of the public.
It was not that she did not understand what went on around her, but that her artistic temperament and her environment had kept her aloof from it all. The horrors of the Place de la Révolution made her shudder, but only in the same way as the tragedies of M. Racine or of Sophocles which she had studied caused her to shudder, and she had exactly the same sympathy for poor Queen Marie Antoinette as she had for Mary Stuart, and shed as many tears for King Louis as she did for Polyeucte.

===Chinese===

“——眼下什么也不会告诉您的，小姐，”德巴茨带着他好脾气的笑接话道；“我向您担保，封住他嘴的，正是我在场。信我吧，他正憋着想向您倾吐，想同您共享那份热情，想在给您讲那位英雄中的王子的功业时，看您那双美丽的眼睛燃起回应的光。哪天*两人对坐*（*En tête-à-tête*）聊起来，我知道，我这位口风紧的朋友阿尔芒的每一样秘密，您都能套得出来。”
小姐对这话没有表示——至少没有出声的表示——只是把整张脸埋进花束里去了几秒钟；阿尔芒隔着花枝，撞见一双亮亮的褐色眼睛，正带着困惑的神色望着他。
那会儿她便不再提红花侠，也不再提英国，过了一阵，谈起些不相干的题目来：天气啦，吃食的价钱啦，还有自家的种种不便——如今仆人们同主人已经完全平起平坐了。
阿尔芒很快便听出来：时下那些火烧眉毛的大事、屠杀的恐怖、政治的狂澜，都还没有深伤到她。对眼下这场沸腾革命的世道人心，她那颗秀美的头颅还不曾多费过神。她也实在不想去想。她是个彻头彻尾的艺人，把青春消磨在认真的劳作里，一心在艺术上求臻至善：白天潜心研习，夜里把学来的东西倾心演绎。
断头台的恐怖，她也略有触动，但还朦朦胧胧。她不曾意识到，自己为公众的艺术赏悦操劳，还会有什么凶险临头。
倒不是她不明白周遭发生的一切，而是她的艺人气质同她的环境，使她远远避开了这一切。革命广场（the Place de la Révolution）上的惨状也令她战栗，可那战栗，同她研读过的拉辛先生或索福克勒斯的悲剧所引起的一模一样；她对可怜的玛丽·安托瓦内特王后的同情，恰如同对玛丽·斯图亚特的同情；为路易王洒的眼泪，也同她为波吕厄克特洒的一样多。

===Original===

Once de Batz mentioned the Dauphin, but mademoiselle put up her hand quickly and said in a trembling voice, whilst the tears gathered in her eyes:
“Do not speak of the child to me, de Batz. What can I, a lonely, hardworking woman, do to help him? I try not to think of him, for if I did, knowing my own helplessness, I feel that I could hate my countrymen, and speak my bitter hatred of them across the footlights; which would be more than foolish,” she added naively, “for it would not help the child, and I should be sent to the guillotine. But oh! sometimes I feel that I would gladly die if only that poor little child-martyr were restored to those who love him and given back once more to joy and happiness. But they would not take my life for his, I am afraid,” she concluded, smiling through her tears. “My life is of no value in comparison with his.”
Soon after this she dismissed her two visitors. De Batz, well content with the result of this evening’s entertainment, wore an urbane, bland smile on his rubicund face. Armand, somewhat serious and not a little in love, made the hand-kiss with which he took his leave last as long as he could.
“You will come and see me again, citizen St. Just?” she asked after that preliminary leave-taking.
“At your service, mademoiselle,” he replied with alacrity.
“How long do you stay in Paris?”
“I may be called away at any time.”
“Well, then, come tomorrow. I shall be free towards four o’clock. Square du Roule. You cannot miss the house. Anyone there will tell you where lives citizeness Lange.”
“At your service, mademoiselle,” he replied.
The words sounded empty and meaningless, but his eyes, as they took final leave of her, spoke the gratitude and the joy which he felt.

===Chinese===

有一回德巴茨提到王太子（the Dauphin），小姐急忙抬手拦住，声音发颤地说，泪水已在她眼里聚起：
“别同我提那孩子，德巴茨。我孤身一人，一个卖力气吃饭的女人，能为他做什么呢？我尽量不去想他；因为一想，想到自己这样无能为力，我怕我会恨起我的同胞来，会站到台口，隔着脚灯把我满腔的恨骂出来——那可就蠢透了，”她天真地补了一句，“因为那救不了孩子，我自己倒要上断头台。可是，啊！有时我想，只要那可怜的小小殉道者能回到爱他的人身边，重新得到欢乐和幸福，我死也甘愿。只怕他们不肯拿我的命换他的命。”她含着泪笑了，收尾道，“比起他的命，我这条命不值什么。”
这之后不久，她便送客了。德巴茨对今晚这场消遣的结果心满意足，红彤彤的脸上挂着一团和气的笑。阿尔芒有些郑重，也有些动了情，临别吻手的那一礼，能拖多长便拖了多长。
客套道别过后，她问：“您还会再来看我么，圣茹斯特公民？”
“听候小姐差遣。”他欣然答道。
“您在巴黎还要待多久？”
“说不定随时就得走。”
“那么，明天就来吧。四点来钟我有空。鲁勒广场（Square du Roule）。那座宅子您一找就着。随便问谁，都会告诉您女公民朗热住在哪儿。”
“听候小姐差遣。”他答道。
话音空洞，了无意味；可他的眼睛——在最后辞别她的那一刻——把他心里的感激和欢喜，都说尽了。

## V The Temple Prison / 圣殿塔监狱
===Original===

It was close on midnight when the two friends finally parted company outside the doors of the theatre. The night air struck with biting keenness against them when they emerged from the stuffy, overheated building, and both wrapped their caped cloaks tightly round their shoulders. Armand﻿—more than ever now﻿—was anxious to rid himself of de Batz. The Gascon’s platitudes irritated him beyond the bounds of forbearance, and he wanted to be alone, so that he might think over the events of this night, the chief event being a little lady with an enchanting voice and the most fascinating brown eyes he had ever seen.
Self-reproach, too, was fighting a fairly even fight with the excitement that had been called up by that same pair of brown eyes. Armand for the past four or five hours had acted in direct opposition to the earnest advice given to him by his chief; he had renewed one friendship which had been far better left in oblivion, and he had made an acquaintance which already was leading him along a path that he felt sure his comrade would disapprove. But the path was so profusely strewn with scented narcissi that Armand’s sensitive conscience was quickly lulled to rest by the intoxicating fragrance.
Looking neither to right nor left, he made his way very quickly up the Rue Richelieu towards the Montmartre quarter, where he lodged.
De Batz stood and watched him for as long as the dim lights of the street lamps illumined his slim, soberly-clad figure; then he turned on his heel and walked off in the opposite direction.
His florid, pockmarked face wore an air of contentment not altogether unmixed with a kind of spiteful triumph.
“So, my pretty Scarlet Pimpernel,” he muttered between his closed lips, “you wish to meddle in my affairs, to have for yourself and your friends the credit and glory of snatching the golden prize from the clutches of these murderous brutes. Well, we shall see! We shall see which is the wiliest﻿—the French ferret or the English fox.”
He walked deliberately away from the busy part of the town, turning his back on the river, stepping out briskly straight before him, and swinging his gold-beaded cane as he walked.
The streets which he had to traverse were silent and deserted, save occasionally where a drinking or an eating house had its swing-doors still invitingly open.

===Chinese===

两位朋友在戏院门口最终分手时，已近午夜。从那座闷热难当的屋子里出来，夜气凛冽刺骨，两人都把带披肩的斗篷紧紧裹上肩头。阿尔芒——此刻比任何时候更急——只想摆脱德巴茨。这加斯科尼人的陈词滥调磨得他忍无可忍；他要独处，好把今夜的事想一想——头一件事，便是那位嗓音醉人、生着他平生所见最摄人心魄的褐色眼睛的小妇人。
自责也正同那双褐眼睛唤起的兴奋，打得难分难解。过去这四五个钟头，阿尔芒的一举一动都同首领恳切的告诫背道而驰；他续上了一段本该永远遗忘的交情，又结了一段新知——这新知已引他走上一条路，他有十足把握，同伴们必不赞成。可这条路上水仙开得那样繁、香得那样郁，阿尔芒那敏感的良心，很快便被熏人的芬芳哄睡了。
他目不斜视，快步沿黎塞留街朝蒙马特区走去——他的住处就在那里。
德巴茨立在原地，望着街灯昏黄的光照亮那深色衣着、身形瘦长的人影，直到望不见；随后他一转身，朝相反方向去了。
他那张红润的麻脸上，挂着一种心满意足的神气，还搅着几分恶狠狠的得胜味道。
“好啊，我漂亮的红花侠，”他从闭紧的齿缝里咕哝，“你想插手我的事，想同你那班朋友抢功邀荣，把金子做的奖赏从这班杀人畜生的爪子里夺走。好，咱们走着瞧！走着瞧——到底是法国的雪貂滑，还是英国的狐狸刁。”
他存心避开闹市，背朝着河，一路笔直大步流星，边走边甩他那根金头手杖。
一路要穿过的街道又静又空，只是偶尔有酒馆饭铺，弹簧门还大开着的，透着招引。

===Original===

From these places, as de Batz strode rapidly by, came sounds of loud voices, rendered raucous by outdoor oratory; volleys of oaths hurled irreverently in the midst of impassioned speeches; interruptions from rowdy audiences that vied with the speaker in invectives and blasphemies; wordy war-fares that ended in noisy vituperations; accusations hurled through the air heavy with tobacco smoke and the fumes of cheap wines and of raw spirits.
De Batz took no heed of these as he passed, anxious only that the crowd of eating-house politicians did not, as often was its wont, turn out pêle-mêle into the street, and settle its quarrel by the weight of fists. He did not wish to be embroiled in a street fight, which invariably ended in denunciations and arrests, and was glad when presently he had left the purlieus of the Palais Royal behind him, and could strike on his left toward the lonely Faubourg du Temple.
From the dim distance far away came at intervals the mournful sound of a roll of muffled drums, half veiled by the intervening hubbub of the busy night life of the great city. It proceeded from the Place de la Révolution, where a company of the National Guard were on night watch round the guillotine. The dull, intermittent notes of the drum came as a reminder to the free people of France that the watchdog of a vengeful revolution was alert night and day, never sleeping, ever wakeful, “beating up game for the guillotine,” as the new decree framed today by the Government of the people had ordered that it should do.
From time to time now the silence of this lonely street was broken by a sudden cry of terror, followed by the clash of arms, the inevitable volley of oaths, the call for help, the final moan of anguish. They were the ever-recurring brief tragedies which told of denunciations, of domiciliary search, of sudden arrests, of an agonising desire for life and for freedom﻿—for life under these same horrible conditions of brutality and of servitude, for freedom to breathe, if only a day or two longer, this air, polluted by filth and by blood.
De Batz, hardened to these scenes, paid no heed to them.

===Chinese===

德巴茨大步经过时，这些地方传出高声喧嚷——被街头演说磨哑了嗓子的人声；一阵阵咒骂，无礼地砸在慷慨激昂的演讲中间；起哄的听众同演讲人对骂，比赛谁的话毒、谁的言渎；一场场舌战，收梢总是响亮的对骂；告发的呼声掷过空中——空气沉甸甸的，全是烟草味、廉价酒和生烈酒的气味。
德巴茨路过全不理会，只担心别叫那帮饭铺政客照老脾气一拥而出到街上，用拳头分量来了结争端。他不愿卷进街头斗殴——那种事十有八九以告发和逮捕收场；不多时他把王宫一带甩在身后，得以左转，奔冷清的圣殿塔郊区去，心里才高兴。
远处昏暗的地方，隔一阵传来一阵闷鼓的哀声，半掩在这座大城热闹夜生活的喧嚣里。声音出自革命广场，一队国民自卫军正在断头台四周守夜。鼓声喑哑、时断时续，提醒法兰西的自由人民：那场复仇革命的看门犬昼夜警醒，不睡不眠，“为断头台赶猎物”——民选的政府今天新颁的法令，正是这样命令它的。
这条冷街的寂静，如今不时被一声突然的恐怖尖叫划破，接着是兵器碰撞、免不了的一顿咒骂、呼救声、最后一声痛苦的哀号。那是一出出反复上演的小悲剧，诉说着告发、抄家、突击逮捕，诉说着对生与自由的垂死渴望——要生，哪怕生在这般残暴奴役的可怖境地里；要自由，哪怕只多喘一两天气，这被污秽和血腥污染的空气。
德巴茨见惯了这些，全不理会。

===Original===

He had heard it so often, that cry in the night, followed by deathlike silence; it came from comfortable bourgeois houses, from squalid lodgings, or lonely cul-de-sac, wherever some hunted quarry was run to earth by the newly-organised spies of the Committee of General Security.
Five and thirty livres for every head that falls trunkless into the basket at the foot of the guillotine! Five and thirty pieces of silver, now as then, the price of innocent blood. Every cry in the night, every call for help, meant game for the guillotine, and five and thirty livres in the hands of a Judas.
And de Batz walked on unmoved by what he saw and heard, swinging his cane and looking satisfied. Now he struck into the Place de la Victoire, and looked on one of the open-air camps that had recently been established where men, women, and children were working to provide arms and accoutrements for the Republican army that was fighting the whole of Europe.
The people of France were up in arms against tyranny; and on the open places of their mighty city they were encamped day and night forging those arms which were destined to make them free, and in the meantime were bending under a yoke of tyranny more complete, more grinding and absolute than any that the most despotic kings had ever dared to inflict.
Here by the light of resin torches, at this late hour of the night, raw lads were being drilled into soldiers, half-naked under the cutting blast of the north wind, their knees shaking under them, their arms and legs blue with cold, their stomachs empty, and their teeth chattering with fear; women were sewing shirts for the great improvised army, with eyes straining to see the stitches by the flickering light of the torches, their throats parched with the continual inhaling of smoke-laden air; even children, with weak, clumsy little fingers, were picking rags to be woven into cloth again﻿—all, all these slaves were working far into the night, tired, hungry, and cold, but working unceasingly, as the country had demanded it: “the people of France in arms against tyranny!” The people of France had to set to work to make arms, to clothe the soldiers, the defenders of the people’s liberty.
And from this crowd of people﻿—men, women, and children﻿—there came scarcely a sound, save raucous whispers, a moan or a sigh quickly suppressed.

===Chinese===

那声夜里的呼喊、随之而来的死寂，他听得太多；它来自体面的布尔乔亚宅子，来自破烂的租屋，或僻静的死巷——凡是治安委员会新编的密探把猎物追到绝处的地方。
每颗身首异处、落进断头台脚下篮子里的头颅，三十五里弗！三十五块银币——从古到今，这是无辜之血的价钱。夜里的每一声呼喊、每一次求救，都意味着断头台的一件猎物，都意味着某个犹大手里的三十五里弗。
德巴茨对所见所闻无动于衷，甩着手杖，一脸满足地走。此刻他拐进胜利广场，望见一处新设的露天营地——男女和孩子们正在那里做工，为同全欧洲作战的共和军赶制军械装具。
法兰西人民武装起来反抗暴政；在他们那座雄城的空场上，他们日夜扎营，锻打那些注定要使他们获得自由的武器；与此同时，他们颈上套着的枷锁，却比最专制的君王从前敢加于人的任何一副都更完备、更磨人、更绝对。
这深夜时分，树脂火把的光下，生手的小伙子正被操练成兵，北风削骨，他们半裸着，膝盖直打晃，胳膊腿冻得发青，肚子空空，牙齿因恐惧咯咯作响；女人们在为这支仓促征集的大军缝衬衫，在火把摇曳的光里眯着眼认针脚，喉咙叫饱含烟浊的空气熏得干哑；连孩子们也用孱弱笨拙的小手撕拣破布，好再织成布——这帮奴隶，男的女的、大的小的，全都劳作到深夜，又累、又饿、又冷，却劳作不辍，正如国家所要求的：“法兰西人民武装反抗暴政！”法兰西人民还必须动手造军械、给士兵做衣裳——他们可是人民自由的捍卫者。
而这男男女女、老老少少的人群里，几乎不闻声息，只有嘶哑的耳语，和一声赶紧压下去的呻吟或叹息。

===Original===

A grim silence reigned in this thickly-peopled camp; only the crackling of the torches broke that silence now and then, or the flapping of canvas in the wintry gale. They worked on sullen, desperate, and starving, with no hope of payment save the miserable rations wrung from poor tradespeople or miserable farmers, as wretched, as oppressed as themselves; no hope of payment, only fear of punishment, for that was ever present.
The people of France in arms against tyranny were not allowed to forget that grim taskmaster with the two great hands stretched upwards, holding the knife which descended mercilessly, indiscriminately on necks that did not bend willingly to the task.
A grim look of gratified desire had spread over de Batz’ face as he skirted the open-air camp. Let them toil, let them groan, let them starve! The more these clouts suffer, the more brutal the heel that grinds them down, the sooner will the Emperor’s money accomplish its work, the sooner will these wretches be clamoring for the monarchy, which would mean a rich reward in de Batz’ pockets.
To him everything now was for the best: the tyranny, the brutality, the massacres. He gloated in the holocausts with as much satisfaction as did the most bloodthirsty Jacobin in the Convention. He would with his own hands have wielded the guillotine that worked too slowly for his ends. Let that end justify the means, was his motto. What matter if the future King of France walked up to his throne over steps made of headless corpses and rendered slippery with the blood of martyrs?
The ground beneath de Batz’ feet was hard and white with the frost. Overhead the pale, wintry moon looked down serene and placid on this giant city wallowing in an ocean of misery.
There, had been but little snow as yet this year, and the cold was intense. On his right now the Cimetière des SS. Innocents lay peaceful and still beneath the wan light of the moon. A thin covering of snow lay evenly alike on grass mounds and smooth stones. Here and there a broken cross with chipped arms still held pathetically outstretched, as if in a final appeal for human love, bore mute testimony to senseless excesses and spiteful desire for destruction.

===Chinese===

这人烟稠密的营地里笼罩着冷森森的沉默；只有火把的噼啪声不时将它打破，或是冬日狂风里帆布的拍打。人们阴沉着、绝望着、挨着饿，干个不停；不指望报酬——除了从穷小贩或苦农户手里挤出来的那点可怜口粮，那些人同他们一样苦、一样受压；不指望报酬，只有对惩罚的恐惧，而惩罚无时不在。
武装反抗暴政的法兰西人民，不许忘记那位冷酷的工头——它两只大手向上平伸，擎着刀，刀落下来不讲情面、不分皂白，专找那些不肯乖乖低头受活的脖颈。
绕着露天营地走时，德巴茨脸上铺开一层阴冷的餍足。让他们苦，让他们哼，让他们饿！这帮贱骨头受得越狠、踩他们的脚跟越凶，皇帝的银子就越快办成它的事，这帮可怜虫就越快嚷着要君主制——那便意味着德巴茨口袋里一笔厚赏。
在他看来，如今样样都再好不过：暴政，凶残，屠杀。他面对大屠杀的垂涎，同公会里最嗜血的雅各宾人一般无二。只要于他的目标有利，他会亲手去扳那架嫌慢的断头机。为达目的，不择手段，这是他的座右铭。法兰西未来的国王，哪怕踩着无头尸首垒成的台阶登基、台阶叫殉道者的血淌得溜滑，又有什么要紧？
德巴茨脚下，地面被霜冻得又硬又白。头顶上，惨淡的冬月安然恬静，俯照着这座在苦海里翻滚的巨城。
今年雪还下得少，天却冷得厉害。此刻他右手边，圣婴墓园在惨淡月光下安卧无声。薄薄一层雪，草坟和光石上铺得一般匀。这儿那儿，断了臂的残十字还可怜巴巴地张着，仿佛最后一声对人间之爱的呼吁——默默见证着那无谓的暴行和存心的毁弃。

===Original===

But here within the precincts of the dwelling of the eternal Master a solemn silence reigned; only the cold north wind shook the branches of the yew, causing them to send forth a melancholy sigh into the night, and to shed a shower of tiny crystals of snow like the frozen tears of the dead.
And round the precincts of the lonely graveyard, and down narrow streets or open places, the night watchmen went their rounds, lantern in hand, and every five minutes their monotonous call rang clearly out in the night:
“Sleep, citizens! everything is quiet and at peace!”
---
We may take it that de Batz did not philosophise overmuch on what went on around him. He had walked swiftly up the Rue St. Martin, then turning sharply to his right he found himself beneath the tall, frowning walls of the Temple prison, the grim guardian of so many secrets, such terrible despair, such unspeakable tragedies.
Here, too, as in the Place de la Révolution, an intermittent roll of muffled drums proclaimed the ever-watchful presence of the National Guard. But with that exception not a sound stirred round the grim and stately edifice; there were no cries, no calls, no appeals around its walls. All the crying and wailing was shut in by the massive stone that told no tales.
Dim and flickering lights shone behind several of the small windows in the façade of the huge labyrinthine building. Without any hesitation de Batz turned down the Rue du Temple, and soon found himself in front of the main gates which gave on the courtyard beyond. The sentinel challenged him, but he had the password, and explained that he desired to have speech with citizen Héron.
With a surly gesture the guard pointed to the heavy bellpull up against the gate, and de Batz pulled it with all his might. The long clang of the brazen bell echoed and reechoed round the solid stone walls. Anon a tiny judas in the gate was cautiously pushed open, and a peremptory voice once again challenged the midnight intruder.
De Batz, more peremptorily this time, asked for citizen Héron, with whom he had immediate and important business, and a glimmer of a piece of silver which he held up close to the judas secured him the necessary admittance.
The massive gates slowly swung open on their creaking hinges, and as de Batz passed beneath the archway they closed again behind him.
The concierge’s lodge was immediately on his left.

===Chinese===

唯有在这永恒之主的居所墙内，一片庄严的静穆；只有寒冷的北风摇着紫杉的枝，叫它向夜发出一声忧郁的叹息，抖落一阵细雪的冰晶，像死者冻凝的泪。
而在这僻静墓园的四周，沿窄街和空场，更夫们提着灯笼巡夜，每隔五分钟，他们单调的呼喊便清亮地响彻夜空：
“睡吧，公民们！四处安静，天下太平！”
---
可以想见，德巴茨对周遭种种并不作过多的哲思。他快步走上圣马丁街，随后猛地右转，便到了圣殿塔监狱高耸阴沉的墙下——那么多秘密、那么多可怕的绝望、那么多难以言传的悲剧，都由它冷面看守着。
这里同革命广场一样，闷鼓时断时续，宣示着国民自卫军永不松懈的驻守。但除此之外，这座森严堂皇的建筑四周不闻一点声息；墙外没有哭喊，没有呼号，没有哀告。一切啼哭呜咽，都关在厚重无言的石头里面。
这座迷宫般大建筑的正面，好几扇小窗后透出昏暗摇曳的灯光。德巴茨毫不迟疑拐上圣殿塔街，很快来到正门前——门内便是院子。哨兵喝问口令，他对上了，说明要见埃龙公民。
卫兵没好气地一指门边那粗重的门铃拉索，德巴茨用力猛拉。铜钟长鸣，在坚实的石墙间荡了又荡。不多时，门上一扇小小的监视孔被小心翼翼推开，一个专横的嗓音再次喝问这位午夜不速之客。
德巴茨这回更加专横地求见埃龙公民，说他有紧急要事；他把一块银币凑到监视孔前一晃，必要的门路便打通了。
沉重的大门在吱嘎的铰链上缓缓荡开；德巴茨从拱门下走过，门又在他身后合拢。
门房的屋子就在他左手边。

===Original===

Again he was challenged, and again gave the password. But his face was apparently known here, for no serious hindrance to proceed was put in his way.
A man, whose wide, lean frame was but ill-covered by a threadbare coat and ragged breeches, and with soleless shoes on his feet, was told off to direct the citoyen to citizen Héron’s rooms. The man walked slowly along with bent knees and arched spine, and shuffled his feet as he walked; the bunch of keys which he carried rattled ominously in his long, grimy hands; the passages were badly lighted, and he also carried a lantern to guide himself on the way.
Closely followed by de Batz, he soon turned into the central corridor, which is open to the sky above, and was spectrally alight now with flagstones and walls gleaming beneath the silvery sheen of the moon, and throwing back the fantastic elongated shadows of the two men as they walked.
On the left, heavily barred windows gave on the corridor, as did here and there the massive oaken doors, with their gigantic hinges and bolts, on the steps of which squatted groups of soldiers wrapped in their cloaks, with wild, suspicious eyes beneath their capotes, peering at the midnight visitor as he passed.
There was no thought of silence here. The very walls seemed alive with sounds, groans and tears, loud wails and murmured prayers; they exuded from the stones and trembled on the frost-laden air.
Occasionally at one of the windows a pair of white hands would appear, grasping the heavy iron bar, trying to shake it in its socket, and mayhap, above the hands, the dim vision of a haggard face, a man’s or a woman’s, trying to get a glimpse of the outside world, a final look at the sky, before the last journey to the place of death tomorrow. Then one of the soldiers, with a loud, angry oath, would struggle to his feet, and with the butt-end of his gun strike at the thin, wan fingers till their hold on the iron bar relaxed, and the pallid face beyond would sink back into the darkness with a desperate cry of pain.
A quick, impatient sigh escaped de Batz’ lips.

===Chinese===

又被喝问，他又对上口令。不过他这张脸这里显然认得，没人认真拦他。
一个男人被派来带这位公民去埃龙公民的住处；他那瘦长骨架上，磨秃的外衣和破裤子遮掩不严，脚上的鞋没有底。他膝弯背弓，一路蹭着脚慢慢走；掌管的一大串钥匙在他乌黑的长手里不祥地哗啦作响；过道里灯光稀落，他还提着一盏灯笼引路。
德巴茨紧跟在后，不一会儿便拐进中央走廊——那里头顶露天，此刻月光银辉洒在石板和墙上，泛着幽幽的光，把两个人行走的影子拉得奇形怪状。
左侧，装着粗铁栏的窗户开向走廊，零星还有厚重的橡木门，配着巨大的铰链和门闩；门前台阶上蹲着一堆堆裹着斗篷的士兵，军帽底下射出凶野多疑的眼睛，盯着这位午夜来客走过。
这里谈不上什么安静。连墙壁都仿佛活着，满是声响：呻吟与哭泣，嚎啕与低祷；它们从石头里渗出来，在凝霜的空气里颤动。
偶尔某扇窗上会现出一双苍白的手，攥住粗铁栏，想把它从槽里摇松；手上头，也许隐约现出一张憔悴的脸，男人的或女人的，想再看一眼外面的世界，最后望一眼天空——明天就要上刑场走最后一程了。这时便有个士兵怒骂一声，挣扎起身，抡起枪托砸那细弱苍白的指头，直砸得它们松开铁栏，栏后那张惨白的脸带着一声惨痛的呼号，重新沉入黑暗。
德巴茨唇间逸出一声急促的不耐烦的叹息。

===Original===

He had skirted the wide courtyard in the wake of his guide, and from where he was he could see the great central tower, with its tiny windows lighted from within, the grim walls behind which the descendant of the world’s conquerors, the bearer of the proudest name in Europe, and wearer of its most ancient crown, had spent the last days of his brilliant life in abject shame, sorrow, and degradation. The memory had swiftly surged up before him of that night when he all but rescued King Louis and his family from this same miserable prison: the guard had been bribed, the keeper corrupted, everything had been prepared, save the reckoning with the one irresponsible factor﻿—chance!
He had failed then and had tried again, and again had failed; a fortune had been his reward if he had succeeded. He had failed, but even now, when his footsteps echoed along the flagged courtyard, over which an unfortunate King and Queen had walked on their way to their last ignominious Calvary, he hugged himself with the satisfying thought that where he had failed at least no one else had succeeded.
Whether that meddlesome English adventurer, who called himself the Scarlet Pimpernel, had planned the rescue of King Louis or of Queen Marie Antoinette at any time or not﻿—that he did not know; but on one point at least he was more than ever determined, and that was that no power on earth should snatch from him the golden prize offered by Austria for the rescue of the little Dauphin.
“I would sooner see the child perish, if I cannot save him myself,” was the burning thought in this man’s tortuous brain. “And let that accursed Englishman look to himself and to his d﻿—﻿—﻿d confederates,” he added, muttering a fierce oath beneath his breath.
A winding, narrow stone stair, another length or two of corridor, and his guide’s shuffling footsteps paused beside a low iron-studded door let into the solid stone. De Batz dismissed his ill-clothed guide and pulled the iron bell-handle which hung beside the door.
The bell gave forth a dull and broken clang, which seemed like an echo of the wails of sorrow that peopled the huge building with their weird and monotonous sounds.

===Chinese===

他随着引路人绕过大院，从站处望得见中央大塔，小窗透着灯光——就在那阴森的墙后，世界征服者的后裔、欧洲最骄傲姓氏的持有者、最古老王冠的佩戴者，度过了他辉煌一生的最后日子：耻辱、悲伤、贬损，无以复加。
一段记忆蓦地涌上心头：正是这座凄惨的监狱，那一夜他险些把路易国王一家救了出去：卫兵买通了，狱吏喂饱了，万事齐备，只差同那个不负责任的变数——运气！——算账。
他那时败了，又试，又败；若成了，一笔巨财便到手了。他败了，可即便此刻，当他的脚步声回响在石板大院里——不幸的国王和王后曾踏过这院子，走向他们受辱的各各他——他仍暗自庆幸，想得心满意足：他没办成的地方，好歹也没有旁人办成。
那个自称红花侠的爱管闲事的英国冒险家，从前可曾图谋营救路易国王或玛丽·安托瓦内特王后——这他不知道；但至少有一点，他比任何时候都拿定主意：普天之下，任何力量都休想从他手里夺走奥地利为营救小王太子悬下的金赏。
“我自己救不成，宁可看着那孩子死掉。”这念头在这人九曲的脑回里烧着。“让那该咒的英国人自己当心，也当心他那些该死的同党。”他压低嗓音，恶狠狠咒骂着补了一句。
一道盘旋的窄石梯，再走过一两截走廊，引路人蹭蹭的脚步在一扇矮门前停住——门嵌在整块石头里，包着铁钉。德巴茨打发走那衣衫褴褛的向导，拉了门边垂着的铁铃柄。
铃发出喑哑破碎的一声，像是那些哀哭的回声——正是它们，用古怪而单调的声响住满了这座大屋。

===Original===

De Batz﻿—a thoroughly unimaginative person﻿—waited patiently beside the door until it was opened from within, and he was confronted by a tall stooping figure, wearing a greasy coat of snuff-brown cloth, and holding high above his head a lantern that threw its feeble light on de Batz’ jovial face and form.
“It is even I, citizen Héron,” he said, breaking in swiftly on the other’s ejaculation of astonishment, which threatened to send his name echoing the whole length of corridors and passages, until round every corner of the labyrinthine house of sorrow the murmur would be borne on the wings of the cold night breeze: “Citizen Héron is in parley with ci-devant Baron de Batz!”
A fact which would have been equally unpleasant for both these worthies.
“Enter!” said Héron curtly.
He banged the heavy door to behind his visitor; and de Batz, who seemed to know his way about the place, walked straight across the narrow landing to where a smaller door stood invitingly open.
He stepped boldly in, the while citizen Héron put the lantern down on the floor of the couloir, and then followed his nocturnal visitor into the room.

===Chinese===

德巴茨——一个毫无想象力的人——耐心地在门边候着，直到门从里面开了；迎着他的是个高个子伛偻的身影，穿一件鼻烟褐的油腻外衣，把灯笼高高举过头顶，昏弱的光照在德巴茨那张快活的脸和身上。
“正是在下，埃龙公民。”他抢道，截断了对方那声惊呼——那声惊呼险些把他的名字顺着走廊过道一路传响出去，让这迷宫般的愁苦之家的每个角落，都借着寒夜的翅膀飘着一句嘀咕：“埃龙公民在会同前贵族德巴茨男爵密谈！”
这场面，对这两位贵人同样不妙。
“进来！”埃龙简短地说。
他把沉重的门砰地在来客身后关上；德巴茨看来熟门熟路，径直穿过窄小的楼梯口，走向一扇敞着的小门。
他大步跨进去；埃龙公民把灯笼搁在过道地板上，随后也跟着这位夜客进了屋。

## VI The Committee’s Agent / VI 委员会的密探
===Original===

It was a narrow, ill-ventilated place, with but one barred window that gave on the courtyard. An evil-smelling lamp hung by a chain from the grimy ceiling, and in a corner of the room a tiny iron stove shed more unpleasant vapour than warm glow around.
There was but little furniture: two or three chairs, a table which was littered with papers, and a corner-cupboard﻿—the open doors of which revealed a miscellaneous collection﻿—bundles of papers, a tin saucepan, a piece of cold sausage, and a couple of pistols. The fumes of stale tobacco-smoke hovered in the air, and mingled most unpleasantly with those of the lamp above, and of the mildew that penetrated through the walls just below the roof.
Héron pointed to one of the chairs, and then sat down on the other, close to the table, on which he rested his elbow. He picked up a short-stemmed pipe, which he had evidently laid aside at the sound of the bell, and having taken several deliberate long-drawn puffs from it, he said abruptly:
“Well, what is it now?”
In the meanwhile de Batz had made himself as much at home in this uncomfortable room as he possibly could. He had deposited his hat and cloak on one rickety rush-bottomed chair, and drawn another close to the fire. He sat down with one leg crossed over the other, his podgy beringed hand wandering with loving gentleness down the length of his shapely calf.
He was nothing if not complacent, and his complacency seemed highly to irritate his friend Héron.
“Well, what is it?” reiterated the latter, drawing his visitor’s attention roughly to himself by banging his fist on the table. “Out with it! What do you want? Why have you come at this hour of the night﻿—to compromise me, I suppose﻿—bring your own d﻿—﻿—﻿d neck and mine into the same noose﻿—what?”
“Easy, easy, my friend,” responded de Batz imperturbably; “waste not so much time in idle talk. Why do I usually come to see you? Surely you have had no cause to complain hitherto of the unprofitableness of my visits to you?”
“They will have to be still more profitable to me in the future,” growled the other across the table. “I have more power now.”
“I know you have,” said de Batz suavely. “The new decree? What? You may denounce whom you please, search whom you please, arrest whom you please, and send whom you please to the Supreme Tribunal without giving them the slightest chance of escape.”

===Chinese===

这是一间狭窄、不通风的屋子，只有一扇开向院子的铁栅窗。一盏气味难闻的油灯用链子挂在油腻腻的天花板上；屋角一只小小的铁炉，散出来的浊气比暖气还多。
家具寥寥：两三把椅子，一张堆满纸的桌子，还有一角柜——柜门敞着，露出一堆杂七杂八的收藏：一捆捆文书、一只白铁小锅、一段冷香肠、两支手枪。隔夜的烟味悬浮在空气里，同头顶的油灯味、同屋顶下从墙缝里渗出来的霉味，攦在一处，说不出的难闻。
埃龙指了指其中一把椅子，自己坐了另一把，紧挨着桌子，一支胳膊肘支在桌上。他拾起一杆短柄烟斗——听铃响时显然刚搁下的——不紧不慢、长长地吸了几口，才没头没脑地说：
“怎么，又有什么事？”
这当口，德巴茨已在这间不舒适的屋子里，把“宾至如归”四个字做到了力所能及的地步。他把帽子和外套摌在一把摇晃的灯心草垫椅上，又拖了一把到炉边。他趐起二郎腿坐下，一只戴戒指的胖手带着爱怜的温柔，顺着他自己那线条漂亮的小腿肚一路抚下去。
他这人一无所长，唯独那份自得天下第一；而这份自得，似乎大大地刺激着他的朋友埃龙。
“喂，什么事？”埃龙又问了一遍，一拳砸在桌上，粗鲁地把访客的注意力拽回自己身上。“有话快讲！你要什么？半夜三更跑来做什么——想来拖累我，是不是——把你那该死的脖颈子同我这条拴进同一个绞套——啊？”
“别急，别急，我的朋友，”德巴茨不为所动地答道，“闲话少说，别浪费功夫。我平素为什么来见你？我的拜访给你带来的进项，你总还不至于有什么可抱怨的吧？”
“往后给我的好处，还得更大些。”隔着桌子，那一位低吼道，“我如今的权势更大了。”
“这我知道，”德巴茨温言软语地说，“新颁的法令嘛，是不是？你可以随意告发谁、随意搜查谁、随意逮捕谁，随意把谁送到最高法庭去，不容他有半点逃命的机会。”

===Original===

“Is it in order to tell me all this that you have come to see me at this hour of the night?” queried Héron with a sneer.
“No; I came at this hour of the night because I surmised that in the future you and your hellhounds would be so busy all day ‘beating up game for the guillotine’ that the only time you would have at the disposal of your friends would be the late hours of the night. I saw you at the theatre a couple of hours ago, friend Héron; I didn’t think to find you yet abed.”
“Well, what do you want?”
“Rather,” retorted de Batz blandly, “shall we say, what do *you* want, citizen Héron?”
“For what?
“For my continued immunity at the hands of yourself and your pack?”
Héron pushed his chair brusquely aside and strode across the narrow room deliberately facing the portly figure of de Batz, who with head slightly inclined on one side, his small eyes narrowed till they appeared mere slits in his pockmarked face, was steadily and quite placidly contemplating this inhuman monster who had this very day been given uncontrolled power over hundreds of thousands of human lives.
Héron was one of those tall men who look mean in spite of their height. His head was small and narrow, and his hair, which was sparse and lank, fell in untidy strands across his forehead. He stooped slightly from the neck, and his chest, though wide, was hollow between the shoulders. But his legs were big and bony, slightly bent at the knees, like those of an ill-conditioned horse.
The face was thin and the cheeks sunken; the eyes, very large and prominent, had a look in them of cold and ferocious cruelty, a look which contrasted strangely with the weakness and petty greed apparent in the mouth, which was flabby, with full, very red lips, and chin that sloped away to the long thin neck.
Even at this moment as he gazed on de Batz the greed and the cruelty in him were fighting one of those battles the issue of which is always uncertain in men of his stamp.
“I don’t know,” he said slowly, “that I am prepared to treat with you any longer. You are an intolerable bit of vermin that has annoyed the Committee of General Security for over two years now. It would be excessively pleasant to crush you once and for all, as one would a buzzing fly.”

===Chinese===

“你半夜三更来见我，就为了跟我说这些？”埃龙冷笑。
“不是；我挑这个时辰来，是因为我料想，往后你和你的那群地狱猎犬，白天要整日忙着‘给断头台赶猎物’，肯留给朋友们支配的，只有深夜这几个钟头。两个钟头前我在剧院瞧见你了，埃龙朋友；我倒没指望你已经上床。”
“到底要什么？”
“倒不如这样说，”德巴茨和颜悦色地回敬，“埃龙公民，*你*要什么？”
“要什么？”
“为了让我在你和你那一伙手里，继续太平无事？”
埃龙猛地推开椅子，大步走到狭窄的屋子那头，故意正对着德巴茨肥硕的身躯站定；德巴茨微微侧着头，两只小眼睛眯成麻脸上一道细缝，平心静气、目不转睛地打量着这个吃人的怪物——就在今天，这个怪物刚领到了对成千上万条人命不受约束的生杀大权。
埃龙属于那一类个子高却透着猥琐的人。头颅又小又窄，头发稀疏油塌，一缩一缩乱披在额前。脖子微微前悦；胸膛虽宽，肩胛之间却陷下去。唯独两条腿又粗又大、骨节嶙峋，膝盖处微弯，像一匹劣马的腿。
脸瘦，两颊塌陷；眼睛又大又凸，里头含着一种阴冷的、凶残的狠劲——同嘴上的懦弱与屑小贪欲恰成古怪对照：那张嘴松软，唇厚而极红，下巴一路斜削下去，连着细长的脖颈。
就在此刻，他盯着德巴茨，体内那贪婪与凶残正打着一场内战——这一号人打这种仗，胜负从来难说。
“我不知道，”他慢吞吞地说，“我以后还愿不愿同你打交道。你是只叫人忍无可忍的虫豸，攦扰治安委员会足足两年多了。把你一巴掌拍死、永绝后患——像拍死一只嗡嗡叫的苍蝇——那才叫痛快呢。”

===Original===

“Pleasant, perhaps, but immeasurably foolish,” rejoined de Batz coolly; “you would only get thirty-five livres for my head, and I offer you ten times that amount for the selfsame commodity.”
“I know, I know; but the whole thing has become too dangerous.”
“Why? I am very modest. I don’t ask a great deal. Let your hounds keep off my scent.”
“You have too many d﻿—﻿—﻿d confederates.”
“Oh! Never mind about the others. I am not bargaining about them. Let them look after themselves.”
“Every time we get a batch of them, one or the other denounces you.”
“Under torture, I know,” rejoined de Batz placidly, holding his podgy hands to the warm glow of the fire. “For you have started torture in your house of Justice now, eh, friend Héron? You and your friend the Public Prosecutor have gone the whole gamut of devilry﻿—eh?”
“What’s that to you?” retorted the other gruffly.
“Oh, nothing, nothing! I was even proposing to pay you three thousand five hundred livres for the privilege of taking no further interest in what goes on inside this prison!”
“Three thousand five hundred!” ejaculated Héron involuntarily, and this time even his eyes lost their cruelty; they joined issue with the mouth in an expression of hungering avarice.
“Two little zeros added to the thirty-five, which is all you would get for handing me over to your accursed Tribunal,” said de Batz, and, as if thoughtlessly, his hand wandered to the inner pocket of his coat, and a slight rustle as of thin crisp paper brought drops of moisture to the lips of Héron.
“Leave me alone for three weeks and the money is yours,” concluded de Batz pleasantly.
There was silence in the room now. Through the narrow barred window the steely rays of the moon fought with the dim yellow light of the oil lamp, and lit up the pale face of the Committee’s agent with its lines of cruelty in sharp conflict with those of greed.
“Well! is it a bargain?” asked de Batz at last in his usual smooth, oily voice, as he half drew from out his pocket that tempting little bundle of crisp printed paper. “You have only to give me the usual receipt for the money and it is yours.”
Héron gave a vicious snarl.
“It is dangerous, I tell you. That receipt, if it falls into some cursed meddler’s hands, would send me straight to the guillotine.”

===Chinese===

“痛快许是痛快，只是蠢得没边儿，”德巴茨冷冷地接口，“我这颗脑袋你拿去，只值三十五里弗；同一样货色，我出你十倍的价。”
“我知道，我知道；可这桩买卖越来越悬了。”
“为什么？我很知足。要的不多：让你的猎犬别嗅我的踪迹，就是了。”
“你那帮该死的同党太多了。”
“哦！旁人不用你操心。我不是替他们讨价。他们自求多福。”
“我们每抓一批，总有一个两个把你供出来。”
“刑讯底下招的，我知道。”德巴茨不动声色地答，胖乎乎的双手伸向炉火的暖光。“你们那座正义之府如今已经开用刑讯了，是不是，埃龙朋友？你同你那位公诉人朋友，十八般魔鬼的功课都做全了——嗯？”
“关你什么事？”对方粗声粗气地回。
“哦，不关事，不关事！我甚至还打算付你三千五百里弗，买一个特权：从此对这座监狱里的事不再关心！”
“三千五百！”埃龙脱口而出；这一回，连他那双眼睛里的凶光都敛了，同那张嘴一起，换上了一副饥渴的贪相。
“三十五后头添两个小小的零——你把我交到那座该死的法庭手上，统共也只值那三十五。”德巴茨说。他的手像是无意识似的，摸向上衣内袋；一阵薄脆纸片的轻微窸窣，立时叫埃龙的嘴唇上沁出了潮气。
“三个星期别来碰我，钱就是你的。”德巴茨愉快地拍板。
屋里此刻静了。窄窄的栅窗外，青白的月光同昏黄的灯光交战，照亮了这位委员会密探惨白的脸：脸上凶残的纹路，同贪婪的纹路，斗得针锋相对。
“怎么样！成交么？”德巴茨终于又开口，用的还是他那惯常的圆滑油亮的嗓音，一面把那诱人的、薄脆印刷纸的小纸卷从口袋里抽出半截。“你只消照例给我开张收款凭条，钱就是你的。”
埃龙恶狠狠地嚎了一声。
“我跟你说，这事凶险。那张凭条，落到哪个该死的碎嘴子手里，我立刻就得上断头台。”

===Original===

“The receipt could only fall into alien hands,” rejoined de Batz blandly, “if I happened to be arrested, and even in that case they could but fall into those of the chief agent of the Committee of General Security, and he hath name Héron. You must take some risks, my friend. I take them too. We are each in the other’s hands. The bargain is quite fair.”
For a moment or two longer Héron appeared to be hesitating whilst de Batz watched him with keen intentness. He had no doubt himself as to the issue. He had tried most of these patriots in his own golden crucible, and had weighed their patriotism against Austrian money, and had never found the latter wanting.
He had not been here tonight if he were not quite sure. This inveterate conspirator in the Royalist cause never took personal risks. He looked on Héron now, smiling to himself the while with perfect satisfaction.
“Very well,” said the Committee’s agent with sudden decision, “I’ll take the money. But on one condition.”
“What is it?”
“That you leave little Capet alone.”
“The Dauphin!”
“Call him what you like,” said Héron, taking a step nearer to de Batz, and from his great height glowering down in fierce hatred and rage upon his accomplice; “call the young devil what you like, but leave us to deal with him.”
“To kill him, you mean? Well, how can I prevent it, my friend?”
“You and your like are always plotting to get him out of here. I won’t have it. I tell you I won’t have it. If the brat disappears I am a dead man. Robespierre and his gang have told me as much. So you leave him alone, or I’ll not raise a finger to help you, but will lay my own hands on your accursed neck.”
He looked so ferocious and so merciless then, that despite himself, the selfish adventurer, the careless self-seeking intriguer, shuddered with a quick wave of unreasoning terror. He turned away from Héron’s piercing gaze, the gaze of a hyena whose prey is being snatched from beneath its nails. For a moment he stared thoughtfully into the fire.
He heard the other man’s heavy footsteps cross and re-cross the narrow room, and was conscious of the long curved shadow creeping up the mildewed wall or retreating down upon the carpetless floor.
Suddenly, without any warning he felt a grip upon his shoulder. He gave a start and almost uttered a cry of alarm which caused Héron to laugh.

===Chinese===

“那凭条要落到外人手里，”德巴茨和颜悦色地答，“除非我恰好被捕；果真如此，它也落不到旁人手里，只能落在治安委员会总密探的手里——那位总密探，大名唤作埃龙。风险么，我的朋友，你总得担上几分。我也担着。咱们俩，谁都在对方掌心里。这买卖再公道不过。”
埃龙又迟疑了一两分钟，德巴茨目光锐利、全神贯注地盯着他。至于结局如何，他自己毫不用担心。这班“爱国志士”，大半他都放进自己的黄金坩埚里炼过，拿他们的爱国心同奥地利的金子称过分量——后者还从来没有短过秤。
若非十拿九稳，他今夜就不会在此地了。这个死心塌地为保王党事业效力的阴谋家，从不亲身犯险。此刻他望着埃龙，心里暗暗发笑，说不出的满意。
“好吧，”委员会的密探突然拿定主意，“钱我收。但有一个条件。”
“什么条件？”
“小卡佩那头，你少碰。”
“王太子！”
“随你怎么叫，”埃龙说着，朝德巴茨逼近一步，仗着大高个，恶狠狠、怒冲冲地俯视着他的同伙，“那小魔崽随你怎么叫，但他，留给我们处置。”
“你的意思是，弄死他？哎，那我拦得住么，我的朋友？”
“你同你那一路货，成天密谋把他从这里弄出去。我不答应。我跟你说，我不答应。那小崽子若不见了，我就是个死人。罗伯斯庇尔和他那帮人，明明白白把话说到这个份上了。所以你少碰他；不然，休想我抬一根指头帮你，我还要亲手攥住你那该死的脖子。”
他那时的神情凶狠绝情到这个地步，以致这个自私的冒险家、这个漫不经心、专为一己谋算的阴谋客，也不由自主打了个寒噤，一阵无端的恐怖掠过全身。他避开埃龙刺人的目光——那是鬣狗眼看爪下的猎物被人夺走时的目光——对着炉火，出神地望了片刻。
他听见那人沉重的脚步在窄屋里走来走去；那道长长的弯曲的影子，时而爬上发霉的墙壁，时而退落到没有地毯的地板上，他全看在眼里。
突然，毫无征兆，他觉出肩上一只手攮住了他。他一惊，险些喊出声来，倒引得埃龙大笑。

===Original===

The Committee’s agent was vastly amused at his friend’s obvious access of fear. There was nothing that he liked better than that he should inspire dread in the hearts of all those with whom he came in contact.
“I am just going on my usual nocturnal round,” he said abruptly. “Come with me, citizen de Batz.”
A certain grim humour was apparent in his face as he proffered this invitation, which sounded like a rough command. As de Batz seemed to hesitate he nodded peremptorily to him to follow. Already he had gone into the hall and picked up his lantern. From beneath his waistcoat he drew forth a bunch of keys, which he rattled impatiently, calling to his friend to come.
“Come, citizen,” he said roughly. “I wish to show you the one treasure in this house which your d﻿—﻿—﻿d fingers must not touch.”
Mechanically de Batz rose at last. He tried to be master of the terror which was invading his very bones. He would not own to himself even that he was afraid, and almost audibly he kept murmuring to himself that he had no cause for fear.
Héron would never touch him. The spy’s avarice, his greed of money were a perfect safeguard for any man who had the control of millions, and Héron knew, of course, that he could make of this inveterate plotter a comfortable source of revenue for himself. Three weeks would soon be over, and fresh bargains could be made time and again, while de Batz was alive and free.
Héron was still waiting at the door, even whilst de Batz wondered what this nocturnal visitation would reveal to him of atrocity and of outrage. He made a final effort to master his nervousness, wrapped his cloak tightly around him, and followed his host out of the room.

===Chinese===

朋友这阵明明白白的胆寒，叫委员会的密探大大地开心。天底下他顶喜欢的，就是让一切同他打交道的人心头发怕。
“我正要照例夜巡一趟。”他突兀地说，“跟我来，德巴茨公民。”
他发出这份邀请——听来倒像一道粗声粗气的命令——脸上带着一种狰狞的谐趣。德巴茨似有迟疑，他不容分说地点头命他跟上。他已经走进门厅，提起风灯；又从马甲底下摸出一串钥匙，不耐烦地摇得哗啦响，嘴里催朋友快来。
“来呀，公民，”他粗声说，“我要给你看一样这宅子里独一无二的宝贝——你那双该死的手碰不得的宝贝。”
德巴茨终于机械地站起身。他竭力要压住那阵侵入骨髓的恐怖。他连“怕”字都不肯认，几近出声地对自己喃喃：没什么可怕的。
埃龙绝不会动他。这密探的贪财爱钱，对任何一个手握百万的人，是再稳妥不过的保身符；埃龙当然明白，这个屡教不改的阴谋家，大可以做成他自己一头舒舒服服的财源。三个星期转眼就过；只要德巴茨活着、自在，新买卖一茬接一茬，尽做得成。
埃龙还在门边等着；这时的德巴茨，心里正打着鼓，不知这趟夜巡会给自己揭开何等的凶残、何等的暴行。他使出最后的气力镇住心神，把外套裹紧，跟着主人走出了屋子。

## VII The Most Precious Life in Europe / 欧洲最珍贵的性命
===Original===

Once more he was being led through the interminable corridors of the gigantic building. Once more from the narrow, barred windows close by him he heard the heartbreaking sighs, the moans, the curses which spoke of tragedies that he could only guess.
Héron was walking on ahead of him, preceding him by some fifty mètres or so, his long legs covering the distances more rapidly than de Batz could follow them. The latter knew his way well about the old prison. Few men in Paris possessed that accurate knowledge of its intricate passages and its network of cells and halls which de Batz had acquired after close and persevering study.
He himself could have led Héron to the doors of the tower where the little Dauphin was being kept imprisoned, but unfortunately he did not possess the keys that would open all the doors which led to it. There were sentinels at every gate, groups of soldiers at each end of every corridor, the great﻿—now empty﻿—courtyards, thronged with prisoners in the daytime, were alive with soldiery even now. Some walked up and down with fixed bayonet on shoulder, others sat in groups on the stone copings or squatted on the ground, smoking or playing cards, but all of them were alert and watchful.
Héron was recognised everywhere the moment he appeared, and though in these days of equality no one presented arms, nevertheless every guard stood aside to let him pass, or when necessary opened a gate for the powerful chief agent of the Committee of General Security.
Indeed, de Batz had no keys such as these to open the way for him to the presence of the martyred little King.
Thus the two men wended their way on in silence, one preceding the other. De Batz walked leisurely, thoughtfully, taking stock of everything he saw﻿—the gates, the barriers, the positions of sentinels and warders, of everything in fact that might prove a help or a hindrance presently, when the great enterprise would be hazarded. At last﻿—still in the wake of Héron﻿—he found himself once more behind the main entrance gate, underneath the archway on which gave the guichet of the concierge.
Here, too, there seemed to be an unnecessary number of soldiers: two were doing sentinel outside the guichet, but there were others in a file against the wall.

===Chinese===

他再一次被人领着穿过这座巨厦没完没了的走廊。又一次，从近旁窄窄的铁栏窗里，他听见令人心碎的叹息、呻吟和咒骂——诉说着他只能猜测的悲剧。
埃龙走在前头，领先他约莫五十米；那双长腿跨起路来，快得德巴茨赶不上。后者对这座老监狱的路径烂熟于心。巴黎没几个人能像德巴茨这样，把那些曲折的过道和牢房大厅的连环布局摸得那样准——那是他细心而锲而不舍钻研出来的。
他自己本可以把埃龙领到囚着小王太子的那座塔的门前，可惜他没有钥匙，开不了通往那里的一道道门。每道门都有哨兵，每条走廊两头都是成群的士兵；那些白天挤满囚犯的大院——此刻空着——也到处是兵。有的肩扛上了刺刀的枪来回走，有的成堆坐在石栏上，或蹲在地上抽烟打牌，但个个警觉，个个留神。
埃龙一露面，处处立刻被认出；虽说在这人人平等的年月没人举枪敬礼，可每个卫兵都侧身让路，必要时还为治安委员会这位权势赫赫的首席密探开栅门。
诚然，德巴茨没有这等钥匙，替自己打开通到那位受难小国王面前的路。
两人便一前后后默默前行。德巴茨走得从容，边走边想，把见到的一切都记在心里——门、栅栏、哨兵和看守的位置，凡此种种，将来那桩大业要冒险行事时，都可能成为助力或阻碍。终于——仍跟在埃龙身后——他又回到了正门后头，站在拱道下，门房的小窗就开在这拱道上。
这里士兵也多得没有必要：两名在小窗外站哨，另有一列靠墙排着。

===Original===

Héron rapped with his keys against the door of the concierge’s lodge, then, as it was not immediately opened from within, he pushed it open with his foot.
“The concierge?” he queried peremptorily.
From a corner of the small panelled room there came a grunt and a reply:
“Gone to bed, *quoi*!”
The man who previously had guided de Batz to Héron’s door slowly struggled to his feet. He had been squatting somewhere in the gloom, and had been roused by Héron’s rough command. He slouched forward now still carrying a boot in one hand and a blacking brush in the other.
“Take this lantern, then,” said the chief agent with a snarl directed at the sleeping concierge, “and come along. Why are you still here?” he added, as if in afterthought.
“The citizen concierge was not satisfied with the way I had done his boots,” muttered the man, with an evil leer as he spat contemptuously on the floor; “an aristo, quoi? A hell of a place this﻿ ﻿… twenty cells to sweep out every day﻿ ﻿… and boots to clean for every aristo of a concierge or warder who demands it.﻿ ﻿… Is that work for a free born patriot, I ask?”
“Well, if you are not satisfied, citoyen Dupont,” retorted Héron dryly, “you may go when you like, you know there are plenty of others ready to do your work﻿ ﻿…”
“Nineteen hours a day, and nineteen sous by way of payment.﻿ ﻿… I have had fourteen days of this convict work﻿ ﻿…”
He continued to mutter under his breath, whilst Héron, paying no further heed to him, turned abruptly towards a group of soldiers stationed outside.
“En avant, corporal!” he said; “bring four men with you﻿ ﻿… we go up to the tower.”
The small procession was formed. On ahead the lantern-bearer, with arched spine and shaking knees, dragging shuffling footsteps along the corridor, then the corporal with two of his soldiers, then Héron closely followed by de Batz, and finally two more soldiers bringing up the rear.
Héron had given the bunch of keys to the man Dupont. The latter, on ahead, holding the lantern aloft, opened one gate after another. At each gate he waited for the little procession to file through, then he re-locked the gate and passed on.
Up two or three flights of winding stairs set in the solid stone, and the final heavy door was reached.
De Batz was meditating. Héron’s precautions for the safeguarding of the most precious life in Europe were more complete than he had anticipated.

===Chinese===

埃龙拿钥匙串敲了敲门房的门；里面没有立时来开，他便用脚推开了。
“门房呢？”他专横地问。
小小的板壁房间一角，哼了一声，应道：
“睡下了呗！”
先前给德巴茨引路的那人，慢慢腾腾地挣扎起身。他方才蹲在暗处，被埃龙粗声一喝弄醒了。此刻他懒洋洋上前，一手还拎着一只靴子，一手攥着黑鞋油刷子。
“那就提上这盏灯，”首席密探冲着睡着的门房一吼，“跟我走。你怎么还在这儿？”他又问，像是忽然想起来。
“门房公民嫌我给他刷的靴子不称心，”那人咕哝，斜眼一瞥，朝地上啐了口唾沫；“贵族做派，是不是？这鬼地方……一天要扫二十间牢房……哪个门房看守是贵族大爷，要刷靴子就得伺候……我倒要问问，这活儿是给生而自由的爱国者干的吗？”
“得，你要是不满意，杜邦公民，”埃龙干巴巴地顶回去，“随时请便；你知道，有的是人乐意接你的活……”
“一天十九个钟头，工钱十九个苏。……这苦役我已熬了十四天……”
他还在嗓子底下咕哝；埃龙不再理他，猛地转向外面驻着的一群士兵。
“开步走，下士！”他说，“带上四个人……我们上塔里去。”
小小的队伍排成了。前头是提灯的，弓背颤膝，蹭着脚步沿走廊走；随后是下士带两名士兵；再后是埃龙，德巴茨紧随；末尾又是两名士兵压阵。
埃龙把那串钥匙交给了杜邦。后者在前头高举灯笼，一道门接一道门地开；每到一道门，他等小小的队伍鱼贯而过，再把门重新锁上，继续前行。
上了两三道嵌在整石里的旋梯，最后一道沉重的门到了。
德巴茨在心里盘算。为保卫欧洲最珍贵的那条性命，埃龙的防范比他预想的还要周密。

===Original===

What lavish liberality would be required! what superhuman ingenuity and boundless courage in order to break down all the barriers that had been set up round that young life that flickered inside this grim tower!
Of these three requisites the corpulent, complacent intriguer possessed only the first in a considerable degree. He could be exceedingly liberal with the foreign money which he had at his disposal. As for courage and ingenuity, he believed that he possessed both, but these qualities had not served him in very good stead in the attempts which he had made at different times to rescue the unfortunate members of the Royal Family from prison. His overwhelming egotism would not admit for a moment that in ingenuity and pluck the Scarlet Pimpernel and his English followers could outdo him, but he did wish to make quite sure that they would not interfere with him in the highly remunerative work of saving the Dauphin.
Héron’s impatient call roused him from these meditations. The little party had come to a halt outside a massive iron-studded door.
At a sign from the chief agent the soldiers stood at attention. He then called de Batz and the lantern-bearer to him.
He took a key from his breeches pocket, and with his own hand unlocked the massive door. He curtly ordered the lantern-bearer and de Batz to go through, then he himself went in, and finally once more re-locked the door behind him, the soldiers remaining on guard on the landing outside.
Now the three men were standing in a square antechamber, dank and dark, devoid of furniture save for a large cupboard that filled the whole of one wall; the others, mildewed and stained, were covered with a greyish paper, which here and there hung away in strips.
Héron crossed this antechamber, and with his knuckles rapped against a small door opposite.
“*Holà!*” he shouted, “Simon, *mon vieux, tu es là*?”
From the inner room came the sound of voices, a man’s and a woman’s, and now, as if in response to Héron’s call, the shrill tones of a child. There was some shuffling, too, of footsteps, and some pushing about of furniture, then the door was opened, and a gruff voice invited the belated visitors to enter.

===Chinese===

得要多大的手笔！多么超人的机变、多么无限的胆魄，才能推倒那层层壁垒——它们围住这条年轻的生命，就在这座阴森塔里明灭挣扎！
这三样本钱，这肥胖自得的阴谋家只头一样绰绰有余。手里那笔外国银子，他花得可以极其大方。至于胆量和机变，他自信两样俱全；只是在他先后几回设法营救王室不幸成员出狱的尝试里，这两样本事没怎么帮上他。他那压倒一切的自负，一刻也不肯承认机变与胆气上红花侠和他的英国党羽能胜过他；但营救王太子这桩油水十足的差事，他倒真想确认他们不会来插一杠子。
埃龙不耐烦的一喊把他从盘算里惊醒。一行人已在一扇包铁钉的厚重门外停住。
首席密探一示意，士兵们立正。他把德巴茨和提灯人叫到跟前。
他从马裤兜里摸出一把钥匙，亲手开了那扇重门。他简短地命提灯人和德巴茨先进，随后自己进去，末了又把门反锁上——士兵们留在外头楼梯口守着。
此刻三人立在一间方形的内门厅里，阴潮黑暗，除了一口占满一面墙的大橱外别无家具；其余几面墙发霉带垢，糊着灰败的墙纸，好几处一条条剥落下来。
埃龙穿过门厅，用指节敲了敲对面一扇小门。
“喂！”他喊，“西蒙，老家伙，在吗？”
里屋传来人声，一男一女；接着，像是应着埃龙的喊，又添了孩子的尖嗓。还有趿拉的脚步声、挪动家具声；随后门开了，一个粗哑的嗓音请这几位夜客进屋。

===Original===

The atmosphere in this further room was so thick that at first de Batz was only conscious of the evil smells that pervaded it; smells which were made up of the fumes of tobacco, of burning coke, of a smoky lamp, and of stale food, and mingling through it all the pungent odour of raw spirits.
Héron had stepped briskly in, closely followed by de Batz. The man Dupont with a mutter of satisfaction put down his lantern and curled himself up in a corner of the antechamber. His interest in the spectacle so favoured by citizen Héron had apparently been exhausted by constant repetition.
De Batz looked round him with keen curiosity with which disgust was ready enough to mingle.
The room itself might have been a large one; it was almost impossible to judge of its size, so crammed was it with heavy and light furniture of every conceivable shape and type. There was a monumental wooden bedstead in one corner, a huge sofa covered in black horsehair in another. A large table stood in the centre of the room, and there were at least four capacious armchairs round it. There were wardrobes and cabinets, a diminutive washstand and a huge pier-glass, there were innumerable boxes and packing-cases, cane-bottomed chairs and what-nots everywhere. The place looked like a depot for secondhand furniture.
In the midst of all the litter de Batz at last became conscious of two people who stood staring at him and at Héron. He saw a man before him, somewhat fleshy of build, with smooth, mouse-coloured hair brushed away from a central parting, and ending in a heavy curl above each ear; the eyes were wide open and pale in colour, the lips unusually thick and with a marked downward droop. Close beside him stood a youngish-looking woman, whose unwieldy bulk, however, and pallid skin revealed the sedentary life and the ravages of ill-health.
Both appeared to regard Héron with a certain amount of awe, and de Batz with a vast measure of curiosity.
Suddenly the woman stood aside, and in the far corner of the room there was displayed to the Gascon Royalist’s cold, calculating gaze the pathetic figure of the uncrowned King of France.
“How is it Capet is not yet in bed?” queried Héron as soon as he caught sight of the child.
“He wouldn’t say his prayers this evening,” replied Simon with a coarse laugh, “and wouldn’t drink his medicine. Bah!”

===Chinese===

里间这层空气浊得发实，德巴茨起先只觉出满屋的恶味：烟草、烧焦炭、冒烟的油灯、馊了的饭菜，几样搅在一处，还有生烈酒那股冲鼻的气味透贯其间。
埃龙快步进了屋，德巴茨紧随。杜邦咕哝一声表示满意，放下灯笼，在门厅角落里蜷成一团。埃龙公民爱看的这出戏，看来早看了一遍又一遍，兴味索然了。
德巴茨环顾四周，好奇里随时要掺进恶心。
屋子本身也许不小；可塞满了各式各样、或重或轻的家什，大小几乎没法判断。一角立着一架纪念碑似的木床，另一角是一张马鬃面的黑大沙发。屋子正中一张大桌，四围至少围着四把宽大的扶手椅。还有大衣柜、橱、一只小小的洗脸架和一面巨大的穿衣镜；箱笼货箱不计其数，藤面椅和搁架随处都是。这地方活像一家旧家具栈。
在这一堆杂乱的物什中间，德巴茨终于看清两个人，正瞪着眼望他和埃龙。眼前是个身量微胖的男人，鼠灰色的头发从当中间隔梳向两边，每只耳朵上头收成一绺厚厚的卷；眼睛瞪得老大，颜色淡；嘴唇厚得出奇，明显地往下耷拉。紧挨着他站着个看去还年轻的女人，可那臃肿的身躯和苍白的皮肤，透出久坐的生活和疾病的摧残。
两人望着埃龙都带几分畏，望着德巴茨却是十足的好奇。
女人忽然闪身让开，屋子远角里，便向这位加斯科尼保王党人冷冷的、打着算盘的目光，露出了法兰西未加冕国王那小小的、可怜的身影。
“卡佩怎么还没上床？”埃龙一看见孩子便问。
“他今晚不肯祷告，”西蒙粗声一笑，答道，“也不肯喝药。嗐！”

===Original===

he added with a snarl, “this is a place for dogs and not for human folk.”
“If you are not satisfied, *mon vieux*,” retorted Héron curtly, “you can send in your resignation when you like. There are plenty who will be glad of the place.”
The ex-cobbler gave another surly growl and expectorated on the floor in the direction where stood the child.
“Little vermin,” he said, “he is more trouble than man or woman can bear.”
The boy in the meanwhile seemed to take but little notice of the vulgar insults put upon him by his guardian. He stood, a quaint, impassive little figure, more interested apparently in de Batz, who was a stranger to him, than in the three others whom he knew. De Batz noted that the child looked well nourished, and that he was warmly clad in a rough woollen shirt and cloth breeches, with coarse grey stockings and thick shoes; but he also saw that the clothes were indescribably filthy, as were the child’s hands and face. The golden curls, among which a young and queenly mother had once loved to pass her slender perfumed fingers, now hung bedraggled, greasy, and lank round the little face, from the lines of which every trace of dignity and of simplicity had long since been erased.
There was no look of the martyr about this child now, even though, mayhap, his small back had often smarted under his vulgar tutor’s rough blows; rather did the pale young face wear the air of sullen indifference, and an abject desire to please, which would have appeared heartbreaking to any spectator less self-seeking and egotistic than was this Gascon conspirator.
Madame Simon had called him to her while her man and the citizen Héron were talking, and the child went readily enough, without any sign of fear. She took the corner of her coarse dirty apron in her hand, and wiped the boy’s mouth and face with it.
“I can’t keep him clean,” she said with an apologetic shrug of the shoulders and a look at de Batz. “There now,” she added, speaking once more to the child, “drink like a good boy, and say your lesson to please *maman*, and then you shall go to bed.”
She took a glass from the table, which was filled with a clear liquid that de Batz at first took to be water, and held it to the boy’s lips. He turned his head away and began to whimper.
“Is the medicine very nasty?” queried de Batz.
“*Mon Dieu!

===Chinese===

他又狺了一声，“这是养狗的地方，不是住人的。”
“你若不乐意，老家伙，”埃龙没好气地顶回去，“随时递辞呈。想要这差事的人有的是。”
前鞋匠又闷吼一声，朝孩子站的方向往地上啐了一口。
“小畜生，”他说，“他比神仙受得了的还麻烦。”
那孩子对这些来自监护人的粗俗羞辱，似乎并不怎么在意。他站着，一个小小的、古怪而无动于衷的身影，对生人德巴茨显然倒比对那三个熟人有兴致。德巴茨注意到孩子营养的模样不坏，穿得也暖：粗呢衬衫、布马裤、灰粗袜、厚鞋；可他也看见，那衣裳脏得没法形容，孩子的手和脸也一样。那一头金色的鬈发——当年一位年轻庄严的母亲最爱把纤细喷香的手指插进去梳弄——如今湿塌塌、油腻腻、乱蓬蓬地耷拉在小脸四周；那张小脸上的每一根线条，尊严与天真的痕迹都早已被磨去。
这孩子此刻全无殉道者的气象，虽说他那小小的脊背，多半常在这粗鄙塾师的巴掌下发麻发疼；那张苍白的娃娃脸，反倒挂着一副阴郁的漠然，外加一股低声下气的讨好——这光景落在任何一个不像这加斯科尼阴谋家那样自私自负的旁观者眼里，都该心碎。
男人同埃龙公民说话那阵儿，西蒙太太已把孩子唤到跟前，孩子乖乖就去了，毫无惧色。她捏起粗脏围裙的一角，给男孩擦嘴擦脸。
“我没法叫他干净，”她说，抱歉地耸耸肩，望了德巴茨一眼。“好了，”她又对孩子说，“乖乖喝了，把功课背了哄哄妈妈，就放你上床。”
她从桌上端起一只杯子，盛着清亮的液体，德巴茨起初当是水，凑到男孩唇边。孩子把头扭开，呜咽起来。
“药很难喝吗？”德巴茨问。
“我的天！

===Original===

* but no, citizen,” exclaimed the woman, “it is good strong eau-de-vie, the best that can be procured. Capet likes it really﻿—don’t you, Capet? It makes you happy and cheerful, and sleep well of nights. Why, you had a glassful yesterday and enjoyed it. Take it now,” she added in a quick whisper, seeing that Simon and Héron were in close conversation together; “you know it makes papa angry if you don’t have at least half a glass now and then.”
The child wavered for a moment longer, making a quaint little grimace of distaste. But at last he seemed to make up his mind that it was wisest to yield over so small a matter, and he took the glass from Madame Simon.
And thus did de Batz see the descendant of St. Louis quaffing a glass of raw spirit at the bidding of a rough cobbler’s wife, whom he called by the fond and foolish name sacred to childhood, *maman*!
Selfish egoist though he was, de Batz turned away in loathing.
Simon had watched the little scene with obvious satisfaction. He chuckled audibly when the child drank the spirit, and called Héron’s attention to him, whilst a look of triumph lit up his wide, pale eyes.
“And now, *mon petit*,” he said jovially, “let the citizen hear you say your prayers!”
He winked toward de Batz, evidently anticipating a good deal of enjoyment for the visitor from what was coming. From a heap of litter in a corner of the room he fetched out a greasy red bonnet adorned with a tricolour cockade, and a soiled and tattered flag, which had once been white, and had golden fleur-de-lys embroidered upon it.
The cap he set on the child’s head, and the flag he threw upon the floor.
“Now, Capet﻿—your prayers!” he said with another chuckle of amusement.
All his movements were rough, and his speech almost ostentatiously coarse. He banged against the furniture as he moved about the room, kicking a footstool out of the way or knocking over a chair. De Batz instinctively thought of the perfumed stillness of the rooms at Versailles, of the army of elegant highborn ladies who had ministered to the wants of this child, who stood there now before him, a cap on his yellow hair, and his shoulder held up to his ear with that gesture of careless indifference peculiar to children when they are sullen or uncared for.

===Chinese===

哪儿的话，公民，”女人嚷道，“这是上好的烧酒，能弄到的顶好的货色。卡佩其实爱喝——是不是，卡佩？喝了就高兴、就快活，夜里睡得香。瞧，你昨天还喝了满满一杯，受用得很。现在喝了它，”见西蒙同埃龙正谈得起劲，她飞快地压低嗓子添道，“你知道的，隔三五回不喝上小半杯，爸爸要生气的。”
孩子又踌躇了片刻，做出一副嫌恶的小小怪相。但末了他似乎拿定主意：这点小事，还是顺从最明智。他从西蒙太太手里接过了杯子。
圣路易的后裔，就这样遵着一个粗鞋匠老婆的吩咐，灌下一杯生烧酒——他叫她的，正是那个娇痴的、只属于童年的名字：妈妈！
德巴茨虽是个自私自利的自我主义者，也嫌恶地扭过了头。
西蒙瞧着这幕小戏，显然很满意。孩子喝酒时他出声地笑，还唤埃龙来看，宽阔的淡色眼睛里燃起得胜的光。
“现在，小东西，”他乐呵呵地说，“让公民听听你做祷告！”
他朝德巴茨眨眨眼，显然料定接下来的把戏够客人受用的。他从屋角一堆破烂里翻出一顶油腻的红便帽，缀着三色帽徽，还有一面污损破碎的旗——它原是白的，绣着金色的百合花。
帽子扣在孩子头上，旗撑在地上。
“来，卡佩——祷告！”他又乐得咯咯一笑。
他一举一动都粗鲁，说话几乎故意往粗里说。在屋里走动，他撞得家什乱响，一脚踢开脚凳，或一把抵翻椅子。德巴茨不由自主想起凡尔赛那些静静的、熏香的殿堂，想起伺候过这孩子起居的那一队雍容华贵的命妇——而孩子此刻就站在他面前，黄头发上扣着顶帽子，肩膀耸到耳朵边，一副孩童闹性子或没人疼时特有的、满不在乎的架势。

===Original===

Obediently, quite mechanically it seemed, the boy trod on the flag which Henri IV had borne before him at Ivry, and *le Roi Soleil* had flaunted in the face of the armies of Europe. The son of the Bourbons was spitting on their flag, and wiping his shoes upon its tattered folds. With shrill cracked voice he sang the Carmagnole, “*Ça ira! ça ira! les aristos à la lanterne!*” until de Batz himself felt inclined to stop his ears and to rush from the place in horror.
Louis XVII, whom the hearts of many had proclaimed King of France by the grace of God, the child of the Bourbons, the eldest son of the Church, was stepping a vulgar dance over the flag of St. Louis, which he had been taught to defile. His pale cheeks glowed as he danced, his eyes shone with the unnatural light kindled in them by the intoxicating liquor; with one slender hand he waved the red cap with the tricolour cockade, and shouted “*Vive la République!*”
Madame Simon was clapping her hands, looking on the child with obvious pride, and a kind of rough maternal affection. Simon was gazing on Héron for approval, and the latter nodded his head, murmuring words of encouragement and of praise.
“Thy catechism now, Capet﻿—thy catechism,” shouted Simon in a hoarse voice.
The boy stood at attention, cap on head, hands on his hips, legs wide apart, and feet firmly planted on the fleur-de-lys, the glory of his forefathers.
“Thy name?” queried Simon.
“Louis Capet,” replied the child in a clear, high-pitched voice.
“What art thou?”
“A citizen of the Republic of France.”
“What was thy father?”
“Louis Capet, ci-devant king, a tyrant who perished by the will of the people!”
“What was thy mother?”
“A —﻿—﻿—.”
De Batz involuntarily uttered a cry of horror. Whatever the man’s private character was, he had been born a gentleman, and his every instinct revolted against what he saw and heard. The scene had positively sickened him. He turned precipitately towards the door.
“How now, citizen?” queried the Committee’s agent with a sneer. “Are you not satisfied with what you see?”
“Mayhap the citizen would like to see Capet sitting in a golden chair,” interposed Simon the cobbler with a sneer, “and me and my wife kneeling and kissing his hand﻿—what?”
“ ’Tis the heat of the room,” stammered de Batz, who was fumbling with the lock of the door; “my head began to swim.”

===Chinese===

孩子乖乖地——简直像机械地——踩上了那面旗：亨利四世当年在伊夫里擎着它冲锋，太阳王曾挥舞着它炫示于欧洲列强的军前。
波旁之子朝自家王旗啐唾沫，在那破烂的褶子上蹭鞋底。他扯着尖哑的嗓子唱卡尔马尼奥勒：“*就这样！就这样！把贵族挂上灯笼！*”唱得德巴茨自己都恨不能捂住耳朵，毛骨悚然地夺路而逃。
路易十七——多少人的心已奉他为蒙神恩典的法兰西国王，波旁的子嗣，教会的长子——正踩着圣路易的王旗跳一支粗俗的舞，那旗就是他们教会他玷污的。他跳着，苍白的两颊烧起红，眼里燃着烈酒点起的非自然的光；一只纤细的手挥着缀三色徽的红帽，喊着“*共和国万岁！*”
西蒙太太拍着手望孩子，明显带着得意，还有一股粗粝的母性的疼爱。西蒙则眼巴巴望着埃龙讨表扬，后者点头，嘴里念叨着鼓励和夸赞。
“现在背要理问答，卡佩——要理问答。”西蒙哑着嗓子喊。
男孩立正站好，头上扣帽，双手叉腰，两腿大开，双脚结结实实踩在百合花上——他列祖列宗的荣光。
“你叫什么？”西蒙问。
“路易·卡佩。”孩子用清亮的高音答。
“你是什么人？”
“法兰西共和国的公民。”
“你父亲是什么人？”
“路易·卡佩，前国王，一个按人民意志伏诛的暴君！”
“你母亲是什么人？”
“一个————。”
德巴茨不由自主发出一声恐怖的喊叫。此人为人如何且不论，他生来是绅士，所见所闻激起他全部本能的反感。这场面真把他恶心坏了。他仓皇转向门口。
“怎么了，公民？”委员会的密探讥诮地问，“看到的还不够你满意？”
“兴许公民还想看卡佩坐金椅子呢，”鞋匠西蒙讥笑着插嘴，“看我和我老婆跪下亲他的手——是不是？”
“是屋里太热，”德巴茨摸着门锁，结结巴巴地说，“我头发晕。”

===Original===

“Spit on their accursed flag, then, like a good patriot, like Capet,” retorted Simon gruffly. “Here, Capet, my son,” he added, pulling the boy by the arm with a rough gesture, “get thee to bed; thou art quite drunk enough to satisfy any good Republican.”
By way of a caress he tweaked the boy’s ear and gave him a prod in the back with his bent knee. He was not wilfully unkind, for just now he was not angry with the lad; rather was he vastly amused with the effect Capet’s prayer and Capet’s recital of his catechism had had on the visitor.
As to the lad, the intensity of excitement in him was immediately followed by an overwhelming desire for sleep. Without any preliminary of undressing or of washing, he tumbled, just as he was, on to the sofa. Madame Simon, with quite pleasing solicitude, arranged a pillow under his head, and the very next moment the child was fast asleep.
“ ’Tis well, *citoyen* Simon,” said Héron in his turn, going towards the door. “I’ll report favourably on you to the Committee of Public Security. As for the *citoyenne*, she had best be more careful,” he added, turning to the woman Simon with a snarl on his evil face. “There was no cause to arrange a pillow under the head of that vermin’s spawn. Many good patriots have no pillows to put under their heads. Take that pillow away; and I don’t like the shoes on the brat’s feet; sabots are quite good enough.”
Citoyenne Simon made no reply. Some sort of retort had apparently hovered on her lips, but had been checked, even before it was uttered, by a peremptory look from her husband. Simon the cobbler, snarling in speech but obsequious in manner, prepared to accompany the citizen agent to the door.
De Batz was taking a last look at the sleeping child; the uncrowned King of France was wrapped in a drunken sleep, with the last spoken insult upon his dead mother still hovering on his childish lips.

===Chinese===

“那就朝他们那破旗啐一口，做个好爱国者，学卡佩的样。”西蒙粗声抢白。“过来，卡佩，我的儿，”他又粗暴地拽起孩子的胳膊，“睡觉去；你这醉样，够叫任何一个好共和派满意了。”
算是亲昵，他拧了拧孩子的耳朵，又用屈起的膝盖往他背上一顶。他不是存心虐待；这会儿他也没生这小子的气，倒是有滋有味地欣赏着卡佩的祷告和要理问答在客人身上起的效果。
至于孩子，那一阵亢奋过后，随即便是排山倒海的瞠睡。他不脱衣、不洗漱，就那么一头栽到沙发上。西蒙太太带着相当可亲的体贴，在他头下安置了个枕头；下一刻，孩子已沉沉睡去。
“很好，西蒙公民，”轮到埃龙发话了，一边朝门口走，“我会向治安委员会替你说好话。至于公民太太，她最好当心点，”他转向西蒙女人，恶脸上带着狺相，又添道，“没什么道理给那畜生的崽子头下垫枕头。多少好爱国者头上还没枕头垫呢。把枕头拿走；还有，我不喜欢那小子脚上的鞋；木鞋就尽够好了。”
西蒙女公民没作声。看得出有句回敬已到她唇边，却还没出口就叫丈夫一记专断的眼色堵了回去。鞋匠西蒙嘴里咕哝着，态度却毕恭毕敬，张罗着送密探公民到门口。
德巴茨最后望了一眼睡着的孩子；法兰西未加冕的国王裹在醉梦里，那声骂他亡母的话，犹自挂在他孩子气的唇上。

## VIII Arcades Ambo / VIII 一丘之貉
===Original===

“That is the way we conduct our affairs, citizen,” said Héron gruffly, as he once more led his guest back into his office.
It was his turn to be complacent now. De Batz, for once in his life cowed by what he had seen, still wore a look of horror and disgust upon his florid face.
“What devils you all are!” he said at last.
“We are good patriots,” retorted Héron, “and the tyrant’s spawn leads but the life that hundreds of thousands of children led whilst his father oppressed the people. Nay! what am I saying? He leads a far better, far happier life. He gets plenty to eat and plenty of warm clothes. Thousands of innocent children, who have not the crimes of a despot father upon their conscience, have to starve whilst he grows fat.”
The leer in his face was so evil that once more de Batz felt that eerie feeling of terror creeping into his bones. Here were cruelty and bloodthirsty ferocity personified to their utmost extent. At thought of the Bourbons, or of all those whom he considered had been in the past the oppressors of the people, Héron was nothing but a wild and ravenous beast, hungering for revenge, longing to bury his talons and his fangs into the body of those whose heels had once pressed on his own neck.
And de Batz knew that even with millions or countless money at his command he could not purchase from this carnivorous brute the life and liberty of the son of King Louis. No amount of bribery would accomplish that; it would have to be ingenuity pitted against animal force, the wiliness of the fox against the power of the wolf.
Even now Héron was darting savagely suspicious looks upon him.
“I shall get rid of the Simons,” he said; “there’s something in that woman’s face which I don’t trust. They shall go within the next few hours, or as soon as I can lay my hands upon a better patriot than that mealymouthed cobbler. And it will be better not to have a woman about the place. Let me see﻿—today is Thursday, or else Friday morning. By Sunday I’ll get those Simons out of the place. Methought I saw you ogling that woman,” he added, bringing his bony fist crashing down on the table so that papers, pen, and inkhorn rattled loudly; “and if I thought that you﻿—”
De Batz thought it well at this point to finger once more nonchalantly the bundle of crisp paper in the pocket of his coat.

===Chinese===

“咱们办事，就是这个路数，公民。”埃龙粗声粗气地说，一面重新把客人领回自己的办公房。
这回轮到他得意了。德巴茨平生头一遭被亲眼所见镇住了，红润的脸上还挂着一副又惊又憎的神色。
“你们这帮人，真是魔鬼！”他终于开口。
“我们是好爱国者，”埃龙反唇相讥，“暴君的种过的日子，不过是他爹压迫人民那阵子，千千万万孩子过的日子罢了。呸！我说什么来着？他过的日子好得多、美得多。他吃得饱饱的，暖衣厚厚的。成千上万清白无辜的孩子——他们良心上可没有暴君父亲的罪孽——却得饿着肚子，眼看他长膘。”
他脸上那副斜睨的神情邪恶透顶，德巴茨又觉出那股阴森森的恐怖钻进骨缝里。眼前这简直就是残忍与嗜血兽性登峰造极的化身。一想起波旁家，想起他认定昔日压迫过人民的那一干人，埃龙便只剩一头疯狂的饿兽，复仇的饥火中烧，恨不得把利爪獠牙深深埋进那些人的躯体——那些曾经用脚跟踩过他脖颈的人。
德巴茨心里明白：纵然手握百万千万，他也休想从这头吃人的畜生手里，买下路易之子的性命和自由。多少贿金都办不成这件事；能指望的，只有拿机智去斗蛮力——狐狸的诡谲，对恶狼的爪牙。
就在此刻，埃龙还朝他射来一道道凶狠的猜疑目光。
“我要把西蒙夫妇打发走，”他说，“那女人脸上有点什么，我不放心。不出几个钟头，或是一等我找到个比那个甜嘴滑舌的鞋匠更够格的爱国者，他们就得滚。这地方还是不留女人的好。让我算算——今天是星期四，要不就是星期五早晨。到星期天，我准把那两个西蒙弄走。我恍惚瞧见你朝那女人眉来眼去了，”他补充道，骨节嶙峋的拳头砰地砸在桌上，震得纸笔墨盒哗啦乱响，“我要是想到你——”
德巴茨觉得，此刻最好还是装作漫不经心，再摸一摸上衣口袋里那卷薄脆的纸。

===Original===

“Only on that one condition,” reiterated Héron in a hoarse voice; “if you try to get at Capet, I’ll drag you to the Tribunal with my own hands.”
“Always presuming that you can get me, my friend,” murmured de Batz, who was gradually regaining his accustomed composure.
Already his active mind was busily at work. One or two things which he had noted in connection with his visit to the Dauphin’s prison had struck him as possibly useful in his schemes. But he was disappointed that Héron was getting rid of the Simons. The woman might have been very useful and more easily got at than a man. The avarice of the French bourgeoise would have proved a promising factor. But this, of course, would now be out of the question. At the same time it was not because Héron raved and stormed and uttered cries like a hyena that he, de Batz, meant to give up an enterprise which, if successful, would place millions into his own pocket.
As for that meddling Englishman, the Scarlet Pimpernel, and his crackbrained followers, they must be effectually swept out of the way first of all. De Batz felt that they were the real, the most likely hindrance to his schemes. He himself would have to go very cautiously to work, since apparently Héron would not allow him to purchase immunity for himself in that one matter, and whilst he was laying his plans with necessary deliberation so as to ensure his own safety, that accursed Scarlet Pimpernel would mayhap snatch the golden prize from the Temple prison right under his very nose.
When he thought of that the Gascon Royalist felt just as vindictive as did the chief agent of the Committee of General Security.
While these thoughts were coursing through de Batz’ head, Héron had been indulging in a volley of vituperation.
“If that little vermin escapes,” he said, “my life will not be worth an hour’s purchase. In twenty-four hours I am a dead man, thrown to the guillotine like those dogs of aristocrats! You say I am a night-bird, citizen. I tell you that I do not sleep night or day thinking of that brat and the means to keep him safely under my hand. I have never trusted those Simons﻿—”
“Not trusted them!” exclaimed de Batz; “surely you could not find anywhere more inhuman monsters!”
“Inhuman monsters?” snarled Héron. “Bah! they don’t do their business thoroughly; we want the tyrant’s spawn to become a true Republican and a patriot﻿—aye!

===Chinese===

“只此一个条件，”埃龙哑着嗓子重申，“你敢打卡佩的主意，我就亲手把你拖到法庭去。”
“那还得先看你抓不抓得着我，我的朋友。”德巴茨喃喃道——他已渐渐找回惯常的镇定。
他那颗活络的脑子早已忙开了。这趟王太子监狱之行，他记下的一两处细节，看来对他的盘算兴许有用。只是埃龙要攦走西蒙夫妇，叫他失望。那女人本可以大有用处，比男人容易下手。法国市井妇人的贪财，本是个大有可为的因素。当然，如今无从谈起了。可话说回来，埃龙便是学鬣狗一般又嚎又闹，他德巴茨也不打算摌下这桩营生——一旦办成，几百万就进他自己的腰包。
至于那个爱管闲事的英国人红花侠，还有他那帮疯疯癫癫的党羽，首先得把他们干干净净扫到一边去。德巴茨觉得，这才是他计划真正的、最可能的心腹之患。他自己行事必须加倍谨慎——显然，单在卡佩这一件事上，埃龙是不容他花钱买太平的；而他还在步步为营、为自己留后路的时候，那个该死的红花侠，保不齐就从他鼻子底下，把坦普尔狱里那份金灿灿的厚利夺了去。
一想到这里，这位加斯科尼的保王党人，心头的怨毒便不输给治安委员会的总密探半分。
这些念头在德巴茨脑子里翻腾的当口，埃龙正痛痛快快骂个不休。
“那小虫豸若是跑了，”他说，“我这条命就撑不过一个钟头。不出二十四小时我就是个死人，像那些贵族狗一样扔上断头台！你们说我是个夜猫子，公民。我告诉你：白天黑夜，我合不上眼，想的都是那崽子，想怎么把他稳稳攮在手心里。那两个西蒙，我从来没信过——”
“没信过他们！”德巴茨叫起来，“天底下你还能上哪儿找更没人心的怪物去！”
“没人心的怪物？”埃龙狞笑一声。“呸！他们办事不干净彻底。我们要那暴君的种变成个真正的共和党人、爱国者——对！

===Original===

to make of him such a one that even if you and your cursed confederates got him by some hellish chance, he would be no use to you as a king, a tyrant to set above the people, to set up in your Versailles, your Louvre, to eat off golden plates and wear satin clothes. You have seen the brat! By the time he is a man he should forget how to eat save with his fingers, and get roaring drunk every night. That’s what we want!﻿—to make him so that he shall be no use to you, even if you did get him away; but you shall not! You shall not, not if I have to strangle him with my own hands.”
He picked up his short-stemmed pipe and pulled savagely at it for awhile. De Batz was meditating.
“My friend,” he said after a little while, “you are agitating yourself quite unnecessarily, and gravely jeopardising your prospects of getting a comfortable little income through keeping your fingers off my person. Who said I wanted to meddle with the child?”
“You had best not,” growled Héron.
“Exactly. You have said that before. But do you not think that you would be far wiser, instead of directing your undivided attention to my unworthy self, to turn your thoughts a little to one whom, believe me, you have far greater cause to fear?”
“Who is that?”
“The Englishman.”
“You mean the man they call the Scarlet Pimpernel?”
“Himself. Have you not suffered from his activity, friend Héron? I fancy that citizen Chauvelin and citizen Collot would have quite a tale to tell about him.”
“They ought both to have been guillotined for that blunder last autumn at Boulogne.”
“Take care that the same accusation be not laid at your door this year, my friend,” commented de Batz placidly.
“Bah!”
“The Scarlet Pimpernel is in Paris even now.”
“The devil he is!”
“And on what errand, think you?”
There was a moment’s silence, and then de Batz continued with slow and dramatic emphasis:
“That of rescuing your most precious prisoner from the Temple.”
“How do you know?” Héron queried savagely.
“I guessed.”
“How?”
“I saw a man in the Théâtre National today﻿ ﻿…”
“Well?”
“Who is a member of the League of the Scarlet Pimpernel.”
“D﻿—﻿— him! Where can I find him?”
“Will you sign a receipt for the three thousand five hundred livres, which I am pining to hand over to you, my friend, and I will tell you?”
“Where’s the money?”
“In my pocket.”

===Chinese===

——要把他造就成这样一号人：哪怕你和你那帮该死的同党撞上什么鬼运气把他弄到手，他也当不成你们的国王、当不成骑在人民头上的暴君，供不进你们的凡尔赛、你们的卢浮宫，用不得金盘子吃饭、穿不得绸缎衣裳。你见过那崽子了！等他长大成人，他该连不用手抓饭吃都不会了，夜夜烂醉如泥。我们要的就是这个！——把他造就得对你们毫无用处，就算你们真把他弄出去也没用；可你们弄不出去！弄不出去，除非我亲手攮死他。”
他抓起短柄烟斗，恶狠狠地猛吸了一阵。德巴茨在沉吟。
“我的朋友，”过了片刻他说，“你这火气发得全无必要，倒严重危及了你那笔舒坦的小进项——那可是靠不碰我一根汗毛才挣得下的。谁说我要打那孩子的主意了？”
“你顶好不要。”埃龙低吼。
“正是。这话你说过了。可你想过没有：与其把全副心思都攮在我这么个不足道的人身上，还不如匀出一点，去想想另一位——信我吧，那一位才更值得你怕。”
“谁？”
“那个英国人。”
“你是说他们叫作红花侠的那个？”
“正是他。他的活跃，你还没领教过么，埃龙朋友？我料想肖弗兰公民和科洛公民讲起他来，怕有一整部书好讲。”
“去年秋天布洛涅那场昏招，这俩本该一起上断头台。”
“小心今年的同一桩罪名，别落到你自己门上，我的朋友。”德巴茨平平静静地点了一句。
“呸！”
“红花侠此刻就在巴黎。”
“活见鬼！”
“你猜，他来干什么营生？”
静了片刻，德巴茨又开了口，一字一顿，专等那句效果：
“把坦普尔狱里你那件顶金贵的囚犯，救出去。”
“你怎么知道？”埃龙凶巴巴地质问。
“我猜的。”
“怎么猜的？”
“今天我在国家剧院看见一个人……”
“说下去。”
“那人是红花侠同盟的一分子。”
“该死的！上哪儿能逮着他？”
“朋友，这三千五百里弗我正巴不得交到你手上——你肯不肯签张凭条？签了我就告诉你。”
“钱在哪儿？”
“在我口袋里。”

===Original===

Without further words Héron dragged the inkhorn and a sheet of paper towards him, took up a pen, and wrote a few words rapidly in a loose, scrawly hand. He strewed sand over the writing, then handed it across the table to de Batz.
“Will that do?” he asked briefly.
The other was reading the note through carefully.
“I see you only grant me a fortnight,” he remarked casually.
“For that amount of money it is sufficient. If you want an extension you must pay more.”
“So be it,” assented de Batz coolly, as he folded the paper across. “On the whole a fortnight’s immunity in France these days is quite a pleasant respite. And I prefer to keep in touch with you, friend Héron. I’ll call on you again this day fortnight.”
He took out a letter-case from his pocket. Out of this he drew a packet of banknotes, which he laid on the table in front of Héron, then he placed the receipt carefully into the letter-case, and this back into his pocket.
Héron in the meanwhile was counting over the banknotes. The light of ferocity had entirely gone from his eyes; momentarily the whole expression of the face was one of satisfied greed.
“Well!” he said at last when he had assured himself that the number of notes was quite correct, and he had transferred the bundle of crisp papers into an inner pocket of his coat﻿—“well, what about your friend?”
“I knew him years ago,” rejoined de Batz coolly; “he is a kinsman of citizen St. Just. I know that he is one of the confederates of the Scarlet Pimpernel.”
“Where does he lodge?”
“That is for you to find out. I saw him at the theatre, and afterwards in the greenroom; he was making himself agreeable to the citizeness Lange. I heard him ask for leave to call on her tomorrow at four o’clock. You know where she lodges, of course!”
He watched Héron while the latter scribbled a few words on a scrap of paper, then he quietly rose to go. He took up his cloak and once again wrapped it round his shoulders. There was nothing more to be said, and he was anxious to go.
The leave-taking between the two men was neither cordial nor more than barely courteous. De Batz nodded to Héron, who escorted him to the outside door of his lodging, and there called loudly to a soldier who was doing sentinel at the further end of the corridor.
“Show this citizen the way to the guichet,” he said curtly. “Good night, citizen,” he added finally, nodding to de Batz.

===Chinese===

埃龙二话不说，把墨盒和一张纸拖到跟前，抓起笔，潦草散漫地飞快写了几行。他在字上撒了层吸墨沙，然后隔着桌子递给德巴茨。
“行不行？”他简短地问。
那一位正逐字逐句细读那张条子。
