“我看你只给我两个星期。”他随口说。
“那笔钱，只够这个数。想延长，加钱。”
“就这么办。”德巴茨冷冷地应承，把纸对折收好。“总的说来，在如今的法兰西，两星期的太平，也算一段挺惬意的喘息了。而且我乐意同你保持联络，埃龙朋友。两星期后的今天，我再来拜望。”
他从口袋里取出一只皮夹，从夹里抽出一叠钞票，摌在埃龙面前的桌上；再把那张凭条仔细收进皮夹，皮夹揣回口袋。
这当口埃龙正在点数钞票。眼里的凶光褪了个干净；一时间，整张脸上只剩一副心满意足的贪婪。
“好！”他终于开口——票数点得毫厘不差、那卷脆响的纸已转移进上衣内袋之后——“好了，你那位朋友怎么说？”
“多年前我就认得他，”德巴茨冷冷地答，“是圣茹斯特公民的一位亲戚。我知道他是红花侠的同党之一。”
“他落脚在哪儿？”
“这就留给你去查了。我在剧院见着他，随后又在后台休息室；他正对女公民朗热大献殷勤。我听见他求了明儿四点钟登门拜访的许可。她住哪儿，你自然是知道的！”
他看着埃龙在一张纸片上疾书了几个字，便悄然起身告辞。他拿起外套，重新披上肩头。话已说尽，他也急着要走。
两人的道别，谈不上热络，连客气也只是勉强够数。德巴茨朝埃龙点了点头；埃龙送他到寓所外门，在那里高声唤住走廊尽头站岗的一名士兵。
“送这位公民到门房去。”他简短地说。又朝德巴茨点点头，末了添一句：“晚安，公民。”

===Original===

Ten minutes later the Gascon once more found himself in the Rue du Temple between the great outer walls of the prison and the silent little church and convent of St. Elizabeth. He looked up to where in the central tower a small grated window lighted from within showed the place where the last of the Bourbons was being taught to desecrate the traditions of his race, at the bidding of a mender of shoes﻿—a naval officer cashiered for misconduct and fraud.
Such is human nature in its self-satisfied complacency that de Batz, calmly ignoring the vile part which he himself had played in the last quarter of an hour of his interview with the Committee’s agent, found it in him to think of Héron with loathing, and even of the cobbler Simon with disgust.
Then with a self-righteous sense of duty performed, and an indifferent shrug of the shoulders, he dismissed Héron from his mind.
“That meddlesome Scarlet Pimpernel will find his hands overfull tomorrow, and mayhap will not interfere in my affairs for some time to come,” he mused; “meseems that that will be the first time that a member of his precious League has come within the clutches of such unpleasant people as the sleuthhounds of my friend Héron!”

===Chinese===

十分钟后，这位加斯科尼人又站在了坦普尔街上，夹在监狱高大的外墙与圣伊丽莎白教堂修道院那座静悄悄的小楼之间。他抬眼望去：中央塔楼上一扇安着栅栏的小窗透出里头的灯光，照出那个地方——波旁家最后的血脉，正在那里受人调教，学着玷辱自己家族的传统；调教他的，是个补鞋匠——一个因劣迹和欺诈被革了职的海军军官。
人性沾沾自喜起来，便是这副模样：方才同委员会密探对坐的最后一刻钟里，他自己扮演的那个卑劣角色，德巴茨心安理得地撇在一边不算，倒很有余裕去嫌恶埃龙，甚至厌恶那鞋匠西蒙。
然后，怀着一股问心无愧、差事已毕的自得，肩膀满不在乎地一耸，他把埃龙从心里打发掉了。
“那个爱管闲事的红花侠，明天有得手忙脚乱了，兴许好一阵子都顾不上攪我的事，”他自忖道，“想来这还是头一回，他那宝贝同盟里的人，落进我朋友埃龙那群猎犬这样不好惹的家伙爪子里！”

## IX What Love Can Do / 爱能做什么
===Original===

“Yesterday you were unkind and ungallant. How could I smile when you seemed so stern?”
“Yesterday I was not alone with you. How could I say what lay next my heart, when indifferent ears could catch the words that were meant only for you?”
“Ah, monsieur, do they teach you in England how to make pretty speeches?”
“No, mademoiselle, that is an instinct that comes into birth by the fire of a woman’s eyes.”
Mademoiselle Lange was sitting upon a small sofa of antique design, with cushions covered in faded silks heaped round her pretty head. Armand thought that she looked like that carved cameo which his sister Marguerite possessed.
He himself sat on a low chair at some distance from her. He had brought her a large bunch of early violets, for he knew that she was fond of flowers, and these lay upon her lap, against the opalescent grey of her gown.
She seemed a little nervous and agitated, his obvious admiration bringing a ready blush to her cheeks.
The room itself appeared to Armand to be a perfect frame for the charming picture which she presented. The furniture in it was small and old; tiny tables of antique Vernis-Martin, softly faded tapestries, a pale-toned Aubusson carpet. Everything mellow and in a measure pathetic. Mademoiselle Lange, who was an orphan, lived alone under the duennaship of a middle-aged relative, a penniless hanger-on of the successful young actress, who acted as her chaperone, housekeeper, and maid, and kept unseemly or overbold gallants at bay.
She told Armand all about her early life, her childhood in the backshop of Maitre Mézière, the jeweller, who was a relative of her mother’s; of her desire for an artistic career, her struggles with the middle-class prejudices of her relations, her bold defiance of them, and final independence.
She made no secret of her humble origin, her want of education in those days; on the contrary, she was proud of what she had accomplished for herself. She was only twenty years of age, and already held a leading place in the artistic world of Paris.
Armand listened to her chatter, interested in everything she said, questioning her with sympathy and discretion. She asked him a good deal about himself, and about his beautiful sister Marguerite, who, of course, had been the most brilliant star in that most brilliant constellation, the Comédie Française. She had never seen Marguerite St.

===Chinese===

“昨天您冷淡又不解风情。您那样绷着脸，叫我怎么笑得出来？”
“昨天我不是单独同您在一起。心里贴着的话，怎么好在无关人等的耳朵底下说？那些话，原是只说给您一个人听的。”
“哎呀，先生，英国人就教你们说这些漂亮话吗？”
“不是，小姐，那是一种本能，是女人眼里的火把它点着生的。”
郎格小姐坐在一张古式小沙发上，褪色的绸面软垫堆在她那可爱的头颅四周。阿尔芒觉得，她活像妹妹玛格丽特珍藏的那件浮雕玉髓。
他自己坐在离她稍远的一张矮椅上。他给她带来一大束早开的紫罗兰——他知道她爱花——此刻花搁在她膝上，映着她那件蛋白石灰的裙子。她似乎有几分紧张激动，他毫不掩饰的爱慕，动不动就惹得她双颊飞红。
这屋子本身，在阿尔芒看来，正配得上她构成的这幅动人图画：一件完美的画框。家具小巧而古旧；古漆的小桌，色彩柔褪的挂毯，一铺色调清淡的奥布松地毯。样样温润，也隐隐带着几分惹人怜惜的旧气。郎格小姐是个孤儿，独自过活，由一位中年亲戚充当监护人——那是这位走红年轻女演员身边一个分文不沾的食客，兼任她的陪媪、管家和女仆，替她挡开那些不成体统、胆子太大的风月客。
她对阿尔芒讲了自己的早年：在梅齐埃师傅——那位珠宝匠，她母亲的亲戚——店铺后间的童年；她投身艺术的志愿，同亲戚们小市民偏见的挣扎，她对他们的公然反抗，以及最终的独立。她不讳言自己出身微贱、幼时失学；相反，她为自己一手挣来的一切感到骄傲。她才二十岁，在巴黎艺术界已占得一席要位。
阿尔芒听她絮絮而谈，她说什么他都有兴味，问话问得体贴而有分寸。她也问了他许多自己的事，还问起他美丽的妹妹玛格丽特——那自然是法兰西喜剧院这灿烂星座中最亮的一颗星。她从未见过玛格丽特·圣

===Original===

Just act, but, of course, Paris still rang with her praises, and all art-lovers regretted that she should have married and left them to mourn for her.
Thus the conversation drifted naturally back to England. Mademoiselle professed a vast interest in the citizen’s country of adoption.
“I had always,” she said, “thought it an ugly country, with the noise and bustle of industrial life going on everywhere, and smoke and fog to cover the landscape and to stunt the trees.”
“Then, in future, mademoiselle,” he replied, “must you think of it as one carpeted with verdure, where in the spring the orchard trees covered with delicate blossom would speak to you of fairyland, where the dewy grass stretches its velvety surface in the shadow of ancient monumental oaks, and ivy-covered towers rear their stately crowns to the sky.”
“And the Scarlet Pimpernel? Tell me about him, monsieur.”
“Ah, mademoiselle, what can I tell you that you do not already know? The Scarlet Pimpernel is a man who has devoted his entire existence to the benefit of suffering mankind. He has but one thought, and that is for those who need him; he hears but one sound﻿—the cry of the oppressed.”
“But they do say, monsieur, that philanthropy plays but a sorry part in your hero’s schemes. They aver that he looks on his own efforts and the adventures through which he goes only in the light of sport.”
“Like all Englishmen, mademoiselle, the Scarlet Pimpernel is a little ashamed of sentiment. He would deny its very existence with his lips, even whilst his noble heart brimmed over with it. Sport? Well! mayhap the sporting instinct is as keen as that of charity﻿—the race for lives, the tussle for the rescue of human creatures, the throwing of a life on the hazard of a die.”
“They fear him in France, monsieur. He has saved so many whose death had been decreed by the Committee of Public Safety.”
“Please God, he will save many yet.”
“Ah, monsieur, the poor little boy in the Temple prison!”
“He has your sympathy, mademoiselle?”
“Of every right-minded woman in France, monsieur. Oh!” she added with a pretty gesture of enthusiasm, clasping her hands together, and looking at Armand with large eyes filled with tears, “if your noble Scarlet Pimpernel will do aught to save that poor innocent lamb, I would indeed bless him in my heart, and help him with all my humble might if I could.”

===Chinese===

茹斯特登台，可巴黎自然还回荡着对她的赞誉，艺坛爱好者无不惋惜她嫁了人，撂下他们空自想念。
话头便自然而然漂回了英格兰。小姐对这位公民的归化之邦表现出极大兴趣。
“我一向，”她说，“把它想成个丑国：到处是工业生活的喧闹忙乱，烟和雾罩住山水，熏得树木长不高。”
“那么从今往后，小姐，”他答道，“您得把它想成一片铺满新绿的国度：春天里，果园的树缀满娇花，向您诉说仙境；带露的青草在苍老巨橡的浓荫下铺开天鹅绒般的绒面；爬满常春藤的塔楼，把庄严的冠冕高高举向天空。”
“还有红花侠呢？给我讲讲他吧，先生。”
“啊，小姐，您不知道的，我还有什么可讲？红花侠是这样一个人：他把整个生命献给了受苦的人类。他只有一个念头，就是那些需要他的人；他只听得见一种声音——被压迫者的呼号。”
“可是人家都说，先生，您那位英雄的算盘里，博爱只占个可怜的位置。他们断言，他看待自己的辛劳和冒险，只当是消遣竞技。”
“同所有英国人一样，小姐，红花侠对感情有点害臊。嘴上他死不认账，哪怕他高贵的胸怀正满得往外溢。竞技？唔！兴许这竞技的本能，同慈悲一样灼热——为性命赛跑，为拯救生灵角力，把一条性命掷上骰子的孤注。”
“法兰西怕他，先生。救国委员会判了死的人，他救回了那么多。”
“若蒙上帝垂允，他还要救许多。”
“啊，先生，圣殿塔监狱里那个可怜的小男孩！”
“他得到您的怜惜了，小姐？”
“法兰西每个心地纯正的女人都怜惜他，先生。哦！”她十指相扣，热切地做了个娇美的姿势，噙泪的大眼睛望着阿尔芒，又道，“您那位高贵的红花侠若肯出力救那可怜的无辜羔羊，我打心底为他祝福，只要做得到，我愿以绵薄之力相助。”

===Original===

“May God’s saints bless you for those words, mademoiselle,” he said, whilst, carried away by her beauty, her charm, her perfect femininity, he stooped towards her until his knee touched the carpet at her feet. “I had begun to lose my belief in my poor misguided country, to think all men in France vile, and all women base. I could thank you on my knees for your sweet words of sympathy, for the expression of tender motherliness that came into your eyes when you spoke of the poor forsaken Dauphin in the Temple.”
She did not restrain her tears; with her they came very easily, just as with a child, and as they gathered in her eyes and rolled down her fresh cheeks they in no way marred the charm of her face. One hand lay in her lap fingering a diminutive bit of cambric, which from time to time she pressed to her eyes. The other she had almost unconsciously yielded to Armand.
The scent of the violets filled the room. It seemed to emanate from her, a fitting attribute of her young, wholly unsophisticated girlhood. The citizen was goodly to look at; he was kneeling at her feet, and his lips were pressed against her hand.
Armand was young and he was an idealist. I do not for a moment imagine that just at this moment he was deeply in love. The stronger feeling had not yet risen up in him; it came later when tragedy encompassed him and brought passion to sudden maturity. Just now he was merely yielding himself up to the intoxicating moment, with all the abandonment, all the enthusiasm of the Latin race. There was no reason why he should not bend the knee before this exquisite little cameo, that by its very presence was giving him an hour of perfect pleasure and of aesthetic joy.
Outside the world continued its hideous, relentless way; men butchered one another, fought and hated. Here in this small old-world salon, with its faded satins and bits of ivory-tinted lace, the outer universe had never really penetrated. It was a tiny world﻿—quite apart from the rest of mankind, perfectly peaceful and absolutely beautiful.

===Chinese===

“愿上帝的圣徒为这几句话赐福您，小姐，”他说；他被她的美貌、她的风韵、她十足的女儿性摄住了，向她俯下身去，直到膝盖触到她脚边的地毯。“我已开始对我不幸误入歧途的祖国失去信念，开始想法兰西的男人个个卑劣、女人个个下作。为您这甜美的同情话，为您说起圣殿塔里那被弃的小王太子时、眼里泛起的慈母柔情，我真能跪下来谢您。”
她不忍住眼泪；她的泪来得容易，同孩子一样；泪珠在她眼里聚拢，滚下鲜嫩的脸颊，却毫不损她脸上的动人。一只手搁在膝上，捻着一小方细麻纱手帕，不时按到眼上；另一只手，她几乎不知不觉地交给了阿尔芒。
紫罗兰的芬芳盈满一室。它仿佛从她身上散发出来，正配她这份年轻的、全然不谙世事的少女时光。这位公民仪表堂堂；他跪在她脚边，唇贴着她的手。
阿尔芒年轻，又是个理想主义者。我绝不想说，此刻他已深堕爱河。更强烈的感情尚未在他心里升起；那是后来的事——悲剧围拢来，把激情催得骤然成熟。眼下他只是把自己交给这醉人的时刻，带着拉丁民族全部的忘我、全部的热忱。在这件精美的小浮雕面前屈膝，原没有什么不该：正是她本人，赠了他一个钟头完美的欢愉和审美的喜悦。
窗外，世界照旧走它狰狞无情的路；人与人屠戮、争斗、仇视。而在这间旧世界的小客厅里，在褪色的绸缎和象牙色的蕾丝之间，外面的宇宙从来不曾真正浸透进来。这是一个小小的世界——同其余的人类全然隔开，绝对安宁，绝对美。

===Original===

If Armand had been allowed to depart from here now, without having been the cause as well as the chief actor in the events that followed, no doubt that Mademoiselle Lange would always have remained a charming memory with him, an exquisite bouquet of violets pressed reverently between the leaves of a favourite book of poems, and the scent of spring flowers would in after years have ever brought her dainty picture to his mind.
He was murmuring pretty words of endearment; carried away by emotion, his arm stole round her waist; he felt that if another tear came like a dewdrop rolling down her cheek he must kiss it away at its very source. Passion was not sweeping them off their feet﻿—not yet, for they were very young, and life had not as yet presented to them its most unsolvable problem.
But they yielded to one another, to the springtime of their life, calling for Love, which would come presently hand in hand with his grim attendant, Sorrow.
Even as Armand’s glowing face was at last lifted up to hers asking with mute lips for that first kiss which she already was prepared to give, there came the loud noise of men’s heavy footsteps tramping up the old oak stairs, then some shouting, a woman’s cry, and the next moment Madame Belhomme, trembling, wide-eyed, and in obvious terror, came rushing into the room.
“Jeanne! Jeanne! My child! It is awful! It is awful! *Mon Dieu*﻿—*mon Dieu!* What is to become of us?”
She was moaning and lamenting even as she ran in, and now she threw her apron over her face and sank into a chair, continuing her moaning and her lamentations.
Neither Mademoiselle nor Armand had stirred. They remained like graven images, he on one knee, she with large eyes fixed upon his face. They had neither of them looked on the old woman; they seemed even now unconscious of her presence. But their ears had caught the sound of that measured tramp of feet up the stairs of the old house, and the halt upon the landing; they had heard the brief words of command:
“Open, in the name of the people!”
They knew quite well what it all meant; they had not wandered so far in the realms of romance that reality﻿—the grim, horrible reality of the moment﻿—had not the power to bring them back to earth.

===Chinese===

倘若阿尔芒此刻被允许离开这里——不曾成为随后种种事变的肇因与主角——那么毫无疑问，郎格小姐将永远是他记忆里一个迷人的影象：一束精美的紫罗兰，被恭恭敬敬压在一册心爱诗集的书页间；往后年年，春花的香气总会把她的倩影带回他心头。
他喃喃说着缠绵的情话；情难自禁，一条手臂悄悄环上她的腰；他觉得，再要有一颗泪像露珠似的滚下她的脸颊，他非得从源头把它吻掉不可。激情还没有把他们双双卷走——还没有；他们太年轻，生活尚未把它最无解的难题摆到他们面前。但他们彼此相就，就向他们生命的春天；春天呼唤着爱——而爱不多时便会携着它那位冷面的侍者，忧愁，一同前来。
正当阿尔芒发热的脸终于仰向她，无声的唇乞着那第一个吻——她本已备好相赠——楼梯上传来男子沉重的脚步，踏着老橡木楼梯咚咚而上，接着是喝叫，是女人的一声尖叫；转眼间，贝尔霍姆太太浑身发抖、双眼圆睁、满面惊怖地冲进屋来。
“让娜！让娜！我的孩子！糟了！糟了！*我的天——我的天！*咱们可怎么办哪？”
她一路跑一路哀号，此刻把围裙蒙在脸上，跌坐进一把椅子，哀号不止。
小姐和阿尔芒都一动未动。他们呆如石像：他单膝点地，她睁大眼睛定定望着他的脸。两人都没看那老妇人；此刻仿佛仍未察觉她在场。可他们的耳朵已捕到那阵整齐的脚步踏上一层层老楼梯、在楼梯口停住的声音；他们听到了那一声简短的号令：
“以人民的名义，开门！”
他们完全明白这意味着什么；他们还没有在传奇的国度里走得太远，远得叫现实——此刻这狰狞可怖的现实——失了把他们拽回地面的力气。

===Original===

That peremptory call to open in the name of the people was the prologue these days to a drama which had but two concluding acts: arrest, which was a certainty; the guillotine, which was more than probable. Jeanne and Armand, these two young people who but a moment ago had tentatively lifted the veil of life, looked straight into each other’s eyes and saw the hand of death interposed between them: they looked straight into each other’s eyes and knew that nothing but the hand of death would part them now. Love had come with its attendant, Sorrow; but he had come with no uncertain footsteps. Jeanne looked on the man before her, and he bent his head to imprint a glowing kiss upon her hand.
“Aunt Marie!”
It was Jeanne Lange who spoke, but her voice was no longer that of an irresponsible child; it was firm, steady and hard. Though she spoke to the old woman, she did not look at her; her luminous brown eyes rested on the bowed head of Armand St. Just.
“Aunt Marie!” she repeated more peremptorily, for the old woman, with her apron over her head, was still moaning, and unconscious of all save an overmastering fear.
“Open, in the name of the people!” came in a loud harsh voice once more from the other side of the front door.
“Aunt Marie, as you value your life and mine, pull yourself together,” said Jeanne firmly.
“What shall we do? Oh! what shall we do?” moaned Madame Belhomme. But she had dragged the apron away from her face, and was looking with some puzzlement at meek, gentle little Jeanne, who had suddenly become so strange, so dictatorial, all unlike her habitual somewhat diffident self.
“You need not have the slightest fear, Aunt Marie, if you will only do as I tell you,” resumed Jeanne quietly; “if you give way to fear, we are all of us undone. As you value your life and mine,” she now repeated authoritatively, “pull yourself together, and do as I tell you.”
The girl’s firmness, her perfect quietude had the desired effect. Madame Belhomme, though still shaken up with sobs of terror, made a great effort to master herself; she stood up, smoothed down her apron, passed her hand over her ruffled hair, and said in a quaking voice:
“What do you think we had better do?”
“Go quietly to the door and open it.”
“But﻿—the soldiers﻿—”
“If you do not open quietly they will force the door open within the next two minutes,” interposed Jeanne calmly.

===Chinese===

这一声以人民之名的开门令，如今是一出戏的序幕，而全剧只有两幕收场：逮捕，这是笃定的；断头台，十有八九跑不了。让娜和阿尔芒，这两个方才试探着掠开生命帷幕的年轻人，直直望进彼此的眼睛，看见死神的手横在两人中间；他们直直望进彼此的眼睛，知道此刻除了死神的手，再没有什么能把他们分开。爱带着它的侍者忧愁来了；它来得脚步铿锵，毫不含糊。让娜望着眼前的男人；他低下头，在她手上印下一个滚烫的吻。
“玛丽姨妈！”
说话的是让娜·郎格，可那嗓音已不是没心没肺的孩子腔；它坚定、沉着、冷硬。话是对老妇人说的，眼睛却没看她；那双明亮的褐色眼睛，停在阿尔芒·圣茹斯特低垂的头上。
“玛丽姨妈！”她更专断地重复，因为老妇人还蒙着围裙哀号个不停，除了压倒一切的恐惧什么都不知道。
“以人民的名义，开门！”大门外又一个粗厉的声音喝道。
“玛丽姨妈，您还惜您我的性命，就定下神来。”让娜坚决地说。
“怎么办哪？哦！怎么办哪？”贝尔霍姆太太哀号。可她已把围裙从脸上扯下，正纳闷地望着温顺柔和的小让娜——她忽然变得这样陌生、这样说一不二，全不像平日那个总带几分怯生生的她。
“您一点也用不着怕，玛丽姨妈，只要您照我说的做，”让娜平静地接道，“您要是由着性子怕，咱们就全完了。您还惜您我的性命，”她又威严地重复一遍，“就定下神来，照我说的做。”
姑娘的坚决、她那份十足的镇定，起了应有的效用。贝尔霍姆太太虽还被恐惧的呜咽抖着，仍竭力克制住自己；她站起身，抳平围裙，抹了抹蓬乱的头发，颤声问：
“你看咱们最好怎么办？”
“悄悄走过去，把门开了。”
“可——那些兵——”
“您要不悄悄地开，不出两分钟他们就要破门了。”让娜不动声色地插道。

===Original===

“Go quietly and open the door. Try and hide your fears, grumble in an audible voice at being interrupted in your cooking, and tell the soldiers at once that they will find mademoiselle in the boudoir. Go, for God’s sake!” she added, whilst suppressed emotion suddenly made her young voice vibrate; “go, before they break open that door!”
Madame Belhomme, impressed and cowed, obeyed like an automaton. She turned and marched fairly straight out of the room. It was not a minute too soon. From outside had already come the third and final summons:
“Open, in the name of the people!”
After that a crowbar would break open the door.
Madame Belhomme’s heavy footsteps were heard crossing the antechamber. Armand still knelt at Jeanne’s feet, holding her trembling little hand in his.
“A love-scene,” she whispered rapidly, “a love-scene﻿—quick﻿—do you know one?”
And even as he had tried to rise she held him back, down on his knees.
He thought that fear was making her distracted.
“Mademoiselle﻿—” he murmured, trying to soothe her.
“Try and understand,” she said with wonderful calm, “and do as I tell you. Aunt Marie has obeyed. Will you do likewise?”
“To the death!” he whispered eagerly.
“Then a love-scene,” she entreated. “Surely you know one. Rodrigue and Chimene! Surely﻿—surely,” she urged, even as tears of anguish rose into her eyes, “you must﻿—you must, or, if not that, something else. Quick! The very seconds are precious!”
They were indeed! Madame Belhomme, obedient as a frightened dog, had gone to the door and opened it; even her well-feigned grumblings could now be heard and the rough interrogations from the soldiery.
“Citizeness Lange!” said a gruff voice.
“In her boudoir, *quoi*!”
Madame Belhomme, braced up apparently by fear, was playing her part remarkably well.
“Bothering good citizens! On baking day, too!” she went on grumbling and muttering.
“Oh, think﻿—think!” murmured Jeanne now in an agonised whisper, her hot little hand grasping his so tightly that her nails were driven into his flesh. “You must know something that will do﻿—anything﻿—for dear life’s sake.﻿ ﻿… Armand!”
His name﻿—in the tense excitement of this terrible moment﻿—had escaped her lips.

===Chinese===

“悄悄去开门。想法子藏住您的怕，大声抱怨做饭叫人搅了，立刻告诉那些兵，小姐在闺房里。去吧，看上帝份上！”她添道，强抑的情绪突然叫她年轻的嗓音发颤；“去吧，趁他们还没砸门！”
贝尔霍姆太太被镇住了，吓服了，像个自动机器似的从命。她转身，走得相当笔直地出了屋。时间再巧不过。外面已响起第三遍、也是最后一遍的通牒：
“以人民的名义，开门！”
再往后，就该是铁撬棍破门了。
贝尔霍姆太太沉重的脚步穿过门厅，清晰可闻。阿尔芒仍跪在让娜脚边，把她颤抖的小手握在自己手里。
“一场情戏，”她急速地低语，“一场情戏——快——你会演吗？”
他刚要起身，她把他按住，仍按他跪着。
他只当她怕得乱了方寸。
“小姐——”他柔声安慰。
“你要明白，”她说，镇定得出奇，“照我说的做。玛丽姨妈从命了。你也一样吗？”
“至死从命！”他急切地低声道。
“那就来一场情戏，”她恳求道，“你准会一出。罗德里格和施曼娜！准会——准会，”她催着，痛苦的泪已涌上眼睛，“你一定要——一定要；不演这个，演别的也行。快！一秒一秒都金贵！”
何止金贵！贝尔霍姆太太像条受惊的狗一样听话，已到门口把门开了；连她装得很像的抱怨声都听得见，还有士兵们粗声的盘问。
“郎格女公民！”一个粗嗓子说。
“在闺房里呗！”
贝尔霍姆太太看来全靠恐惧撑着，戏演得出奇地好。
“搅扰安分良民！还专挑烤面包的日子！”她一路咕哝叨唠个不停。
“哦，想——快想！”让娜此刻痛楚地低语，滚烫的小手把他攥得死紧，指甲都掐进他肉里。“你总会一出顶得上的——什么都行——看在性命份上。……阿尔芒！”
他的名字——在这可怕时刻的紧绷里——脱口而出。

===Original===

All in a flash of sudden intuition he understood what she wanted, and even as the door of the boudoir was thrown violently open Armand﻿—still on his knees, but with one hand pressed to his heart, the other stretched upwards to the ceiling in the most approved dramatic style, was loudly declaiming:

> “Pour venger son honneur il perdit son amour,

Pour venger sa maitresse il a quitté le jour!”

===Chinese===

灵光一闪，他霎时懂了她的需要。就在闺房门被猛然推开的一瞬，阿尔芒——仍单膝跪着，一手按胸，另一手以最标准的戏台姿势直指天花板——朗声诵道：

> “Pour venger son honneur il perdit son amour,

Pour venger sa maitresse il a quitté le jour!”

（为雪父耻他失却了爱人，为报情仇他舍却了此生！）

===Original===

Whereupon Mademoiselle Lange feigned the most perfect impatience.
“No, no, my good cousin,” she said with a pretty moue of disdain, “that will never do! You must not thus emphasise the end of every line; the verses should flow more evenly, as thus.﻿ ﻿…”
Héron had paused at the door. It was he who had thrown it open﻿—he who, followed by a couple of his sleuthhounds, had thought to find here the man denounced by de Batz as being one of the followers of that irrepressible Scarlet Pimpernel. The obviously Parisian intonation of the man kneeling in front of citizeness Lange in an attitude no ways suggestive of personal admiration, and coolly reciting verses out of a play, had somewhat taken him aback.
“What does this mean?” he asked gruffly, striding forward into the room and glaring first at mademoiselle, then at Armand.
Mademoiselle gave a little cry of surprise.
“Why, if it isn’t citizen Héron!” she cried, jumping up with a dainty movement of coquetry and embarrassment. “Why did not Aunt Marie announce you?﻿ ﻿… It is indeed remiss of her, but she is so ill-tempered on baking days I dare not even rebuke her. Won’t you sit down, citizen Héron? And you, cousin,” she added, looking down airily on Armand, “I pray you maintain no longer that foolish attitude.”
The febrileness of her manner, the glow in her cheeks were easily attributable to natural shyness in face of this unexpected visit. Héron, completely bewildered by this little scene, which was so unlike what he expected, and so unlike those to which he was accustomed in the exercise of his horrible duties, was practically speechless before the little lady who continued to prattle along in a simple, unaffected manner.
“Cousin,” she said to Armand, who in the meanwhile had risen to his knees, “this is citizen Héron, of whom you have heard me speak. My cousin Belhomme,” she continued, once more turning to Héron, “is fresh from the country, citizen. He hails from Orléans, where he has played leading parts in the tragedies of the late citizen Corneille. But, ah me! I fear that he will find Paris audiences vastly more critical than the good Orléanese. Did you hear him, citizen, declaiming those beautiful verses just now? He was murdering them, say I﻿—yes, murdering them﻿—the gaby!”

===Chinese===

郎格小姐随即装出一副十二分的不耐烦。
“不，不，我的好表哥，”她嗔怪地一撇嘴，说不出的好看，“这样可不行！不能每到行尾就这样重读；诗句应当流得更匀净，像这样。……”
埃龙在门口收住了脚。推开门的正是他——身后跟着两名鹰犬，本以为能在这里抓到德巴茨告发的那个家伙：那个压不垮的红花侠的一名党羽。跪在郎格女公民面前的那个男人，姿势毫无半点倾慕可言，正从容不迫地背诵戏文，一口地道的巴黎腔——这光景叫他一时有些发懵。
“这是怎么回事？”他粗声问，大步跨进屋，先瞪小姐，再瞪阿尔芒。
小姐小小地惊呼一声。
“哎呀，这不是埃龙公民吗！”她喊道，带着三分娇羞七分媚态，轻盈地跳起身，“玛丽姨妈怎么不通报一声？……她真是失礼；不过烤面包的日子她脾气坏，我连说她都不敢。您坐呀，埃龙公民？还有你，表哥，”她轻快地俯视着阿尔芒，添道，“求你别再摆那副傻姿势了。”
她举止里的那阵慌乱、颊上的红晕，尽可以归之于这趟不速之造访面前姑娘家的天然羞怯。埃龙被这场小戏彻底搞糊涂了——同他预想的满不是一回事，同他行使那可怕职分时惯见的场面也满不是一回事——在这位照旧天真烂漫、絮絮叨叨的小妇人面前，简直张口结舌。
“表哥，”她对阿尔芒说——他这会儿已从跪姿撑起身，“这位就是埃龙公民，我同你提过的。我这位贝尔霍姆表哥，”她又转向埃龙，“是刚从乡下来的，公民。他是奥尔良人，在已故高乃依公民的悲剧里演过主角。可是，唉！只怕他要把巴黎的看客，想得比善良的奥尔良人挑剔得多哩。公民，您方才听见他念那些美妙的诗句了吧？我说他简直在谋杀——对，谋杀——这大傻瓜！”

===Original===

Then only did it seem as if she realised that there was something amiss, that citizen Héron had come to visit her, not as an admirer of her talent who would wish to pay his respects to a successful actress, but as a person to be looked on with dread.
She gave a quaint, nervous little laugh, and murmured in the tones of a frightened child:
“La, citizen, how glum you look! I thought you had come to compliment me on my latest success. I saw you at the theatre last night, though you did not afterwards come to see me in the greenroom. Why! I had a regular ovation! Look at my flowers!” she added more gaily, pointing to several bouquets in vases about the room. “Citizen Danton brought me the violets himself, and citizen Santerre the narcissi, and that laurel wreath﻿—is it not charming?﻿—that was a tribute from citizen Robespierre himself.”
She was so artless, so simple, and so natural that Héron was completely taken off his usual mental balance. He had expected to find the usual setting to the dramatic episodes which he was wont to conduct﻿—screaming women, a man either at bay, sword in hand, or hiding in a linen cupboard or up a chimney.
Now everything puzzled him. De Batz﻿—he was quite sure﻿—had spoken of an Englishman, a follower of the Scarlet Pimpernel; every thinking French patriot knew that all the followers of the Scarlet Pimpernel were Englishmen with red hair and prominent teeth, whereas this man.﻿ ﻿…
Armand﻿—who deadly danger had primed in his improvised role﻿—was striding up and down the room declaiming with ever-varying intonations:

> “Joignez tous vos efforts contre un espoir si doux

Pour en venir à bout, c’est trop peu que de vous.”

===Chinese===

直到这时，她仿佛才觉出情形不对：埃龙公民的来访，不是仰慕者向一位成功的女演员致敬，而是作为一个令人胆寒的人物来的。
她神经质地轻笑一声，用受了惊的孩子的声调喃喃道：
“哎呀，公民，您绷着脸做什么！我还当您是来贺我最近的成功呢。昨晚我在戏院里瞧见您了，只是您后来没到后台来看我。真的！我可是博了个满堂彩！瞧我的花！”她更欢快地添道，指着屋里几瓶花束。“这束紫罗兰，是丹东公民亲手送的；水仙是索泰尔公民；还有那顶桂冠——多惹人爱吧？——那是罗伯斯庇尔公民亲自的敬意。”
她那样天真、那样单纯、那样自然，埃龙的心神被彻底带出了惯常的轨道。他原以为会撞见他那类拿手好戏的老一套布景——尖叫的女人，一个或持剑负隅、或躲进衣柜、或爬上烟囱的男人。
如今事事叫他犯疑。德巴茨——他记得千真万确——说的是一个英国人，红花侠的党羽；但凡动脑子的法兰西爱国者都知道，红花侠的党羽个个是红头发、暴牙齿的英国人；可眼前这位……
阿尔芒——致命的危险给他这即兴的角色上足了弦——正在屋里踱来踱去，抑扬顿挫地高诵：

> “Joignez tous vos efforts contre un espoir si doux

Pour en venir à bout, c’est trop peu que de vous.”

（合力同心，对付这甜蜜的希望吧——要成此事，光靠你们还嫌太少！）

===Original===

“No! no!” said mademoiselle impatiently; “you must not make that ugly pause midway in the last line: ‘*pour en venir à bout, c’est trop peu que de vous!*’ ”
She mimicked Armand’s diction so quaintly, imitating his stride, his awkward gesture, and his faulty phraseology with such funny exaggeration that Héron laughed in spite of himself.
“So that is a cousin from Orléans, is it?” he asked, throwing his lanky body into an armchair, which creaked dismally under his weight.
“Yes! a regular gaby﻿—what?” she said archly. “Now, citizen Héron, you must stay and take coffee with me. Aunt Marie will be bringing it in directly. Hector,” she added, turning to Armand, “come down from the clouds and ask Aunt Marie to be quick.”
This certainly was the first time in the whole of his experience that Héron had been asked to stay and drink coffee with the quarry he was hunting down. Mademoiselle’s innocent little ways, her desire for the prolongation of his visit, further addled his brain. De Batz had undoubtedly spoken of an Englishman, and the cousin from Orléans was certainly a Frenchman every inch of him.
Perhaps had the denunciation come from anyone else but de Batz, Héron might have acted and thought more circumspectly; but, of course, the chief agent of the Committee of General Security was more suspicious of the man from whom he took a heavy bribe than of anyone else in France. The thought had suddenly crossed his mind that mayhap de Batz had sent him on a fool’s errand in order to get him safely out of the way of the Temple prison at a given hour of the day.
The thought took shape, crystallised, caused him to see a rapid vision of de Batz sneaking into his lodgings and stealing his keys, the guard being slack, careless, inattentive, allowing the adventurer to pass barriers that should have been closed against all comers.
Now Héron was sure of it; it was all a conspiracy invented by de Batz. He had forgotten all about his theories that a man under arrest is always safer than a man that is free. Had his brain been quite normal, and not obsessed, as it always was now by thoughts of the Dauphin’s escape from prison, no doubt he would have been more suspicious of Armand, but all his worst suspicions were directed against de Batz. Armand seemed to him just a fool, an actor *quoi*? and so obviously not an Englishman.

===Chinese===

“不！不！”小姐不耐烦地说，“末一行中间不能那样难看地断气——‘要成此事，光靠你们还嫌太少！’”
她把阿尔芒的念白学得惟妙惟肖，迈他的台步、比他那僵硬的手势、学他那错误的咬字，夸张得这样好笑，埃龙不由自主笑出了声。
“这就是那位奥尔良来的表哥，是吧？”他问，把瘦长的身子扔进一把扶手椅，椅子在他身下凄惨地吱嘎作响。
“是啊！十足的大傻瓜——对不对？”她狡黠地说。“好了，埃龙公民，您一定要留下陪我喝杯咖啡。玛丽姨妈这就端来。埃克托，”她又转向阿尔芒，“从云端里下来吧，去催催玛丽姨妈。”
平生阅历里，这实在是头一回：埃龙被自己猎捕的对象留下来喝咖啡。小姐这副天真小模样、她巴望他多坐一会儿的心思，更搅浑了他的脑子。德巴茨分明说的是英国人，而这位奥尔良表哥，从头到脚是个法国人。
倘若告发出自旁人之口，埃龙行事或许会更周章些；可惜，治安委员会这位首席密探，对那个塞了他一大笔贿赂的人，比对法兰西任何人都多三分疑心。一个念头蓦地掠过他脑海：兴许德巴茨支他来跑这一趟傻差，就为着在一天的某个时辰，把他稳稳调离圣殿塔监狱。
这念头成形、凝固，叫他眼前闪过一幕：德巴茨溜进他的住处偷他的钥匙；守卫松懈、粗心、走神，放那冒险家过了那道本该对来人一律紧闭的关卡。
此刻埃龙笃定了：这全是德巴茨设下的圈套。他那套“人在押总比人在逃稳当”的理论，忘了个精光。他的脑子若还正常——不像如今这样，无时无刻不被王太子越狱的念头缠死——无疑会对阿尔芒多疑几分；可他最恶毒的怀疑，全冲着德巴茨去了。阿尔芒在他眼里不过是个傻子，一个戏子，是不是？明摆着不是英国人。

===Original===

He jumped to his feet, curtly declining mademoiselle’s offers of hospitality. He wanted to get away at once. Actors and actresses were always, by tacit consent of the authorities, more immune than the rest of the community. They provided the only amusement in the intervals of the horrible scenes around the scaffolds; they were irresponsible, harmless creatures who did not meddle in politics.
Jeanne the while was gaily prattling on, her luminous eyes fixed upon the all-powerful enemy, striving to read his thoughts, to understand what went on behind those cruel, prominent eyes, the chances that Armand had of safety and of life.
She knew, of course, that the visit was directed against Armand﻿—someone had betrayed him, that odious de Batz mayhap﻿—and she was fighting for Armand’s safety, for his life. Her armoury consisted of her presence of mind, her cool courage, her self-control; she used all these weapons for his sake, though at times she felt as if the strain on her nerves would snap the thread of life in her. The effort seemed more than she could bear.
But she kept up her part, rallying Héron for the shortness of his visit, begging him to tarry for another five minutes at least, throwing out﻿—with subtle feminine intuition﻿—just those very hints anent little Capet’s safety that were most calculated to send him flying back towards the Temple.
“I felt so honoured last night, citizen,” she said coquettishly, “that you even forgot little Capet in order to come and watch my debut as Célimène.”
“Forget him!” retorted Héron, smothering a curse, “I never forget the vermin. I must go back to him; there are too many cats nosing round my mouse. Good day to you, citizeness. I ought to have brought flowers, I know; but I am a busy man﻿—a harassed man.”
“*Je te crois*,” she said with a grave nod of the head; “but do come to the theatre tonight. I am playing Camille﻿—such a fine part! one of my greatest successes.”
“Yes, yes, I’ll come﻿—mayhap, mayhap﻿—but I’ll go now﻿—glad to have seen you, citizeness. Where does your cousin lodge?” he asked abruptly.
“Here,” she replied boldly, on the spur of the moment.
“Good. Let him report himself tomorrow morning at the Conciergerie, and get his certificate of safety. It is a new decree, and you should have one, too.”
“Very well, then. Hector and I will come together, and perhaps Aunt Marie will come too.

===Chinese===

他一跃而起，简慢地回绝了小姐的款留。他恨不得立刻脱身。男女伶人，凭着当局心照不宣的默许，向来比旁人多一层豁免。在断头台旁那一场场惨戏的间隙，他们是唯一的消遣；是不担责任、无害于人、不沾政治的生灵。
让娜这边依旧欢欢喜喜地絮语，明亮的眼睛却钉着这位生杀予夺的敌人，竭力读出他的心思，猜透那双凸出的狠眼背后转的是什么，掂量阿尔芒保全性命、逃出生天的成算。
她当然知道，这一趟是冲着阿尔芒来的——有人出卖了他，多半就是那个可憎的德巴茨——她在为阿尔芒的平安、为他的性命而战。她的武库，是她的镇定、她冷静的胆量、她的自持；为了他，这些武器她全用上了，尽管有几次，她觉得神经上的重负几乎要绷断她性命的线。这份撑持，像是超出她所能承受的。
可她把戏撑住了：笑埃龙坐不了片刻就走，求他至少再留五分钟，又凭着女性精细的直觉，恰到好处地抛出几句关于小卡佩安危的话——句句最能催他飞奔回圣殿塔去。
“昨晚我真觉得荣幸呢，公民，”她娇滴滴地说，“您竟把小卡佩都撂下了，来看我头一回演塞利梅娜。”
“撂下！”埃龙憋着一句咒骂回道，“那小畜生我从没忘。我得回他那儿去；围着耗子嗅的猫太多了。祝您日安，女公民。我本该带花来的，我知道；可我是个忙人——一个焦头烂额的人。”
“这我信，”她郑重地点点头；“可今晚一定要来戏院。我演卡米耶——多好的角色！我最叫座的戏之一。”
“好，好，我来——兴许，兴许——可我现在就得走——见了您很高兴，女公民。你表哥住哪儿？”他突然问。
“就这儿。”她灵机一动，大胆答道。
“好。叫他明早到古监狱去报到，领一份安分证。这是新法令；你本人也该领一份。”
“那就这么说定。我和埃克托一块儿去，兴许玛丽姨妈也去。

===Original===

Don’t send us to *maman* guillotine yet awhile, citizen,” she said lightly; “you will never get such another Camille, nor yet so good a Célimène.”
She was gay, artless to the last. She accompanied Héron to the door herself, chaffing him about his escort.
“You are an aristo, citizen,” she said, gazing with well-feigned admiration on the two sleuthhounds who stood in wait in the anteroom; “it makes me proud to see so many citizens at my door. Come and see me play Camille﻿—come tonight, and don’t forget the greenroom door﻿—it will always be kept invitingly open for you.”
She bobbed him a curtsey, and he walked out, closely followed by his two men; then at last she closed the door behind them. She stood there for a while, her ear glued against the massive panels, listening for their measured tread down the oak staircase. At last it rang more sharply against the flagstones of the courtyard below; then she was satisfied that they had gone, and went slowly back to the boudoir.

===Chinese===

先别急着把咱们送去断头台妈妈那儿，公民，”她轻快地说，“这么好的卡米耶，再好的塞利梅娜，您可找不着第二份了。”
她欢快，天真到最后一刻。她亲自送埃龙到门口，还打趣他带的随从。
“您真是位贵族老爷，公民，”她说，装出一脸倾慕，望着门厅里候着的两名鹰犬；“我门口站着这么多公民，真叫我脸上有光。来看我演卡米耶吧——今晚就来，别忘了后台的门——永远为您敞开着，透着热乎气儿。”
她飞快屈膝行了个礼；他走出去，两名随从紧随；她终于把门在他们身后关上。她立了一阵，耳朵贴着厚实的门板，听那整齐的脚步踏下一级级橡木楼梯。末了，脚步声更清脆地敲在楼下院子的石板上；她这才确信他们走了，慢慢踱回闺房。

## X Shadows / X 阴影
===Original===

The tension on her nerves relaxed; there was the inevitable reaction. Her knees were shaking under her, and she literally staggered into the room.
But Armand was already near her, down on both his knees this time, his arms clasping the delicate form that swayed like the slender stems of narcissi in the breeze.
“Oh! you must go out of Paris at once﻿—at once,” she said through sobs which no longer would be kept back.
“He’ll return﻿—I know that he will return﻿—and you will not be safe until you are back in England.”
But he could not think of himself or of anything in the future. He had forgotten Héron, Paris, the world; he could only think of her.
“I owe my life to you!” he murmured. “Oh, how beautiful you are﻿—how brave! How I love you!”
It seemed that he had always loved her, from the moment that first in his boyish heart he had set up an ideal to worship, and then, last night, in the box of the theatre﻿—he had his back turned toward the stage, and was ready to go﻿—her voice had called him back; it had held him spellbound; her voice, and also her eyes.﻿ ﻿… He did not know then that it was Love which then and there had enchained him. Oh, how foolish he had been! for now he knew that he had loved her with all his might, with all his soul, from the very instant that his eyes had rested upon her.
He babbled along﻿—incoherently﻿—in the intervals of covering her hands and the hem of her gown with kisses. He stooped right down to the ground and kissed the arch of her instep; he had become a devotee worshipping at the shrine of his saint, who had performed a great and a wonderful miracle.
Armand the idealist had found his ideal in a woman. That was the great miracle which the woman herself had performed for him. He found in her all that he had admired most, all that he had admired in the leader who hitherto had been the only personification of his ideal. But Jeanne possessed all those qualities which had roused his enthusiasm in the noble hero whom he revered. Her pluck, her ingenuity, her calm devotion which had averted the threatened danger from him!
What had he done that she should have risked her own sweet life for his sake?
But Jeanne did not know. She could not tell. Her nerves now were somewhat unstrung, and the tears that always came so readily to her eyes flowed quite unchecked.

===Chinese===

绷紧的神经一松，随之而来的是必不可免的反激。她双膝发颤，实实在在是踉跄着挪进屋来的。
可阿尔芒早已抢到她身边，这一回双膝都跪了下去，两臂搂住那纤细的身躯——那身躯攦晃着，像微风中细细的水仙花茎。
“啊！你必须马上离开巴黎——马上，”她说，再也忍不住的呜咽从话里透出来。
“他会回来的——我知道他一定回来——你一天不回英国，就一天不得安全。”
可他顾不上自己，也顾不上往后的任何事。埃龙、巴黎、整个世界，他都忘了；他心里只有她。
“我的命是你救的！”他喃喃道，“啊，你多美——多勇敢！我多爱你！”
仿佛他从来就爱着她：打少年时代，他头一回在那颗心里竖起一尊偶像来供奉时，就爱着她；而后，是昨夜，在剧院的包厢里——他背朝着戏台，正要动身走——是她的嗓音唤回了他；那嗓音把他钉在了原地；还有她的眼睛。……那时他还不知道，把他当场锁住的，正是爱神。啊，他多么傻！因为如今他知道了：打他的目光第一次落在她身上那一瞬起，他就倾尽全身力气、倾尽整个灵魂地爱着她。
他絮絮叨叨——语无伦次——说几句，便把吻落满她的双手和裙裾。他一直俯身到地上，吻她足背的弓弯；他已成了一名信徒，在自家圣徒的神龛前礼拜——她行了一个伟大而奇妙的奇迹。
理想主义者阿尔芒，在一个女人身上找到了他的理想。这便是这女人亲自为他行的伟大奇迹。他发现，他最崇敬的一切——他一向只在首领身上见到的一切，那唯一把他理想化作血肉的人——在她身上应有尽有。让娜具备着他在那位敬仰的高贵英雄身上为之热血沸腾的全部品质：她的胆气，她的急智，她那份临危不乱、为他挡开险祸的赤诚！
他何德何能，值得她拿自己甜蜜的性命为他一搏？
让娜自己也不知道。她说不上来。她的神经这时已有些支撑不住，那双说来就来的眼泪，此刻流了个畅快。

===Original===

She could not very well move, for he held her knees imprisoned in his arms, but she was quite content to remain like this, and to yield her hands to him so that he might cover them with kisses.
Indeed, she did not know at what precise moment love for him had been born in her heart. Last night, perhaps﻿ ﻿… she could not say﻿ ﻿… but when they parted she felt that she must see him again﻿ ﻿… and then today﻿ ﻿… perhaps it was the scent of the violets﻿ ﻿… they were so exquisitely sweet﻿ ﻿… perhaps it was his enthusiasm and his talk about England﻿ ﻿… but when Héron came she knew that she must save Armand’s life at all cost﻿ ﻿… that she would die if they dragged him away to prison.
Thus these two children philosophised, trying to understand the mystery of the birth of Love. But they were only children; they did not really understand. Passion was sweeping them off their feet, because a common danger had bound them irrevocably to one another. The womanly instinct to save and to protect had given the young girl strength to bear a difficult part, and now she loved him for the dangers from which she had rescued him, and he loved her because she had risked her life for him.
The hours sped on; there was so much to say, so much that was exquisite to listen to. The shades of evening were gathering fast; the room, with its pale-toned hangings and faded tapestries, was sinking into the arms of gloom. Aunt Marie was no doubt too terrified to stir out of her kitchen; she did not bring the lamps, but the darkness suited Armand’s mood, and Jeanne was glad that the gloaming effectually hid the perpetual blush in her cheeks.
In the evening air the dying flowers sent their heady fragrance around. Armand was intoxicated with the perfume of violets that clung to Jeanne’s fingers, with the touch of her satin gown that brushed his cheek, with the murmur of her voice that quivered through her tears.
No noise from the ugly outer world reached this secluded spot. In the tiny square outside a street lamp had been lighted, and its feeble rays came peeping in through the lace curtains at the window. They caught the dainty silhouette of the young girl, playing with the loose tendrils of her hair around her forehead, and outlining with a thin band of light the contour of neck and shoulder, making the satin of her gown shimmer with an opalescent glow.
Armand rose from his knees.

===Chinese===

她动弹不得——她的双膝被他锁在臂弯里——可她也十分甘愿就这样待着，把手交给他，任他把吻落满。
真的，她也说不清对他的爱究竟是哪一刻在心里生出来的。也许是昨夜……说不好……只是分手的时候，她只觉得非再见他不可……再后来是今天……也许是紫罗兰的香气……那花香甜得那样雅致……也许是他那股热诚，他讲的那些英国……可等埃龙进门的那一刻，她知道了：她必须不惜一切救阿尔芒的命……他们若把他拖进监狱，她也活不成。
这两个孩子就这样探讨起哲理来，想参透爱神降生的玄机。可他们终究只是孩子；并没有真懂。是激情把他们卷倒了脚跟，因为共同的危难已把两人无可挽回地缚在了一处。救护与卫护的女人天性，给了这少女扮好一场难戏的气力；如今，她为他从危难中脱身而爱他，他为她舍过性命而爱她。
钟点飞也似的过去；要说的话那么多，那么好听的那么多。暮色四合得很快；挂着浅色帷幔、陈旧花毯的屋子，正一寸寸沉入幽暗的怀抱。玛丽姨妈多半吓得不敢单从厨房里挪窝；她没有送灯来——可昏暗正合阿尔芒的心境，让娜也乐得让这暮色，把她脸上那褪不下去的红晕遮个严实。
暮气里，将残的花吐着醉人的芬芳。阿尔芒醉了——醉在萦绕让娜指尖的紫罗兰香里，醉在拂过他脸颊的缎裙的触感里，醉在她那隔着泪水颤动呢喃的嗓音里。
外间那个丑恶世界的声响，一丝也透不进这方清幽之地。外面的小广场上，一盏街灯点起来了，微弱的光线从窗前的花边帘缝里窥进来，描出少女娇俏的侧影——她正拨弄着额前散落的发卷——又用一线细细的光勾出她颈项和肩头的轮廓，把她裙上的缎子照出一层珠光，莹莹流转。
阿尔芒从膝头站起身。

===Original===

Her eyes were calling to him, her lips were ready to yield.
“*Tu m’aimes?*” he whispered.
And like a tired child she sank upon his breast.
He kissed her hair, her eyes, her lips; her skin was fragrant as the flowers of spring, the tears on her cheeks glistened like morning dew.
---
Aunt Marie came in at last, carrying the lamp. She found them sitting side by side, like two children, hand in hand, mute with the eloquence which comes from boundless love. They were under a spell, forgetting even that they lived, knowing nothing except that they loved.
The lamp broke the spell, and Aunt Marie’s still trembling voice:
“Oh, my dear! how did you manage to rid yourself of those brutes?”
But she asked no other question, even when the lamp showed up quite clearly the glowing cheeks of Jeanne and the ardent eyes of Armand. In her heart, long since atrophied, there were a few memories, carefully put away in a secret cell, and those memories caused the old woman to understand.
Neither Jeanne nor Armand noticed what she did; the spell had been broken, but the dream lingered on; they did not see Aunt Marie putting the room tidy, and then quietly tiptoeing out by the door.
But through the dream, reality was struggling for recognition. After Armand had asked for the hundredth time: “*Tu m’aimes?*” and Jeanne for the hundredth time had replied mutely with her eyes, her fears for him suddenly returned.
Something had awakened her from her trance﻿—a heavy footstep, mayhap, in the street below, the distant roll of a drum, or only the clash of steel saucepans in Aunt Marie’s kitchen. But suddenly Jeanne was alert, and with her alertness came terror for the beloved.
“Your life,” she said﻿—for he had called her his life just then, “your life﻿—and I was forgetting that it is still in danger﻿ ﻿… your dear, your precious life!”
“Doubly dear now,” he replied, “since I owe it to you.”
“Then I pray you, I entreat you, guard it well for my sake﻿—make all haste to leave Paris﻿ ﻿… oh, this I beg of you!” she continued more earnestly, seeing the look of demur in his eyes; “every hour you spend in it brings danger nearer to your door.”
“I could not leave Paris while you are here.”
“But I am safe here,” she urged; “quite, quite safe, I assure you. I am only a poor actress, and the Government takes no heed of us mimes. Men must be amused, even between the intervals of killing one another.

===Chinese===

她的眼睛在唤他，她的唇正待俯就。
“*你爱我么？*（*Tu m'aimes?*）”他悄声问。
她像个倦透的孩子，偎进他的胸怀。
他吻她的发、她的眼睛、她的唇；她的肌肤馥郁如春花，颊上的泪莹莹闪动，像朝露。
---
玛丽姨妈终于端着灯进来了。她见他们并肩坐着，像两个孩子，手牵着手，沉默里自有一种无边深情才有的雄辩。他们还在魔咒里，连自己的存在都忘了，只知道彼此相爱。
灯光破了魔咒，还有玛丽姨妈犹自发颤的声音：
“啊，亲爱的！你到底是怎么打发掉那些畜生的？”
可她再没问第二句——哪怕灯光把让娜灼灼的面颊、阿尔芒灼灼的眼睛照得清清楚楚。她那颗久已枯萎的心里，藏着几桩旧事，仔细收在一间密室中；正是这几桩旧事，叫老妇人懂了。
让娜和阿尔芒谁也没留意她做了什么；魔咒虽破，梦境犹存；他们没有见玛丽姨妈收拾屋子，又踮着脚悄悄退出门去。
可穿过梦境，现实挣扎着要人认它。阿尔芒第一百次问过“*你爱我么？*”，让娜第一百次用眼睛默然作答之后，她对他的忧惧突然又回来了。
不知是什么把她从迷离中惊醒——也许是街上一声沉重的脚步，也许是远处一阵鼓响，也许不过是玛丽姨妈厨房里钢锅相碰。可让娜蓦地警觉起来，警觉一至，对爱人的恐怖也随之而至。
“你的性命，”她说——方才他正唤她作他的性命，“你的性命——我竟忘了它还在险里……你亲爱的、你宝贵的性命！”
“如今是双倍的亲了，”他答，“因为它承你之情。”
“那我求你，我恳求你，看在我的份上把它保重好——快快离开巴黎……啊，我求你了！”见他眼里浮起迟疑，她又说得更恳切了；“你在这城里多待一个钟头，危险就朝你门口多逼近一步。”
“你在这里，我走不了。”
“可我在这里是安全的，”她急急劝道，“十分、十分安全，我向你担保。我不过是个穷戏子，政府从不把我们这些做戏的放在心上。男人们总要寻开心——哪怕在互相杀戮的间隙里。

===Original===

Indeed, indeed, I should be far safer here now, waiting quietly for awhile, while you make preparations to go﻿ ﻿… My hasty departure at this moment would bring disaster on us both.”
There was logic in what she said. And yet how could he leave her? now that he had found this perfect woman﻿—this realisation of his highest ideals, how could he go and leave her in this awful Paris, with brutes like Héron forcing their hideous personality into her sacred presence, threatening that very life he would gladly give his own to keep inviolate?
“Listen, sweetheart,” he said after awhile, when presently reason struggled back for first place in his mind. “Will you allow me to consult with my chief, with the Scarlet Pimpernel, who is in Paris at the present moment? I am under his orders; I could not leave France just now. My life, my entire person are at his disposal. I and my comrades are here under his orders, for a great undertaking which he has not yet unfolded to us, but which I firmly believe is framed for the rescue of the Dauphin from the Temple.”
She gave an involuntary exclamation of horror.
“No, no!” she said quickly and earnestly; “as far as you are concerned, Armand, that has now become an impossibility. Someone has betrayed you, and you are henceforth a marked man. I think that odious de Batz had a hand in Héron’s visit of this afternoon. We succeeded in putting these spies off the scent, but only for a moment﻿ ﻿… within a few hours﻿—less perhaps﻿—Héron will repent him of his carelessness; he’ll come back﻿—I know that he will come back. He may leave me, personally, alone; but he will be on your track; he’ll drag you to the Conciergerie to report yourself, and there your true name and history are bound to come to light. If you succeed in evading him, he will still be on your track. If the Scarlet Pimpernel keeps you in Paris now, your death will be at his door.”
Her voice had become quite hard and trenchant as she said these last words; womanlike, she was already prepared to hate the man whose mysterious personality she had hitherto admired, now that the life and safety of Armand appeared to depend on the will of that elusive hero.
“You must not be afraid for me, Jeanne,” he urged. “The Scarlet Pimpernel cares for all his followers; he would never allow me to run unnecessary risks.”

===Chinese===

真的，真的，此刻我留在这里安安静静等一阵，反倒安全得多，正好等你打点动身……我这个节骨眼上仓皇出走，倒要把大祸引到咱们两个头上。”
她的话在理。可他怎么离得开她？如今他既寻到了这完美的女人——他至高理想的化身——他怎么能走，把她摌在这个可怕的巴黎，任凭埃龙那样的畜生把他们狰狞的嘴脸挤进她圣洁的所在，威胁那条性命——那条他甘愿拿自己性命去保全、不容一丝玷污的性命？
“听我说，亲爱的，”过了一阵，理智终于在他心里抢回头把交椅，他开口道，“你可容我去请教一下我的首领——红花侠？他眼下正在巴黎。我受他节制；眼下不能离开法国。我的性命、我的全副身心，都听他调遣。我和我的同伴们奉命在此，为着一桩大业——他还没有向我们交底，但我坚信，那大业就是为了把王太子从坦普尔狱里救出去。”
她不由得失声惊呼。
“不，不！”她又急又恳切地说，“阿尔芒，就你而言，如今这已不可能了。有人把你出卖了，从今往后你就是个被记了名的人。我想，今天下午埃龙那一趟，那个可憎的德巴茨就有一手。我们固然把那群密探引开了嗅迹——可只骗得了一时……不出几个钟头——兴许还用不了——埃龙就会懊悔自己的大意；他会回来的——我知道他一定回来。他本人兴许不来找我；可他必定盯你的梢；他会把你拖到古监狱去登记报到，在那里，你的真名实姓、你的来历，必定露馅。你便躲过了这一遭，他还是咬着你不放。红花侠此刻若还把你留在巴黎，你的死就要记在他的账上。”
说到末几句，她的嗓音已经相当冷硬锋利；女人家的性子就是这样——阿尔芒的性命安危，如今既然系在那位来去无踪的英雄一念之间，那么此人神秘的人格，纵然她先前百般倾慕，如今也已恨得起来。
“你别为我害怕，让娜，”他劝道，“红花侠爱惜他每一个部下；他绝不会容我去冒无谓的险。”

===Original===

She was unconvinced, almost jealous now of his enthusiasm for that unknown man. Already she had taken full possession of Armand; she had purchased his life, and he had given her his love. She would share neither treasure with that nameless leader who held Armand’s allegiance.
“It is only for a little while, sweetheart,” he reiterated again and again. “I could not, anyhow, leave Paris whilst I feel that you are here, maybe in danger. The thought would be horrible. I should go mad if I had to leave you.”
Then he talked again of England, of his life there, of the happiness and peace that were in store for them both.
“We will go to England together,” he whispered, “and there we will be happy together, you and I. We will have a tiny house among the Kentish hills, and its walls will be covered with honeysuckle and roses. At the back of the house there will be an orchard, and in May, when the fruit-blossom is fading and soft spring breezes blow among the trees, showers of sweet-scented petals will envelop us as we walk along, falling on us like fragrant snow. You will come, sweetheart, will you not?”
“If you still wish it, Armand,” she murmured.
Still wish it! He would gladly go tomorrow if she would come with him. But, of course, that could not be arranged. She had her contract to fulfil at the theatre, then there would be her house and furniture to dispose of, and there was Aunt Marie.﻿ ﻿… But, of course, Aunt Marie would come too.﻿ ﻿… She thought that she could get away some time before the spring; and he swore that he could not leave Paris until she came with him.
It seemed a terrible deadlock, for she could not bear to think of him alone in those awful Paris streets, where she knew that spies would always be tracking him. She had no illusions as to the impression which she had made on Héron; she knew that it could only be a momentary one, and that Armand would henceforth be in daily, hourly danger.
At last she promised him that she would take the advice of his chief; they would both be guided by what he said. Armand would confide in him tonight, and if it could be arranged she would hurry on her preparations and, mayhap, be ready to join him in a week.
“In the meanwhile, that cruel man must not risk your dear life,” she said. “Remember, Armand, your life belongs to me. Oh, I could hate him for the love you bear him!”
“Sh﻿—sh﻿—sh!” he said earnestly.

===Chinese===

她不信，此刻对他敬慕那个无名之人，几乎生出醋意。她已把阿尔芒整个儿占下了；他的性命是她买下的，他的爱情是他献上的。这两件宝，哪一件她也不肯同那个无名的首领分享——阿尔芒的忠心，凭什么叫他占着？
“不过是一小阵子，亲爱的，”他翻来覆去地说，“只要你还在这里、还可能有险，我无论如何走不出巴黎。那念头太可怕了。叫我离开你，我会发疯的。”
接着他又讲起英国，讲他在那里的生活，讲等着他们两个的幸福与安宁。
“我们一起去英国，”他悄声说，“在那里我们一起过日子，你和我，快快活活。我们在肯特群山里安一座小宅，宅墙上爬满忍冬和蔷薇。宅子后面要有一座果园；到了五月，果树的花谢了，柔和的春风在树间吹拂，我们一路走，一阵阵甜香的花瓣便兒头兒脑裹住我们，落在身上，像芳香的雪。你会来的，亲爱的，会不会？”
“只要你还是这样盼着，阿尔芒。”她喃喃说。
还是这样盼着！只要她肯同去，他明天就走才好。可这自然一时办不成。她在剧院还有合同要履行；还有她的宅子、家具要处置；还有玛丽姨妈。……不过，玛丽姨妈自然也一起去。……她想着，开春以前总能抽身；而他赌咒发誓，她不同行，他绝不离开巴黎。
这简直是个可怕的僵局：她怎么忍心想他独自一个，走在那些可怕的巴黎街上——她知道那里永远有密探跟踪他。她在埃龙心里留下了个什么印象，她不存幻想；她知道那至多骗得一时，阿尔芒从今往后，是日日、时时在险中的。
末了她答应他：她愿听从他首领的主意；两人都凭那位的话行事。阿尔芒今夜就去同他商量；若办得成，她就赶紧张罗，兴许一星期后就动身去会他。
“在那之前，那个狠心人不得拿你亲爱的性命冒险，”她说，“记住，阿尔芒，你的性命是我的。啊，你敬他爱他，我就恨他！”
“嘘——嘘——嘘！”他正色道。

===Original===

“Dear heart, you must not speak like that of the man whom, next to your perfect self, I love most upon earth.”
“You think of him more than of me. I shall scarce live until I know that you are safely out of Paris.”
Though it was horrible to part, yet it was best, perhaps, that he should go back to his lodgings now, in case Héron sent his spies back to her door, and since he meant to consult with his chief. She had a vague hope that if the mysterious hero was indeed the noble-hearted man whom Armand represented him to be, surely he would take compassion on the anxiety of a sorrowing woman, and release the man she loved from bondage.
This thought pleased her and gave her hope. She even urged Armand now to go.
“When may I see you tomorrow?” he asked.
“But it will be so dangerous to meet,” she argued.
“I must see you. I could not live through the day without seeing you.”
“The theatre is the safest place.”
“I could not wait till the evening. May I not come here?”
“No, no. Héron’s spies may be about.”
“Where then?”
She thought it over for a moment.
“At the stage-door of the theatre at one o’clock,” she said at last. “We shall have finished rehearsal. Slip into the guichet of the concierge. I will tell him to admit you, and send my dresser to meet you there; she will bring you along to my room, where we shall be undisturbed for at least half an hour.”
He had perforce to be content with that, though he would so much rather have seen her here again, where the faded tapestries and soft-toned hangings made such a perfect background for her delicate charm. He had every intention of confiding in Blakeney, and of asking his help for getting Jeanne out of Paris as quickly as may be.
Thus this perfect hour was past; the most pure, the fullest of joy that these two young people were ever destined to know. Perhaps they felt within themselves the consciousness that their great love would rise anon to yet greater, fuller perfection when Fate had crowned it with his halo of sorrow. Perhaps, too, it was that consciousness that gave to their kisses now the solemnity of a last farewell.

===Chinese===

“心肝儿，除了你这天仙化人，天底下我最爱的就是他，你可不能这样说他。”
“你惦记他比惦记我还多。一天不听说你安安稳稳出了巴黎，我一天活不好。”
分手固然锥心，可此刻就回他的住处，或许顶好——一来防埃龙把密探再派回她门口，二来他也要去找首领商量。她怀着一星朦胧的希望：那位神秘英雄若真是阿尔芒所说的那样一副侠骨柔肠，那么，看在一个愁苦妇人这份焦灼的份上，他总会动一动恻隐，放她所爱的人脱出羁縻。
这个念头叫她宽心，给了她希望。她甚至催阿尔芒这就走。
“明天什么时候能见你？”他问。
“可见面太危险了。”她争道。
“我必须见你。见不着你，这一天我熬不过去。”
“剧院顶安全。”
“我等不到晚上。就不能来这里么？”
“不行，不行。埃龙的密探兴许就在左近。”
“那在哪儿？”
她想了一想。
“一点钟，剧院的后台门，”她终于说，“那时排戏刚完。你溜进门房的传达小屋里。我会嘱咐他放你进来，再打发我的梳妆娘去接你；她领你到我的房间，那儿至少半个钟头没人搅扰。”
他也只得依了——虽说他多么宁愿再到这里来见她：这满屋陈旧的花毯、柔和的帷幔，衬她那份娇柔的风致，天造地设。他打定了主意要向布莱克尼推心置腹，求他相助，尽早把让娜送出巴黎。
这完美的一个钟头就这样过去了；这两个年轻人此生命里注定的，便是最纯净、最圆满的欢乐。也许他们心里已隐隐自知：待命运为这份大爱加冕——戴上那顶忧患的光环——它还要升到更高、更圆满的境界。也许，正是这份自知，给了他们此刻的亲吻一种诀别般的庄严。

## XI The League of the Scarlet Pimpernel / XI 红花侠同盟
===Original===

Armand never could say definitely afterwards whither he went when he left the Square du Roule that evening. No doubt he wandered about the streets for some time in an absent, mechanical way, paying no heed to the passersby, none to the direction in which he was going.
His mind was full of Jeanne, her beauty, her courage, her attitude in face of the hideous bloodhound who had come to pollute that charming old-world boudoir by his loathsome presence. He recalled every word she uttered, every gesture she made.
He was a man in love for the first time﻿—wholly, irremediably in love.
I suppose that it was the pangs of hunger that first recalled him to himself. It was close on eight o’clock now, and he had fed on his imaginings﻿—first on anticipation, then on realisation, and lastly on memory﻿—during the best part of the day. Now he awoke from his daydream to find himself tired and hungry, but fortunately not very far from that quarter of Paris where food is easily obtainable.
He was somewhere near the Madeleine﻿—a quarter he knew well. Soon he saw in front of him a small eating-house which looked fairly clean and orderly. He pushed open its swing-door, and seeing an empty table in a secluded part of the room, he sat down and ordered some supper.
The place made no impression upon his memory. He could not have told you an hour later where it was situated, who had served him, what he had eaten, or what other persons were present in the dining-room at the time that he himself entered it.
Having eaten, however, he felt more like his normal self﻿—more conscious of his actions. When he finally left the eating-house, he realised, for instance, that it was very cold﻿—a fact of which he had for the past few hours been totally unaware. The snow was falling in thin close flakes, and a biting northeasterly wind was blowing those flakes into his face and down his collar. He wrapped his cloak tightly around him. It was a good step yet to Blakeney’s lodgings, where he knew that he was expected.
He struck quickly into the Rue St. Honoré, avoiding the great open places where the grim horrors of this magnificent city in revolt against civilisation were displayed in all their grim nakedness﻿—on the Place de la Révolution the guillotine, on the Carrousel the open-air camps of workers under the lash of slave-drivers more cruel than the uncivilised brutes of the Far West.

===Chinese===

那天傍晚离开鲁勒广场之后，阿尔芒后来始终说不清自己究竟去了哪里。无疑，他失魂落魄地在街上转了好一阵，路人也好，去向也好，一概不曾留意。
他满心都是让娜：她的美，她的胆气，她在那条丑恶的警犬面前的那份气度——那畜生闯进她那间可爱的旧式闺阁，一身的腌臜玷污了满室的清雅。她说的每一句话、做的每一个动作，他一一回味。
他这是头一回恋爱——彻头彻尾、无可救药地恋爱了。
我想，先把他唤回自身的，是饥饿的绞痛。此时已近八点；大半个白天，他都以遐想为食——起先是憧憬，继而是实历，末了是追忆——此刻从白日梦里醒来，只觉又倦又饿，幸好离巴黎那个寻吃食容易的地段，已没有多远。
他正走在玛德莱娜教堂左近——这一带他熟。不多时，前面出现一家小饭铺，看着还算干净齐整。他推开弹簧门，见里间僻静处有张空桌，便坐下叫了些晚饭。
那地方在他记忆里没留下半点印子。一个钟头后，你要问他铺子坐落何方、何人伺候、吃的是什么、进店时堂中还有哪些食客，他一样也答不上来。
然而饭一下肚，他倒回了几分本色——对自己的举动有了知觉。比如，最后走出饭铺时，他发觉天很冷——这情形前几个钟头里他竟全无所觉。雪下得又细又密，凛冽的东北风把雪片刮上他的脸、灌进他的领口。他紧紧裹住外套。到布莱克尼的寓所还有好一段路；他知道，那边正等着他。
他快步折入圣奥诺雷街，绕开那些开阔的大广场——这座反抗文明的煌煌都城，正把它狰狞的恐怖赤条条陈列在那些地方：革命广场上，断头台；卡鲁塞尔广场上，露天苦役营，赶工的苦力在监工的皮鞭下——那狠劲，比远西蛮荒之地的畜生还要残忍。

===Original===

And Armand had to think of Jeanne in the midst of all these horrors. She was still a petted actress today, but who could tell if on the morrow the terrible law of the “suspect” would not reach her in order to drag her before a tribunal that knew no mercy, and whose sole justice was a condemnation?
The young man hurried on; he was anxious to be among his own comrades, to hear his chief’s pleasant voice, to feel assured that by all the sacred laws of friendship Jeanne henceforth would become the special care of the Scarlet Pimpernel and his league.
Blakeney lodged in a small house situated on the Quai de l’École, at the back of St. Germain l’Auxerrois, from whence he had a clear and uninterrupted view across the river, as far as the irregular block of buildings of the Châtelet prison and the house of Justice.
The same tower-clock that two centuries ago had tolled the signal for the massacre of the Huguenots was even now striking nine. Armand slipped through the half-open porte-cochère, crossed the narrow dark courtyard, and ran up two flights of winding stone stairs. At the top of these, a door on his right allowed a thin streak of light to filtrate between its two folds. An iron bell handle hung beside it; Armand gave it a pull.
Two minutes later he was amongst his friends. He heaved a great sigh of content and relief. The very atmosphere here seemed to be different. As far as the lodging itself was concerned, it was as bare, as devoid of comfort as those sort of places﻿—so-called *chambres garnies*﻿—usually were in these days. The chairs looked rickety and uninviting, the sofa was of black horsehair, the carpet was threadbare, and in places in actual holes; but there was a certain something in the air which revealed, in the midst of all this squalor, the presence of a man of fastidious taste.
To begin with, the place was spotlessly clean; the stove, highly polished, gave forth a pleasing warm glow, even whilst the window, slightly open, allowed a modicum of fresh air to enter the room. In a rough earthenware jug on the table stood a large bunch of Christmas roses, and to the educated nostril the slight scent of perfumes that hovered in the air was doubly pleasing after the fetid air of the narrow streets.
Sir Andrew Ffoulkes was there, also my Lord Tony, and Lord Hastings. They greeted Armand with wholehearted cheeriness.
“Where is Blakeney?”

===Chinese===

置身这重重恐怖之间，阿尔芒不能不想让娜。今日她还是个众人捧着的名角；可谁保得准，明天那部可怕的“嫌疑犯”法不会伸到她头上，把她拖上一座不知慈悲为何物的法庭——那法庭唯一的公道，就是判罪？
年轻人加紧了脚步；他急于回到自己的伙伴中间，听首领那悦耳的声音，急于确信：凭友谊的一切神圣律法，让娜从今往后，将成为红花侠和他同盟特别的守护对象。
布莱克尼下榻在埃科勒滨河道上的一座小宅，背倚圣日耳曼欧塞罗瓦教堂；从那里隔河望去，视野空阔无碍，一直望到夏特莱监狱和司法府那片参差的楼群。
两百年前敲响屠杀胡格诺教徒信号的那座塔钟，此刻正报九点。阿尔芒从半开的车马门里侧身溜入，穿过窄而黑的院子，蹬上两段螺旋石梯。梯顶右手一扇门，两扇门板间漏出一线灯光。门边垂着一只铁铃拉手；阿尔芒拉了一把。
两分钟后，他已置身朋友们中间。他长舒了一口气，又满足、又释然。连这里的空气都仿佛是另一重天地。单论寓所本身，它同眼下那些所谓的*带家具客房*（*chambres garnies*）一样空疏、一样无舒适可言：椅子摇摇晃晃、没人想坐，沙发是黑马鬃的，地毯磨得露了线，有几处实实在在破了洞；可空气里有那么一股说不出的东西，透出在这寒酸中间，住着一位口味挑剔的人物。
首先，屋里一尘不染；炉子擦得锃亮，吐着一圈可人的暖光；窗户又微微开着，放进些许新鲜空气。桌上一只粗陶罐里，插着一大束圣诞玫瑰；而在识货的鼻子里，那萦绕室中的淡淡香气——经过外头窄街的浊气之后——加倍地沁人心脾。
安德鲁·福尔克斯爵士在座，还有托尼勋爵和黑斯廷斯勋爵。他们热热络络地招呼阿尔芒。
“布莱克尼呢？”

===Original===

asked the young man as soon as he had shaken his friends by the hand.
“Present!” came in loud, pleasant accents from the door of an inner room on the right.
And there he stood under the lintel of the door, the man against whom was raised the giant hand of an entire nation﻿—the man for whose head the revolutionary government of France would gladly pay out all the savings of its Treasury﻿—the man whom human bloodhounds were tracking, hot on the scent﻿—for whom the nets of a bitter revenge and relentless reprisals were constantly being spread.
Was he unconscious of it, or merely careless? His closest friend, Sir Andrew Ffoulkes, could not say. Certain it is that, as he now appeared before Armand, picturesque as ever in perfectly tailored clothes, with priceless lace at throat and wrists, his slender fingers holding an enamelled snuffbox and a handkerchief of delicate cambric, his whole personality that of a dandy rather than a man of action, it seemed impossible to connect him with the foolhardy escapades which had set one nation glowing with enthusiasm and another clamouring for revenge.
But it was the magnetism that emanated from him that could not be denied; the light that now and then, swift as summer lightning, flashed out from the depths of the blue eyes usually veiled by heavy, lazy lids, the sudden tightening of firm lips, the setting of the square jaw, which in a moment﻿—but only for the space of a second﻿—transformed the entire face, and revealed the born leader of men.
Just now there was none of that in the debonair, easygoing man of the world who advanced to meet his friend. Armand went quickly up to him, glad to grasp his hand, slightly troubled with remorse, no doubt, at the recollection of his adventure of today. It almost seemed to him that from beneath his half-closed lids Blakeney had shot a quick inquiring glance upon him. The quick flash seemed to light up the young man’s soul from within, and to reveal it, naked, to his friend.
It was all over in a moment, and Armand thought that mayhap his conscience had played him a trick: there was nothing apparent in him﻿—of this he was sure﻿—that could possibly divulge his secret just yet.
“I am rather late, I fear,” he said. “I wandered about the streets in the late afternoon and lost my way in the dark. I hope I have not kept you all waiting.”

===Chinese===

年轻人同各位握过手，头一句就问这个。
“到！”右首里间门口，传来一声响亮悦耳的应答。
他就立在门楣底下——这个举国之力向他压来的人；这个法国革命政府情愿倾尽国库储蓄来买他头颅的人；这个人类的猎犬们正循着热嗅追踪的人；苦味的复仇与无情的清算，正为他张着一面又一面不断的罗网。
他自己浑然不觉呢，还是满不在乎？连他最亲的朋友安德鲁·福尔克斯爵士也说不好。只是此刻他现身在阿尔芒面前，依旧一表人物：衣衫剪裁到无可挑剔，领口和腕间镶着价值连城的花边，修长的手指间托着一只珐琅鼻烟壶，一方细麻纱手帕——通身一副纨绔做派，全不像个行动中人；要把他同那些令一国热血沸腾、另一国鼓噪报仇的大胆奇行联系在一处，实在难以想象。
可他身上散发的那股磁力，谁也否认不了：那双蓝眼睛平时藏在厚重慵懒的眼睑底下，时不时一道光如夏日闪电般从深处迸出；坚实的嘴唇倏然抿紧；方正的下颌蓦地一沉——刹那之间，只是电光石火的一刹那——整张脸变了模样，露出天生的统帅本色。
此刻迎上前来同他见面的，只是这位温文尔雅、随和散淡的世间人物，身上全无那股气象。阿尔芒快步上前，欢喜地握住他的手——想起自己今日的遭遇，心里难免隐隐不安。他几乎觉得，布莱克尼从半阖的眼睑下，朝他夳地投来了迅疾的一瞥。那道急闪仿佛从内里照亮了年轻人的灵魂，把它赤条条照给他的朋友看。
转眼一切如常。阿尔芒只道是自己良心捣的鬼：他身上——这个他有把握——眼下还看不出半点会走漏秘密的形迹。
“我怕是来迟了，”他说，“傍晚我在街上乱走，天黑迷了路。但愿没叫各位久等。”

===Original===

They all pulled chairs closely round the fire, except Blakeney, who preferred to stand. He waited awhile until they were all comfortably settled, and all ready to listen, then:
“It is about the Dauphin,” he said abruptly without further preamble.
They understood. All of them had guessed it, almost before the summons came that had brought them to Paris two days ago. Sir Andrew Ffoulkes had left his young wife because of that, and Armand had demanded it as a right to join hands in this noble work. Blakeney had not left France for over three months now. Backwards and forwards between Paris, or Nantes, or Orléans to the coast, where his friends would meet him to receive those unfortunates whom one man’s wholehearted devotion had rescued from death; backwards and forwards into the very hearts of those cities wherein an army of sleuthhounds were on his track, and the guillotine was stretching out her arms to catch the foolhardy adventurer.
Now it was about the Dauphin. They all waited, breathless and eager, the fire of a noble enthusiasm burning in their hearts. They waited in silence, their eyes fixed on the leader, lest one single word from him should fail to reach their ears.
The full magnetism of the man was apparent now. As he held these four men at this moment, he could have held a crowd. The man of the world﻿—the fastidious dandy﻿—had shed his mask; there stood the leader, calm, serene in the very face of the most deadly danger that had ever encompassed any man, looking that danger fully in the face, not striving to belittle it or to exaggerate it, but weighing it in the balance with what there was to accomplish: the rescue of a martyred, innocent child from the hands of fiends who were destroying his very soul even more completely than his body.
“Everything, I think, is prepared,” resumed Sir Percy after a slight pause. “The Simons have been summarily dismissed; I learned that today. They remove from the Temple on Sunday next, the nineteenth. Obviously that is the one day most likely to help us in our operations. As far as I am concerned, I cannot make any hard-and-fast plans. Chance at the last moment will have to dictate. But from every one of you I must have cooperation, and it can only be by your following my directions implicitly that we can even remotely hope to succeed.”

===Chinese===

大家都把椅子拉到炉边围坐，只有布莱克尼宁愿站着。他等了一等，等众人个个坐定、个个凝神，才开口：
“是为王太子的事。”他开门见山，一句铺垫也无。
大家都懂了。其实两天前召集他们来巴黎的信到之前，人人就已猜到了。安德鲁·福尔克斯爵士为此撇下了年轻的妻子；阿尔芒则把这当作一份权利，执意要在这桩义举里搭一把手。布莱克尼已有三个多月不曾离开法国。巴黎、南特、奥尔良，再到海岸，来来回回——在海岸，他的朋友们接应那些不幸的人，那是一个人赤诚以赴从死里捞回来的；来来回回，直入那些城市的腹地——城里面，整军的猎犬循着他的踪迹，断头台正张开双臂，等着捉拿这个胆大包天的冒险家。
如今，是为王太子。众人屏息待命，热切之火在心头燃烧。他们静静等着，眼睛盯住首领，惟恐他吐出的哪一个字，逃过自己的耳朵。
此刻，这人全部的磁力尽显无遗。这一刻他握得住这四个人，也能握得住千军。世间人物——挑剔的纨绔——已卸下面具；站在那里的是统帅，冷静，安详，面对着人所遭遇过的最致命的重围，正视那危险的全貌：既不贬它，也不夸它，只把它放上秤盘，同那件必须办成的事较量——把一个殉难的、无辜的孩子，从恶魔们手里救出来；那群恶魔毁他的灵魂，比毁他的肉体还要彻底。
“该备的，我想都备齐了，”珀西爵士略停一停，接着说，“西蒙夫妇已被即时辞退；这是我今天得的消息。他们下星期天——十九号——从坦普尔狱搬走。显而易见，那是顶可能助我们行事的一天。至于我本人，定不出一成不变的死计划，临到最后，须凭机变做主。可你们每一个人，我都须要全力配合；只有你们毫无保留地遵我的调遣，我们才有一线成功的指望。”

===Original===

He crossed and recrossed the room once or twice before he spoke again, pausing now and again in his walk in front of a large map of Paris and its environs that hung upon the wall, his tall figure erect, his hands behind his back, his eyes fixed before him as if he saw right through the walls of this squalid room, and across the darkness that overhung the city, through the grim bastions of the mighty building far away, where the descendant of an hundred kings lived at the mercy of human fiends who worked for his abasement.
The man’s face now was that of a seer and a visionary; the firm lines were set and rigid as those of an image carved in stone﻿—the statue of heart-whole devotion, with the self-imposed task beckoning sternly to follow, there where lurked danger and death.
“The way, I think, in which we could best succeed would be this,” he resumed after a while, sitting now on the edge of the table and directly facing his four friends. The light from the lamp which stood upon the table behind him fell full upon those four glowing faces fixed eagerly upon him, but he himself was in shadow, a massive silhouette broadly cut out against the light-coloured map on the wall beyond.
“I remain here, of course, until Sunday,” he said, “and will closely watch my opportunity, when I can with the greatest amount of safety enter the Temple building and take possession of the child. I shall, of course choose the moment when the Simons are actually on the move, with their successors probably coming in at about the same time. God alone knows,” he added earnestly, “how I shall contrive to get possession of the child; at the moment I am just as much in the dark about that as you are.”
He paused a moment, and suddenly his grave face seemed flooded with sunshine, a kind of lazy merriment danced in his eyes, effacing all trace of solemnity within them.
“La!” he said lightly, “on one point I am not at all in the dark, and that is that His Majesty King Louis XVII will come out of that ugly house in my company next Sunday, the nineteenth day of January in this year of grace seventeen hundred and ninety-four; and this, too, do I know﻿—that those murderous blackguards shall not lay hands on me whilst that precious burden is in my keeping.

===Chinese===

再开口之前，他把屋子来回踱了一两趟，时时在那幅悬在墙上的巴黎及近郊大地图前停步；他身形挺拔，双手背后，目光直视前方，仿佛看穿了这间寒酸屋子的四壁，看穿了笼罩全城的黑暗，望进远处那座巍峨巨堡的深处——百王的后裔住在那里，性命捏在一群专事作践他的恶魔手里。
此刻这张脸，是先知与梦想家的脸；坚毅的线条凝定如石雕——一尊赤诚的雕像，那自加于身的使命正凛然召唤他前行，去那凶险与死亡潜伏之地。
“我看，最稳妥的路子是这样的，”过了一会他接着说，此刻坐在桌沿上，正面四位朋友。身后桌上那盏灯，把光满满洒在那四张热切仰望的脸上；他自己却在暗处，一个厚重的侧影，深深衬在墙那幅浅色地图上。
“我自然留在这里，直到星期天，”他说，“紧紧盯住时机，等我万全地进入坦普尔狱、把那孩子拿到手。我当然会挑西蒙夫妇正搬动的那一刻——他们的继任者多半也是前后脚进。只有上帝知道，”他恳切地补上一句，“我到底怎样把孩子弄到手；眼下这一节，我跟你们一样两眼一抹黑。”
他顿了一顿，忽然，那张凝重的脸上仿佛洒满了阳光，眼里跳起一种懒洋洋的谐趣，把庄重扫了个干净。
“嗐！”他轻巧地说，“有一节我一点也不黑：路易十七国王陛下，下星期天——恩典的一千七百九十四年一月十九日——必得随我一道走出那座丑房子；还有一节我也知道：只要那件金贵的负担在我怀里，那帮杀人不眨眼的恶棍休想碰我一根毫毛。

===Original===

So I pray you, my good Armand, do not look so glum,” he added with his pleasant, merry laugh; “you’ll need all your wits about you to help us in our undertaking.”
“What do you wish me to do, Percy?” said the young man simply.
“In one moment I will tell you. I want you all to understand the situation first. The child will be out of the Temple on Sunday, but at what hour I know not. The later it will be the better would it suit my purpose, for I cannot get him out of Paris before evening with any chance of safety. Here we must risk nothing; the child is far better off as he is now than he would be if he were dragged back after an abortive attempt at rescue. But at this hour of the night, between nine and ten o’clock, I can arrange to get him out of Paris by the Villette gate, and that is where I want you, Ffoulkes, and you, Tony, to be, with some kind of covered cart, yourselves in any disguise your ingenuity will suggest. Here are a few certificates of safety; I have been making a collection of them for some time, as they are always useful.”
He dived into the wide pocket of his coat and drew forth a number of cards, greasy, much-fingered documents of the usual pattern which the Committee of General Security delivered to the free citizens of the new republic, and without which no one could enter or leave any town or country commune without being detained as “suspect.” He glanced at them and handed them over to Ffoulkes.
“Choose your own identity for the occasion, my good friend,” he said lightly; “and you too, Tony. You may be stonemasons or coal-carriers, chimney-sweeps or farm-labourers, I care not which so long as you look sufficiently grimy and wretched to be unrecognisable, and so long as you can procure a cart without arousing suspicions, and can wait for me punctually at the appointed spot.”
Ffoulkes turned over the cards, and with a laugh handed them over to Lord Tony. The two fastidious gentlemen discussed for awhile the respective merits of a chimney-sweep’s uniform as against that of a coal-carrier.
“You can carry more grime if you are a sweep,” suggested Blakeney; “and if the soot gets into your eyes it does not make them smart like coal does.”
“But soot adheres more closely,” argued Tony solemnly, “and I know that we shan’t get a bath for at least a week afterwards.”
“Certainly you won’t, you sybarite!” asserted Sir Percy with a laugh.

===Chinese===

所以我求你啦，我的好阿尔芒，别把脸拉得这么长，”他带着那愉快爽朗的笑补了一句，“这桩大业要借重你，你得把全副心思都带在身上。”
“你要我做什么，珀西？”年轻人直截了当地问。
“这就告诉你。先要你们各位把大局弄明白。孩子星期天出坦普尔狱，几点钟我不知道。越晚越合我的用途，因为入夜之前送他出巴黎，万难保得安全。这里头我们冒不得半点险：营救半途而废、孩子被拖回去，那还不如他眼下这样。但夜里九点到十点之间，我能设法送他从维莱特门出巴黎；福尔克斯，我要你，还有你，托尼——守在那儿，备一辆有篷的货车，你们自己呢，凭你们的巧思，随意化装。这里是几张通行凭证；我收藏它们有些日子了——这类东西永远用得着。”
他探手进宽大的衣袋，掏出一叠卡片——那种治安委员会颁给新共和国自由公民的油亮、被无数手指摸过的老式文书；没有它，谁也别想进出任何城镇乡社，不然就要当作“嫌疑犯”扣下。他扫了一眼，把它们递给福尔克斯。
“这场合用什么身份，自个儿挑吧，我的好朋友，”他轻巧地说，“你也一样，托尼。石匠也罢，运煤夫也罢，扫烟囱的、种田的也罢，是哪样我不在乎——只要你们脏得够、惨得够，叫人认不出；只要你们能不惹疑心地弄到一辆车，还能在约定地点准时候着我。”
福尔克斯把卡片翻检了一遍，笑着递给托尼勋爵。两位挑剔的绅士就扫烟囱装与运煤装孰优孰劣，认真研讨了一阵。
“当扫烟囱的，一身黑灰能挂得更多，”布莱克尼献策，“煤灰进了眼睛呛得慌，烟灰不呛。”
“可烟灰沾得更牢，”托尼一本正经地反驳，“而且我知道，往后少说一个礼拜洗不上澡。”
“那还用说，你这个娇少爷！”珀西爵士笑着断言。

===Original===

“After a week soot might become permanent,” mused Sir Andrew, wondering what, under the circumstance, my lady would say to him.
“If you are both so fastidious,” retorted Blakeney, shrugging his broad shoulders, “I’ll turn one of you into a reddleman, and the other into a dyer. Then one of you will be bright scarlet to the end of his days, as the reddle never comes off the skin at all, and the other will have to soak in turpentine before the dye will consent to move.﻿ ﻿… In either case﻿ ﻿… oh, my dear Tony!﻿ ﻿… the smell.﻿ ﻿…”
He laughed like a schoolboy in anticipation of a prank, and held his scented handkerchief to his nose. My Lord Hastings chuckled audibly, and Tony punched him for this unseemly display of mirth.
Armand watched the little scene in utter amazement. He had been in England over a year, and yet he could not understand these Englishmen. Surely they were the queerest, most inconsequent people in the world. Here were these men, who were engaged at this very moment in an enterprise which for cool-headed courage and foolhardy daring had probably no parallel in history. They were literally taking their lives in their hands, in all probability facing certain death; and yet they now sat chaffing and fighting like a crowd of third-form schoolboys, talking utter, silly nonsense, and making foolish jokes that would have shamed a Frenchman in his teens. Vaguely he wondered what fat, pompous de Batz would think of this discussion if he could overhear it. His contempt, no doubt, for the Scarlet Pimpernel and his followers would be increased tenfold.
Then at last the question of the disguise was effectually dismissed. Sir Andrew Ffoulkes and Lord Anthony Dewhurst had settled their differences of opinion by solemnly agreeing to represent two over-grimy and overheated coal-heavers. They chose two certificates of safety that were made out in the names of Jean Lepetit and Achille Grospierre, labourers.
“Though you don’t look at all like an Achille, Tony,” was Blakeney’s parting shot to his friend.
Then without any transition from this schoolboy nonsense to the serious business of the moment, Sir Andrew Ffoulkes said abruptly:
“Tell us exactly, Blakeney, where you will want the cart to stand on Sunday.”
Blakeney rose and turned to the map against the wall, Ffoulkes and Tony following him.

===Chinese===

“一个礼拜下来，烟灰怕要长在身上啦，”安德鲁爵士沉吟着，暗想事到如今，我家夫人见了这副尊容会有什么话说。
“你们俩这么挑剔，”布莱克尼耸耸宽阔的肩膀回敬道，“我就把你们一个变作贩红土的，一个变作染匠。那样，一个到死都是通红——红土沾了皮肤就再不褪；另一个得在松节油里泡上半天，颜料才肯让步。……反正哪一样……哦，我亲爱的托尼！……那股味儿。……”
他像指望恶作剧的学童般笑起来，把洒过香水的帕子按在鼻子上。黑斯廷斯勋爵咯咯笑出了声，托尼一拳捶他，怪他这阵幸灾乐祸不成体统。
阿尔芒看着这一幕，惊讶到了极点。他来英国一年有余，却还是弄不懂这些英国人。他们准是天下顶古怪、顶没正形的一族人。瞧瞧这几位：此刻投身的事业，论头脑之冷静、胆气之亡命，历史上怕也寻不出第二桩。他们实实在在是把性命攮在手心里，多半直面着必死之局；可他们眼下坐在这里，斗嘴胡闹，活像一群三年级学童，满口傻话，讲的那些浑笑话，连十几岁的法国少年都要脸红。他恍惚想：那位肥头大耳、派头十足的德巴茨若是偷听到这段议论，会作何感想？不用问，他对红花侠及其党羽的轻蔑，准要翻了十倍。
化装的争端终于圆满了结。安德鲁·福尔克斯爵士与安东尼·迪尤赫斯特勋爵郑重达成协议、化解分歧：两人分扮煤灰挂得过头、热得冒汗的运煤工人各一名。他们挑了两张通行凭证，名字是让·勒珀蒂与阿希尔·格罗皮埃尔，职业：苦力。
“不过你半点不像个阿希尔，托尼。”这是布莱克尼丢给朋友的临别一枪。
紧接着，从学童的浑话到眼下的正事，中间没有半分过渡，安德鲁·福尔克斯爵士突兀地说：
“跟我们说仔细了，布莱克尼，星期天车要停在哪儿。”
布莱克尼起身转向墙上那幅地图，福尔克斯和托尼跟了过去。

===Original===

They stood close to his elbow whilst his slender, nervy hand wandered along the shiny surface of the varnished paper. At last he placed his finger on one spot.
“Here you see,” he said, “is the Villette gate. Just outside it a narrow street on the right leads down in the direction of the canal. It is just at the bottom of that narrow street at its junction with the towpath there that I want you two and the cart to be. It had better be a coal-car by the way; they will be unloading coal close by there tomorrow,” he added with one of his sudden irrepressible outbursts of merriment. “You and Tony can exercise your muscles coal-heaving, and incidentally make yourselves known in the neighbourhood as good if somewhat grimy patriots.”
“We had better take up our parts at once then,” said Tony. “I’ll take a fond farewell of my clean shirt tonight.”
“Yes, you will not see one again for some time, my good Tony. After your hard day’s work tomorrow you will have to sleep either inside your cart, if you have already secured one, or under the arches of the canal bridge, if you have not.”
“I hope you have an equally pleasant prospect for Hastings,” was my Lord Tony’s grim comment.
It was easy to see that he was as happy as a schoolboy about to start for a holiday. Lord Tony was a true sportsman. Perhaps there was in him less sentiment for the heroic work which he did under the guidance of his chief than an inherent passion for dangerous adventures. Sir Andrew Ffoulkes, on the other hand, thought perhaps a little less of the adventure, but a great deal of the martyred child in the Temple. He was just as buoyant, just as keen as his friend, but the leaven of sentiment raised his sporting instincts to perhaps a higher plane of self-devotion.
“Well, now, to recapitulate,” he said, in turn following with his finger the indicated route on the map. “Tony and I and the coal-cart will await you on this spot, at the corner of the towpath on Sunday evening at nine o’clock.”
“And your signal, Blakeney?” asked Tony.
“The usual one,” replied Sir Percy, “the seamew’s cry thrice repeated at brief intervals. But now,” he continued, turning to Armand and Hastings, who had taken no part in the discussion hitherto, “I want your help a little further afield.”
“I thought so,” nodded Hastings.

===Chinese===

他们紧挨着他的臂肘站着；他那只瘦削而有力道的手，沿着上过清漆、亮闪闪的图纸缓缓游走。末了，他把手指按在一处。
“你们看，”他说，“这是维莱特门。门外右手一条窄街，一路斜下去通向运河。就在那条窄街的街底、同纤道交接的地方，我要你们两个带着车守着。对了，顶好是辆运煤车；明天那一带正有人卸煤，”他又添一句，忍不住又迸出那阵突发的兴头，“你和托尼正好抡抡胳膊练练扛煤，顺带在街坊里挂个号——做两个爱国好公民，就是脏了点。”
“那咱们不如这就入戏，”托尼说，“今晚我跟我的干净衬衫深情话别。”
“是啊，往后有段日子见不着它了，我的好托尼。明天苦干一天，晚上要么睡在你车里——如果车已经到手；要么就睡运河桥的桥洞底下——如果还没到手。”
“但愿你给黑斯廷斯也备下了同样美妙的前景。”托尼勋爵幽幽地说。
谁都看得出，他快活得像个要出门度假的学童。托尼勋爵是个真正的运动家。在他身上，对首领麾下这件义举的深情，怕是不如对危险冒险的天然嗜好。安德鲁·福尔克斯爵士则相反：冒险的成分他想得少些，坦普尔狱里那个受难的孩子，他想得多得多。他同他的朋友一样兴高采烈、一样热切；只是情感这味酵素，把他竞技的天性，抬到了近乎更高一层的舍己之境。
“好，现在再捋一遍，”他一面说，一面也用手指顺着图上指过的路线走，“星期天晚上九点，托尼、我和运煤车，在纤道拐角这个地方等你。”
“暗号呢，布莱克尼？”托尼问。
“老规矩，”珀西爵士答，“海鸥的叫声，短间隔连叫三遍。不过现在，”他转向至今未加入讨论的阿尔芒和黑斯廷斯，接着说，“我要你们二位到稍远些的地方出力。”
“我就猜是这么着。”黑斯廷斯点头。

===Original===

“The coal-cart, with its usual miserable nag, will carry us a distance of fifteen or sixteen kilometres, but no more. My purpose is to cut along the north of the city, and to reach St. Germain, the nearest point where we can secure good mounts. There is a farmer just outside the commune; his name is Achard. He has excellent horses, which I have borrowed before now; we shall want five, of course, and he has one powerful beast that will do for me, as I shall have, in addition to my own weight, which is considerable, to take the child with me on the pillion. Now you, Hastings and Armand, will have to start early tomorrow morning, leave Paris by the Neuilly gate, and from there make your way to St. Germain by any conveyance you can contrive to obtain. At St. Germain you must at once find Achard’s farm; disguised as labourers you will not arouse suspicion by so doing. You will find the farmer quite amenable to money, and you must secure the best horses you can get for our own use, and, if possible, the powerful mount I spoke of just now. You are both excellent horsemen, therefore I selected you amongst the others for this special errand, for you two, with the five horses, will have to come and meet our coal-cart some seventeen kilometres out of St. Germain, to where the first signpost indicates the road to Courbevoie. Some two hundred mètres down this road on the right there is a small spinney, which will afford splendid shelter for yourselves and your horses. We hope to be there at about one o’clock after midnight of Monday morning. Now, is all that quite clear, and are you both satisfied?”
“It is quite clear,” exclaimed Hastings placidly; “but I, for one, am not at all satisfied.”
“And why not?”
“Because it is all too easy. We get none of the danger.”
“Oho! I thought that you would bring that argument forward, you incorrigible grumbler,” laughed Sir Percy good-humouredly. “Let me tell you that if you start tomorrow from Paris in that spirit you will run your head and Armand’s into a noose long before you reach the gate of Neuilly. I cannot allow either of you to cover your faces with too much grime; an honest farm labourer should not look over-dirty, and your chances of being discovered and detained are, at the outset, far greater than those which Ffoulkes and Tony will run﻿—”
Armand had said nothing during this time.

===Chinese===

“运煤车，加上它那匹照例的可怜驽马，能载我们走十五六公里，再远不成。我打算沿城北插过去，直奔圣日耳曼——那是我们弄得到好坐骑的最近一处。公社外头有个农户，名叫阿沙尔。他有好马，我从前就‘借’过；我们自然要五匹；他还有一匹健骒，正合我用——因为我这份体重本就不轻，还得把小孩带在后鞍上。现在说你们俩：黑斯廷斯，阿尔芒，明天一早动身，从讷伊门出巴黎，再想方设法寻代步去圣日耳曼。到了圣日耳曼，立刻找阿沙尔的农庄；你们扮作苦力，去找农庄不惹眼。那农户见钱就肯通融；你们务必为我们挑到顶好的马，可能的话，把我方才说的那匹健骒也定下。你们两位都是一等一的骑手，所以我才在众人里单挑你们办这桩差：你们俩带着五匹马，得赶到圣日耳曼城外约十七公里、第一块路牌指着库尔布瓦去路的地方，同我们的运煤车碰头。那条路右手下去约两百米，有一小片杂树林，正好给你们和马匹藏身。我们指望星期一凌晨一点上下到那儿。都清楚了么？你们俩可满意？”
“清楚是清楚，”黑斯廷斯不动声色地说，“可要说满意，我头一个就不满意。”
“哦？为何？”
“因为这差事也太容易了。危险一点儿没轮着我们。”
“哦嗬！我就知道你要搬出这套理，你这无可救药的怨袋子，”珀西爵士好脾气地笑道，“我告诉你：你明天若带着这股心气出巴黎，不到讷伊门，你那颗脑袋连同阿尔芒的，就先钻进绞索了。你们俩的脸，我不许抹得太脏；老实的田家苦力不该脏过了头；你们的形迹一开始被人识破、被扣下的风险，可比福尔克斯和托尼要冒的大得多——”
这半天，阿尔芒一言未发。

===Original===

While Blakeney was unfolding his plan for him and for Lord Hastings﻿—a plan which practically was a command﻿—he had sat with his arms folded across his chest, his head sunk upon his breast. When Blakeney had asked if they were satisfied, he had taken no part in Hastings’ protest nor responded to his leader’s good-humoured banter.
Though he did not look up even now, yet he felt that Percy’s eyes were fixed upon him, and they seemed to scorch into his soul. He made a great effort to appear eager like the others, and yet from the first a chill had struck at his heart. He could not leave Paris before he had seen Jeanne.
He looked up suddenly, trying to seem unconcerned; he even looked his chief fully in the face.
“When ought we to leave Paris?” he asked calmly.
