The accused had walked down the Champs Élysées without wearing a tricolour cockade; the other had invested some savings in an English industrial enterprise; yet another had sold public funds, causing them to depreciate rather suddenly in the market!
Sometimes from one of these unfortunates led thus wantonly to butchery there would come an excited protest, or from a woman screams of agonised entreaty. But these were quickly silenced by rough blows from the butt-ends of muskets, and condemnations﻿—wholesale sentences of death﻿—were quickly passed amidst the cheers of the spectators and the howls of derision from infamous jury and judge.
Oh! the mockery of it all﻿—the awful, the hideous ignominy, the blot of shame that would forever sully the historic name of France. Armand, sickened with horror, could not bear more than a few minutes of this monstrous spectacle. The same fate might even now be awaiting Jeanne. Among the next batch of victims to this sacrilegious butchery he might suddenly spy his beloved with her pale face and cheeks stained with her tears.
He fled from the great chamber, keeping just a sufficiency of presence of mind to join a knot of idlers who were drifting leisurely towards the corridors.

===Chinese===

公众同囚犯之间隔着高大的铁门，门前一列哨兵把守；可谁要是胆大眼馋，尽可以把鼻子贴上铁栅，看那些旧日贵族——衣衫磨得露线——怎样演一出轻快的滑稽戏，骗过自己对死亡的恐怖；而他们苍白的脸、泪翳蒙蒙的眼睛，把那戏拆穿得干干净净。
这些阿尔芒全知道，也正打的是这个算盘。有一阵子，他随着人群待在“失步厅”里，在列柱巍峨的大厅中无目的地踱来踱去。有一刻，他也挤在人堆里，看法庭大堂里上演的一出速决惨剧：一批囚犯鱼贯押进；仓促的讯问，被打断的回答，公诉人富基耶-坦维尔掷出火速的起诉书——那份公然的不公，骇人听闻；而台下一班人竟敢自称同胞的法官，还听得一本正经。
这一名，走香榭丽舍大街没戴三色帽徽；那一名，把积蓄投了一家英国实业；又一名，抛售了公债，害得行情猛然跌了一跌！
这些被恣意驱向屠场的可怜人，有时会迸出一声激愤的抗辩；女人则发出苦苦哀号的尖叫。可枪托几记重击，立刻叫它们嚇声；判决——成批的死刑——在观者的喝彩、无耻陪审团和法官的哄笑里，飞快落定。
啊！这一切的荒唐——这可怕的、丑恶的耻辱，这桩将永远玷污法兰西历史之名的污点！阿尔芒恶心透了，这等骇人的场面，他一刻也多看不下去。同样的命运，此刻说不定正等着让娜。下一批押上这场亵渎人性的屠戮的牺牲品里，他怕会突然看见他的爱人——苍白的脸，泪痕斑斑的颊。
他逃出大堂，尚存的那一点理智，刚好够他挤进一小撮懒洋洋荡向长廊的闲人堆里。

===Original===

He followed in their wake and soon found himself in the long Galerie des Prisonniers, along the flagstones of which two days ago de Batz had followed his guide towards the lodgings of Héron.
On his left now were the arcades shut off from the courtyard beyond by heavy iron gates. Through the ironwork Armand caught sight of a number of women walking or sitting in the courtyard. He heard a man next to him explaining to his friend that these were the female prisoners who would be brought to trial that day, and he felt that his heart must burst at the thought that mayhap Jeanne would be among them.
He elbowed his way cautiously to the front rank. Soon he found himself beside a sentinel who, with a good-humoured jest, made way for him that he might watch the aristos. Armand leaned against the grating, and his every sense was concentrated in that of sight.
At first he could scarcely distinguish one woman from another amongst the crowd that thronged the courtyard, and the close ironwork hindered his view considerably. The women looked almost like phantoms in the grey misty air, gliding slowly along with noiseless tread on the flagstones.
Presently, however, his eyes, which mayhap were somewhat dim with tears, became more accustomed to the hazy grey light and the moving figures that looked so like shadows. He could distinguish isolated groups now, women and girls sitting together under the colonnaded arcades, some reading, others busy, with trembling fingers, patching and darning a poor, torn gown. Then there were others who were actually chatting and laughing together, and﻿—oh, the pity of it! the pity and the shame!﻿—a few children, shrieking with delight, were playing hide and seek in and out amongst the columns.
And, between them all, in and out like the children at play, unseen, yet familiar to all, the spectre of Death, scythe and hourglass in hand, wandered, majestic and sure.
Armand’s very soul was in his eyes. So far he had not yet caught sight of his beloved, and slowly﻿—very slowly﻿—a ray of hope was filtering through the darkness of his despair.
The sentinel, who had stood aside for him, chaffed him for his intentness.
“Have you a sweetheart among these aristos, citizen?” he asked. “You seem to be devouring them with your eyes.”

===Chinese===

他跟着他们，不多时便到了长长的囚犯长廊；两天前，德巴茨正是沿着这条石板路，由人引路，走向埃龙的住处。
此刻他左手是一排拱廊，同那边的院子隔着沉重的铁门。透过铁栅，阿尔芒望见院中若干女子，或走或坐。他听见身旁一个人向同伴解释：这些是当天就要过堂的女犯。一想到让娜兴许就在其中，他觉得心要炸开了。
他小心用胳膊肘开路，挤到最前排。不多时便挨到一个哨兵旁边；那哨兵说了句玩笑，让开半步，由他看那些贵族。阿尔芒倚着铁栅，浑身的知觉都归拢进了眼睛。
起先，满院子攒动的人影里，他几乎分不清谁是谁；密密的铁栅也大大挡了视线。灰蒙蒙的雾气里，那些女子几乎像一群幽灵，在石板上无声地移动，缓缓滑行。
然而不多时，他那双也许已被泪水润湿的眼睛，渐渐习惯了灰蒙蒙的光线和那些影子般游动的人形。他分得出零星的小群了：妇人和姑娘们坐在列柱拱廊下，有的读书，有的抖着手在缝补一条破旧的裙子。还有一些竟说说笑笑；而——啊，可怜！可怜又可耻！——还有几个孩子，欢叫着在柱子中间捉迷藏。
而在她们大家中间，像游戏的孩子那样穿进穿出——无人看见，却人人熟识——死神那幽灵，一手持镰，一手擎着沙漏，气度俨然，笃定地巡行。
阿尔芒整副灵魂都悬在眼睛上。到目前为止，他还没有望见爱人；一线希望正缓缓地——极缓极缓地——透过绝望的黑暗渗进来。
方才给他让路的哨兵，见他这般全神贯注，打趣起他来。
“这里头的贵族里，有你相好的吧，公民？”他问，“瞧你这眼睛，都快把她们吞下去了。”

===Original===

Armand, with his rough clothes soiled with coal-dust, his face grimy and streaked with sweat, certainly looked to have but little in common with the ci-devant aristos who formed the hulk of the groups in the courtyard. He looked up; the soldier was regarding him with obvious amusement, and at sight of Armand’s wild, anxious eyes he gave vent to a coarse jest.
“Have I made a shrewd guess, citizen?” he said. “Is she among that lot?”
“I do not know where she is,” said Armand almost involuntarily.
“Then why don’t you find out?” queried the soldier.
The man was not speaking altogether unkindly. Armand, devoured with the maddening desire to know, threw the last fragment of prudence to the wind. He assumed a more careless air, trying to look as like a country bumpkin in love as he could.
“I would like to find out,” he said, “but I don’t know where to inquire. My sweetheart has certainly left her home,” he added lightly; “some say that she has been false to me, but I think that, mayhap, she has been arrested.”
“Well, then, you gaby,” said the soldier good-humouredly, “go straight to La Tournelle; you know where it is?”
Armand knew well enough, but thought it more prudent to keep up the air of the ignorant lout.
“Straight down that first corridor on your right,” explained the other, pointing in the direction which he had indicated, “you will find the guichet of La Tournelle exactly opposite to you. Ask the concierge for the register of female prisoners﻿—every freeborn citizen of the Republic has the right to inspect prison registers. It is a new decree framed for safeguarding the liberty of the people. But if you do not press half a livre in the hand of the concierge,” he added, speaking confidentially, “you will find that the register will not be quite ready for your inspection.”
“Half a livre!” exclaimed Armand, striving to play his part to the end. “How can a poor devil of a labourer have half a livre to give away?”
“Well! a few sous will do in that case; a few sous are always welcome these hard times.”
Armand took the hint, and as the crowd had drifted away momentarily to a further portion of the corridor, he contrived to press a few copper coins into the hand of the obliging soldier.

===Chinese===

阿尔芒一身粗衣沾满煤灰，脸也脏了，汗一道一道——同院子里成群结伙的旧贵族，看着实在扯不上一丝干系。他抬起头；那士兵正明显带着消遣的神气打量他，一见阿尔芒那双狂乱焦灼的眼睛，便吐出一句粗话逗趣。
“我这一猜，猜着了没有，公民？”他说，“她就在那一堆里？”
“我不知道她在哪儿。”阿尔芒几乎是不由自主地说。
“那干吗不去打听打听？”士兵问。
这人说这话倒不全是恶意。阿尔芒被那知道下落的疯狂欲望咬噬着，把最后一点谨慎也抛到了九霄云外。他摆出一副更随便的神气，尽力扮成一个害了相思的乡下傻小子。
“我是想打听来着，”他说，“可不知道上哪儿问。我那相好的，确实是不在家了，”他轻巧地添一句，“有人说她变了心；可依我看，八成是叫人抓走了。”
“那你这个呆子，”士兵好脾气地说，“直接上拉图奈尔去；知道那地方么？”
阿尔芒清楚得很，却觉得还是装傻小子稳妥。
“右手第一条走廊一直走到底，”那人朝他指的方向比了比，“拉图奈尔的传达窗口正对着你。找管门人要女犯名册——共和国每个自由公民都有权查阅监狱名册。新颁的法令，说是保障人民自由。不过，”他压低嗓音、推心置腹地补上一句，“你要不往管门人手里塞上半个里弗，那本名册，怕就要‘一时半会儿备不好’喽。”
“半个里弗！”阿尔芒叫起来，一心把戏做到底，“我们这号卖苦力的穷鬼，哪来半个里弗白送人？”
“唔！那样的话，几个苏也行；这年头，几个苏什么时候都受欢迎。”
阿尔芒心领神会；趁人群暂时荡到长廊另一头，他把几枚铜子塞进了这位热心士兵的手心。

===Original===

Of course, he knew his way to La Tournelle, and he would have covered the distance that separated him from the guichet there with steps flying like the wind, but, commending himself for his own prudence, he walked as slowly as he could along the interminable corridor, past the several minor courts of justice, and skirting the courtyard where the male prisoners took their exercise.
At last, having struck sharply to his left and ascended a short flight of stairs, he found himself in front of the guichet﻿—a narrow wooden box, wherein the clerk in charge of the prison registers sat nominally at the disposal of the citizens of this free republic.
But to Armand’s almost overwhelming chagrin he found the place entirely deserted. The guichet was closed down; there was not a soul in sight. The disappointment was doubly keen, coming as it did in the wake of hope that had refused to be gainsaid. Armand himself did not realise how sanguine he had been until he discovered that he must wait and wait again﻿—wait for hours, all day mayhap, before he could get definite news of Jeanne.
He wandered aimlessly in the vicinity of that silent, deserted, cruel spot, where a closed trapdoor seemed to shut off all his hopes of a speedy sight of Jeanne. He inquired of the first sentinels whom he came across at what hour the clerk of the registers would be back at his post; the soldiers shrugged their shoulders and could give no information. Then began Armand’s aimless wanderings round La Tournelle, his fruitless inquiries, his wild, excited search for the hidebound official who was keeping from him the knowledge of Jeanne.
He went back to his sentinel well-wisher by the women’s courtyard, but found neither consolation nor encouragement there.
“It is not the hour﻿—*quoi*?” the soldier remarked with laconic philosophy.
It apparently was not the hour when the prison registers were placed at the disposal of the public. After much fruitless inquiry, Armand at last was informed by a *bon bourgeois*, who was wandering about the house of Justice and who seemed to know its multifarious rules, that the prison registers all over Paris could only be consulted by the public between the hours of six and seven in the evening.
There was nothing for it but to wait.

===Chinese===

去拉图奈尔的路，他自然认得；若由着他，恨不得一阵风刮到那窗口跟前。可他暗自嘉许自己的谨慎，偏沿着那条望不到头的长廊，走一步挨一步——路过几座小法庭，绕过男囚放风的院子。
末了，他猛地左拐，蹬上一小截楼梯，便到了那传达窗口跟前——一只窄窄的木匣子，掌管监狱名册的文书坐在里面，名义上随时听候这个自由共和国公民们的使唤。
可是迎接阿尔芒的，是一场几乎把他击垮的懊丧：那里空空如也。窗口板放下了；四下不见一个人影。方才那股不容分说的希望还热在胸口，这一下失望便加倍扎人。直到此刻阿尔芒才明白自己刚才怀着多大的指望——如今他却必须一等再等，等上几个钟头，兴许一整天，才能得到让娜的确切消息。
他在这处沉寂、无人、冷酷的地点周围无目的地打转——一扇放下的窗板，仿佛隔断了他早日见到让娜的全部指望。他逢哨兵便问：管名册的文书几时回岗？士兵们耸耸肩，一问三不知。于是阿尔芒绕着拉图奈尔盲目游荡起来，一次次徒劳的打探，狂热焦躁地搜寻那个死板的官儿——就是他，扣着让娜的消息不放。
他折回女犯院子旁那位好心的哨兵那里，却也没讨得半句安慰或鼓舞。
“时辰没到——*可不是么*（*quoi*）？”士兵言简意赚地发了一句哲理。
显然，监狱名册向公众开放的时辰还没到。又是许多趟白跑之后，阿尔芒总算从一位在司法府里转悠、似乎深谙其中种种章程的*好市民*（*bon bourgeois*）口中得知：全巴黎的监狱名册，只有晚间六点到七点，才准公众查阅。
别无他法，只有等。

===Original===

Armand, whose temples were throbbing, who was footsore, hungry, and wretched, could gain nothing by continuing his aimless wanderings through the labyrinthine building. For close upon another hour he stood with his face glued against the ironwork which separated him from the female prisoners’ courtyard. Once it seemed to him as if from its further end he caught the sound of that exquisitely melodious voice which had rung forever in his ear since that memorable evening when Jeanne’s dainty footsteps had first crossed the path of his destiny. He strained his eyes to look in the direction whence the voice had come, but the centre of the courtyard was planted with a small garden of shrubs, and Armand could not see across it. At last, driven forth like a wandering and lost soul, he turned back and out into the streets. The air was mild and damp. The sharp thaw had persisted through the day, and a thin, misty rain was falling and converting the ill-paved roads into seas of mud.
But of this Armand was wholly unconscious. He walked along the quay holding his cap in his hand, so that the mild south wind should cool his burning forehead.
How he contrived to kill those long, weary hours he could not afterwards have said. Once he felt very hungry, and turned almost mechanically into an eating-house, and tried to eat and drink. But most of the day he wandered through the streets, restlessly, unceasingly, feeling neither chill nor fatigue. The hour before six o’clock found him on the Quai de l’Horloge in the shadow of the great towers of the Hall of Justice, listening for the clang of the clock that would sound the hour of his deliverance from this agonising torture of suspense.
He found his way to La Tournelle without any hesitation. There before him was the wooden box, with its guichet open at last, and two stands upon its ledge, on which were placed two huge leather-bound books.
Though Armand was nearly an hour before the appointed time, he saw when he arrived a number of people standing round the guichet. Two soldiers were there keeping guard and forcing the patient, long-suffering inquirers to stand in a queue, each waiting his or her turn at the books.

===Chinese===

阿尔芒太阳穴突突地跳，脚底生疼，饥肠辘辘，狼狈不堪；再在这座迷宫般的建筑里乱转，也转不出什么名堂。又有差不多一个钟头，他把脸贴在铁栅上——铁栅那面就是女犯的院子。有一刻，他仿佛从院子那头，捕捉到一缕妙曼婉转的嗓音——自那个难忘的夜晚、让娜轻盈的脚步初次踏上他命运的小径起，这嗓音便长鸣在他耳畔。他竭尽目力朝声音来处望去，可院子中央种着一小方灌木园，望不过去。末了，他像个游荡的孤魂，被撵了出来，转身走上街头。天气温和而潮润。解冻整整持续了一天，细雨蒙蒙地下着，把本就坎坷的路面化成了泥海。
这些阿尔芒浑然不觉。他沿着滨河道走，帽子拿在手里，好教和暖的南风吹一吹滚烫的额头。
那些漫长难熬的钟点，他到底是怎样磨过去的，事后他自己也说不上来。有一阵他饿极了，几乎是下意识地拐进一家饭铺，勉强吃了点、喝了点。可这一整天的大半，他都在街上走——心神不宁，走个不停，不觉得冷，也不觉得累。六点前的一个钟头，他已站在时钟滨河道上，立在司法大厅高塔的影子里，静候钟声一响——那将是把他从悬心的酷刑里解放出来的一刻。
他径直寻到拉图奈尔，毫不含糊。木匣子就在眼前，窗口终于开了；窗台上支着两只书架，架上摌着两大本皮面册子。
虽离约定时辰还差将近一个钟头，阿尔芒到时，窗口边已围了一群人。两名士兵在旁把守，逼着这些耐心、久受煎熬的查询人排成一列，一个挨一个，等着轮到自己翻那册子。

===Original===

It was a curious crowd that stood there, in single file, as if waiting at the door of the cheaper part of a theatre; men in substantial cloth clothes, and others in ragged blouse and breeches; there were a few women, too, with black shawls on their shoulders and kerchiefs round their wan, tear-stained faces.
They were all silent and absorbed, submissive under the rough handling of the soldiery, humble and deferential when anon the clerk of the registers entered his box, and prepared to place those fateful books at the disposal of those who had lost a loved one﻿—father, brother, mother, or wife﻿—and had come to search through those cruel pages.
From inside his box the clerk disputed every inquirer’s right to consult the books; he made as many difficulties as he could, demanding the production of certificates of safety, or permits from the section. He was as insolent as he dared, and Armand from where he stood could see that a continuous if somewhat thin stream of coppers flowed from the hands of the inquirers into those of the official.
It was quite dark in the passage where the long queue continued to swell with amazing rapidity. Only on the ledge in front of the guichet there was a guttering tallow candle at the disposal of the inquirers.
Now it was Armand’s turn at last. By this time his heart was beating so strongly and so rapidly that he could not have trusted himself to speak. He fumbled in his pocket, and without unnecessary preliminaries he produced a small piece of silver, and pushed it towards the clerk, then he seized on the register marked “Femmes” with voracious avidity.
The clerk had with stolid indifference pocketed the half-livre; he looked on Armand over a pair of large bone-rimmed spectacles, with the air of an old hawk that sees a helpless bird and yet is too satiated to eat. He was apparently vastly amused at Armand’s trembling hands, and the clumsy, aimless way with which he fingered the book and held up the tallow candle.
“What date?” he asked curtly in a piping voice.
“What date?” reiterated Armand vaguely.
“What day and hour was she arrested?” said the man, thrusting his beak-like nose closer to Armand’s face. Evidently the piece of silver had done its work well; he meant to be helpful to this country lout.
“On Friday evening,” murmured the young man.

===Chinese===

站在那里的是一支古怪的队伍，单行鱼贯，仿佛等在剧院廉价座席的门口；有穿着厚实呢衣的男人，也有短衫马裤褴褛的；还有几个女人，肩上披黑披肩，憔悴泪痕的脸上裹着头巾。
人人沉默出神，任凭兵丁推擬，俯首帖耳；待管名册的文书进得匣中、准备把那两本决定生死的册子交到查询人面前时，又个个低声下气——他们都是失了亲人的人，父亲、兄弟、母亲或妻子，来这几页薄纸上翻寻残酷的答案。
那文书坐在匣子里，对每个查询人的查阅资格都要驳一驳；能设多少难关便设多少，一会儿要验通行凭证，一会儿要分区当局的许可。他放肆到多放肆的地步；阿尔芒在自己站的地方看得清楚：一股细细不断的铜钱流，从查询人手里，源源淌进那官员的手里。
过道里已相当黑；长队还在以惊人的速度增长。只有窗口前的台面上，点着一支淌油的牛油烛，供查询人取用。
终于轮到阿尔芒了。这时他的心跳得又猛又急，连开口都不敢保险。他在口袋里摸索，不绕一点弯子，摸出一小块银币，推向文书；随即一把抓住标着“Femmes”（女犯）的那册名册，如饥似渴。
那文书木然地把半里弗揣进兜里；他从一副大骨框眼镜上方打量阿尔芒，那神情像只老鹰，望见一只无从逃遁的小鸟，却吃得太饱，懒得动爪。阿尔芒那双发抖的手、他捧册子举蜡烛那副笨拙无措的样子，显然叫他大大地开心。
“哪天的？”他尖着嗓子，简短地问。
“哪天的？”阿尔芒茫茫然重复。
“她几号几点抓的？”那人把鹰喙似的鼻子凑近阿尔芒的脸。那块银币显然功效卓著；他打算给这乡下小子帮个忙了。
“星期五晚上。”年轻人喃喃道。

===Original===

The clerk’s hands did not in character gainsay the rest of his appearance; they were long and thin, with nails that resembled the talons of a hawk. Armand watched them fascinated as from above they turned over rapidly the pages of the book; then one long, grimy finger pointed to a row of names down a column.
“If she is here,” said the man curtly, “her name should be amongst these.”
Armand’s vision was blurred. He could scarcely see. The row of names was dancing a wild dance in front of his eyes; perspiration stood out on his forehead, and his breath came in quick, stertorous gasps.
He never knew afterwards whether he actually saw Jeanne’s name there in the book, or whether his fevered brain was playing his aching senses a cruel and mocking trick. Certain it is that suddenly amongst a row of indifferent names hers suddenly stood clearly on the page, and to him it seemed as if the letters were writ out in blood.

> 582. Belhomme, Louise, aged sixty. Discharged.

And just below, the other entry:

> 583. Lange, Jeanne, aged twenty, actress. Square du Roule No. 5. Suspected of harbouring traitors and ci-devants. Transferred 29th Nivôse to the Temple, cell 29.

===Chinese===

那文书的双手，同他通身的品貌一脉相承：又长又瘦，指甲好似鹰爪。阿尔芒看得入迷：那双手从上方飞快掀动册页；随后一根细长污秽的手指，点向一栏里的一列名字。
“她要是在这儿，”那人简短地说，“名字该在这一串里。”
阿尔芒的视线模糊了。几乎什么都看不见。那一列名字在他眼前狂舞；额上沁出汗珠，呼吸急促沉重，一声声拉风箱似的。
他后来始终不知道，让娜的名字，他是真在册上看见了，还是他烧热的脑子捉弄他酸痛的感官，开了个残忍的玩笑。只是千真万确：一列漠然的名字当中，她的名字蓦地清清楚楚立在页面上；在他眼里，那一个个字母，仿佛是用血写就。

> 582. 贝洛姆，路易丝，六十岁。开释。

紧挨着下面，是另一条：

> 583. 朗热，让娜，二十岁，女演员。鲁勒广场五号。涉嫌窝藏叛国者及旧贵族。雪月二十九日移送坦普尔狱，二十九号囚室。

===Original===

He saw nothing more, for suddenly it seemed to him as if someone held a vivid scarlet veil in front of his eyes, whilst a hundred claw-like hands were tearing at his heart and at his throat.
“Clear out now! it is my turn﻿—what? Are you going to stand there all night?”
A rough voice seemed to be speaking these words; rough hands apparently were pushing him out of the way, and someone snatched the candle out of his hand; but nothing was real. He stumbled over a corner of a loose flagstone, and would have fallen, but something seemed to catch hold of him and to lead him away for a little distance, until a breath of cold air blew upon his face.
This brought him back to his senses.
Jeanne was a prisoner in the Temple; then his place was in the prison of the Temple, too. It could not be very difficult to run one’s head into the noose that caught so many necks these days. A few cries of “*Vive le roi!*” or “*À bas la république!*” and more than one prison door would gape invitingly to receive another guest.
The hot blood had rushed into Armand’s head. He did not see clearly before him, nor did he hear distinctly. There was a buzzing in his ears as of myriads of mocking birds’ wings, and there was a veil in front of his eyes﻿—a veil through which he saw faces and forms flitting ghostlike in the gloom, men and women jostling or being jostled, soldiers, sentinels; then long, interminable corridors, more crowd and more soldiers, winding stairs, courtyards and gates; finally the open street, the quay, and the river beyond.
An incessant hammering went on in his temples, and that veil never lifted from before his eyes. Now it was lurid and red, as if stained with blood; anon it was white like a shroud but it was always there.
Through it he saw the Pont-au-Change, which he crossed, then far down on the Quai de l’École to the left the corner house behind St. Germain l’Auxerrois, where Blakeney lodged﻿—Blakeney, who for the sake of a stranger had forgotten all about his comrade and Jeanne.
Through it he saw the network of streets which separated him from the neighbourhood of the Temple, the gardens of ruined habitations, the closely-shuttered and barred windows of ducal houses, then the mean streets, the crowded drinking bars, the tumbledown shops with their dilapidated awnings.

===Chinese===

他再什么都看不见了；仿佛突然有人在他眼前张起一幅猩红的纱，一百只利爪似的手，撕扯着他的心、他的咽喉。
“让让！该我了——听见没有？你打算在这儿站一宿？”
一个粗喉咙仿佛在说这些话；几只粗手似乎正把他往旁边推，有人一把夺走了他手里的蜡烛；可没有一样是真的。他绊在一块松动的石板角上，险些跌倒；可仿佛有什么东西扶住了他，领他走了一小段，直到一口冷气扑上他的脸。
这口冷气把他吹回了神。
让娜是坦普尔狱的囚徒了；那么他的位置，也该在坦普尔狱里。如今这世道，把脑袋伸进那副套走无数脖颈的绞索里，想来不是难事。喊几声“*国王万岁！*（*Vive le roi!*）”或“*打倒共和国！*（*À bas la république!*）”，不止一座狱门便会热情地张开，迎候又一位客人。
热血直冲进阿尔芒的头顶。他看不清眼前，也听不真切。耳边嗡嗡，如千万只嘲弄的鸟在振翅；眼前也蒙着一层纱——隔着纱，他看见人面人影在昏暝里鬼魅般掠过，男男女女推人被人推，士兵、哨兵；而后是长长的、望不尽的走廊，更多的人群更多的兵，旋转的楼梯，院子与门洞；末了是敞开的街、滨河道，和河。
太阳穴里锤子不停；那层纱，始终没从他眼前掀开。一时猩红如血染，一时惨白如裹尸布——可它总在那里。
隔着纱，他望见换钱桥，便走了过去；又望见埃科勒滨河道左手远处、圣日耳曼欧塞罗瓦教堂后身拐角上那座宅子——布莱克尼的寓所。布莱克尼——为了一个外人，把同伴和让娜忘了个干净。
隔着纱，他望见横在他同坦普尔狱一带之间的街巷网，败落宅邸的园子，公爵府钉死上闩的窗，再往后是陋街窄巷，挤挤挨挨的酒肆，塌了架的小铺和破烂的凉棚。

===Original===

He saw with eyes that did not see, heard the tumult of daily life round him with ears that did not hear. Jeanne was in the Temple prison, and when its grim gates closed finally for the night, he﻿—Armand, her chevalier, her lover, her defender﻿—would be within its walls as near to cell No. 29 as bribery, entreaty, promises would help him to attain.
Ah! there at last loomed the great building, the pointed bastions cut through the surrounding gloom as with a sable knife.
Armand reached the gate; the sentinels challenged him; he replied:
“*Vive le roi!*” shouting wildly like one who is drunk.
He was hatless, and his clothes were saturated with moisture. He tried to pass, but crossed bayonets barred the way. Still he shouted:
“*Vive le roi!*” and “*À bas la république!*”
“*Allons!* the fellow is drunk!” said one of the soldiers.
Armand fought like a madman; he wanted to reach that gate. He shouted, he laughed, and he cried, until one of the soldiers in a fit of rage struck him heavily on the head.
Armand fell backwards, stunned by the blow; his foot slipped on the wet pavement. Was he indeed drunk, or was he dreaming? He put his hand up to his forehead; it was wet, but whether with the rain or with blood he did not know; but for the space of one second he tried to collect his scattered wits.
“Citizen St. Just!” said a quiet voice at his elbow.
Then, as he looked round dazed, feeling a firm, pleasant grip on his arm, the same quiet voice continued calmly:
“Perhaps you do not remember me, citizen St. Just. I had not the honour of the same close friendship with you as I had with your charming sister. My name is Chauvelin. Can I be of any service to you?”

===Chinese===

他用看不见的眼睛看，用听不见的耳朵听周遭生计的嘈杂。让娜在坦普尔狱里；那么待那狰狞的大门今夜最后合上时，他——阿尔芒，她的骑士、她的爱人、她的护卫——也要进到那高墙之内，离二十九号囚室多近算多近——贿赂、哀恳、许诺，能送多近送多近。
啊！那座巨堡终于在前头显现了，尖尖的堡楼刺破四周的黑暗，像一柄黑刃豁开夜色。
阿尔芒到了门前；哨兵喝问；他答：
“*国王万岁！*”他狂呼大喊，像个醉汉。
他光着头，衣裳湿透。他想往里闯，交叉的刺刀拦住了去路。他还在喊：
“*国王万岁！*”，“*打倒共和国！*”
“*行了！*这家伙喝多了！”一个士兵说。
阿尔芒疯子一般挣扎；他一心要够着那道门。他喊，他笑，他哭，直到一名士兵火起，朝他头上重重一击。
阿尔芒仰面栽倒，被那一击打懵了；脚在湿漉漉的路面上打着滑。他真是醉了，还是在做梦？他抬手摸了摸额头；湿的——是雨还是血，他分不清；可就在那一秒里，他竭力收拢涣散的神智。
“圣鞠斯特公民！”他肘边响起一个平静的声音。
他晕头转向地四顾，觉出臂上有一只坚定而舒适的手，那同一个平静的声音又从容地接了下去：
“您也许不记得我了，圣鞠斯特公民。我不曾有幸同您结下同令姐那般的深交。我叫肖弗兰。能为您效一点劳么？”

## XVII Chauvelin / 肖维兰
===Original===

Chauvelin! The presence of this man here at this moment made the events of the past few days seem more absolutely like a dream. Chauvelin!﻿—the most deadly enemy he, Armand, and his sister Marguerite had in the world. Chauvelin!﻿—the evil genius that presided over the Secret Service of the Republic. Chauvelin﻿—the aristocrat turned revolutionary, the diplomat turned spy, the baffled enemy of the Scarlet Pimpernel.
He stood there vaguely outlined in the gloom by the feeble rays of an oil lamp fixed into the wall just above. The moisture on his sable clothes glistened in the flickering light like a thin veil of crystal; it clung to the rim of his hat, to the folds of his cloak; the ruffles at his throat and wrist hung limp and soiled.
He had released Armand’s arm, and held his hands now underneath his cloak; his pale, deep-set eyes rested gravely on the younger man’s face.
“I had an idea, somehow,” continued Chauvelin calmly, “that you and I would meet during your sojourn in Paris. I heard from my friend Héron that you had been in the city; he, unfortunately, lost your track almost as soon as he had found it, and I, too, had begun to fear that our mutual and ever enigmatical friend, the Scarlet Pimpernel, had spirited you away, which would have been a great disappointment to me.”
Now he once more took hold of Armand by the elbow, but quite gently, more like a comrade who is glad to have met another, and is preparing to enjoy a pleasant conversation for a while. He led the way back to the gate, the sentinel saluting at sight of the tricolour scarf which was visible underneath his cloak. Under the stone rampart Chauvelin paused.
It was quiet and private here. The group of soldiers stood at the further end of the archway, but they were out of hearing, and their forms were only vaguely discernible in the surrounding darkness.
Armand had followed his enemy mechanically like one bewitched and irresponsible for his actions. When Chauvelin paused he too stood still, not because of the grip on his arm, but because of that curious numbing of his will.
Vague, confused thoughts were floating through his brain, the most dominant one among them being that Fate had effectually ordained everything for the best. Here was Chauvelin, a man who hated him, who, of course, would wish to see him dead.

===Chinese===

肖维兰！这个人此刻在此地出现，使过去几天的种种更像一场梦了。肖维兰！——他阿尔芒和妹妹玛格丽特在世上最致命的仇敌。肖维兰！——掌管共和国特务机关的那位凶神。肖维兰——变成革命者的贵族，变成密探的外交官，红花侠屡屡受挫的对头。
他立在暗处，只有头顶壁上一盏油灯的微光勾出个模糊轮廓。他黑貂色衣裳上的潮气在曳曳灯光里闪灼，像罩了一层晶纱；它凝在帽檐上，凝在斗篷的折缝里；喉咙和腕上的褶边湿答答地垂着，已见污渍。
他松开了阿尔芒的胳膊，双手笼回斗篷底下；那双深陷的淡色眼睛，郑重地停在年轻人脸上。
“不知怎么，我总有预感，”肖维兰平静地接着说，“你这次容留巴黎期间，你我总会碰面。我听朋友埃龙说你进了城；可惜他几乎一摸到你的踪就又丢了；我也开始担心，咱们共同的那位永远猜不透的朋友红花侠，把你摄走了——那可要叫我大失所望。”
此刻他又扶住阿尔芒的肘，却扶得很轻，倒像个老战友喜相逢，预备畅谈一阵的样子。他在前头引路回到门边；哨兵一见他斗篷下露出的三色腰带，行了个礼。石垣下，肖维兰收住脚。
这里安静而僻静。那群士兵立在拱道的另一头，听不见这边，人影也只在家家的黑暗里辨出个大概。
阿尔芒像中了邪，身不由己地跟着仇敌走。肖维兰停，他也停——不是因为臂上那只手，而是因为意志上那阵古怪的麻瘆。
模糊纷乱的念头在脑子里漂浮，占上风的一个是：命运已把一切安排到了好处。肖维兰在这里——一个恨他的人，当然巴不得他死。

===Original===

Well, surely it must be an easier matter now to barter his own life for that of Jeanne; she had only been arrested on suspicion of harbouring him, who was a known traitor to the Republic; then, with his capture and speedy death, her supposed guilt would, he hoped, be forgiven. These people could have no ill-will against her, and actors and actresses were always leniently dealt with when possible. Then surely, surely, he could serve Jeanne best by his own arrest and condemnation, than by working to rescue her from prison.
In the meanwhile Chauvelin shook the damp from off his cloak, talking all the time in his own peculiar, gently ironical manner.
“Lady Blakeney?” he was saying﻿—“I hope that she is well!”
“I thank you, sir,” murmured Armand mechanically.
“And my dear friend, Sir Percy Blakeney? I had hoped to meet him in Paris. Ah! but no doubt he has been busy﻿—very busy; but I live in hopes﻿—I live in hopes. See how kindly Chance has treated me,” he continued in the same bland and mocking tones. “I was taking a stroll in these parts, scarce hoping to meet a friend, when, passing the postern-gate of this charming hostelry, whom should I see but my amiable friend St. Just striving to gain admission. But, la! here am I talking of myself, and I am not reassured as to your state of health. You felt faint just now, did you not? The air about this building is very dank and close. I hope you feel better now. Command me, pray, if I can be of service to you in any way.”
Whilst Chauvelin talked he had drawn Armand after him into the lodge of the concierge. The young man now made a great effort to pull himself vigorously together and to steady his nerves.
He had his wish. He was inside the Temple prison now, not far from Jeanne, and though his enemy was older and less vigorous than himself, and the door of the concierge’s lodge stood wide open, he knew that he was indeed as effectually a prisoner already as if the door of one of the numerous cells in this gigantic building had been bolted and barred upon him.
This knowledge helped him to recover his complete presence of mind. No thought of fighting or trying to escape his fate entered his head for a moment. It had been useless probably, and undoubtedly it was better so.

===Chinese===

好，那么拿自己这条命换让娜那条，眼下想必容易多了；她不过因窝藏他这个共和国众所周知的叛徒而涉嫌被捕；那么他一落网、一伏法，她那嫌疑罪名，他指望，便可宽免。这班人对她本无仇恨；而且伶人只要可能，向来从宽发落。那么，比起设法劫狱，他自己的被捕与定罪，岂不才是最能帮让娜的路？
这当儿，肖维兰正抖去斗篷上的潮气，口里用他那套特有的温文讥诮，絮絮说着。
“布莱克尼夫人？”他说——“她身子可好！”
“谢谢您，先生。”阿尔芒机械地喃喃。
“还有我亲爱的朋友珀西·布莱克尼爵士？我本指望在巴黎见他。啊！不过他想必一定很忙——很忙；可我怀着希望——怀着希望。燋机运待我多么仁厚，”他还是那种温和的、带刺的调子，“我在这附近散步，本不敢指望遇见朋友，不料路过这家迷人客栈的后门，竟看见我可爱的朋友圣茹斯特在设法进门。不过，嗐！光顾着说自己了——您的身子我可还不放心呢。方才您发晕了，是不是？这房子四周的空气又湿又闷。现在好些了吧。若有用得着我的地方，务必吩咐。”
肖维兰说话间，已把阿尔芒引进门房的屋子。年轻人此刻竭力振作，把神经攑稳。
他如愿了。他此刻就在圣殿塔监狱里，离让娜不远；虽然这仇敌比他年老力衰，门房的门又敞得大开，可他知道，自己实实在在已是阶下囚——同这座巨厦里哪一间牢房的门在他身后上了闩、落了杠，毫无二致。
这份明白，反倒帮他彻底回过神来。搏斗、逃命，一概没进过他的脑子。那多半无用，况且这样确实更好。

===Original===

If he only could see Jeanne, and assure himself that she would be safe in consequence of his own arrest, then, indeed, life could hold no greater happiness for him.
Above all now he wanted to be cool and calculating, to curb the excitement which the Latin blood in him called forth at every mention of the loved one’s name. He tried to think of Percy, of his calmness, his easy banter with an enemy; he resolved to act as Percy would act under these circumstances.
Firstly, he steadied his voice, and drew his well-knit, slim figure upright. He called to mind all his friends in England, with their rigid manners, their impassiveness in the face of trying situations. There was Lord Tony, for instance, always ready with some boyish joke, with boyish impertinence always hovering on his tongue. Armand tried to emulate Lord Tony’s manner, and to borrow something of Percy’s calm impudence.
“Citizen Chauvelin,” he said, as soon as he felt quite sure of the steadiness of his voice and the calmness of his manner, “I wonder if you are quite certain that that light grip which you have on my arm is sufficient to keep me here walking quietly by your side instead of knocking you down, as I certainly feel inclined to do, for I am a younger, more vigorous man than you.”
“H’m!” said Chauvelin, who made pretence to ponder over this difficult problem; “like you, citizen St. Just, I wonder﻿—”
“It could easily be done, you know.”
“Fairly easily,” rejoined the other; “but there is the guard; it is numerous and strong in this building, and﻿—”
“The gloom would help me; it is dark in the corridors, and a desperate man takes risks, remember﻿—”
“Quite so! And you, citizen St. Just, are a desperate man just now.”
“My sister Marguerite is not here, citizen Chauvelin. You cannot barter my life for that of your enemy.”
“No! no! no!” rejoined Chauvelin blandly; “not for that of my enemy, I know, but﻿—”
Armand caught at his words like a drowning man at a reed.
“For hers!” he exclaimed.
“For hers?” queried the other with obvious puzzlement.
“Mademoiselle Lange,” continued Armand with all the egoistic ardour of the lover who believes that the attention of the entire world is concentrated upon his beloved.
“Mademoiselle Lange! You will set her free now that I am in your power.”
Chauvelin smiled, his usual suave, enigmatical smile.
“Ah, yes!” he said. “Mademoiselle Lange. I had forgotten.”

===Chinese===

只要能见上让娜一面，确认她将因他的被捕而安然，那这辈子，实在再没有更大的幸福了。
此刻他首先要求冷静、会算计，要压住拉丁血液里每一提及爱人名字便腾起的冲动。他试着想珀西，想他的镇定，他同敌人周旋时那副轻松的打趣；他打定主意，照珀西在这种情况下会做的样子做。
先稳住嗓音，把瘦削结实的身量挺直。他一一想起英格兰的诸位朋友，想起他们刻板的做派、临大变而不动声色的本领。比如托尼勋爵，学生腔的笑谈永远现成，少年人的孟浪永远挂在舌尖。阿尔芒试着摹仿托尼勋爵的风度，再借一点珀西那份从容的无赖。
“肖维兰公民，”等嗓音和仪态都有了十分的把握，他才说，“我倒想请教：您把着我胳膊的这一点点力气，您就确信，够把我安安分分拴在您身边走，而不是一拳把您撞倒？我是真有这个心气的，您知道，我比您年轻，比您健壮。”
“嗯！”肖维兰应了一声，装作思索这个难题；“同您一样，圣茹斯特公民，我也纳闷——”
“这轻而易举，您知道的。”
“相当容易，”对方接道，“可这里有卫兵；这座楼里卫兵又多又壮，而且——”
“黑暗帮我的忙；走廊里黑；亡命的人是什么险都敢冒的，记住——”
“正是！而您，圣茹斯特公民，眼下正是个亡命的人。”
“我妹妹玛格丽特不在这里，肖维兰公民。您拿我的命，换不来您仇敌的命了。”
“不！不！不！”肖维兰温和地应道，“换仇敌的，我知道换不来；不过——”
阿尔芒抓住他的话，像溺水人抓一根芦苇。
“换她的！”他喊。
“换她的？”对方问，一脸的莫名。
“郎格小姐，”阿尔芒接着说，带着情人特有的那股自恋的热切——总以为全世界的目光都聚在他的爱人身上。“郎格小姐！我既落在您手里，您就得放她走。”
肖维兰笑了，还是他那种圆滑莫测的笑。
“啊，是！”他说，“郎格小姐。我倒忘了。”

===Original===

“Forgotten, man?﻿—forgotten that those murderous dogs have arrested her?﻿—the best, the purest, this vile, degraded country has ever produced. She sheltered me one day just for an hour. I am a traitor to the Republic﻿—I own it. I’ll make full confession; but she knew nothing of this. I deceived her; she is quite innocent, you understand? I’ll make full confession, but you must set her free.”
He had gradually worked himself up again to a state of feverish excitement. Through the darkness which hung about in this small room he tried to peer in Chauvelin’s impassive face.
“Easy, easy, my young friend,” said the other placidly; “you seem to imagine that I have something to do with the arrest of the lady in whom you take so deep an interest. You forget that now I am but a discredited servant of the Republic whom I failed to serve in her need. My life is only granted me out of pity for my efforts, which were genuine if not successful. I have no power to set anyone free.”
“Nor to arrest me now, in that case!” retorted Armand.
Chauvelin paused a moment before he replied with a deprecating smile:
“Only to denounce you, perhaps. I am still an agent of the Committee of General Security.”
“Then all is for the best!” exclaimed St. Just eagerly. “You shall denounce me to the Committee. They will be glad of my arrest, I assure you. I have been a marked man for some time. I had intended to evade arrest and to work for the rescue of Mademoiselle Lange; but I will give up all thought of that﻿—I will deliver myself into your hands absolutely; nay, more, I will give you my most solemn word of honour that not only will I make no attempt at escape, but that I will not allow anyone to help me to do so. I will be a passive and willing prisoner if you, on the other hand, will effect Mademoiselle Lange’s release.”
“H’m!” mused Chauvelin again, “it sounds feasible.”
“It does! it does!” rejoined Armand, whose excitement was at fever-pitch. “My arrest, my condemnation, my death, will be of vast deal more importance to you than that of a young and innocent girl against whom unlikely charges would have to be tricked up, and whose acquittal mayhap public feeling might demand. As for me, I shall be an easy prey; my known counterrevolutionary principles, my sister’s marriage with a foreigner﻿—”
“Your connection with the Scarlet Pimpernel,” suggested Chauvelin blandly.
“Quite so.

===Chinese===

“忘了？你这个人——忘了那群杀人的狗把她抓走了？忘了她是这个卑劣、堕落的国度产出的最好、最纯洁的人？她收留过我，只收留了一个钟头。我是共和国的叛徒——我认。我全招；可她对此一无所知。是我骗了她；她是全然清白的，你明白么？我全招，但你们必须放她。”
他不知不觉又把自己通到了发热的地步。他隔着这间小屋里弥漫的黑暗，竭力去看肖维兰那张不动声色的脸。
“慢来，慢来，我年轻的朋友，”对方安详地说，“您似乎以为，同您这样关切的那位小姐的被捕，我有什么干系。您忘了，如今我不过是共和国失势的仆役，当初没能在她危难时效力。如今留我一条命，不过是对我那些努力的一点体恤——努力是真心，虽然不成功。放人，我无此权。”
“那也无权逮捕我！”阿尔芒顶道。
肖维兰顿了一顿，才舍笑答道：
“告发，或许还有权。我仍是治安委员会的密探。”
“那就再好不过！”圣茹斯特喜出望外。“您就把我告到委员会去。我的被捕他们必定乐见，我保证。我早就是挂了号的人了。我本打算躲抓捕，设法营救郎格小姐；可这些念头我全撂下——我把我整个人交到您手里；不止如此，我以最庄严的名誉向您担保：我不但自己不逃，也绝不让任何人帮我逃。我做一个束手就擒、甘愿伏罪的囚徒——只要您那边，把郎格小姐放了。”
“嗯！”肖维兰又沉吟，“听来可行。”
“可行！当然可行！”阿尔芒接道，兴奋到了极点。“我的被捕、我的定罪、我的死，对你们远比一个年轻无辜的姑娘重要得多——对付她还得捏造站不住的罪名，而且民心兴许还要求开释她。至于我，唾手可得：我那众所周知的反革命立场，我妹妹同外国人的婚姻——”
“还有您同红花侠的干系。”肖维兰温和地提示。
“正是。

===Original===

I should not defend myself﻿—”
“And your enigmatical friend would not attempt your rescue. *C’est entendu*,” said Chauvelin with his wonted blandness. “Then, my dear, enthusiastic young friend, shall we adjourn to the office of my colleague, citizen Héron, who is chief agent of the Committee of General Security, and will receive your﻿—did you say confession?﻿—and note the conditions under which you place yourself absolutely in the hands of the Public Prosecutor and subsequently of the executioner. Is that it?”
Armand was too full of schemes, too full of thoughts of Jeanne to note the tone of quiet irony with which Chauvelin had been speaking all along. With the unreasoning egoism of youth he was quite convinced that his own arrest, his own affairs were as important to this entire nation in revolution as they were to himself. At moments like these it is difficult to envisage a desperate situation clearly, and to a young man in love the fate of the beloved never seems desperate whilst he himself is alive and ready for every sacrifice for her sake. “My life for hers” is the sublime if often foolish battle-cry that has so often resulted in wholesale destruction. Armand at this moment, when he fondly believed that he was making a bargain with the most astute, most unscrupulous spy this revolutionary Government had in its pay﻿—Armand just then had absolutely forgotten his chief, his friends, the league of mercy and help to which he belonged.
Enthusiasm and the spirit of self-sacrifice were carrying him away. He watched his enemy with glowing eyes as one who looks on the arbiter of his fate.
Chauvelin, without another word, beckoned to him to follow. He led the way out of the lodge, then, turning sharply to his left, he reached the wide quadrangle with the covered passage running right round it, the same which de Batz had traversed two evenings ago when he went to visit Héron.
Armand, with a light heart and springy step, followed him as if he were going to a feast where he would meet Jeanne, where he would kneel at her feet, kiss her hands, and lead her triumphantly to freedom and to happiness.

===Chinese===

我到时不辩一言——”
“而您那位猜不透的朋友，也不会来救您。*一言为定*，”肖维兰用惯常的圆滑说道。“那么，我亲爱的热血青年朋友，咱们这就移步到我同僚、治安委员会首席密探埃龙公民的办公室去；他会收下您的——您方才说自供书？——并记下您开出的条件：把您全然交到公诉人手里，随后交到刽子手手里。是这样吧？”
阿尔芒满脑子计划、满脑子让娜，全然没听出肖维兰一路话里那股静静的讥讽。他怀着年轻人不动脑筋的自恋，深信自己的被捕、自己的事，对这整个革命中的国家，同对他本人一样重大。这样的时刻，人很难把绝境看分明；对一个恋爱中的年轻人，只要他自己活着、甘愿为她牺牲一切，爱人的命运就永远算不得绝境。“以我换她”——这句崇高却时常愚蠢的战号，多少回换来的是成批的毁灭。此刻的阿尔芒，自以为在同这个革命政府豢养的最精明、最无所不为的密探讨价还价——此刻的阿尔芒，把首领、把朋友、把他所属的那座仁义救助的同盟，忘了个干净。
热忱与自我牺牲的精神冲昏了他。他目光灼灼地望着仇敌，像望一位命运的仲裁者。
肖维兰不再多说，一招手唤他跟上。他引路出了门房，随即猛地左转，走进那座四合大院——四边环着带顶的回廊，正是两晚前德巴茨去见埃龙时穿过的那一座。
阿尔芒脚步轻快、心里松泛地跟着，倒像去赶一场盛宴：在那儿他将见到让娜，跪在她脚边，亲她的手，得胜似的领她走向自由与幸福。

## XVIII The Removal / 转移
===Original===

Chauvelin no longer made any pretence to hold Armand by the arm. By temperament as well as by profession a spy, there was one subject at least which he had mastered thoroughly: that was the study of human nature. Though occasionally an exceptionally complex mental organisation baffled him﻿—as in the case of Sir Percy Blakeney﻿—he prided himself, and justly, too, on reading natures like that of Armand St. Just as he would an open book.
The excitable disposition of the Latin races he knew out and out; he knew exactly how far a sentimental situation would lead a young Frenchman like Armand, who was by disposition chivalrous, and by temperament essentially passionate. Above all things, he knew when and how far he could trust a man to do either a sublime action or an essentially foolish one.
Therefore he walked along contentedly now, not even looking back to see whether St. Just was following him. He knew that he did.
His thoughts only dwelt on the young enthusiast﻿—in his mind he called him the young fool﻿—in order to weigh in the balance the mighty possibilities that would accrue from the present sequence of events. The fixed idea ever working in the man’s scheming brain had already transformed a vague belief into a certainty. That the Scarlet Pimpernel was in Paris at the present moment Chauvelin had now become convinced. How far he could turn the capture of Armand St. Just to the triumph of his own ends remained to be seen.
But this he did know: the Scarlet Pimpernel﻿—the man whom he had learned to know, to dread, and even in a grudging manner to admire﻿—was not like to leave one of his followers in the lurch. Marguerite’s brother in the Temple would be the surest decoy for the elusive meddler who still, and in spite of all care and precaution, continued to baffle the army of spies set upon his track.
Chauvelin could hear Armand’s light, elastic footsteps resounding behind him on the flagstones. A world of intoxicating possibilities surged up before him. Ambition, which two successive dire failures had atrophied in his breast, once more rose up buoyant and hopeful. Once he had sworn to lay the Scarlet Pimpernel by the heels, and that oath was not yet wholly forgotten; it had lain dormant after the catastrophe of Boulogne, but with the sight of Armand St. Just it had reawakened and confronted him again with the strength of a likely fulfilment.

===Chinese===

肖维兰不再装模作样地拽着阿尔芒的胳膊了。他生性是密探，职业也是密探，至少有一门学问他早已吃透：人心。偶尔也有格外复杂的心性叫他无可奈何——如珀西·布莱克尼爵士那样的人——可读阿尔芒·圣茹斯特这一类性子，他自诩能像摊开一本书那样读；这自诩也不算过分。
拉丁民族的易激动，他了如指掌；像阿尔芒这样天性侠义、气质火烈的法国青年，一碰上感情的关口会走到哪一步，他拿捏得准准的。尤其要紧的一门：什么时候、到什么份上，可以放心让一个人去干一件壮举——或者一件十足的傻事，他都知道。
所以他此刻一路走得心安理得，连头也不回，看圣茹斯特跟没跟。他知道他跟在。
他心里只盘算着这个热心少年——在他心里，这是“年轻的傻瓜”——把眼下这串事态将结出的种种可能，放到天平上掂量。这个人算计的脑子里那股盘踞的执念，早已把一个模糊的信念炼成了定论：红花侠此刻就在巴黎，这一点肖维兰如今已确信无疑。能把这个阿尔芒·圣茹斯特的被捕，翻弄到什么地步来成就自己的目的，且看下文。
但这一点他心中有数：红花侠——那个他识得、怕过、甚至不由得不佩的人——不会丢下自己的党羽不管。玛格丽特的弟弟一关进圣殿塔，便是钓那条滑溜溜的爱管闲事的鱼最稳的饵——任凭多少防范，那人至今仍在戏弄追在他后头的那支密探大军。
阿尔芒轻快弹性的脚步声，在他身后的石板上回响，肖维兰听得清楚。一整个世界的醉人可能涌到他眼前。野心——那被接连两场惨败在他胸膛里麻痒掉的东西——又昂然升起，满怀着望。他曾发誓要把红花侠绊倒擒拿；那个誓言还不曾全忘：布洛涅的惨败之后它蛰伏了，可一见到阿尔芒·圣茹斯特，它醒了，又带着即将应验的气势，重新立到他面前。

===Original===

The courtyard looked gloomy and deserted. The thin drizzle which still fell from a persistently leaden sky effectually held every outline of masonry, of column, or of gate hidden as beneath a shroud. The corridor which skirted it all round was ill-lighted save by an occasional oil-lamp fixed in the wall.
But Chauvelin knew his way well. Héron’s lodgings gave on the second courtyard, the Square du Nazaret, and the way thither led past the main square tower, in the top floor of which the uncrowned King of France eked out his miserable existence as the plaything of a rough cobbler and his wife.
Just beneath its frowning bastions Chauvelin turned back towards Armand. He pointed with a careless hand upwards to the central tower.
“We have got little Capet in there,” he said dryly. “Your chivalrous Scarlet Pimpernel has not ventured in these precincts yet, you see.”
Armand was silent. He had no difficulty in looking unconcerned; his thoughts were so full of Jeanne that he cared but little at this moment for any Bourbon king or for the destinies of France.
Now the two men reached the postern gate. A couple of sentinels were standing by, but the gate itself was open, and from within there came the sound of bustle and of noise, of a good deal of swearing, and also of loud laughter.
The guardroom gave on the left of the gate, and the laughter came from there. It was brilliantly lighted, and Armand, peering in, in the wake of Chauvelin, could see groups of soldiers sitting and standing about. There was a table in the centre of the room, and on it a number of jugs and pewter mugs, packets of cards, and overturned boxes of dice.
But the bustle did not come from the guardroom; it came from the landing and the stone stairs beyond.
Chauvelin, apparently curious, had passed through the gate, and Armand followed him. The light from the open door of the guardroom cut sharply across the landing, making the gloom beyond appear more dense and almost solid. From out the darkness, fitfully intersected by a lantern apparently carried to and fro, moving figures loomed out ghostlike and weirdly gigantic.

===Chinese===

院子看上去阴沉空旷。缠绵的细雨仍从一片死铅色的天上下着，把石造的每一道轮廓——柱子也好，门也要也好——都裹进一幅殓衣似的雨幕里。环绕四边的回廊灯光稀落，只靠壁上间或一盏油灯。
可肖维兰路径娴熟。埃龙的住处开向第二个院子，拿撒勒广场院；去那儿要路过正中的大方塔——塔的顶层里，法兰西未加冕的国王，正作为一个粗鞋匠和他老婆的玩物，打发着他凄惨的日子。
就在塔那阴森的堆堞底下，肖维兰回身朝向阿尔芒，一只手随意地往上一指。
“小卡佩就关在那上头，”他干巴巴地说，“您燋，您那位侠义的红花侠，可还不敢闯进这块地界。”
阿尔芒沉默。他不难装出若无其事：满心都是让娜，此刻任什么波旁的国王、法兰西的国运，他都顾不得了。
两人到了后门。门边站着两名哨兵，门本身开着，里面传出忙乱和喧哗，不少咒骂，还有响亮的大笑。
卫兵室在门左边，笑声就从那儿来。屋里灯火通明；阿尔芒跟在肖维兰身后往里張望，看见成堆的士兵或坐或立。屋子正中一张桌子，上面摆着酒壶和锡杯，几叠纸牌，还有掀翻的骰子盒。
可忙乱不是出自卫兵室，而是出自楼梯口和里面的石楼梯。
肖维兰显然起了好奇，穿过门洞；阿尔芒跟着。卫兵室洞开的门里射出的光，锐利地切过楼梯口，把外面的黑暗衬得更浓，几乎凝成实体。黑暗里有一盏显然来回移动的灯笼，光一闪一闪地切开夜，人影幢幢，像鬼魅，也怪诞地巨大。

===Original===

Soon Armand distinguished a number of large objects that encumbered the landing, and as he and Chauvelin left the sharp light of the guardroom behind them, he could see that the large objects were pieces of furniture of every shape and size; a wooden bedstead﻿—dismantled﻿—leaned against the wall, a black horsehair sofa blocked the way to the tower stairs, and there were numberless chairs and several tables piled one on the top of the other.
In the midst of this litter a stout, flabby-cheeked man stood, apparently giving directions as to its removal to persons at present unseen.
“*Holà*, Papa Simon!” exclaimed Chauvelin jovially; “moving out today? What?”
“Yes, thank the Lord!﻿—if there be a Lord!” retorted the other curtly. “Is that you, citizen Chauvelin?”
“In person, citizen. I did not know you were leaving quite so soon. Is citizen Héron anywhere about?”
“Just left,” replied Simon. “He had a last look at Capet just before my wife locked the brat up in the inner room. Now he’s gone back to his lodgings.”
A man carrying a chest, empty of its drawers, on his back now came stumbling down the tower staircase. Madame Simon followed close on his heels, steadying the chest with one hand.
“We had better begin to load up the cart,” she called to her husband in a high-pitched querulous voice; “the corridor is getting too much encumbered.”
She looked suspiciously at Chauvelin and at Armand, and when she encountered the former’s bland, unconcerned gaze she suddenly shivered and drew her black shawl closer round her shoulders.
“Bah!” she said, “I shall be glad to get out of this Godforsaken hole. I hate the very sight of these walls.”
“Indeed, the citizeness does not look over robust in health,” said Chauvelin with studied politeness. “The stay in the tower did not, mayhap, bring forth all the fruits of prosperity which she had anticipated.”
The woman eyed him with dark suspicion lurking in her hollow eyes.
“I don’t know what you mean, citizen,” she said with a shrug of her wide shoulders.
“Oh! I meant nothing,” rejoined Chauvelin, smiling. “I am so interested in your removal; busy man as I am, it has amused me to watch you. Whom have you got to help you with the furniture?”
“Dupont, the man-of-all-work, from the concierge,” said Simon curtly. “Citizen Héron would not allow anyone to come in from the outside.”
“Rightly too. Have the new commissaries come yet?”

===Chinese===

不多时，阿尔芒辨出一堆堵在楼梯口的大件，等他和肖维兰把卫兵室那道亮光甩在身后，才看清那些大件是各式各样、大大小小的家具：一张拆卸了的木床斜靠着墙，一张黑马鬃沙发堵住通往塔梯的路，还有数不清的椅子和好几张桌子，叠在一起。
这一堆乱七八糟中间站着一个胖胖的男人，面颊松肥，正对眼下看不见的人发号施令，指挥搬运。
“喂，西蒙老爹！”肖维兰快活地喊，“今儿搬家？啊？”
“可不，谢天谢地——天要是有的话！”对方没好气地答。“是您哪，肖维兰公民？”
“正是本人，公民。我倒不知道您走得这么快。埃龙公民在附近么？”
“刚走，”西蒙答，“我老婆把那小崽子锁进里屋之前，他最后看了一眼卡佩。这会儿回他住处去了。”
一个背着空抽屉柜的男人正趔趄着从塔梯上下来，西蒙太太紧跟在他脚后，一只手扶着柜子。
“咱们还是赶紧装车吧，”她冲丈夫高声抱怨，“过道里已经堆得下不去脚了。”
她狐疑地看了看肖维兰和阿尔芒；一撞上前者那温和淡漠的目光，她突然打了个寒噤，把黑披巾往肩上裹紧了些。
“呸！”她说，“能离开这个鬼地方我才高兴。这些墙我看都看腻了。”
“可不是，女公民气色瞧着不大好，”肖维兰彬彬有礼，礼貌得刻意，“在塔里这一住，怕是没有结出她当初指望的那么多顺遂的果子吧。”
女人睃着他，眍䁖的眼睛里藏着阴沉沉的疑。
“不懂您什么意思，公民。”她宽阔的肩膀一耸。
“哦！没什么意思，”肖维兰笑着接道，“我对您的搬家太感兴趣了；我虽是个忙人，看您搬家却看得很乐。请了哪些人帮您搬家具呀？”
“杜邦，门房那儿的杂役，”西蒙简短地说，“埃龙公民不许外头的人进来。”
“也在理。新任的专员们到了么？

===Original===

“Only citizen Cochefer. He is waiting upstairs for the others.”
“And Capet?”
“He is all safe. Citizen Héron came to see him, and then he told me to lock the little vermin up in the inner room. Citizen Cochefer had just arrived by that time, and he has remained in charge.”
During all this while the man with the chest on his back was waiting for orders. Bent nearly double, he was grumbling audibly at his uncomfortable position.
“Does the citizen want to break my back?” he muttered. “We had best get along﻿—*quoi*?”
He asked if he should begin to carry the furniture out into the street.
“Two sous have I got to pay every ten minutes to the lad who holds my nag,” he said, muttering under his breath; “we shall be all night at this rate.”
“Begin to load then,” commanded Simon gruffly. “Here!﻿—begin with this sofa.”
“You’ll have to give me a hand with that,” said the man. “Wait a bit; I’ll just see that everything is all right in the cart. I’ll be back directly.”
“Take something with you then as you are going down,” said Madame Simon in her querulous voice.
The man picked up a basket of linen that stood in the angle by the door. He hoisted it on his back and shuffled away with it across the landing and out through the gate.
“How did Capet like parting from his papa and *maman*?” asked Chauvelin with a laugh.
“H’m!” growled Simon laconically. “He will find out soon enough how well off he was under our care.”
“Have the other commissaries come yet?”
“No. But they will be here directly. Citizen Cochefer is upstairs mounting guard over Capet.”
“Well, goodbye, Papa Simon,” concluded Chauvelin jovially. “Citizeness, your servant!”
He bowed with unconcealed irony to the cobbler’s wife, and nodded to Simon, who expressed by a volley of motley oaths his exact feelings with regard to all the agents of the Committee of General Security.
“Six months of this penal servitude have we had,” he said roughly, “and no thanks or pension. I would as soon serve a ci-devant aristo as your accursed Committee.”
The man Dupont had returned. Stolidly, after the fashion of his kind, he commenced the removal of citizen Simon’s goods. He seemed a clumsy enough creature, and Simon and his wife had to do most of the work themselves.
Chauvelin watched the moving forms for a while, then he shrugged his shoulders with a laugh of indifference, and turned on his heel.

===Chinese===

“只到了科什费尔公民。他在楼上等旁人。”
“那卡佩呢？”
“稳当着呢。埃龙公民来看过他，又叫我把他那小畜生锁进里屋。那时科什费尔公民刚到，就留下他看着了。”
这一阵，那背柜子的男人一直站着候命。他几乎弯成对折，嘴里嘟嘟囔囔，抱怨这姿势难受。
“这位公民是想压断我的腰么？”他咕哝，“咱们还是赶紧的吧——成不成？”
他问是不是可以开始把家具往外搬到街上。
“每十分钟我就得付两苏给替我看牲口的小子，”他压着嗓子嘟囔，“照这个磨法，咱们得干一宿。”
“那就开始装车，”西蒙粗声下令，“喂！——先搬这沙发。”
“这个您可得搭把手，”那人说，“等等；我先下去看看车装得怎么样。马上回来。”
“那就顺手指点东西下去，”西蒙太太抱怨着说。
那人提起门角边一篮子衣裳，背到背上，趿拉着穿过楼梯口，出了门洞。
“卡佩同他爸爸妈妈分手，舍得幺？”肖维兰笑着问。
“哼！”西蒙懒懒地吼了一声，“他很快就知道在我们手底下有多享福了。”
“别的专员到了么？”
“没。不过也快了。科什费尔公民在楼上看守卡佩。”
“好，再会，西蒙老爹，”肖维兰欢快地收尾，“女公民，告辞！”
他对鞋匠老婆一躬到地，讽刺毫不掩饰；又对西蒙点点头。西蒙回敬了一串五花八门的咒骂，把他对照安委员会全体密探的真实感想，表达得淋漓尽致。
“这苦役我们服了半年，”他粗声说，“没谢也没饷。老子宁可视候前贵族，也不伺候你们那该咒的委员会。”
杜邦回来了。照他那号人的做派，闷头闷脑地开始搬运西蒙公民的家什。这蠢家伙手脚似乎很笨，西蒙两口子只好亲自动手干了大半。
肖维兰看了一阵挪动的人影，随后无所谓地耸耸肩，一笑，转身走了。

## XIX It Is About the Dauphin / 事关王太子
===Original===

Héron was not at his lodgings when, at last, after vigorous pulls at the bell, a great deal of waiting and much cursing, Chauvelin, closely followed by Armand, was introduced in the chief agent’s office.
The soldier who acted as servant said that citizen Héron had gone out to sup, but would surely be home again by eight o’clock. Armand by this time was so dazed with fatigue that he sank on a chair like a log, and remained there staring into the fire, unconscious of the flight of time.
Anon Héron came home. He nodded to Chauvelin, and threw but a cursory glance on Armand.
“Five minutes, citizen,” he said, with a rough attempt at an apology. “I am sorry to keep you waiting, but the new commissaries have arrived who are to take charge of Capet. The Simons have just gone, and I want to assure myself that everything is all right in the Tower. Cochefer has been in charge, but I like to cast an eye over the brat every day myself.”
He went out again, slamming the door behind him. His heavy footsteps were heard treading the flagstones of the corridor, and gradually dying away in the distance. Armand had paid no heed either to his entrance or to his exit. He was only conscious of an intense weariness, and would at this moment gladly have laid his head on the scaffold if on it he could find rest.
A white-faced clock on the wall ticked off the seconds one by one. From the street below came the muffled sounds of wheeled traffic on the soft mud of the road; it was raining more heavily now, and from time to time a gust of wind rattled the small windows in their dilapidated frames, or hurled a shower of heavy drops against the panes.
The heat from the stove had made Armand drowsy; his head fell forward on his chest. Chauvelin, with his hands held behind his back, paced ceaselessly up and down the narrow room.
Suddenly Armand started﻿—wide awake now. Hurried footsteps on the flagstones outside, a hoarse shout, a banging of heavy doors, and the next moment Héron stood once more on the threshold of the room. Armand, with wide-opened eyes, gazed on him in wonder. The whole appearance of the man had changed. He looked ten years older, with lank, dishevelled hair hanging matted over a moist forehead, the cheeks ashen-white, the full lips bloodless and hanging, flabby and parted, displaying both rows of yellow teeth that shook against each other.

===Chinese===

好容易拽响门铃、久等、又骂了半晌之后，肖维兰领着紧随身后的阿尔芒进了首席密探的办公室，埃龙却不在。
充作仆役的士兵说，埃龙公民出去吃晚饭了，八点准回。阿尔芒这时已乏得发懵，像根本头似的倒在椅子上，两眼直勾勾望着炉火，时光流逝全然不觉。
不多时埃龙回来了。他朝肖维兰点点头，对阿尔芒只扫了一眼。
“五分钟，公民，”他粗声粗气，勉强算致歉，“叫你久等了，对不住；接手卡佩的新专员们到了。西蒙两口子刚走，我要亲自去看一眼塔里是不是都安顿妥了。科什费尔一直看着呢，可那小畜生，我每天都乐意亲自瞄一眼。”
他砰地带上门又出去了。沉重的脚步踏过走廊的石板，渐渐远去。阿尔芒对他的进出一概无觉。他只觉得极度的倦怠，此刻若能在断头台上寻得安歇，他情愿把头摘上去。
墙上一只白脸钟，一秒一秒地数着。楼下街上，车轮碾过路面软泥的声音闷闷传来；雨下大了，一阵阵风摇得小窗在破框子里哐哐响，或泼一阵大雨点在玻璃上。
炉火烘得阿尔芒昏昏欲睡；头垂到了胸前。肖维兰双手背在身后，在窄屋里陵着不停。
突然阿尔芒一惊——这回彻底醒了。外头石板上一阵急步、一声嘶喊、几记重门砰响；转眼间，埃龙又一次立在门口。阿尔芒睁大眼睛，惊愕地望着他。这人的模样全变了：老了十岁，稀脏的头发纠结着耷在汗涔涔的额头上，两颊灰白，厚厚的嘴唇没了血色，松松地耷着、张着，露出两排相磕的黄牙。

===Original===

The whole figure looked bowed, as if shrunk within itself.
Chauvelin had paused in his restless walk. He gazed on his colleague, a frown of puzzlement on his pale, set face.
“Capet!” he exclaimed, as soon as he had taken in every detail of Héron’s altered appearance, and seen the look of wild terror that literally distorted his face.
Héron could not speak; his teeth were chattering in his mouth, and his tongue seemed paralysed. Chauvelin went up to him. He was several inches shorter than his colleague, but at this moment he seemed to be towering over him like an avenging spirit. He placed a firm hand on the other’s bowed shoulders.
“Capet has gone﻿—is that it?” he queried peremptorily.
The look of terror increased in Héron’s eyes, giving its mute reply.
“How? When?”
But for the moment the man was speechless. An almost maniacal fear seemed to hold him in its grip. With an impatient oath Chauvelin turned away from him.
“Brandy!” he said curtly, speaking to Armand.
A bottle and glass were found in the cupboard. It was St. Just who poured out the brandy and held it to Héron’s lips. Chauvelin was once more pacing up and down the room in angry impatience.
“Pull yourself together, man,” he said roughly after a while, “and try and tell me what has occurred.”
Héron had sunk into a chair. He passed a trembling hand once or twice over his forehead.
“Capet has disappeared,” he murmured; “he must have been spirited away while the Simons were moving their furniture. That accursed Cochefer was completely taken in.”
Héron spoke in a toneless voice, hardly above a whisper, and like one whose throat is dry and mouth parched. But the brandy had revived him somewhat, and his eyes lost their former glassy look.
“How?” asked Chauvelin curtly.
“I was just leaving the Tower when he arrived. I spoke to him at the door. I had seen Capet safely installed in the room, and gave orders to the woman Simon to let citizen Cochefer have a look at him, too, and then to lock up the brat in the inner room and install Cochefer in the antechamber on guard. I stood talking to Cochefer for a few moments in the antechamber. The woman Simon and the man-of-all-work, Dupont﻿—whom I know well﻿—were busy with the furniture. There could not have been anyone else concealed about the place﻿—that I’ll swear.

===Chinese===

整个身形佝偻了，仿佛缩进了自己里头。
肖维兰在踱步中收住了脚。他望着同僚，苍白紧绷的脸上拧着一道困惑。
“卡佩！”他叫道——埃龙变样的每个细节他都扫过了，那张脸上明明白白的、扭曲了的惊怖他也看见了。
埃龙说不出话；牙关在嘴里格格打战，舌头像瘫了。肖维兰走上前去。他比同僚矮着好几英寸，此刻却像一尊复仇的凶神罩在他头顶。他把一只沉稳的手按在对方佝偻的肩上。
“卡佩没了——是不是？”他专断地问。
埃龙眼里的惊恐更甚，无言作答。
“怎么没的？什么时候？”
可这人一时说不出话。一种近乎疯狂的恐惧攥着他。肖维兰不耐烦地骂了一声，转过身去。
“白兰地！”他冲阿尔芒简短地说。
橱里找出一瓶一盏。倒酒、递到埃龙唇边的，是圣茹斯特。肖维兰又愤又急，在屋里陵来陵去。
“定下神来，老兄，”过了一会儿他粗声说，“想法子告诉我出了什么事。”
埃龙已瘫坐进椅子，一只发抖的手在额头上抹了一两回。
“卡佩不见了，”他喃喃道，“多半是西蒙两口子搬家具那会儿被人摄走的。那个该咒的科什费尔，被骗了个透。”
埃龙说话有气无力，几乎不成声，像喉咙干、嘴里燥的人。可白兰地多少回了他的神，眼睛也失了先前那股呆滞的玻璃光。
“怎么回事？”肖维兰简短地问。
“他到的时候我正要出塔。我在门口同他说了话。我亲眼看着卡佩安顿进屋里，吩咐西蒙女人领科什费尔公民也看一眼，然后把小畜生锁进里屋，让科什费尔守在外间。我在外间同科什费尔说了几句。西蒙女人和杂役杜邦——我熟得很——正忙家具。那地方绝不可能藏得下别人——这个我可以打包票。

===Original===

Cochefer, after he took leave of me, went straight into the room; he found the woman Simon in the act of turning the key in the door of the inner chamber. ‘I have locked Capet in there,’ she said, giving the key to Cochefer; ‘he will be quite safe until tonight; when the other commissaries come.’ ”
“Didn’t Cochefer go into the room and ascertain whether the woman was lying?”
“Yes, he did! He made the woman reopen the door and peeped in over her shoulder. She said the child was asleep. He vows that he saw the child lying fully dressed on a rug in the further corner of the room. The room, of course, was quite empty of furniture and only lighted by one candle, but there was the rug and the child asleep on it. Cochefer swears he saw him, and now﻿—when I went up﻿—”
“Well?”
“The commissaries were all there﻿—Cochefer and Lasnière, Lorinet and Legrand. We went into the inner room, and I had a candle in my hand. We saw the child lying on the rug, just as Cochefer had seen him, and for a while we took no notice of it. Then someone﻿—I think it was Lorinet﻿—went to have a closer look at the brat. He took up the candle and went up to the rug. Then he gave a cry, and we all gathered round him. The sleeping child was only a bundle of hair and of clothes, a dummy﻿—what?”
There was silence now in the narrow room, while the white-faced clock continued to tick off each succeeding second of time. Héron had once more buried his head in his hands; a trembling﻿—like an attack of ague﻿—shook his wide, bony shoulders. Armand had listened to the narrative with glowing eyes and a beating heart. The details which the two Terrorists here could not probably understand he had already added to the picture which his mind had conjured up.
He was back in thought now in the small lodging in the rear of St. Germain l’Auxerrois; Sir Andrew Ffoulkes was there, and my Lord Tony and Hastings, and a man was striding up and down the room, looking out into the great space beyond the river with the eyes of a seer, and a firm voice said abruptly:
“It is about the Dauphin!”
“Have you any suspicions?” asked Chauvelin now, pausing in his walk beside Héron, and once more placing a firm, peremptory hand on his colleague’s shoulder.
“Suspicions!” exclaimed the chief agent with a loud oath. “Suspicions! Certainties, you mean.

===Chinese===

“科什费尔同我分手后径直进了屋；正撞见西蒙女人在里间门上拧钥匙。‘我把卡佩锁那里面了，’她说着把钥匙交给科什费尔；‘到今晚其他专员来之前，他稳稳当当。’
“科什费尔没进屋验证那女人撒没撒谎？”
“进了！他叫那女人重开了门，从她肩膀上方往里骏了一眼。她说孩子睡着了。他赌咒说他看见孩子衣裳齐整地躺在屋角那块地毯上。屋里家具自然搬空了，只有一支蜡烛照明，可地毯在，孩子睡在上头。科什费尔赌咒说他看见了；可如今——等我上去一看——”
“怎么样？”
“专员们全到了——科什费尔、拉尼埃、洛里内、勒格朗。我们进了里屋，我手里端着蜡烛。我们看见孩子躺在地毯上，同科什费尔看见的一模一样，一时谁也没在意。后来不知是谁——我记得是洛里内——过去细看那小畜生。他端起蜡烛走到地毯跟前，忽然一声喊，我们全围了上去。那睡着的孩子只是一团头发一堆衣裳，一个假人——你说！”
窄屋里一片寂静，只有那只白脸钟，还在一秒一秒地数。埃龙又把头埋进双手；一阵颤抖——像打摆子——搧着他宽阔的骨头肩膀。阿尔芒听着这段叙述，眼睛发光，心口砰砰地跳。这两个恐怖分子多半咂摸不出的细节，他早已添进心里那幅图景。
他的思绪此刻已飞回圣日耳曼洛克塞鲁瓦后面那间小寓所：安德鲁·福菲克斯爵士在那里，托尼勋爵和黑斯廷斯在那里；一个男人在屋里陵着大步，用先知的眼睛望向河对岸的旷远，一个坚定的嗓音突然说：
“事关王太子！”
“你有怀疑对象么？”肖维兰此刻问。他踱到埃龙身旁停下，又把一只沉稳专断的手搭上同僚的肩。
“怀疑！”首席密探大声咒骂着喊道，“怀疑！你是说，铁证如山。

===Original===

The man sat here but two days ago, in that very chair, and bragged of what he would do. I told him then that if he interfered with Capet I would wring his neck with my own hands.”
And his long, talon-like fingers, with their sharp, grimy nails, closed and unclosed like those of feline creatures when they hold the coveted prey.
“Of whom do you speak?” queried Chauvelin curtly.
“Of whom? Of whom but that accursed de Batz? His pockets are bulging with Austrian money, with which, no doubt, he has bribed the Simons and Cochefer and the sentinels﻿—”
“And Lorinet and Lasniere and you,” interposed Chauvelin dryly.
“It is false!” roared Héron, who already at the suggestion was foaming at the mouth, and had jumped up from his chair, standing at bay as if prepared to fight for his life.
“False, is it?” retorted Chauvelin calmly; “then be not so quick, friend Héron, in slashing out with senseless denunciations right and left. You’ll gain nothing by denouncing anyone just now. This is too intricate a matter to be dealt with a sledgehammer. Is anyone up in the Tower at this moment?” he asked in quiet, businesslike tones.
“Yes. Cochefer and the others are still there. They are making wild schemes to cover their treachery. Cochefer is aware of his own danger, and Lasniere and the others know that they arrived at the Tower several hours too late. They are all at fault, and they know it. As for that de Batz,” he continued with a voice rendered raucous with bitter passion, “I swore to him two days ago that he should not escape me if he meddled with Capet. I’m on his track already. I’ll have him before the hour of midnight, and I’ll torture him﻿—yes! I’ll torture him﻿—the Tribunal shall give me leave. We have a dark cell down below here where my men know how to apply tortures worse than the rack﻿—where they know just how to prolong life long enough to make it unendurable. I’ll torture him! I’ll torture him!”
But Chauvelin abruptly silenced the wretch with a curt command; then, without another word, he walked straight out of the room.
In thought Armand followed him. The wild desire was suddenly born in him to run away at this moment, while Héron, wrapped in his own meditations, was paying no heed to him.

===Chinese===

“那人两天前就坐在这儿，就坐那把椅子上，吹他有多能耐。我当时就告诉他：他敢碰卡佩，我亲手拧断他的脖子。”
他那长长的、鹰爪似的手指——指甲尖而乌黑——一屈一伸，像猫科动物攋住垂涎猎物时的样子。
“你说谁？”肖维兰简短地问。
“说谁？除了那个该咒的德巴茨还能说谁？他口袋里奥地利银子撑得鼓鼓的，十有八九买通了西蒙两口子、科什费尔，还有哨兵——”
“还有洛里内、拉尼埃，和你。”肖维兰干巴巴地插了一句。
“胡说！”埃龙咆哮。这指控已叫他口吐白沫，从椅子上跳起，作势负隅，仿佛要拼死一搏。
“胡说，是么？”肖维兰平静地反问，“那就别这么急，埃龙朋友，抡起莫名其妙的告发左右开弓。现在告谁都没好处。这桩案子太错综，不是抡大锤砸得开的。此刻塔上还有谁？”他换上安静、办公事的口吻问。
“有。科什费尔和其余几个还在。他们正撒着欢儿编幌子遮自己的叛卖。科什费尔明白自己危险，拉尼埃他们也知道自己到塔上晚了整整几个钟头。人人有责，人人心里有数。至于那个德巴茨，”他接下去，嗓音被狠毒的激愤磨得嘶哑，“两天前我对他发过誓：他敢插手卡佩，休想逃出我手心。我已经追上他的踪了。午夜之前我拿到他，我要上刑——对！我要上刑——法庭会准我。我们楼下有间黑牢，我手下人会使比拉肢架还狠的刑——会恰到好处地吊住命，吊得人生不如死。我要上刑！我要上刑！”
肖维兰一声简短的喝止，把这番疯话掐了；随后一言不发，径直出了屋。
阿尔芒的心思跟着他去了。一个狂野的念头蓦地在他心里生出：趁这会儿逃走——埃龙正沉在自己的盘算里，没把他放在心上。

===Original===

Chauvelin’s footsteps had long ago died away in the distance; it was a long way to the upper floor of the Tower, and some time would be spent, too, in interrogating the commissaries. This was Armand’s opportunity. After all, if he were free himself he might more effectually help to rescue Jeanne. He knew, too, now where to join his leader. The corner of the street by the canal, where Sir Andrew Ffoulkes would be waiting with the coal-cart; then there was the spinney on the road to St. Germain. Armand hoped that, with good luck, he might yet overtake his comrades, tell them of Jeanne’s plight, and entreat them to work for her rescue.
He had forgotten that now he had no certificate of safety, that undoubtedly he would be stopped at the gates at this hour of the night; that his conduct proving suspect he would in all probability he detained, and, mayhap, be brought back to this selfsame place within an hour. He had forgotten all that, for the primeval instinct for freedom had suddenly been aroused. He rose softly from his chair and crossed the room. Héron paid no attention to him. Now he had traversed the antechamber and unlatched the outer door.
Immediately a couple of bayonets were crossed in front of him, two more further on ahead scintillated feebly in the flickering light. Chauvelin had taken his precautions. There was no doubt that Armand St. Just was effectually a prisoner now.
With a sigh of disappointment he went back to his place beside the fire. Héron had not even moved whilst he had made this futile attempt at escape. Five minutes later Chauvelin reentered the room.

===Chinese===

肖维兰的脚步声早已远去；塔的顶层离这儿路远，盘问那几位专员也要耽搁一阵。这是阿尔芒的机会。说到底，他自己脱了身，救让娜才更使得上力。何况他如今知道去哪儿寻首领了：运河边街角，安德鲁·福菲克斯爵士会守着煤车在那儿等；还有去圣日耳曼路上的小树林。阿尔芒盼着运气好，兴许还追得上伙伴们，告诉他们让娜的险境，求他们设法营救。
他忘了：他如今没有安分证，这个时辰到城门准被拦下；行迹可疑，十有八九被扣，兴许不出一个钟头就被押回这同一间屋子。这些他全忘了：求自由的原初本能猛然醒了。他轻手轻脚从椅子上起身，穿过屋子。埃龙没理会他。他已穿过外间，拨开了外门的门闩。
立刻两杆刺刀交叉在他面前，更前面还有两杆，在曳曳灯光里微微发亮。肖维兰早有布防。毫无疑问，阿尔芒·圣茹斯特如今已是实实在在的囚徒。
他一声失望的叹息，回到炉边原位。他那番徒劳的逃亡尝试，埃龙竟连动也没动。五分钟后，肖维兰回到屋里。

## XX The Certificate of Safety / XX 通行凭证
===Original===

“You can leave de Batz and his gang alone, citizen Héron,” said Chauvelin, as soon as he had closed the door behind him; “he had nothing to do with the escape of the Dauphin.”
Héron growled out a few words of incredulity. But Chauvelin shrugged his shoulders and looked with unutterable contempt on his colleague. Armand, who was watching him closely, saw that in his hand he held a small piece of paper, which he had crushed into a shapeless mass.
“Do not waste your time, citizen,” he said, “in raging against an empty windbag. Arrest de Batz if you like, or leave him alone an you please﻿—we have nothing to fear from that braggart.”
With nervous, slightly shaking fingers he set to work to smooth out the scrap of paper which he held. His hot hands had soiled it and pounded it until it was a mere rag and the writing on it illegible. But, such as it was, he threw it down with a blasphemous oath on the desk in front of Héron’s eyes.
“It is that accursed Englishman who has been at work again,” he said more calmly; “I guessed it the moment I heard your story. Set your whole army of sleuthhounds on his track, citizen; you’ll need them all.”
Héron picked up the scrap of torn paper and tried to decipher the writing on it by the light from the lamp. He seemed almost dazed now with the awful catastrophe that had befallen him, and the fear that his own wretched life would have to pay the penalty for the disappearance of the child.
As for Armand﻿—even in the midst of his own troubles, and of his own anxiety for Jeanne, he felt a proud exultation in his heart. The Scarlet Pimpernel had succeeded; Percy had not failed in his self-imposed undertaking. Chauvelin, whose piercing eyes were fixed on him at that moment, smiled with contemptuous irony.
“As you will find your hands overfull for the next few hours, citizen Héron,” he said, speaking to his colleague and nodding in the direction of Armand, “I’ll not trouble you with the voluntary confession this young citizen desired to make to you. All I need tell you is that he is an adherent of the Scarlet Pimpernel﻿—I believe one of his most faithful, most trusted officers.”
Héron roused himself from the maze of gloomy thoughts that were again paralysing his tongue. He turned bleary, wild eyes on Armand.
“We have got one of them, then?” he murmured incoherently, babbling like a drunken man.
“M’yes!”

===Chinese===

“德巴茨和他那伙人，你尽可以攦开不管了，埃龙公民，”肖弗兰关上身后的门，立即说道，“王太子出逃的事，与他无干。”
埃龙吼出几句不信的话。肖弗兰只耸耸肩，用说不尽的轻蔑望着他的同僚。阿尔芒紧盯着他，看见他手里摃着一小片纸，已被攮成一团不成形的疙瘩。
“不必浪费时间，公民，”他说，“去跟一个空风箱动火。德巴茨，你愿意便抓，不愿意便随他去——那个牛皮匠，没什么可怕的。”
他用紧张而微微发抖的手指，把那片纸慢慢抚平。滚烫的手已把它污了、揉烂了，成了一条破布，字迹漫漶难辨。可就是这样，他还是把它往埃龙眼前的桌上一摔，咒天骂地赌了个咒。
“又是那个该死的英国人做的手脚，”他平静了些，说，“一听你讲经过，我便猜着了。把你那一整军的猎犬全放出去追他吧，公民；一兵一卒也少不得。”
埃龙拾起那片撕皱的纸，凑着灯光想辨认上面的字。此刻他几乎已被这场临头大祸砸懵了——孩子失踪，他自己那条贱命，怕要拿去抵账。
至于阿尔芒——即便身处自身的困厄、心悬让娜的安危，他心里也涌起一阵骄傲的狂喜。红花侠成功了；珀西没有辜负他自领的重任。肖弗兰那双锐利的眼睛正钉在他身上，带着轻蔑的讥诮笑了。
“往后的几个钟头，你的两只手还不够用呢，埃龙公民，”他对同僚说，朝阿尔芒那边点了点头，“这位年轻公民本想向你投案自首，我也就不拿这个来烦你了。只消告诉你一句：他是红花侠的同党——而且我相信，是他手下最忠实、最亲信的干将之一。”
埃龙从那团又麻痹了他舌头的阴惨思绪里挣出来，转过一双浑浊狂乱的眼睛，望着阿尔芒。
“这么说，逮住他们一个了？”他语无伦次地咕哝，像醉汉一样絮叨。
“唔——是！”

===Original===

replied Chauvelin lightly; “but it is too late now for a formal denunciation and arrest. He cannot leave Paris anyhow, and all that your men need to do is to keep a close lookout on him. But I should send him home tonight if I were you.”
Héron muttered something more, which, however, Armand did not understand. Chauvelin’s words were still ringing in his ear. Was he, then, to be set free tonight? Free in a measure, of course, since spies were to be set to watch him﻿—but free, nevertheless? He could not understand Chauvelin’s attitude, and his own self-love was not a little wounded at the thought that he was of such little account that these men could afford to give him even this provisional freedom. And, of course, there was still Jeanne.
“I must, therefore, bid you good night, citizen,” Chauvelin was saying in his bland, gently ironical manner. “You will be glad to return to your lodgings. As you see, the chief agent of the Committee of General Security is too much occupied just now to accept the sacrifice of your life which you were prepared so generously to offer him.”
“I do not understand you, citizen,” retorted Armand coldly, “nor do I desire indulgence at your hands. You have arrested an innocent woman on the trumped-up charge that she was harbouring me. I came here tonight to give myself up to justice so that she might be set free.”
“But the hour is somewhat late, citizen,” rejoined Chauvelin urbanely. “The lady in whom you take so fervent an interest is no doubt asleep in her cell at this hour. It would not be fitting to disturb her now. She might not find shelter before morning, and the weather is quite exceptionally unpropitious.”
“Then, sir,” said Armand, a little bewildered, “am I to understand that if I hold myself at your disposition Mademoiselle Lange will be set free as early tomorrow morning as may be?”
“No doubt, sir﻿—no doubt,” replied Chauvelin with more than his accustomed blandness; “if you will hold yourself entirely at our disposition, Mademoiselle Lange will be set free tomorrow. I think that we can safely promise that, citizen Héron, can we not?” he added, turning to his colleague.
But Héron, overcome with the stress of emotions, could only murmur vague, unintelligible words.
“Your word on that, citizen Chauvelin?” asked Armand.
“My word on it an you will accept it.”
“No, I will not do that.

===Chinese===

肖弗兰轻巧地答道；“不过此刻正式告发、正式拘捕，都太晚了。反正他出不了巴黎；你的人只消盯紧他就是。若依我，今晚就放他回家去。”
埃龙又咕哝了几句什么，阿尔芒没有听懂。肖弗兰的话还在他耳边嗡嗡作响。这么说，今晚他就要被放了？自然，只是有限度的自由——还会派密探盯着他——可到底是放了？他弄不懂肖弗兰的路数；想到自己竟无足轻重到这个地步，这几个人连这点暂时的自由都舍得给他，他的自尊又着实受了几分伤。当然，还有让娜。
“那么，我只好向你道晚安了，公民，”肖弗兰正用他那温文而微带讥诮的口吻说着，“回你的住处去，你该高兴了。你也看见了，治安委员会的总密探眼下实在忙不过来，你那条慨然欲舍的性命，他老人家暂且顾不上领受。”
“我不懂你的意思，公民，”阿尔芒冷冷回敬，“我也不稀罕你们的宽纵。你们捏造罪名，说一位无辜的女子窝藏我，把她抓了。我今晚来，就是投案自首，好换她自由。”
“可是时辰未免晚了些，公民，”肖弗兰彬彬有礼地接道，“你如此热切惦念的那位女士，此刻想必正在囚室里安睡。这会儿去搅扰她，不甚相宜。天亮前她也未必寻得到落脚处，何况天气又这样坏得出奇。”
“那么，先生，”阿尔芒有些发懵地说，“我是否可以这样理解：只要我听凭你们处置，朗热小姐明天一早便能得到自由，越早越好？”
“毫无疑问，先生——毫无疑问，”肖弗兰答道，那份和气比平常又添了几分，“只要你全然听凭我们处置，朗热小姐明天便获释。我想，这个我们大可以担保，埃龙公民，是不是？”他转向同僚，补了一句。
可埃龙被这一连串变故压垮了，只能含含糊糊咕哝几个谁也听不懂的字。
“你以言辞担保么，肖弗兰公民？”阿尔芒问。
“我以言辞担保——只要你肯受。”
“不，我不受这个。

===Original===

Give me an unconditional certificate of safety and I will believe you.”
“Of what use were that to you?” asked Chauvelin.
“I believe my capture to be of more importance to you than that of Mademoiselle Lange,” said Armand quietly. “I will use the certificate of safety for myself or one of my friends if you break your word to me anent Mademoiselle Lange.”
“H’m! the reasoning is not illogical, citizen,” said Chauvelin, whilst a curious smile played round the corners of his thin lips. “You are quite right. You are a more valuable asset to us than the charming lady who, I hope, will for many a day and year to come delight pleasure-loving Paris with her talent and her grace.”
“Amen to that, citizen,” said Armand fervently.
“Well, it will all depend on you, sir! Here,” he added, coolly running over some papers on Héron’s desk until he found what he wanted, “is an absolutely unconditional certificate of safety. The Committee of General Security issue very few of these. It is worth the cost of a human life. At no barrier or gate of any city can such a certificate be disregarded, nor even can it be detained. Allow me to hand it to you, citizen, as a pledge of my own good faith.”
Smiling, urbane, with a curious look that almost expressed amusement lurking in his shrewd, pale eyes, Chauvelin handed the momentous document to Armand.
The young man studied it very carefully before he slipped it into the inner pocket of his coat.
“How soon shall I have news of Mademoiselle Lange?” he asked finally.
“In the course of tomorrow. I myself will call on you and redeem that precious document in person. You, on the other hand, will hold yourself at my disposition. That’s understood, is it not?”
“I shall not fail you. My lodgings are﻿—”
“Oh! do not trouble,” interposed Chauvelin, with a polite bow; “we can find that out for ourselves.”
Héron had taken no part in this colloquy. Now that Armand prepared to go he made no attempt to detain him, or to question his colleague’s actions. He sat by the table like a log; his mind was obviously a blank to all else save to his own terrors engendered by the events of this night.
With bleary, half-veiled eyes he followed Armand’s progress through the room, and seemed unaware of the loud slamming of the outside door. Chauvelin had escorted the young man past the first line of sentry, then he took cordial leave of him.

===Chinese===

给我一张无条件的通行凭证，我便信你。”
“那东西于你有何用？”肖弗兰问。
“我想，抓到我，比抓到朗热小姐，对你们要紧得多，”阿尔芒平静地说，“你们若在朗热小姐的事上失信于我，我便拿这张凭证，救我自己，或救我的一位朋友。”
“唔！这番推理，倒不算不合逻辑，公民，”肖弗兰说，薄薄的嘴角浮起一丝古怪的笑意。“你说得不错。比起那位迷人的小姐，你于我们是更有价值的家当——至于她，我盼她凭才艺风致，还不知要给这贪欢的巴黎添多少年的乐子呢。”
“这一句但愿应验，公民。”阿尔芒热切地说。
“好，那便全看你自己的了，先生！喏，”他又冷冷地翻检埃龙桌上的几份文书，直到找到要找的那张，“这是一张绝对无条件的通行凭证。治安委员会签发这种凭证，为数极少。它值一条人命的价钱。任何城市的关卡城门，见了它都不容驳回，连扣留也不容。请容我把它交到您手上，公民——权当我个人诚意的一件押物。”
肖弗兰微笑着，温文尔雅，精明淡漠的眼底潜着一丝近乎打趣的神气，把这张干系重大的文书递给阿尔芒。
年轻人仔仔细细验看了一遍，才把它收进上衣内袋。
“朗热小姐的消息，几时能到？”他末了问。
“明天之内。我亲自登门拜望，把那张贵重文书赎回来。而您呢，则听凭我的处置。这算是讲定了，是不是？”
“我不失信。我住在——”
“哦！不必费神，”肖弗兰彬彬有礼地一躬，插断道，“我们自己查得出来。”
这场对答，埃龙始终没有插一句。阿尔芒要走，他也不拦，也不质问同僚的行止。他木头一般坐在桌边；除了今夜变故生出的自身恐惧，脑子里显然一片空白。
他眯着一双半遮的浊眼，目送阿尔芒穿过房间；大门砰的一声大响，他也仿佛无所觉。肖弗兰送年轻人过了头一道岗哨，然后热热络络地道了别。

===Original===

“Your certificate will, you will find, open every gate to you. Good night, citizen. *A demain.*”
“Good night.”
Armand’s slim figure disappeared in the gloom. Chauvelin watched him for a few moments until even his footsteps had died away in the distance; then he turned back towards Héron’s lodgings.
“*À nous deux*,” he muttered between tightly clenched teeth; “*à nous deux* once more, my enigmatical Scarlet Pimpernel.”

===Chinese===

“您那张凭证，您会发现，替您开得动每一道门。晚安，公民。*明日见。*（*A demain.*）”
“晚安。”
阿尔芒颀长的身影消失在昏暝里。肖弗兰目送他片刻，直到连脚步声也在远处消尽；然后，他转身折回埃龙的住处。
“*该咱俩算账了*（*À nous deux*），”他从咬紧的牙缝里挤出一句，“*该咱俩算账了*，又来一回——我那猜不透的红花侠。”

## XXI Back to Paris / 重返巴黎
===Original===

It was an exceptionally dark night, and the rain was falling in torrents. Sir Andrew Ffoulkes, wrapped in a piece of sacking, had taken shelter right underneath the coal-cart; even then he was getting wet through to the skin.
He had worked hard for two days coal-heaving, and the night before he had found a cheap, squalid lodging where at any rate he was protected from the inclemencies of the weather; but tonight he was expecting Blakeney at the appointed hour and place. He had secured a cart of the ordinary ramshackle pattern used for carrying coal. Unfortunately there were no covered ones to be obtained in the neighbourhood, and equally unfortunately the thaw had set in with a blustering wind and driving rain, which made waiting in the open air for hours at a stretch and in complete darkness excessively unpleasant.
But for all these discomforts Sir Andrew Ffoulkes cared not one jot. In England, in his magnificent Suffolk home, he was a confirmed sybarite, in whose service every description of comfort and luxury had to be enrolled. Here tonight in the rough and tattered clothes of a coal-heaver, drenched to the skin, and crouching under the body of a cart that hardly sheltered him from the rain, he was as happy as a schoolboy out for a holiday.
Happy, but vaguely anxious.
He had no means of ascertaining the time. So many of the church-bells and clock towers had been silenced recently that not one of those welcome sounds penetrated to the dreary desolation of this canal wharf, with its abandoned carts standing ghostlike in a row. Darkness had set in very early in the afternoon, and the heavers had given up work soon after four o’clock.
For about an hour after that a certain animation had still reigned round the wharf, men crossing and going, one or two of the barges moving in or out alongside the quay. But for some time now darkness and silence had been the masters in this desolate spot, and that time had seemed to Sir Andrew an eternity. He had hobbled and tethered his horse, and stretched himself out at full length under the cart. Now and again he had crawled out from under this uncomfortable shelter and walked up and down in ankle-deep mud, trying to restore circulation in his stiffened limbs; now and again a kind of torpor had come over him, and he had fallen into a brief and restless sleep.

===Chinese===

夜黑得异乎寻常，雨瓢泼似的下。安德鲁·福菲克斯爵士裹着一片麻袋布，躲在煤车正底下；饶是这样，也淋了个透。
他连着两天扛煤，昨夜总算寻着个廉价破败的落脚处，好歹避了风雨；今晚却要在约定的时辰地点候布莱克尼。他弄到的，是运煤常用的那种碰散架的敞车。不巧左近雇不到带篷的；更不巧解冻带着狂风和瓢雨，一连几个钟头在露天里、在伸手不见五指的黑里干等，实在不好受。
可这些苦，安德鲁·福菲克斯爵士毫不在意。在英格兰，在他苏福克那座堂皇的宅子里，他是坐实了的享乐派，种种舒适奢华都得听他差遣；今夜在这里，穿着扛煤工人的破衣，浑身透湿，蜷在几乎遮不住雨的车板底下，他却快活得像个放假的学童。
快活，却隐隐地焦。
他没法知道时辰。教堂的钟和钟塔近来拆的拆、哑的哑，没有一声欢迎的钟响传到运河码头这片凄凉的荒地来；弃着的车一排，像鬼影。下午天黑得早，四点刚过，扛煤的就撂了工。
那之后约莫一个钟头，码头四周还有点人气，人来人往，一两条驳船贴着岸进出。可这会儿，黑暗和寂静早已做了这荒地主人；这段时光，在安德鲁爵士眼里长得像一世。他把马绊好拴牢，在车下伸直了躺。时不时从这不舒服的檐底下爬出来，在没踝的泥里来回走，好叫僵了的肢体回暖；时不时一阵昏沉袭来，他便坠入短暂而不安的睡眠。

===Original===

He would at this moment have given half his fortune for knowledge of the exact time.
But through all this weary waiting he was never for a moment in doubt. Unlike Armand St. Just, he had the simplest, most perfect faith in his chief. He had been Blakeney’s constant companion in all these adventures for close upon four years now; the thought of failure, however vague, never once entered his mind.
He was only anxious for his chief’s welfare. He knew that he would succeed, but he would have liked to have spared him much of the physical fatigue and the nerve-racking strain of these hours that lay between the daring deed and the hope of safety. Therefore he was conscious of an acute tingling of his nerves, which went on even during the brief patches of fitful sleep, and through the numbness that invaded his whole body while the hours dragged wearily and slowly along.
Then, quite suddenly, he felt wakeful and alert; quite a while﻿—even before he heard the welcome signal﻿—he knew, with a curious, subtle sense of magnetism, that the hour had come, and that his chief was somewhere near by, not very far.
Then he heard the cry﻿—a seamew’s call﻿—repeated thrice at intervals, and five minutes later something loomed out of the darkness quite close to the hind wheels of the cart.
“Hist! Ffoulkes!” came in a soft whisper, scarce louder than the wind.
“Present!” came in quick response.
“Here, help me to lift the child into the cart. He is asleep, and has been a dead weight on my arm for close on an hour now. Have you a dry bit of sacking or something to lay him on?”
“Not very dry, I am afraid.”
With tender care the two men lifted the sleeping little King of France into the rickety cart. Blakeney laid his cloak over him, and listened for awhile to the slow regular breathing of the child.
“St. Just is not here﻿—you know that?” said Sir Andrew after a while.
“Yes, I knew it,” replied Blakeney curtly.
It was characteristic of these two men that not a word about the adventure itself, about the terrible risks and dangers of the past few hours, was exchanged between them. The child was here and was safe, and Blakeney knew the whereabouts of St. Just﻿—that was enough for Sir Andrew Ffoulkes, the most devoted follower, the most perfect friend the Scarlet Pimpernel would ever know.

===Chinese===

此刻他情愿拿一半家产，换一个准确的钟点。
可在这场疲惫的守候里，他从没有一刻犹疑过。同阿尔芒·圣茹斯特不一样，他对首领抱着最简单、最完全的信心。这些冒险里，他做布莱克尼的固定伙伴快四个年头了；失败这个念头，哪怕再模糊，也从不曾进过他的脑子。
他只是挂心首领的安危。他知道他会成；可那些钟点里——胆大包天的行动同脱险的希望之间的那些钟点——他多么替他省去几分体力的耗损、神经的煎熬。因此他神经里那阵尖锐的刺麻始终不停，连断续的浅睡里也在；几个钟头拖沓地爬过去，那股麻木透进全身，它也还在。
忽然间，他清醒了，警醒了；老早——在那声欢迎的信号之前——他就凭着一股奇妙的、磁一般的感觉知道：时辰到了，首领就在近处，不远。
然后他听见了叫声——一声海鸥的啼——隔一阵一声，啼了三遍；五分钟后，一个影子从黑暗里冒出来，就在车后轮旁边。
“嘘！福菲克斯！”一声轻悄的耳语，比风声大不了多少。
“在！”立刻一声回音。
“来，帮我把这孩子抬上车。他睡着了，在我胳膊上已经死沉死沉压了快一个钟头。有块干麻袋布什么的给他垫垫么？”
“怕是不大干。”
两人小心翼翼，把熟睡的法兰西小国王抬上摇摇晃晃的煤车。布莱克尼把自己的斗篷盖在他身上，听了一会儿孩子缓慢均匀的呼吸。
“圣茹斯特不在这里——你知道吧？”过了一会儿，安德鲁爵士说。
“是，我知道。”布莱克尼简短地答。
这两个人就是这样：冒险本身、过去几个钟头里可怕的凶险，彼此一句不提。孩子在这儿了、平安了，布莱克尼也知道圣茹斯特的下落——对安德鲁·福菲克斯，这便是够了。他是红花侠平生所能有的最忠的追随者、最完美的朋友。

===Original===
